Tributary

Prologue: The Men Who Could Answer

The woman who had washed his father’s body came to Constano while he was eating an egg.

He had not wanted the egg. Someone had cut the top from it and set it in the silver cup his father disliked, the one with a foot too narrow for its bowl. When Constano put his spoon into it, the cup rocked. He caught it with his other hand.

The woman stood inside the door with her hands folded at her waist. There was a white mark across one knuckle where she had burned herself, years ago. He knew the mark better than he knew her name.

“Sit down,” he said.

“I have a matter, my lord.”

“Yes. Sit down for it.”

She chose the chair by the door. He had meant the one opposite him but did not correct her. He could hear men in the courtyard taking apart the trestles. The procession had returned less than an hour before, and already the house was putting things away.

“The duke said my boy was to have the tenancy.”

Constano set the spoon beside his plate.

“At the willow sluice?”

“Yes, my lord.”

“What did he say?”

“He said when Master Harl died, we were to have it. My boy and his wife. The cottage and the six strips, and the use of the water as Harl had it.”

He remembered now. Her name was Nessa. Her son had lost two fingers clearing a root out of a sluice while the river was high. There had been an argument over who had ordered the work done. His father had come down to the accounts room to complain about the argument, and Constano had told him that establishing who had given the order was necessary if they meant to prevent it happening again.

His father had said something impatient. They had both been right. Constano could remember the argument almost word for word, and could not remember what they had done about the boy.

“Has Harl died?”

“Yesterday morning.”

Yesterday morning there had been other things to do. He had not been told.

“The duke gave his word,” Nessa said.

“I believe you.”

Her hands moved against one another.

“The steward said he would need to see it.”

“Yes.”

“He said it might have been written somewhere else.”

“Yes.”

“The duke didn’t write everything he said.”

Constano looked at the egg. Its cut lid lay beside the cup with a little of the white clinging to it. He had been about to eat that too, from habit. His father had eaten the lid first and complained that people who threw it away had never kept hens.

“No,” he said. “He didn’t.”

Nessa waited.

The willow sluice had six strips of good soil because the house maintained the drain below them. In a dry year the tenant paid his rent in money. In a wet year he helped keep the banks. Harl’s grandson had married a woman with three children and was already living in the cottage. Constano had signed the permission himself.

“Does your boy have somewhere to live now?”

“With us.”

“And his wife?”

“With us.”

He could hear a hammer in the courtyard. The man using it struck three times, stopped, struck again. A nail had bent.

“I cannot send Harl’s grandson out this afternoon,” Constano said.

Nessa’s face did not change. He would have found it easier if it had.

“I can give your son the empty cottage below the mill. The roof needs work. The house will do it. He can have the garden this year and the six strips when the present sowing is brought in. Harl’s grandson keeps the sluice cottage until I have spoken to him.”

“Is that what the duke said?”

“No. It is what I can do.”

She looked at him for a moment, then down at her hands.

“Will you write it?”

He reached for the bell.

The letter from Istovar had been laid beneath the salt cellar to keep it flat.

Constano read it again after Nessa left. Harald’s seal was whole on the separate sheet that announced his father’s death; the covering letter had been opened and folded and opened until the paper had begun to part along one edge. There were four questions in it. Two concerned holdings on which both houses collected something. One concerned an escort. The fourth was written in a different ink, after the letter had otherwise been finished.

What arrangements did our fathers intend for the lower passage?

Oswyn had been dead for some time before the letter arrived. The roads were poor, and the man carrying it had waited at a crossing for an escort that had been ordered somewhere else. Constano had received it beside his father’s bed. Braedyn had been asleep. The physician had asked him to come back later.

Later the old man had not been able to speak.

Constano drew a clean sheet toward him. He wrote Harald’s name, the proper style, the necessary condolences. Then he stopped.

There had been arrangements. He knew there had. Oswyn had come to Tarlington once without announcing himself and eaten two portions of a fish his father insisted was badly cooked. They had quarrelled for an hour in the garden. The next week the lower passage had opened and three families had stopped paying two tolls.

Constano had asked what had been agreed.

“Enough,” his father had said.

He had been very pleased with himself.

Constano put down the pen and laughed once, without meaning to. There was nobody in the room to hear it.

He wrote that the existing passage should remain open on its current terms until representatives of both houses could meet. He offered the accounts that survived. He did not promise that they contained the agreement.

Then he wrote a separate order for the Fairport factor.

He would come himself.

His brother Braedyn, the third of the five, came in while the Fairport instructions were drying.

Braedyn the Younger had taken off his mourning cloak but still wore the black band at his sleeve. Their father’s name had suited him less comfortably with each year that passed. Now nobody in the house seemed certain whether to continue adding the distinction, and he had begun supplying it himself before anyone could hesitate.

“There are six people downstairs who want to speak to the duke,” he said.

Constano looked towards the empty chair.

“I told them you would hear them after you ate.”

“I have eaten.”

“Then after you’ve eaten something somebody else has seen you eat.”

Outside, a hay cart rumbled beneath the window. The driver called to the porter to lift the bar. Grass clung to the cart’s wheels, bright against the dark earth. Black cloth hung from the stair rail inside the house, tied clear of the newel where servants carrying dishes would otherwise have caught it. Constano had paid for the mourning cloth and the harvest wages on the same sheet that morning.

His brother sat without waiting to be asked.

“Wymund has gone back to the river.”

“I know.”

“He said to tell you he took the grey.”

“He always takes the grey.”

“He wanted there to be something ordinary in the message.”

Constano looked at him. The younger man was not smiling.

“For a while,” Braedyn said, “I thought he was going to remain until you ordered him away. Then a rider came.”

“Was there news?”

“There was work.”

They sat with that. Beyond the open door a servant passed carrying the silver cup their father used at supper. Halfway along the passage she stopped, looked down at it, and went back the way she had come.

“Will you go to Wymund?” Constano asked.

“Yes. He needs somebody the barons can insult without beginning a battle.”

“Try not to enjoy it.”

“I shall be inconsolable.”

Constano smiled despite himself. His brother had always been good at giving people a moment in which to recover without making the recovery visible.

“Lyonel is still with the northern train?”

“Yes. If he has found a way to be heard from here, I haven’t discovered it.”

“He’ll write.”

“He’ll dictate to somebody with a larger hand.”

The younger Braedyn took a piece of bread from the tray and put it in front of Constano. He did not tell him again to eat. They discussed which officers would remain at Tarlington, who had the keys to the lower stores, and how much of the old household could be kept together without leaving Fairport short of people who knew the family’s business.

When his brother left, Constano drew another sheet towards him.

The letter to Riarhin was brief. He told her his father had died, that he had not been alone, and where he had been buried. He did not try to tell her what Braedyn might have wished to say. If the old man had said anything intended for her, she would have known its meaning better than Constano could.

He addressed it care of the last commission from which she had written. He did not know whether she was still there. On the outer fold he asked that it be sent onward if a better direction was known.

He left room at the bottom for something personal, sat looking at the space, and finally signed his familiar name.

Sister Oria found him in the passage outside the study.

“You’ve eaten?” she asked.

“Some.”

“Then go to bed.”

“It is not yet noon.”

“You have been awake since the night before last.”

He leaned his weight onto the stick. The long walk behind the bier had hurt him. Standing still afterward had been worse. He had arranged the return through the nearer court so that he would not have to climb the south steps in front of everybody, and then hated himself for thinking of it.

Oria glanced toward the study door.

“Do you want me to come in with you?”

“No.”

She did not tell him that his father would have wanted anything. He was grateful enough for that to look at her.

“Will you stay until tomorrow?”

“Yes.”

“There are people who will want to ask you things.”

“They have already begun.”

He nodded. She put her hand briefly on his sleeve and went down the passage, lifting the front of her robe clear of a basin a servant had left against the wall.

The study smelled of the same things it had always smelled of: wood smoke, leather, the bitter ointment the physician had used on his father’s hands. A window stood open. Someone had taken the medicine bottles away, but the pale rings where they had rested were still on the table.

The board was where it had been.

Constano did not go near it. There was a coat over the back of the other chair, his father’s old brown coat with one cuff let down farther than the other. He stood looking at it until his hip began to shake.

He had brought the Fairport order to use the desk. There were other desks in the house. He had one of his own.

After a while he sat in his father’s chair and found that it was too low.

He signed the order there anyway.

Part I: The War Without Them

Chapter One: A Day at the Crossing

Seniro took the captain’s hand off the map.

It was a large hand, with two rings on it, and it had been covering the only road that mattered for the last quarter of an hour.

“There,” he said. “Now we can both see it.”

Captain Avren drew himself upright. He had served under Seniro for seven years and still did it when embarrassed.

“The mill road is impassable.”

“To a wagon.”

“To a horse as well, my lord.”

“To your horse.”

“My horse went to the girth.”

“Then we won’t send your horse.”

The young officer at the end of the table laughed. Seniro looked at him until he stopped.

Outside the mill, someone was hammering an iron shoe onto a cartwheel. Each blow rang through the wall and made the little brass weight on the map tremble. Seniro put his palm over it.

The crossing at Ashford was a narrow bridge over one of the eastern feeders of the Belworth. It had a hump in the middle and low stone parapets worn smooth by generations of loads too wide for it. The road from the western meadows approached its northern end through a narrow strip of firm ground. On the southern bank, a lane led east past the miller’s orchard; another followed the water west to the sheepwash, where carts could cross back to the northern stores without using the bridge road.

Valdais’s forward men had reached the meadow road the evening before. They had lit fires well back from the bridge and eaten supper, which Seniro disliked more than an attack.

“How many?”

“Two hundred at the fires. More behind them.”

“How many more?”

“We couldn’t see.”

“Good. Keep that distinction.”

He lifted the weight and folded the map along the river.

“The wagons go west to the sheepwash. Across there, then up to the stores. The enemy has the bridge road watched, not the bank below it.”

“The sheepwash won’t take them.”

“It will when we put the mill floor in it.”

The miller, who had been standing beside the door, said, “My lord.”

Seniro turned.

The man was holding his cap. White flour lay in the creases of his ears. He had spent half the night carrying grain out of the bins and had not yet been told whether anybody would pay him for it.

“What is underneath your floor?” Seniro asked.

“Air.”

“How much?”

“Six feet in places.”

“And can you grind with no grain?”

“No.”

“Then we will try to leave you the machinery.”

“My father laid that floor.”

Seniro looked at the man’s hands on the cap.

“Have him show my men how it comes up.”

“He’s dead.”

“Then you.”

He rode out to the bridge before the first boards had been lifted.

The horse did not like the smell from the millrace. Something had died against the grating. Seniro leaned down and rubbed the animal’s neck until it went forward. He had ridden it north with the queen and south again on orders that had reached him after two changes of messenger. There were white rubs beneath both sides of the saddle. His groom had asked him to change mounts. He had said he would tomorrow.

On the bridge, an archer stood with his bow under his arm and a piece of cloth over the string. He was watching the northern approach.

“What can you see?” Seniro asked.

“A man pissing behind a willow.”

“Anything else?”

“He’s taking his time.”

Seniro looked. The man behind the willow finished, adjusted himself, and went back toward the enemy fires.

“Someone ought to tell him there are archers here.”

“He knows, my lord. He waved.”

Beyond the fires there were no banners. The men sat in groups small enough that a frightened scout might count them twice. A wagon stood across the road with its team still in harness. They had not unharnessed because they did not mean to stay long.

Seniro dismounted and handed the reins to the archer.

“Don’t let him eat the parapet. He thinks moss is breakfast.”

He went down the southern bank to where his men were lifting stones from the bridge’s nearest abutment. The mason had marked the ones they could remove without bringing the whole thing down before they were ready. A boy with a hammer looked up at him and then immediately down again.

“How long?” Seniro asked the mason.

“Until the last wagon is over.”

“That is when. I asked how long.”

“An hour afterward. Perhaps less.”

“You have half.”

“Then perhaps more.”

Seniro grinned. The mason did not.

“An hour,” he said. “Tell me if it changes.”

The boy with the hammer followed him as far as the stable yard.

“My lord.”

Seniro stopped. The boy had lime in his hair and a bruise on the side of his neck. He looked at the horse before he looked at the marshal.

“My mother’s house is beyond the bridge.”

“Which one?”

“Above the willow. White wall. She won’t leave the goats.”

Seniro could see the roof from where he stood. Beyond it, the enemy’s little groups were still enlarging. Men had begun taking off their boots to dry their feet. They were making themselves comfortable while someone elsewhere decided how many of them should die for a bridge.

“When did you see her?”

“Before light.”

“Did she have a chance to come over?”

“I told her to.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

The boy’s mouth tightened. Seniro recognised the beginning of tears fought back through anger and regretted his tone.

“Yes,” the boy said.

“How many goats?”

“Four.”

“Go and ask your mother to bring the four goats. Say I will feed them on this side.”

“They won’t let me cross.”

Seniro looked at the bridge guard. The sergeant had heard enough to understand why he was being looked at.

“We’ve stopped everybody, my lord. Your order.”

“Then this is a new one. The boy goes across under a white cloth. No weapons. He asks for his mother and the goats. If they tell him no, he returns. He doesn’t run, doesn’t hide, doesn’t try to be clever.”

The boy nodded too quickly.

“Say it back.”

He did. Seniro sent a soldier as far as the midpoint with him and remained in the yard until he saw the boy reach the enemy side. A man there took him by the shoulder. They spoke. The man pointed towards the house and let him go.

It would have been pleasant if that had been the end of it.

While Seniro waited, a carter came in with a horse whose harness had rubbed a raw place beneath its shoulder. He wanted the animal changed. The stable corporal told him there were no spare teams. The carter began to shout that the mare would die if they put another load behind her.

“She won’t die of that,” the corporal said.

Seniro left the gate.

“She may stop,” he said. “What does she do then?”

The corporal looked at him. “My lord?”

“What’s behind her?”

“Millstones.”

Seniro stared at the carter.

“Why?”

“The miller’s order.”

He found the miller standing over the men taking up his floor. A young woman beside him held a basket of iron fittings, wrapped separately in cloth so they would not damage one another. She was strong through the shoulders, with sleeves rolled to the elbow and a mark on her forehead from wiping away sweat with a dirty wrist.

“Your stones stay,” Seniro told the miller.

The man put down the pry bar he was holding.

“You said the machinery.”

“I said we would try to leave it usable.”

“Here, then? For them?”

The young woman looked between them. Seniro realised she had expected him to order it broken. Perhaps she had already hidden the fittings for that reason.

“For whoever is grinding here after us,” he said.

“That will be them.”

“Perhaps.”

The miller picked up the pry bar again, then put it down more carefully.

“Have you seen what happens when they requisition a mill? They feed their men first. They take the sacks people brought to be ground and call that their due. Then our neighbours come looking for flour they’ve already paid for.”

“Yes.”

“And I stand here and tell them I let you take the floor.”

Seniro could hear men lifting another board behind them. One end had split despite the care they were taking. An older carpenter swore softly and asked for a shorter wedge.

“Do you want me to burn it?” Seniro asked.

“No.” The woman’s answer came before her father’s. “No, my lord.”

“Then I won’t.”

“That is kind,” the miller said, with a bitterness too deep to be polished into courtesy.

Seniro let it stand. There were arguments a man earned the right to make when soldiers began taking his house apart.

He sent the carter back to unload the stones. They would take the smaller fittings, the miller’s household goods, and his family. The young woman said her name was Ista and that she would remain until the last board was up. She knew which timbers supported the gearing. If his carpenters cut the wrong one, nobody would grind anything there again.

“Do what she says,” Seniro told the carpenter.

The carpenter grunted. “Been doing that.”

Outside, the boy’s mother was at the far end of the bridge with the goats.

Three goats.

One had run under the willow. The boy was trying to catch it. An enemy soldier had tied a rope to the animal’s collar and was holding it out to him, laughing. Seniro watched the boy take the rope. The soldier ruffled his hair, almost familiarly, and sent him across.

The woman walked in front, carrying a wooden box against her breast. At the midpoint she stopped.

“Keep moving,” the sergeant called.

She looked back towards her house.

“Keep moving.”

She began again. The boy followed with the goats, his face rigid with the effort of not looking anywhere except the end of the bridge. One animal slipped and struck its knees on the stones. He pulled too hard on the rope. The woman turned and snapped at him, and the familiar quarrel carried them the remaining distance.

The sergeant moved to wave them into the yard. Seniro caught his arm.

“Search the box.”

The woman drew it closer.

“My mother’s things.”

“Open it, please.”

She looked at the boy. He had gone pale.

The box contained folded clothing, a cup with a broken handle, two small carvings, and a piece of paper laid on top where it could not be missed. Seniro took the paper. A rough plan of the bridge had been sketched on it, showing the abutments and the work below them. Underneath was a promise of protection for the house and its stock if the boy reported which supports had been cut.

“They said I had to take it,” the woman said.

“Who?”

“The one with the rope.”

Seniro looked across the bridge. The soldier beneath the willow was no longer watching them.

The boy was crying now, silently. His hammer hung in a loop at his belt. Lime dust marked the fabric below it.

“Did you tell him?”

The boy tried to speak.

“Look at me. Did you tell him what the mason was doing?”

“He asked why I was dirty. I said I was working.”

“And then?”

“I said the stones. I didn’t say which stones.”

The sergeant had moved a hand towards the boy’s shoulder. Seniro shook his head.

“He could see where we were working,” the boy said. “He said he could see.”

“Yes,” Seniro said. “He probably could.”

He sent the family to the wagons and told the sergeant to keep the boy away from the demolition work. Then he went back to the mason and asked what could be altered without losing the hour they needed.

Not much. The mason showed him a different place for the final rope. They shifted it behind the parapet and added a screen of old sacks. Seniro put extra archers where they could cover the men beneath the bridge.

Avren waited until they were alone.

“An example should be made.”

“Of which of us? I sent him.”

“The enemy will try it again.”

“Then we’ll know to look.”

He folded the sketch and put it inside his coat. He could still see the boy’s effort to obey every instruction he had been given: by his mother, by the soldier with the goat, by the marshal who had told him to come straight back.

“Get Ista and her father onto the last wagon,” he said. “Whether they think the work is finished or not.”

At noon the men across the bridge brought forward shields.

They were patient about it. They set the shields where the road narrowed, with crossbowmen behind, and put a pair of riders out into the meadow on either side to see what would happen. Seniro’s archers did not shoot. He had told them not to spend an arrow on a horse they could not recover.

The riders found the water deeper than it looked and came back. One lost a boot. The men at the shields laughed.

Seniro heard his own soldiers laugh too. He let them.

Then a messenger came from the sheepwash.

The boards were down. The first three wagons had crossed. The fourth had broken an axle on the climb to the stores. They needed another team.

“Where are Corbel’s men?” Seniro asked.

The messenger looked at Avren.

Avren said nothing.

“Captain.”

“There are no men from Long Lea.”

“He owes forty.”

“He has sent a letter.”

Seniro held out his hand.

The letter had been carried in a saddlebag that leaked. The words beside the fold were blurred, but the important sentence remained perfectly readable.

Lord Corbel would not furnish service beyond his acknowledged obligations while the scope of those obligations remained under dispute.

Seniro read it again.

He had heard the wording that morning in a letter from another estate. The other lord had used determined instead of acknowledged. Otherwise it had been the same.

“Who brought this?”

“A household rider. He’s waiting at the mill.”

“Feed him. Keep him.”

“Under arrest?”

“Under a roof. I have a reply.”

He looked back toward the western road. A man was walking out from the shields with a white cloth tied to the end of a spear.

“Now?” Avren asked.

“Yes. Now he wants to talk.”

Seniro went to meet him halfway across the bridge.

The man was young, well spoken, and careful to stop beyond sword’s reach.

“My lord marshal. His Grace asks that the bridge be preserved.”

“Does he?”

“It is useful to the country.”

“So is the grain behind it.”

“His Grace will pay for what is required.”

“With whose money?”

The young man smiled politely.

“I am empowered to offer safe withdrawal for your men if the works are left intact.”

Seniro looked at the white cloth. It had been torn from something with a blue stripe along one edge. A tablecloth, perhaps. Someone had sacrificed a good tablecloth for the interview.

“And if I stay?”

“Then we must take it.”

“You should have brought more men.”

The young man did not look behind him. That was well done.

“We have.”

Seniro let him wait while a wagon creaked through the yard beyond the mill. It was the damaged one, lashed between two spare wheels and pulled by a team that had no business moving so much weight. He watched it turn onto the lane beside the water.

“Tell His Grace I appreciate his concern for the works.”

“Is that your answer?”

“For now.”

The attack came after the white cloth had disappeared behind the shields.

Crossbow bolts struck the sacks above the demolition work. Seniro kept his men behind the parapet until the enemy foot reached the hump, then had the archers at the mill open on their flank. The first files stopped. Men behind them pressed forward, unable to see why the bridge had ceased to empty.

An officer on the northern bank waved his sword at the meadow. Six riders went in. They had watched the earlier horses founder and kept to the firmer strip beside the willows; beyond it, Seniro’s spearmen waited behind the low bank of the millrace. He lifted his hand to Avren.

A woman in a pale surcoat rode across the mouth of the meadow before he could lower it. She struck the young officer’s raised sword with her own, pointed her riders back onto the road, and caught the nearest horse by its bridle when its rider failed to turn. A bolt struck her saddle behind her thigh. She did not look down. The horse she held reared, came down facing the road, and took the others with it.

“Leave them,” Seniro told Avren.

“We can still catch the last two.”

“And lose men crossing the open ground. Leave them.”

The woman made her horse stand across the road while the foot came back over the bridge. The young officer was shouting at her. She waited until the last wounded man had been dragged clear, then let him shout at her from farther away.

“Who is she?” Seniro asked.

“Corvaunt. Dame Ysabeau.”

“Remember her. Next time she may be giving the first order.”

He sent fresh archers to relieve the men at the mill and went inside to write to Corbel.

He crossed out a first sentence which came close to threatening to burn the estate, and began again. He named the convoy, the broken wagon, and the men Corbel had promised to provide. He asked for forty foot at the northern granary by the following evening.

Beneath that he wrote, in the smaller hand his soldiers rarely saw: If you cannot furnish forty, tell me what you can furnish. I require an answer that can walk.

He sent the rider away with food for the journey.

At dusk the mill floor lay in the sheepwash and the miller sat beside the road on a sack of his own flour. Seniro stopped his horse beside him.

“The machinery?”

“Still there.”

“Good.”

“Will it be there tomorrow?”

Seniro looked back. His last archers were coming off the bridge. Under its hump, in the failing light, the mason was pulling on a rope.

“I don’t know.”

The miller nodded, as though at last somebody had answered him.

The bridge fell while they were riding towards the sheepwash. Seniro felt the sound through the saddle before he heard it.

Chapter Two: The Empty Encampment

The Earl of Dunmere had left his dinner.

Veralyn stood in the doorway of the tent and looked at the dishes on the table. There was a cold bird, a pot of mustard, six little onions laid in a row, and a cup of wine with a fly walking round its edge. Beside the cup a knife had been driven into the table through a folded piece of paper.

She drew the knife out.

For the comfort of Your Grace upon her arrival.

Not Majesty.

She handed the paper to Tylan.

“He knows my tastes poorly.”

“The onions are good.”

“You’ve tried them?”

“One of the men did. He has not yet died.”

Tylan was too tall for the tent. He stood with his head slightly bent and his shoulders giving the canvas a shape it had not been cut to accommodate. Rain dripped from the edge of his cloak onto the earl’s carpet.

“How long?” she asked.

“Since he left? Two hours, perhaps three. His horses were walked down the streambed. The tracks on the road belong to the men he wanted us to follow.”

“How many went with him?”

“The household. A hundred and fifty mounted. The foot have gone into the villages.”

“He has no villages here.”

“He has acquaintances.”

Veralyn set the knife on the table.

For eight days they had driven him toward this fold in the flint hills. The high road narrowed below it between two steep shoulders of chalk, and beyond that the country opened toward the northern passes. If they could hold the narrow ground before he crossed, he would have to fight or leave his wagons. They had ridden through a night without fires to do it.

He had left the wagons.

They were empty.

“Prisoners?”

“Thirty-one. Mostly carters. Four wounded men he could not move.”

“Could not or would not?”

“We’ll ask when the surgeon has finished.”

She went out into the rain.

The camp stood on a patch of rough pasture above a dry stream that was no longer dry. Water ran through the empty fire pits, carrying ash downhill in grey fingers. Her men were bringing in the abandoned wagons and swearing at the wheels. Beyond them, the hills rose white through the thin soil, flint nodules showing like old teeth.

She had grown to hate the sound of horses on flint. It promised solid ground and gave them sore feet.

“Send the scouts after the household,” she said. “Nobody pursues beyond sight of the next pair. I want the roads he can use, not his head brought in by one man on a dead horse.”

Tylan nodded.

“And find out who supplied the onions.”

That made him look at her.

“They were fresh,” she said. “He did not bring them from his own lands.”

By evening they had an onion grower, two carts of turnips, and a woman who said the earl had stolen her best grey horse.

The woman was brought to the house where Veralyn had taken quarters. She was perhaps thirty, broad in the shoulder, with weather-darkened skin and a mouth that looked as though it seldom had patience with nonsense. She entered carrying the bridle.

“He left me this.”

Veralyn looked up from the map.

“Generous.”

“It was mine before he arrived.”

“What was the horse worth?”

“To him? Whatever he got for running away.”

“To you.”

The woman considered the question properly.

“Two mares in foal and a year of my life.”

“What is your name?”

“Anneth.”

“Do you know where he went?”

“I know where I would go if I wanted to get that horse killed.”

Veralyn moved the lamp.

“Show me.”

Anneth came to the table and bent over the map. Her sleeve brushed Veralyn’s hand. She smelled of wet wool and the sharp green scent of crushed nettles.

“That isn’t a road,” she said, pointing.

“It was drawn as one.”

“It was drawn by somebody who didn’t take a cart there.”

Tylan, from the window, said, “We have met several of those.”

Anneth traced a line up the side of a hill. There was a sheep track below the ridge, then a hollow where a man could wait with his horses out of the wind. Beyond it lay a low saddle that would let the earl go east while the queen’s scouts continued north.

Veralyn sent for a rider.

When he had gone, she asked Anneth whether she had eaten.

“Is that part of getting my horse back?”

“It may be part of getting mine fed.”

Anneth looked at her, and at the map, and then smiled.

“I can tell you where there’s hay.”

Tylan collected the map from under their hands.

“I’ll see about it,” he said.

Anneth did not immediately offer to take them through the hollow.

She stood beside the map while Veralyn waited for the offer, then folded her hands behind her back. Mud had dried on her boots in pale scales. The empty place at her belt, where the stolen horse’s reins might ordinarily have rested when she walked him, seemed to irritate her; she kept touching it and stopping herself.

“What do you want?” Veralyn asked.

“My horse.”

“I know that.”

“Then why did you ask?”

Tylan turned away, ostensibly to call for a rider. Veralyn could see his mouth from the side.

“I can send men after the remounts,” she said. “I can’t promise which animals they’ll find.”

“And if mine is there?”

“He comes back to you.”

“Before someone says the army needs him?”

Veralyn looked at her properly. Anneth did not lower her eyes. She was old enough to have learned how an offer changed when the person making it no longer needed anything.

“Before that,” Veralyn said.

Anneth took the little wooden marker from the map and shifted it towards the hollow.

“The wet ground is worse than this shows. We’ll go on foot for the last part.”

“We?” Tylan asked.

Veralyn was already reaching for her gloves.

“You have captains.”

“I also have legs.”

“And one head, which people persist in counting as more valuable than the others.”

She wanted to answer sharply. He had said it mildly enough that sharpness would look like the petulance it probably was. The men outside the tent could hear them. She could have dismissed him with a word and taken the riders herself. It would cost him nothing except the ability to stop her the next time.

“Bucktree goes,” she said. “I go as far as the ridge, with an escort. If there’s nothing to see, I’ll return and allow you to be pleased with yourself.”

“I shall try to make the wait bearable.”

Captain Arlan Bucktree listened to Anneth without interrupting. He was a lean man whose left eye watered in the wind. When she finished, he asked her to draw the hollow with a stick in the ground. The map’s folds had made the slope look gentler than she said it was.

“Here?” He pointed at a place where the earth had dried around a root.

“That’s the drop. A loaded horse won’t come back up it without help.”

“Then we don’t follow one down.”

Veralyn knew the men had noticed he was speaking to her as well as to Anneth. She let them notice that she listened.

They rode out in the late afternoon. From the ridge she could see how little the map contained. The hills folded into each other until a patch of scrub apparently close enough to touch proved to be across a steep ravine. Flint lay loose on the path. A horse could cut a foot badly without doing anything more foolish than stepping sideways.

Bucktree left six men with her and took the rest down. Anneth went at his shoulder. She had borrowed a short cloak and tied it up behind her so it would not catch on the thorn.

Veralyn waited.

Waiting on a ridge was worse than waiting in a tent. In a tent somebody could bring her a difficulty. On the ridge there was only the wind and the knowledge that if she gave an unnecessary order it would be because she disliked having nothing to do.

One of the escort shifted his saddle and then settled again. She recognised him as a man who had laughed at the abandoned supper. His laughter had irritated her then. Now she was grateful for a face that belonged to an actual person rather than to the general condition of being guarded.

“What’s your name?”

“Evan, Majesty.”

“Is this your first time north?”

“No. My sister married here.”

“Where?”

He pointed beyond the ridge.

“On Dunmere’s land?”

“Yes, Majesty.”

“Have you seen her?”

“No.”

She almost offered leave. Then she remembered what might happen to a royal soldier visiting a village whose lord was being pursued by the queen.

“Does she know you’re here?”

“I hope not.”

The wind moved the grass between them. She could not think of a useful answer.

A whistle sounded below: two short notes repeated, the agreed signal. Bucktree’s men had found the remounts.

Veralyn moved to the edge where she could see into the hollow. Horses stood beneath scrub on the opposite side, saddles piled against a rock. Two men were running. A third remained by the animals, holding a bow he had not managed to string.

Then a horse stumbled down the loose slope and dragged another with it.

Someone shouted. Anneth ran forward and vanished behind the scrub. Veralyn’s own horse moved beneath her. Evan caught its bridle, not pulling, merely making himself an obstacle she would have to order aside.

“They’ve seen it, Majesty.”

She could see that they had. Bucktree was already down from his saddle. A man with a rope went after him. The two fugitives had reached the farther slope, and one of the royal riders began to follow.

“Don’t,” Veralyn said, much too quietly for him to hear.

Bucktree shouted it. The rider stopped reluctantly.

They brought the horses out in ones and twos. Anneth’s grey limped behind her, his head low. She was talking to him continuously. The unstrung bow belonged to a boy scarcely older than the one tending Veralyn’s spare horse. He had thrown it away and was now carrying a saddle under a soldier’s supervision.

Only when they reached the ridge did she see the wounded man.

He was one of Dunmere’s carters, left with the animals because his ankle had swollen and he could not keep pace with the departing wagons. During the scramble a horse had fallen across him. They had freed him, but his breathing sounded wrong. Bucktree brought him up on an improvised litter made from cloaks and two cut poles.

Veralyn dismounted.

“Can you speak?”

He moved his lips. She knelt close enough to hear.

“Didn’t steal them.”

“No one’s asking that now.”

“Fed them.”

“I believe you.”

He was looking past her, towards Anneth and the grey. She brought Anneth over. The woman crouched beside him, one hand on her horse’s cheek.

“He’s all right,” she said. “You kept him fed.”

The man closed his eyes.

They returned more slowly than they had come. Veralyn walked for part of the way so the wounded man could have the steadiest animal, supported between two riders. It was an inefficient arrangement; every dip in the path required them to stop and settle him again. Once he cried out, and the sound made the horses nervous.

At camp the surgeon took him without promising anything. The boy with the bow was searched and given food. Bucktree asked where the earl had gone. The boy answered while eating, then began to cry because he thought answering had made him a traitor.

Veralyn left Bucktree to it.

She found Anneth beside the grey. The cut below his hock had been washed, and he was trying to nose the bandage away.

“You kept your word,” Anneth said.

“This one was easy to recognise.”

“It wasn’t easy for me.”

Veralyn touched the horse’s neck. She could still feel the carter’s breath against her cheek, short and warm, interrupted by the effort of taking another.

“Will you stay until he’s fit to travel?”

Anneth looked at her. “The horse?”

It was an impertinent answer. It improved the afternoon considerably.

Before the meal, Wenna took Veralyn to the dam.

It had been made from a cart laid on its side, earth packed against the wheels, and stakes dragged down from the defended field. The water backed up into a meadow whose grass lay flat beneath the surface. Beyond it, higher ground had already been cut. Small cones of hay stood in rows, grey-green in the evening light.

“The lower mill stopped yesterday,” Wenna said.

“Who owns this meadow?”

“Three households. One is Anneth’s father’s.”

Veralyn looked at the cart. Its axle had been sawn through to make it fit the streambed. A useful cart, broad enough for the harvest, destroyed for a position the earls had never defended.

“Whose oxen brought the stakes?”

“The men at the hall would know.”

“Find out.”

Tylan came down the bank behind them. He examined the water first, then the path by which men could get to the obstruction.

“We can open it before morning.”

“How much hay has been lost?” she asked.

He looked across the flooded meadow. “I don’t know.”

“Ask that too.”

He stood beside her without answering. She could see where a team had struggled on the slope, the grooves cut by dragging timber, a broken yoke left half hidden in the reeds. Each mark belonged to a task somebody had been made to perform. The earl had gone. The marks remained.

At the farther edge of the meadow, an old herder was turning his sheep back from a strip of dry ground. Wenna asked him why he would not bring them across it. He shook his head and called to his dog.

“Is it wet?” Tylan asked.

The man shook his head again.

“Does someone claim it?”

The dog moved behind the flock. The sheep began climbing towards the higher pasture.

Tylan watched them go, then told the horse-master to move the lines to the ground the flock had just left. He gave no other order about the meadow.

Anneth’s father received them at the hall with his daughter beside him. He was an old steward, his hands bent by years of writing and work he had continued to do after he could afford to have it done. Veralyn could see where Anneth’s directness came from. When she asked about the meadow, he named the households before naming himself.

They ate in a room with whitewashed walls and a hearth too broad for the fire burning in it. A shelf of flat stones stood beside the chimney breast. Nobody put a cup there, though the table was crowded.

The wine had been sweetened. Veralyn took a second sip to make certain the first had not been a mistake.

“You don’t like it,” Anneth said.

“I was considering it.”

“You’ve considered it twice. I can bring the other jug.”

Her father began an apology. Veralyn stopped him, less graciously than she meant to, and asked what they drank when there were no royal guests to please.

“Usually the other jug,” Anneth said.

Later somebody brought a small pipe, and the old steward persuaded one of the stablemen to play. The tune was familiar but the pace wrong for the steps Veralyn knew. She asked Anneth to show her.

“Here?”

“Unless there’s a better floor.”

There was not. They moved the bench.

Anneth danced with a concentration that gave way to amusement when Veralyn caught the turn. The stableman played faster. Her father protested that he had never taught his household to make a queen breathless at supper.

“You taught her well,” Veralyn said.

Anneth’s hand tightened briefly in hers. It was the first answer either had given that could not also belong to the dance.

The letter from the south arrived after supper.

Veralyn was alone when she read it. Constano’s hand was more compressed than usual, the letters leaning against one another as though he had tried to fit the news into less paper. He had written that Braedyn had been buried. That the house was functioning. That he was going to Fairport.

At the bottom he had added that her old room was being kept ready.

She read that sentence for longer than the rest.

Tylan knocked once and came in when she answered. He had taken off his wet cloak. A dark patch remained across one shoulder where water had found the seam.

“There’s enough hay for two days. Paid for.”

She passed him the letter.

He read it standing. When he reached the bottom, his jaw moved once.

“Did you know?” she asked.

“No.”

“I thought somebody would tell you first.”

“So did I.”

For a little while they listened to the rain on the roof.

She had seen Braedyn last in a chair beside an open window. He had been angry about the quality of the information reaching him. She had told him that he was angry because he could not go and look for himself. He had said he could go if the physician stopped making a religion of stairs.

She had laughed.

“Do you want the room?” Tylan asked.

“No.”

He drew the other chair up to the table and sat down. It was too small for him. He folded himself into it carefully.

“They’ve gone east,” he said after a while. “Anneth was right.”

“Can we get ahead of them?”

“Not tonight.”

“Tomorrow?”

“If we let the horses eat.”

She nodded.

Tylan looked at the letter again, then folded it along the crease Constano had made.

“He’d have told us to let the horses eat,” he said.

“He’d have told us three other things first, and made us work it out.”

“Yes.”

She began to laugh and stopped before she could decide what sound would come next.

Later, Anneth came to the door to ask whether the queen wanted the fire kept in. Veralyn had seen her standing in the passage for a moment before she knocked. There had been time for either of them to decide that the visit was about a fire.

“Yes,” Veralyn said. “Come in.”

Anneth closed the door, then looked at Shadowsteel lying beside the bed. She lifted it by the scabbard, carefully, and laid it on the bench outside.

“Not in here,” she said.

Veralyn could have asked why. Instead she held out her hand.

Anneth had been widowed for three years. She told Veralyn that while unfastening the clasp at her shoulder, plainly enough that it did not seem a request for sympathy. Veralyn asked whether she wanted to speak of him. Anneth said not tonight. They found other things to say, and later less need to say them.

Before dawn, Veralyn woke to find the sword on the chest beneath the window. A servant coming to mend the fire said she had brought it in when she found it outside. Anneth watched the girl kneel at the hearth, then got up to fetch water.

Veralyn wrote to Jaennis while the room warmed. She described the dance, the sweetened wine, and the song Anneth had sung afterwards, including the lines she could remember. It was a poor transcription. Jaennis would know that and enjoy telling her.

In the morning she ordered the advance delayed until the horses had been fed. She rode out with Constano’s letter inside her coat. Anneth remained at the hall, where the grey was being cared for and there was hay to recover from the flooded meadow.

Chapter Three: The First Invitation

Margery Ashcombe arrived with her husband and left him in the wrong room.

Jaennis saw the mistake being made from the upper gallery. Lord Ashcombe followed a gentleman in green through the door beside the stairs, certain that a door opened by a gentleman must lead to the people he wished to meet. His wife paused at the foot of the stairs, looked up, and caught Jaennis watching.

She did not call him back.

Jaennis descended slowly. She had learned as a child that coming down stairs too quickly made people anxious, and later that coming down them slowly gave everyone time to look at her. The difference had become useful.

“Lady Ashcombe.”

“Your Majesty.”

Margery’s curtsey was good without being deep. Her gown was blue, cut high at the throat, with a little silver clasp shaped like a pear. She was older than Jaennis by some years and had been married long enough to know what it meant when a husband discovered himself among the wrong people.

“Will he mind?” Jaennis asked.

“Only if they’re poor.”

“They are reviewing the drains.”

“Then he may be useful. He arranges quarters for the Valora garrison. Drains are among his complaints.”

Jaennis laughed. Margery looked pleased, but not surprised. That was a promising beginning.

She took her upstairs.

The room above the gallery had been called the green room under Brightstride, although its hangings were brown. Jaennis had ordered the name removed from the household lists after a visiting ambassador spent an hour looking for it. Now it was the upper room, which gave nobody anything to argue about.

Joan Hask stood at the sideboard cutting the last of the pears. She had been in Jaennis’s service since before the marriage and could judge a visitor’s temper from the way they waited to be offered a chair. She was in her forties, dark-haired, and very good at allowing someone to think she had not understood an insult until they had repeated it in front of a witness.

“Another plate,” Jaennis said.

“There are no more pears.”

“Then I shall share.”

Joan looked at Margery, then at the plate already laid beside Jaennis’s chair. She moved it between two places.

The other women made room. There were five that afternoon, some there because they liked Jaennis, some because they liked the others, and one because her husband was asking for a licence she had been told would take less time if she became agreeable to somebody. Jaennis had not decided whether to grant it. The woman had an excellent singing voice.

They talked about a coat that had arrived from Silkstrand with one sleeve a different shade from the other. They talked about the price of saffron and a man at the lower court who had tried to pass off his aunt as his wife because the invitation admitted spouses and not additional guests. Margery knew the aunt. She said the deception had been the man’s best chance of respectable company.

Jaennis watched Joan try not to laugh while pouring wine.

“Sit down,” she told her.

“I have the candles to see to.”

“The candles can see to themselves for a moment.”

Joan set down the jug and took the narrow chair by the hearth. Margery watched her do it. Her expression did not change, but when the conversation moved on she asked Joan what she thought of the coat.

Jaennis gave her another slice of pear.

Dame Ysolt Harrow arrived before the musicians.

She had served three queens and a king who disliked being served by anybody he had not chosen himself. Her hair was nearly white. She wore it beneath a plain linen cap and dressed as though nothing in the world could persuade her to wait for a maid to do up her sleeves.

She stopped inside the door and counted the women without moving her lips.

“Majesty.”

“Ysolt. Wine?”

“A petitioner.”

“That is a poor exchange.”

“She has come from the river.”

Jaennis put her plate down.

The petitioner was a widow with two sons, one living and one whose death had not yet been agreed upon by the people who owed her money. The younger son had gone with a levy escort and not returned. A captain said he had deserted. A ferryman said he had seen the body. Neither would travel to confirm the other’s account.

The widow had brought the boy’s belt.

Jaennis heard her in the little room beyond the gallery. Ysolt remained by the door. The music began in the upper room while the woman was speaking, then stopped; somebody had understood that they should wait.

“Who gave you the belt?” Jaennis asked.

“The man who washed him.”

“The ferryman?”

“No, Majesty. The man at the rites house. He sent it with my brother.”

“Then we ask him.”

“They said he couldn’t say who killed him.”

“He does not have to say who killed him to say he is dead.”

The widow looked at her.

Jaennis had learned to recognise that look. People who had been told no often enough began to suspect a different word meant they had misunderstood.

She asked Ysolt to bring Master Pellam.

When the clerk arrived, she had the widow repeat the names. Pellam wrote them down, asked which rites house, and asked whether the belt had a maker’s mark. He did not ask the widow to tell the whole story again. Jaennis liked him for that.

“An allowance until the deposition returns,” she said.

“From which purse, Majesty?”

“Mine.”

“The household purse or the—”

“The one that bought the pears.”

Pellam bowed.

After the woman had gone, he remained with the paper in his hand.

“There will be others.”

“I expect there already are.”

“The levy office ought to deal with it.”

“Then make them.”

He did not move.

Jaennis rubbed the place between her eyes. She had a headache coming on. She had always been liable to them after rooms full of perfume.

“Write to the levy office. Ask them how many families are waiting for a confirmation of death because nobody can confirm the killer. Ask for the number first. If you ask for an explanation, they will send one instead.”

Pellam’s mouth moved at one corner.

“Yes, Majesty.”

The next petition concerned a carrier stopped at Corbel’s landing.

He had brought the earlier ferry agreement in a leather cover, the names of the three passages written large enough to be read without opening it. Jaennis recognised Oria’s hand on the attached letter. The carrier had paid at one of the named ferries, then been charged again when his cargo reached the landing farther upriver.

“For the guard,” he said.

“Was there a guard?”

“Six men. I saw them.”

“Did they protect the cargo?”

“From anyone but themselves, perhaps.”

A few people smiled. Jaennis did not. She was looking at the list of goods: cloth, small iron tools, two chests belonging to households that had sent them ahead before moving. Nothing a captain could reasonably describe as a threat to his lord’s estate.

Pellam asked for the instrument under which the second charge had been taken. The carrier held out a narrow strip of paper, folded so often that it had nearly separated across the middle.

It named maintenance of the approach. The sum exceeded what the earlier ferries would have charged for the whole load.

“Was the approach repaired?” Jaennis asked.

“They had put a new plank over the ditch.”

“Before you arrived?”

“I paid to have it put down. Then they charged me to cross it.”

Joan made a small sound behind her chair. Jaennis kept her eyes on the paper.

She had Pellam draft an order protecting carriers named under Oria’s settlement from a second charge on the same passage of goods. It seemed a modest instruction: preserve what the houses had already agreed, stop people collecting twice because a cart could not avoid their piece of road. Pellam asked whether she wished the lord’s steward heard first.

“He may answer in writing. The carrier’s goods cannot wait for a visit to Valora.”

“The landing is outside the three ferries.”

“The goods are travelling under their agreement.”

“That may be disputed.”

“Then he will say so. Send the order.”

The carrier bowed so deeply that his leather cover brushed the floor. He asked whether he should show the order to every road captain on the way home.

“To the one named,” Pellam said before Jaennis could answer. “It is addressed to him.”

When the man had gone, Jaennis read the wording again. She could see no injustice in it. She signed beneath the delegated authority and watched the wax set.

At the window, the last light lay across the garden wall. The lower beds had been cleared for the season; gardeners were carrying the dead stems away in baskets. Inside, a servant was lighting the lamps one by one, shielding each flame until it caught. Joan was waiting by the door. Jaennis gave Pellam the signed order and returned with her to the upper room.

The musicians had begun again when she returned.

Margery sat beside Joan near the hearth. They were talking quietly. The woman with the licence had taken a place by the window and was pretending not to listen.

Jaennis stopped for a moment in the doorway.

She had made this room pleasant. It had taken more work than anybody who enjoyed it would ever know. Chairs had been moved, invitations rewritten, one woman rescued from an obligation to sit beside another woman who hated her, a servant dismissed from this duty without losing her livelihood elsewhere because she repeated everything she heard. The wine was good. The musicians were paid. The people in the room wanted to remain in it.

She wanted Veralyn to see it.

The thought arrived with a suddenness that hurt. Veralyn would come through the door in something too dark for the season, look at the women, then at Jaennis, and understand far too much and not quite enough. She would find a chair, or sit on the arm of Jaennis’s, and somebody would say something foolish to make her laugh.

Perhaps Margery would.

Jaennis crossed to the hearth.

“Your husband has been found,” she told her.

“Was he useful?”

“Apparently he knows a man who can get the stone more cheaply.”

“He always does.”

“He asks whether you are ready to leave.”

Margery looked toward the door. Then she looked back at Jaennis.

“Would it be discourteous to finish the song?”

“Very.”

“How unfortunate.”

Jaennis sat beside her and sent word that Lady Ashcombe would be another little while.

Margery stayed until the musicians had begun packing their instruments. Her husband sent a second inquiry, then came up himself and discovered that he was not the only man waiting in the gallery for a wife who had lost interest in the hour.

Jaennis watched the reunion through the open door. Ashcombe did not take his wife’s arm immediately. He waited until she had turned from Joan, then offered it with a smile intended to include the room behind her.

“Have you enjoyed yourself?” he asked.

“Very much.”

“The stone people were most grateful. They had been paying nearly a fifth more than necessary.”

“How good of you to rescue them.”

Something moved in his expression. He knew her tone too well to mistake it for praise, and was too practised to challenge it in the queen’s doorway.

Jaennis went to him before he could decide whom to resent.

“I am told you were helpful.”

“I was fortunate to have the right acquaintance, Majesty.”

“Then I hope he proves as useful as you expect.”

She let him kiss her hand. Margery stood beside him with lowered eyes, the silver pear at her throat catching the lamplight. It was absurdly pleasing to know that she was trying not to laugh.

The woman who had asked for the licence remained after they left.

Her name was Ysabet Morne. Jaennis knew her husband’s name better, and the estate from which his income came better than either of them. He had married Ysabet after his first wife’s death and had brought her to court with a collection of jewellery so conspicuously old-fashioned that everyone understood whose neck it had adorned before hers.

Ysabet touched one of the stones now.

“I hope I haven’t stayed too late.”

“Do you want to speak to me?”

“Yes.”

“Then you haven’t.”

Joan took the last cups to the sideboard. Jaennis expected Ysabet to wait until she left. Instead the younger woman looked towards her and seemed reassured that she remained.

“It isn’t the licence.”

“All right.”

“My husband thinks it is.”

“What do you want it to be?”

Ysabet’s hand closed over the necklace. “I should like to come again.”

Jaennis smiled. “That is easily arranged.”

“Without him.”

“Also easily arranged.”

“It isn’t, Majesty. Not for me.”

The smile left Jaennis’s face. Ysabet was twenty-six, well above the age at which anyone would have mistaken hesitation for simple girlishness. She had made the request knowing it might be repeated. Whatever she wanted from the room, she wanted it enough to risk the request becoming an account of her dissatisfaction at home.

“Does he object to your visiting?”

“He objects to my having no reason.”

“Must you always have one?”

Ysabet looked at the floor. Jaennis realised that she had asked a question whose answer was given by the fact of the woman standing there.

“We are selecting linen for the household next week,” Joan said from the sideboard.

Jaennis glanced at her.

“Are we?”

“We should be. The last purchase was poor.”

Ysabet’s head came up. “It was cut across the width too narrowly. I noticed when I washed my hands.”

“Did you?”

“The border won’t survive many boilings.”

Joan brought one of the folded napkins from a shelf. The two women bent over it, discussing thread and wear with an animation entirely absent from the request for a licence. Jaennis listened. She had expected a pretext and been given a competence.

“Come next week,” she said. “Joan will write to you about what is needed. Your husband may be told that I have asked for your assistance.”

Ysabet drew a breath.

“Thank you.”

“And the licence will be considered on its own terms. You need not inspect every napkin in the palace to earn it.”

“I didn’t mean—”

“I know.”

She let the woman keep the rest of her meaning to herself.

After Ysabet went, Joan brought the napkin to Jaennis. Up close, the cloth was thin enough to show the warmth of a hand beneath it.

“You should have told me.”

“I did. You were reading a letter.”

“Then you should have told me twice.”

“I did that too.”

Jaennis looked up. Joan’s expression was composed, but there was something tired behind it.

“Is that what this evening has been?” Jaennis asked. “Something else you’ve had to arrange while I looked occupied?”

“No.”

“Not entirely?”

Joan sat. It was only when she put her feet out before her that Jaennis remembered she had stood for much of the evening, even after being invited to join them.

“People will say you have favourites.”

“I have favourites.”

“Yes. And when they want to hurt you, they’ll begin with the people who can’t answer them.”

Jaennis took the napkin from her and laid it aside.

“Has someone said something to you?”

“Someone always says something to a woman carrying their wine.”

“Who?”

“It doesn’t matter tonight.”

“It matters to me.”

Joan considered her for a moment. “One of the ushers asked whether I was to be measured for a crown.”

Jaennis felt heat rise beneath her collar.

“He’ll be dismissed.”

“And everyone will know why. Including his cousins in the kitchens, who will continue working here.”

“Then what do you want?”

“I want you to ask me that before announcing what you will do.”

The room was quiet now. Below them someone was closing shutters. Jaennis could hear each fastening set into place, one after another.

She had spent the day being obeyed. Joan’s refusal to let her repair an injury with the first order that occurred to her felt almost like another injury, which was unreasonable and made it harder to master.

“What do you want?” she asked again, more carefully.

“For him to be told whose instructions brought me into the room. Not what you think of me. Whose instructions.”

“I can do that.”

“I know.”

Jaennis moved her chair closer. “And afterward?”

Joan looked at her, and the tiredness did not disappear so much as make room for something else.

“Afterward, I would like to take off these shoes.”

Jaennis knelt before she could change her mind. Joan made a startled movement, then allowed her to unfasten the first shoe. The skin above the heel was reddened. Jaennis set the shoe down and took the other foot into her lap.

“You must let me buy you better ones.”

“These are good shoes. I’ve been wearing them since morning.”

“Then worse shoes for mornings.”

Joan laughed. Jaennis looked up at the sound and found her watching with a tenderness that made the angry heat in her face subside.

She laid the second shoe beside the first. When she stood, Joan caught her hand.

“Stay a little.”

“I was going to.”

They sat close enough that Jaennis could feel the warmth through the other woman’s sleeve. She kissed her once, carefully, then again without quite the same care. Joan’s fingers rested at the back of her neck. There were still lamps to be extinguished, people expecting instructions in the morning, a letter from the north she had read until she knew the shape of Veralyn’s writing better than the words.

She did not stop wanting any of those things by wanting this.

Later, while Joan slept, Jaennis lay awake thinking about the room. The place Margery had chosen. Ysabet’s sudden animation over a piece of cloth. The way Veralyn leaned back when somebody surprised her into laughing, exposing her throat as if there could be nothing to fear in the world.

She wanted to bring that laugh here.

In the morning she summoned the usher and gave him the instruction Joan had requested. She did it while he was alone, allowing him the dignity of no audience to his correction. He apologised so profusely that she had to stop him.

“You will find,” she said, “that people answer me more usefully when they aren’t trying to discover what will embarrass me.”

After he left, she sent Ysabet the invitation about the linen in her own hand.

Chapter Four: What Fairport Could Send

The captain would take the grain, the remounts, or the wounded. He would not take all three.

His name was Wat Crane. Constano had known his barges longer than he had known the man; their shallow draught had made them useful before anyone needed to carry soldiers in them.

Constano stood beneath the warehouse awning with river water dripping from his hat and looked at the boat. It was smaller than the description he had been sent. Most boats were.

“The agreement specifies capacity.”

“The agreement specifies a boat that hasn’t had its bottom patched twice since it was signed.”

“Was I charged for the patches?”

“You were.”

“Then I had hoped to benefit from them.”

The captain scratched beneath his collar. He had a rash there, red against the weathered skin.

“My lord, I can put everything aboard. I can do it while you stand here. What I can’t promise is to keep it above the water.”

Constano looked at the horses standing farther along the quay. One was trying to chew the rope that held it. A groom had wrapped a rag round the rope and rubbed it with something bitter. The horse was considering whether the bitterness was worth it.

“How many wounded?”

“Eleven coming down. That’s what the message says.”

“The message is three days old.”

“Yes.”

Constano took off his hat and wrung the water from its brim.

“The horses go by road. Grain upriver. Wounded down.”

“The road is being held.”

“All of it?”

“Enough.”

He looked at his factor, who had been hoping not to be looked at.

“Find out which enough.”

Seniro’s rider found him before he left the quay. The letter was folded around a second order, shorter and more emphatic than the first. The marshal wanted the powder sent with the next movement. He also wanted the grain diverted from the northern store to a landing nearer his present line.

Constano read the landing’s name and asked the rider when he had left.

“Yesterday after the noon meal.”

“Was the landing still open?”

“It was when I passed.”

“Guarded?”

“His lordship had men there.”

His brother was asking for the movement that would be most useful if everything continued to stand where it had stood the day before. Constano had just spent an hour establishing how much could travel at all.

He sent the rider to eat and went to the guild house.

Its front steps were shallow, worn almost hollow at the centre. As a boy he had counted them every time his father brought him. The number had never changed; the distance between them seemed to have grown. He stopped at the top and let Renn open the door before following him inside.

Master Halven received him in the small dining room, where a white cloth had been spread for a meal nobody had begun. The guild-master’s daughter was cutting bread at a side table. She greeted Constano, then left with the knife still in her hand.

“Powder boats,” Halven said.

“How did you know?”

“Your people asked my people. My people said no. Then you came.”

Constano sat in the chair offered. A portrait hung above the empty hearth: a young man in a blue coat, painted with one hand resting on a ship’s rail. Halven had once complained that the painter charged too much for the railing and not enough for the face. His son had been alive then.

“I need one lighter and a crew who know the upriver shoals. For this passage only.”

“The men are paid for work at sea.”

“I will pay the difference.”

“It isn’t the difference in pay.”

Halven folded his hands on the table. He had thick fingers with knots at the joints. The guild had given him a silver cup on the twenty-fifth year of his mastership, and he still drank from the old pewter one because its handle hurt less.

“They will take grain,” he said. “They will carry wounded men. Some have sons in the companies. Powder to an exposed landing is another matter.”

“The landing is guarded.”

“By men who may be required somewhere else before the boat arrives.”

Constano looked at Seniro’s order. His brother had asked for the nearer landing because moving the powder farther overland required teams already drawing guns. Halven was right about the guard. So was Seniro about the teams. There was no comfortable arrangement waiting beneath the two objections.

“What would they accept?”

“The northern store. Their own pilot. A departure chosen for the water, not for the convenience of the men waiting on shore.”

“Seniro will object.”

“He may.”

Constano put the order away. “Then the northern store. I will answer for the change.”

Halven leaned back, but the movement gave no impression of relief.

“There is another condition.”

“Name it.”

“My people have supplied your house at rates agreed before the war. There have been three extensions of the emergency terms. I want this passage treated as the last service under them.”

“Other captains will ask the same.”

“They already do. I have been refusing to hear them.”

Constano remembered the year a flood had spoiled the lower warehouses. Halven had needed enough credit to buy stock before the northern merchants arrived. Braedyn had supplied it at his own risk, against the advice of men who believed the guild too weak to repay him. For years afterward the boats had been there when Fenwood asked.

“My father would have wanted—”

He stopped.

Halven looked at him without helping.

Constano could have completed the sentence. He had heard his father invoke that old assistance with affection, as if remembering it were one of the pleasures of friendship. Perhaps Halven had once found it pleasant too. His son looked down from the wall with a painted hand on a painted rail.

“I am asking for this passage,” Constano said. “Afterward we agree new terms for whatever follows.”

“And if I refuse the following passage?”

“Then I ask someone else.”

Halven went to the door and spoke to a clerk. When he returned, he carried the name of a lighter and two men qualified to take it. He gave Constano the paper and remained standing.

The meal had gone cold in the kitchen. Constano could hear the daughter telling someone to keep it there until she called. Halven did not invite him to stay.

Outside, Renn offered an arm on the stairs. Constano accepted it.

“Was that what we needed?” the factor asked.

“For today.”

“And the later grain?”

“We find ordinary terms he can accept. Begin asking now.”

At the countinghouse Constano wrote to Seniro. The powder would go to the northern store. So would the grain. The marshal must provide teams for the last stage, because there would otherwise be no powder to move.

He knew how his brother would read it: another explanation from a man indoors, while the men outdoors needed ammunition. He added the lighter’s name, the pilot’s condition, and the time at which the water would allow departure. Then he explained what he had promised Halven.

Renn asked whether the last paragraph was necessary.

“Yes.”

“The marshal may not consider it his concern.”

“He should know what I have used.”

The rider came back smelling of fried onions. Constano asked him to wait while Aldith made a copy. When he left, the quay below the window was filling with men returning for the evening loading. Their voices came up clearly between the creak of tackle and the gulls.

The guild-master’s daughter arrived before dark with the signed agreement.

“My father asks that you return the original when the passage is performed.”

“Of course.”

She did not leave immediately.

“He speaks of the duke kindly,” she said.

“I know.”

“I wanted you to know that.”

Constano set the agreement down. “Please tell him I do.”

After she had gone, he rang for supper and returned to the unanswered letters.

Fairport worked loudly.

In Tarlington, the men who arranged a thing could sometimes mistake the arrangement for the thing itself. In Fairport a load fell off a sling and broke its owner’s foot outside the window. If a warehouse stood empty, gulls got into it. If a ship waited too long, the men who owned its cargo arrived at the door and stayed there, eating food somebody had to provide.

Constano had taken rooms above a rope-maker’s countinghouse because the stairs had a landing halfway up. He had been told there were better rooms by the harbour gate. He had asked how many stairs.

Now the rope-maker’s wife brought his supper on a tray and told him that a man from Marcott had come.

“Which man?”

“The one who won’t give me his wet cloak.”

Constano knew who she meant.

Matteo Tireno came in holding the cloak over one arm. He had been called Matten by his nurse and Matteo by his tutors and answered to either without apparent preference, which Constano considered an evasion. His face had the long, tired lines of a man who had spent most of his life finding ways to say no without closing a negotiation.

“You look cold,” Constano said.

“You look employed.”

“Sit down.”

Matteo lowered himself into the chair opposite the tray. He looked at the fish.

“Is that all?”

“For me.”

“I’ll watch.”

Constano rang the bell.

They had done business for twenty years. There were family connections between them, marriages discussed and marriages made, children whose names both men knew and whose ages neither could reliably remember. Matteo had once lent Constano money without asking for a document and later charged him for the inconvenience of remembering the amount.

“I need three boats,” Constano said.

“You need eight.”

“I can pay for three.”

“Then you need five friends.”

“Will you be one?”

Matteo folded the wet cloak more carefully on his knees.

“Not for nothing.”

Constano waited.

“The western road has new escorts on it.”

“Adrian?”

“Middle-canton men. Different badges. The same permission.”

“To do what?”

“To ask questions about cargo that belongs to people they used to leave alone.”

“Yours.”

“Among others.”

The woman brought a second plate and another piece of fish. Matteo thanked her and put his cloak on the floor at last.

“I can give you three boats,” he said. “And two crews for the boats you have that nobody will take upriver.”

“In return?”

“Warehouse room here. Dry room. Under your authority.”

“For what?”

“Whatever I decide to put in it.”

“No.”

“Grain, cloth, people, books, furniture. Nothing that explodes.”

“People?”

Matteo cut the fish into small pieces before eating any of it.

“Some of ours may want to come across.”

Constano set down his fork.

“Have you been warned?”

“I have been asked whether our house intends to continue obstructing the settlement of the western provinces.”

“By whom?”

“A man who used to ask for introductions to my nephews.”

“Did you give him any?”

“Yes. He was useful.”

Matteo did not go home immediately after supper.

He asked to see the warehouse. Constano looked at the rain beyond the window, then at the swollen seam of his shoe, and rang for his coat.

“You needn’t come,” Matteo said.

“I’ve offered you rooms for your family. I ought to know what they look like.”

“I can tell you.”

“You might be polite.”

“Have I given you reason to fear that?”

They went in a covered handcart normally used to move rope from the countinghouse to the quay. The rope-maker’s wife insisted on a cushion. Constano sat on it with his cane between his knees and Matteo walking beside him. He disliked being lower than the people speaking to him, but disliked arriving in too much pain to think even more.

Fairport after dark smelled of fish guts, wet timber, and smoke trapped beneath the awnings. A man was singing somewhere beyond the customs steps. Another voice called the wrong words back to him. Matteo joined in for half a line before stopping when he caught Constano’s expression.

“You know that song.”

“I know who taught you it.”

“My education was broad.”

“It was loud.”

The warehouse keeper was waiting with a lantern. She was a small woman with silver hair plaited tightly beneath her cap. She introduced herself as Dena and told Constano the roof did not leak, the western door would not lock, and someone had been sleeping upstairs without her permission.

“In that order?” he asked.

“The roof matters to the cargo. The door matters to me.”

“And the sleeper?”

“A boy. He runs when I come.”

They inspected the door first. The bolt had been fitted too high for its socket; years of dragging it downward had loosened the whole plate. Matteo held the lantern while Dena demonstrated. Constano could see how the repair had been postponed each time because forcing the bolt still worked if one knew the trick.

“Tomorrow,” he told the factor who had accompanied them.

The man nodded.

“Your name?”

“Renn, my lord.”

“I know your name. I want Dena to know whose name to ask for if nobody comes.”

Renn introduced himself to her. The woman repeated it once and seemed satisfied.

Upstairs the rooms were better than he expected. They had been used for storing sailcloth, and the air was dry. Screens could divide the space. The stairs were broad enough for two people to pass with bundles, though a chest would block them if someone stopped halfway.

Matteo went to the window. Across the dark water Marcott showed more lights than Fairport, its houses climbing behind the port in narrow bands.

“My youngest nephew used to say he could see our kitchen from here.”

“Could he?”

“No. He pointed to a tavern. We didn’t tell his mother.”

“Which nephew?”

“The one who married the woman you said would frighten him.”

“Did she?”

“Into doing something useful, eventually.”

Constano lowered himself onto a bale. The room would be noisy when the men worked below it, hot when the sun struck the roof, uncomfortable for people accustomed to finding a servant between themselves and every practical difficulty. It could shelter them.

“Are you sending children?” he asked.

“Perhaps.”

“Then send somebody who knows which medicines they take. And their own bedding if there’s time.”

Matteo turned from the window. “You make it sound settled.”

“You asked me to prepare.”

“Yes.”

They were interrupted by a crash below, followed by shouting.

Renn reached the stairs first. Constano waited until Matteo had gone down, then followed at the pace his hip allowed. He could hear a man asking for space, the high, insistent repetition of someone in pain, and Dena ordering people out of her way.

A worker had slipped while moving a small cask on the loading floor. The cask had struck his shin against a post. He lay with both hands round his leg, refusing to let anyone touch him. Another man stood over him holding the rope they had used, its end frayed and dark.

“I said it wouldn’t hold,” he kept saying.

The injured man swore at him.

Constano stopped at the foot of the stairs.

“Get the surgeon from the customs house.”

“He won’t come without payment,” someone said.

“Tell him I am here.”

“That was what we told him last time. Not you. Another lord. He said he was owed from last time too.”

Constano took coins from his purse and put them into Renn’s hand.

“Then bring him with these.”

Renn ran out.

The workers had formed a half-circle. None seemed inclined to resume unloading. A broad man with a red scarf round his neck stood closest to Constano, arms folded.

“How long have you been working?” Constano asked.

“Since light.”

“Who ordered the night work?”

“Your men want grain loaded by dawn. Other people’s cargo has to come off before it can go on.”

He had known that in the abstract. It had not appeared in the figures sent to him as a night without sleep for these particular men.

“How many hours to finish?”

“With this rope? Until someone else falls.”

Dena held up the frayed end. Constano took it. The fibres came apart beneath his thumb with scarcely any force.

“New rope from the countinghouse. No lifting until it comes.”

“Still the same men doing the lifting,” the man with the scarf said.

“Have you others?”

“Have you pay for them?”

Matteo spoke from beside the injured worker. “My crews arrive before midnight. They can take the first part.”

“Boatmen aren’t warehouse men.”

“They know ropes.”

The worker considered that. “And our pay?”
