“For the hours worked,” Constano said. “Including the time we stop.”

“You’ll put that before the boat leaves?”

There was a challenge in the question, but not an unreasonable one. If the grain departed and the men representing its owner departed with it, they would be left chasing a promise upriver.

“Before it leaves.”

They returned to the injured man, who had begun to shiver. Dena brought a blanket. Matteo sat beside him and asked where his family lived. The man answered in bursts between clenched teeth. His daughter would be waiting for him to bring food; he had promised something better than the previous evening’s bread.

Matteo sent a boy to the house with a coin and the address repeated three times.

By the time the surgeon arrived, the rain had stopped. Constano remained until the leg was splinted and the worker taken home on a door from an unused room. He watched the men handle it, one at each corner. They were careful in a way they had not been with the cask.

Renn returned with fresh rope and a name for the man who would mend the lock.

“Before midday,” he said to Dena.

She looked at Constano. He shook his head towards Renn.

“You can tell him,” he said. “He’ll be the one coming back.”

As they left, something moved beneath the outside stair. A boy slipped through the shadow and ran along the quay with a blanket bundled under one arm.

“The sleeper,” Dena said.

Constano almost called after him. He stopped, remembering how much authority his raised voice would carry among tired men looking for someone easier to blame than a broken rope.

“Let him come back tonight,” he said. “Tomorrow ask him whether he wants work. Don’t make shelter depend on the answer.”

Dena nodded once.

On the way to the countinghouse, Matteo walked close beside the handcart.

“You can’t attend every load.”

“I know.”

“It’s not an accusation.”

“I know that too.”

He watched the lamps pass above him. The people who had made them had fitted small covers to keep rain from the wick, a simple improvement repeated along the length of the quay. Somewhere there had been a first lamp without one and a person who had decided not to accept the inconvenience.

He asked Renn to find out who inspected the lifting gear. Then he asked the men pushing the cart to slow down over the next patch of stones.

The grain went aboard before dawn.

Constano watched the last sacks carried over the plank from the shelter of an upturned boat. The rope-maker had found him a stool that fitted beneath it. Rain tapped on the hull above his head.

The captain counted each sack aloud. The men carrying them made fun of his counting until he stopped and made them begin the load again. After that nobody spoke except to give a number.

Across the river, Marcott’s windows showed as little yellow strokes through the rain. He could see a bell tower, the pale front of the customs house, and the dark shape of warehouses between. A boat was coming across from that direction with no cargo visible above its sides.

Matteo had sent the crews.

Constano wrote their names in his pocket book. Beneath them he wrote the warehouse he had promised: the long one behind the salt quay, newly roofed, with space for people above the loading floor if they needed it.

At least the people sheltering there would have a dry roof.

The captain came to ask for the order.

Constano handed it to him and watched him read the destination twice.

“The northern granary?”

“Yes.”

“Corbel’s men are meant to be keeping that road.”

“So they are.”

“And are they?”

Constano closed the pocket book.

“I have sent to ask.”

The captain looked at the paper again, folded it, and put it inside his coat.

“I’ll take it as far as I can.”

“Thank you.”

Constano had meant to sound formal. He had not managed it. The captain heard the difference and looked away before answering.

“All right.”

On the following afternoon Matteo took him to a meal at the pilots’ house.

Constano had meant to refuse. There were four letters awaiting answers and a captain below the stairs who had been told twice that his cargo could not be declared medicinal merely because a physician intended to drink it. Matteo said the letters would still be there after they ate and the captain would be more reasonable if he believed they had gone elsewhere.

The pilots’ house stood above the older quay, where the river traffic came in beside the sea-going vessels. Its windows had been opened to let out the smell of frying fish. Below them, ropes creaked as a loaded hull rose against its moorings. The light on the water was green close to the pilings and bright enough to hurt farther out.

Dunstan was there, with a dog under his chair and a band of fresh cloth around one hand. He said he had cut it on a hinge. The dog had no respect for the injury and nudged the hand whenever the harbourmaster stopped scratching its head.

“Whose hinge?” Matteo asked.

“The harbour’s.”

“Then the harbour should pay.”

“It pays me already.”

“Not enough, if you have to repair it yourself.”

Constano sat where he could stretch his foot beneath the table. The owner brought fish in a shallow dish, onions softened in oil, and bread with a dark crust which broke noisily when Dunstan tore it. He kept a piece for the dog until the owner told him that animal had eaten twice since noon.

They spoke of a quay wall needing work before winter. Fenn wanted the unloading gangs moved until the stones could be reset. Dunstan wanted the work done at night. The owner, whose bedroom faced the quay, asked whether the harbourmaster would like to sleep above the hammering himself.

Constano listened, ate, and found he was enjoying the argument because no one expected him to settle it by declaring somebody loyal.

Afterward they watched a cargo come ashore. The men carried chests wrapped in waxed cloth, their corners dark where salt water had soaked through. A merchant opened one on the quay. Fine wool lay inside, dyed a red so deep that the wet folds looked black. He lifted a length to the sun and swore at a stain near the edge.

“Cut round it,” Matteo called down.

“From your cloth, I would.”

Dunstan’s dog barked at the falling wrapper and had to be brought back from the window. Constano laughed until he coughed. It startled him; he had not laughed so freely since before the funeral.

On the way back they passed a woman selling preserved fruit in jars. He bought one for Jaennis and another for Oria. The seller wrapped them separately and asked which household should receive the larger. He told her to make them the same size.

“They aren’t the same fruit, my lord.”

“Then I am trusting you to choose fairly.”

She considered him, exchanged one jar for another, and tied the covers. He would have to send them before the next cargo crowded them off his desk.

The medicinal wine captain had gone by the time he returned. The four letters were joined by three more. He took off his coat, put the fruit where he could see it, and asked Aldith to begin with the one from the northern stores.

Chapter Five: An Earl’s Terms

The earl’s messenger had washed before asking to see the queen. Tylan told Veralyn he considered this a good sign. Then the man presented his expenses: he had changed horses twice on the road and expected them to pay for both.

Veralyn received him in the open, beside the forge. The farrier was fitting a shoe to Anneth’s grey, recovered with two other horses from the hollow Bucktree had entered with Anneth. The horse was sound except for a cut below one hock. Veralyn had sent word that Anneth could collect him once the farrier was satisfied.

“My lord offers his submission,” the messenger said.

“Where is he?”

“At his house.”

“Then why is his submission here?”

“He asks assurance of his safety.”

“He can have it for the journey.”

“And afterward.”

“That depends on what he submits.”

The man laid out the terms. The earl would acknowledge Veralyn’s title, send the accustomed levy when lawfully summoned, and cease correspondence prejudicial to the peace. In return she would confirm his rights, discharge the extraordinary charges laid on his estates, and receive his younger son at court.

“As a hostage?”

“As a page.”

“Does he have an older son?”

“Two.”

“And how old is the younger?”

“Seven, Majesty.”

The farrier set the shoe against the hoof. Steam rose with a smell of burning horn.

Veralyn watched until he took it away.

“Tell your lord I have enough children to feed.”

Tylan waited until the messenger had been given something to eat.

“He can close the eastern pass.”

“He could have closed it last month.”

“He could. He didn’t. He is offering to close it now.”

“And then we leave him behind us.”

“Yes.”

She walked beside the lines of horses. Her own mount lifted its head and then lowered it again when it saw she carried nothing.

“If I confirm his rights without deciding which rights he has, he’ll spend the winter inventing them.”

“Confirm the ones in the last accepted return.”

“He’ll dispute the return.”

“Then we learn what he actually wants.”

She stopped beside a bay with a swollen foreleg.

“How many like this?”

“Twenty-three today.”

“And tomorrow?”

“If we go over the ridge again, more.”

Tylan laid his hand on the bay’s neck. The horse stood quietly while he checked the binding.

“His son isn’t the security,” he said. “The pass is. Put our captain there with his men and ours. Give the man something he loses the moment the road opens against us.”

“You sound like a lawyer.”

“I have been listening to them for years. It was bound to do damage.”

She sent the messenger back with terms written in her own hand. The earl could keep his youngest child. His eldest would come to camp with forty horse, and the eastern pass would receive a mixed garrison under a captain she named. Disputed rights would remain disputed until the campaign ended. No man would be punished merely for having served under the earl, provided he now obeyed the settlement.

She postponed collection of the charges, leaving the sums owed.

The messenger read that clause twice.

“His lordship may find this difficult.”

“So have I.”

Dunmere came to the shepherds’ hall three mornings later.

The roof was low and blackened above the central hearth. Wet cloaks hung from pegs driven into the posts, their hems dripping onto a floor strewn with dry rushes. Veralyn could smell wool even before she entered. Outside, sheep crowded the bank where the grass remained uncut. Their bells made a scattered, irregular sound whenever the wind fell.

The hall-keeper met her at the threshold and looked at Shadowsteel.

She had been told the rule. No drawn steel beneath the roof. She unbuckled the belt and handed it to the man waiting beside the door. He accepted the weight with both hands and laid the sword on a bench apart from the other weapons.

Dunmere had already surrendered his own.

He was broader than she expected and less handsome than the seal on his letters. He wore a good coat whose sleeves had been turned to hide worn cuffs, and carried his gloves rather than giving them to an attendant. A man accustomed to being obeyed at home and careful to show he knew where home ended.

His eldest son stood beside him. The resemblance was strongest when neither was speaking: the same set of the jaw, the same habit of looking first at the person nearest the door. The son bowed more deeply.

“Majesty.”

“Orren,” his father supplied, as if she had asked.

She inclined her head. Orren was well into adulthood. He wore a ring polished by the habit of turning it with his thumb.

They sat on opposite sides of the hearth, with Tylan at Veralyn’s right. The hall-keeper poured ale into wooden cups and withdrew far enough to appear uninvolved while remaining able to hear every word.

“I am grateful for the assurance of passage,” Dunmere began.

“You asked for it.”

“One may still be grateful.”

He had a good voice, warm enough to make an ordinary disagreement seem unnecessary. Veralyn had heard men like him settle a hall by speaking before anyone else remembered to be angry.

“My messenger tells me we are close to agreement.”

“Your messenger has brought us into the same room.”

Dunmere accepted the correction. He spoke about the northern roads, the old obligations of his house, and the losses his tenants had suffered under requisition. He did not mention the requisitions made by his own men. Tylan asked where he proposed to draw grain for a garrison at the pass.

“From the southern farms.”

“Those farms have already supplied your mounted men.”

“They have more.”

“How much more?”

Dunmere turned his cup in his hands. “My steward can give you the quantities.”

“Before we sign.”

“Certainly.”

Veralyn watched Orren. He had not touched his ale. He was listening to the exchange with the stillness of a man who knew the figures and had been told not to give them.

“Your other offer,” she said.

Dunmere looked back at her.

“You wrote that you could shorten the campaign. I assumed you intended more than your own submission.”

The earl put his cup down.

“Hask is at the upper quarry house. He has fewer men than your officers believe and more horses than he can feed. If you move by the western drove-road, he cannot take the baggage out before you reach him.”

“Why tell me?”

“Because this must end somewhere.”

“And you would prefer it ended at his house.”

“He has refused every means of ending it elsewhere.”

That might be true. It might also be what Dunmere needed to believe before informing against a man whose table he had shared the previous week. Veralyn asked the name of the keeper at the quarry house.

He gave it without hesitation.

Tylan sent two riders while the talk continued. He told them to approach openly, bearing an offer of parley rather than trying to take the place with two men. Dunmere watched them leave through the doorway, his face giving her nothing useful.

They spent the rest of the morning on rights of hearing. Dunmere laid a finger on the clause reserving his own court for all matters arising on his land.

“All?” Veralyn asked. “If one of those farmers accuses your mounted men of taking his last hay, he must bring the complaint to you?”

“To my court. According to custom.”

Orren’s ale stood untouched between his hands. Veralyn drew the draft towards her.

“No,” Veralyn said.

“My people know their own customs.”

“They may continue knowing them. Appeals come to the royal court.”

“Under a judge who has never seen the hills?”

“Under a judge who has not been paid by you.”

Orren moved a hand towards his father’s cup, as if making room on the bench. It was his first movement since they sat down. Dunmere saw it and moderated his answer.

“We can name assessors from the country.”

“We can.”

They agreed to that. The earl seemed relieved enough that Veralyn almost allowed herself to enjoy the prospect of finishing before dinner.

Then the riders returned.

The quarry house was empty. Its keeper had received Hask’s company two nights before. They had departed before light that morning, taking every serviceable horse and leaving their injured behind. A boy had arrived with a message after supper. The keeper did not know his name but described the badge on his coat.

Orren looked at his father.

Veralyn saw the glance. Dunmere had kept his eyes on the rider throughout the report.

“Yours,” she said when it was finished.

“A badge can be borrowed.”

“Was it?”

The earl picked up his gloves. For a moment she thought he intended to leave. Instead he laid them down again, one across the other.

“I warned a man whose salt I had eaten that an offer had been made.”

“Your offer.”

“Yes.”

“So he could refuse it by leaving.”

“So he could choose to receive your herald or go. I did not promise to deliver him bound.”

Tylan’s eyes remained on the rider. “How long has he been gone?”

“Five hours. Perhaps six.”

“Loads?”

“Packed light.”

He nodded and dismissed the man. There would be no profitable pursuit. Veralyn could see the answer in the way he folded his hands.

Dunmere began speaking again. He had meant the offer, he said. Hask had chosen to waste it. It would not serve the north to turn a failed parley into a reason to reject a settlement that still offered men, a pass, and the means of feeding both.

He was right about the settlement. That made her want to punish him more, because he knew exactly how much of his own conduct he expected it to excuse.

“Orren goes to Valora.”

The son looked directly at her.

Dunmere’s voice lost its warmth. “That was not proposed.”

“It is proposed now.”

“My second son can go.”

“He can command the forty at the pass. The eldest goes to Valora as a ward of the crown.”

“Majesty,” Tylan said quietly.

She did not turn.

“The pass gives us the security,” he said. “The son could remain with the mixed company.”

“No.”

A small, complete word. It pleased her to discover that she did not need to improve it.

Orren spoke before his father could answer.

“May my wife accompany me?”

“If she wishes.”

“She has a household to arrange.”

“She will have time to arrange it.”

“How much?”

“Ten days.”

He looked at his father. This time the older man looked away.

“I will need a letter assuring her of passage,” Orren said. “And one to the people who will receive us. I do not want her waiting outside a gate while someone decides whether we are prisoners.”

Veralyn studied him. She had thought he would protest on his own account. His precision about the woman who would travel with him made her aware, belatedly, of the ring he had been turning.

“You’ll have both.”

Dunmere asked to speak with his son alone. She allowed it. They went to the far end of the hall, where their voices remained audible as sound but not as words.

Tylan looked at the hearth.

“He warned a man he meant to betray,” he said. “That is not an uncommon kindness.”

“He meant me to pay for it.”

“Yes.”

She waited for him to argue. He did not.

“It is still worth accepting his submission,” she said.

“Yes.”

“And I am accepting it.”

Tylan looked up then, but only because the two men were returning.

Dunmere signed. His hand was steady. Orren signed beneath him as witness to the terms that named his own removal. The hall-keeper made his mark last, having listened closely enough to ask which stores would furnish the first week’s grain.

Before they left, Veralyn found a small sheet among the papers Tylan had set aside.

Queen’s room, west eaves. Two guards at stair. Sword outside door.

The last entry had been crossed through.

She handed it to him. “You are including the furniture now?”

He folded the sheet.

“An unnecessary entry.”

“Apparently.”

He did not explain it. She did not ask.

Anneth was mending a harness when Veralyn returned to the steward’s house. The grey stood in the yard, putting more weight on the injured leg. Beside the gate a little heap of stones had been gathered from the hayfield so the cart wheels would not strike them.

“You’ve come back hungry,” Anneth said.

“How can you tell?”

“You looked at the kitchen before you looked at me.”

Veralyn crossed the yard and kissed her, making the order less clear to anyone watching.

“Better?”

“Not if you intend to tell me you have no time to eat.”

They ate in the small room behind the hall, where Anneth’s father kept tools too valuable to lend without remembering to whom. The food was plain and hot. Veralyn found she was hungry enough to resent conversation until she had finished the first bowl.

Anneth let her eat. Afterwards she asked about Dunmere.

“He has submitted.”

“Will that hold?”

“His son goes to Valora.”

“Which?”

“Orren.”

Anneth looked down at the harness in her lap. “Elset will go with him.”

“You know her?”

“We know most people who buy hay.”

“Does she want to go?”

“You’d have to ask her.”

Veralyn did not like the answer. She had been asked enough questions about a decision which was sound, enforceable, and less severe than Dunmere could reasonably have expected after the damage his men had done.

Anneth threaded the needle again.

“You look as if you want me to tell you it was right.”

“Was it?”

“I wasn’t there.”

It took Veralyn a moment to let the irritation pass. Anneth had answered as honestly as she could.

“Will you sit with me tonight?” Veralyn asked.

“I was going to.”

“And tomorrow?”

“Perhaps.”

She looked up, smiling now.

“You were rather certain about tomorrow.”

Veralyn laughed. “Am I becoming tedious?”

“Only when you answer your own invitations.”

Later they went upstairs together. The sword remained outside the door. Anneth did not have to move it this time.

In the morning a courier brought the names of barons who had failed to furnish the men asked of them. Veralyn stood at the window reading while Anneth dressed. The list was longer than the previous one. At the bottom, Seniro had written that he would send another count when he knew how many of the promises represented men already on the road.

The date was eight days old.

She put the paper beside Dunmere’s submission. Anneth touched her shoulder as she passed, and Veralyn caught her hand without looking away from the two sheets.

That evening she wrote to Jaennis.

She began with the pass and the horses. After half a page she put the sheet aside and began again.

I have spent the day bargaining with a man who sent me cold poultry and now wishes to send me a child. You would have made him feel honoured to keep both.

She wrote about Anneth’s horse, about Tylan in the little tent, about the rain. She left her sleepless night out of the letter and put in a question about Jaennis’s headaches, then crossed out the beginning because it sounded like her father’s way of asking after an inconvenient weakness.

Are you eating? she wrote instead. Something besides pears.

She folded the letter before she could improve it further.

Chapter Six: Forty Men

Corbel sent twelve men and a priest.

Seniro counted them from the granary steps. He counted the priest separately because he could not tell whether the man had brought a weapon beneath his cloak or only the large opinion visible on his face.

“Forty,” Seniro said.

The eldest of the twelve looked back at the others, as though the missing men might have arrived quietly while he was dismounting.

“My lord sent what could be spared.”

“And the priest?”

“To attest that the obligation had been met.”

Seniro came down the steps.

The priest was a thin man with an old bruise beneath one eye. That gave Seniro pause. A man who had recently been struck might have a better idea of the world’s arrangements than one who only spoke about them.

“Has it?” Seniro asked him.

“Lord Corbel maintains—”

“I asked whether forty men have arrived.”

“Twelve mounted men may reasonably be valued—”

“Can they sleep standing?”

The priest stopped.

“Can each watch three roads at once? Can they drive forty wagons? Because if they can, I owe your lord an apology.”

The captain of the twelve looked at the ground. He was trying not to smile. Seniro turned on him.

“You find this amusing?”

“No, my lord.”

“You should. It is excellent. I shall explain it to the men who have been awake for two nights.”

He sent the riders to eat and kept the priest.

Corbel’s answer was longer than the one before. It cited a judgement from the previous reign, a boundary finding from the reign before that, and the agreement placing the licensed ferries under Oria’s custody. It concluded that extraordinary service could not be imposed while ordinary obligations remained undetermined.

There was the phrase again.

“Who drafted this?”

“The household clerk.”

“Alone?”

“I do not know.”

“Who carried it to you?”

“A man from the lower valley.”

“Whose man?”

“He bore letters from three houses.”

Seniro laid the paper on the table.

At the end of the room, Avren was showing a carter where to put the flour. They had saved it from the mill only to find that the sacks waiting here had been allowed to rot against a damp wall. A boy was separating the spoiled ones, pressing each with his thumb before moving it.

“What has Corbel been promised?” Seniro asked.

“I cannot answer that.”

“Cannot or will not?”

The priest touched the bruise beneath his eye.

“My lord, I have a parish between his manor and the road you are defending. I have been asked that question by three parties. None has stayed afterward.”

Seniro looked at him for a long moment.

“Sit down.”

“I would rather stand.”

“Then stand. Tell me what happens in your parish when my men leave.”

It took some time. There were stores claimed twice, a bridge damaged by a wagon whose driver insisted he served the crown, and a boy taken to guide soldiers through the marsh who had not come back. Corbel had forbidden another levy until the boy returned. Then other reasons had been added to the prohibition, most of them concerning Corbel’s rights rather than the boy.

“What is his name?” Seniro asked.

The priest told him.

Seniro called Avren over and had him write it down.

“If he is with our men, find him. If he is dead, find who can say so.”

“And the forty?” Avren asked afterward.

“Twelve are here. Use them.”

“Corbel will say we accepted the settlement.”

“Then write that we haven’t. I need men more than I need the pleasure of sending men away.”

He went to the door. Beyond the yard the road shone white between the wet fields. Somewhere along it a man was carrying the same sentence from one house to another, and every time it arrived there were fewer soldiers where Seniro had expected them.

“Whoever writes the next letter,” he told Avren, “ask him to come himself.”

Constano’s answer came in the same post as a list of men requiring boots.

Seniro read the first paragraph aloud to Avren, loudly enough that the clerk at the next table stopped writing.

“The northern store. Again. He knows where the guns are.”

“He gives a reason,” Avren said.

“He gives four. None brings powder nearer.”

The clerk resumed work, carefully. Seniro read the rest to himself. Halven’s lighter would not approach the exposed landing. His brother had agreed to release the guild from the old emergency terms after this passage. Seniro had relied on those terms when he ordered the guns moved forward. He had relied on Conlin to find a boat when every sensible boatman would rather be elsewhere.

He called for a fresh sheet and began dictating a reply. Avren waited until he reached the sentence about what men at Fairport might understand if they had ever seen a battle.

“Your brother has,” he said.

Seniro stopped.

“I know.”

“Shall the clerk keep that sentence?”

The clerk looked at the blank space after the words he had already written. Seniro could see the satisfaction Avren was trying to conceal. A captain who had spent a morning being corrected ought to be allowed one successful correction of his own.

“No. Start again.”

He went outside before beginning the second version. At the granary, a teamster was fitting a new collar to an old horse. The animal leaned against the pole between attempts. It was the third team Seniro had taken from the local farms that week. He had told the farmers it would be returned after the short movement from the landing.

“How far to the northern store?” he asked the teamster.

“With a load?”

“Empty going. Powder coming back.”

The man looked towards the road and gave the time. Longer than Seniro had allowed. The direct lane could take a horse or an empty cart, but a loaded powder wagon would have to go around the wet ground. They would need to rest the team before returning.

“Can you do it with these?”

“Yes, my lord. Once.”

“And afterward?”

“We’ll see.”

Seniro rubbed the animal’s neck above the collar. Its coat was thickening for winter. Beneath it he could feel the places where weeks of work had taken flesh away.

The local reeve came up while he stood there. Two households had sent him to inquire about the return of their teams. A third wanted to know why soldiers were paying for fodder at one farm and taking it without payment at the next.

“Who is taking it?”

“Men with your order.”

“Bring the order.”

“They’ve gone on.”

“Then bring whoever saw it.”

The reeve nodded, grateful to have an instruction he could carry back. Seniro watched him go and understood, unwillingly, that he had sent Constano much the same request: make the means appear, and do not require the man asking to follow every difficulty through to the people supplying it.

He returned to the table. The clerk had laid the unfinished answer aside.

“Tell my brother the powder goes to the northern store. I will provide the teams. Tell him I cannot release another escort for the river and that he must say now if the pilot requires one.”

The clerk wrote.

“Ask when the guild’s next passage can be obtained under the new terms.”

“Anything further, my lord?”

Seniro looked at the first draft. His brother would have recognised the anger and answered the useful parts. He had been doing that for years.

“Thank him for the boat.”

The clerk dipped his pen again.

Avren waited until he had finished before asking which horses should be sent. Seniro gave him the two least exhausted teams and ordered an infantry section to accompany them. Taking the section weakened a watch he wanted kept; taking any other men would weaken another.

“Tell the farmers where their animals are going,” he said. “If they cannot have them when I promised, they should at least know why.”

The teamster started before dusk. Seniro stood at the granary door while the wagons turned, watching the horses lean into their collars. The wheels crossed the wet patch by the gate one after another. When the last had passed, he picked up the boot list and asked Avren how many pairs could be mended.

Braedyn arrived while they were turning the wet grain.

Seniro heard his brother speaking outside before he saw him. He had that useful voice which could make a man carrying a sack believe the speaker had come to help rather than to inspect him. By the time he entered the store he knew which corner leaked and whose son had been taken as a guide.

“You’ve been popular,” he said.

“I’ve been busy.”

“Yes. People have remarked on the difference.”

He took off his gloves and helped lift one end of the cloth on which the grain lay spread. The kernels ran into a heap. Seniro caught the opposite edge and together they shifted it towards the door, where there was more air.

For a few minutes neither spoke. Their father’s death lay between them less heavily while they had something to lift.

“Conlin?” Seniro asked.

“Gone to Fairport. He wanted me to tell you he has people he trusts at the house.”

“That means he doesn’t want me sending my people there.”

“It may also mean he has people he trusts.”

Seniro looked at him. His brother smiled, then put the cloth down.

“I’m going to Corbel,” Seniro said.

“I thought you might.”

“You can come.”

“I intended to.”

“Then we are spared an argument.”

“Not the important one.”

They rode the next morning with forty men. The road climbed out of the drained meadows through hedges heavy with late berries. Here the harvest had been taken in. Stubble shone pale where the sun reached it, and women gleaning behind a cart stopped to watch the horsemen pass. One made a sign Seniro recognised from funerals. Another pulled her hand down.

The younger Braedyn saw it too and said nothing.

Corbel’s hall stood on ground that had always remained dry, above the strips his family claimed Fenwood’s drainage had ruined. Its outer wall had been repaired in several kinds of stone. A strip of new limewash below one window made the rest look dirty by comparison. Men waited at the gate with weapons visible and no intention of using them first.

Seniro left most of his company outside.

A steward took them through a passage smelling of apples stored for winter. Lady Corbel met them before her husband did. She had known their father long enough to call both sons by the names they used at home.

“Wymund. Braedyn.”

She offered the kiss of greeting with impeccable warmth. Seniro returned it and wished briefly that he had come for any other reason.

“I was sorry not to attend,” she said.

“My brother received your letter.”

“I hope he knew it was meant.”

“He did.”

Her husband entered while they were still standing. Aymar Corbel had dressed for a visitor, in dark cloth with a narrow fur collar, and retained the dust on his boots to show the visitor he had work outside the hall. Seniro had seen the same combination often enough to know both messages were intended.

“You bring a company to call on an old friend.”

“I bring a company because the road needs one.”

“Mine does not.”

“Then you can lend me some of the men making it safe.”

Corbel gestured towards the inner room. Food had been set out, including a bowl of apples cut into quarters. Seniro took one because refusing hospitality would begin the argument at the wrong point.

They spoke first about the twelve riders. Corbel insisted they were better men than the foot asked of him, with their own mounts and equipment. Seniro replied that a horse could not hold a storehouse door while its rider escorted a wagon, and that assigning three values to one man did not allow him to stand in three places.

“The service is disputed,” Corbel said.

“The need isn’t.”

“The need is always urgent when Fenwood wants something.”

Braedyn put his cup down gently. Seniro heard the warning in the little sound and let Corbel continue.

The grievance began with wet meadows in the grandfather’s time. A cut made to save Fenwood’s lower ground had changed the flooding on Corbel’s boundary. Compensation had been discussed. Some had been paid. A later grant of passage had been taken by one family as settlement of the remainder and by the other as recognition of a separate right. Their father had kept the matter from becoming an open quarrel by visiting, remembering names, finding work for a younger son, and conceding small things whenever the large one threatened to return.

Seniro had known the broad dispute. He had not known that Corbel’s eldest sister still kept her grandfather’s account of the lost pasture wrapped with the marriage papers.

Lady Corbel brought it out.

She laid it before him without triumph. The writing had browned. Water had damaged one edge, but the names of the meadows remained readable.

“Your father saw this,” she said.

“I believe he did.”

“He did not call it foolish.”

“Neither have I.”

“No. You’ve called it badly timed.”

Seniro looked at the page, then at the woman. It would have been easier to answer her husband, whose anger invited an anger of his own. She was asking him to acknowledge something which had survived every reasonable explanation for postponing it.

“It will be heard,” he said.

“When?”

“After we have kept Valdais from taking the land both of us are arguing about.”

Corbel pushed his chair back.

“You speak as though he came to my door uninvited while your men have done nothing but ask leave.”

“Our men keep the queen’s peace.”

“Two thirds of mine came home from keeping it. The rest are names my steward still reads at the chapel. Now you ask for another company, and when I ask what is owed to the households already emptied, you tell me the time is wrong.”

He stood with one hand on the chair. Seniro could see where his knuckles had swollen; the rings sat tightly above them.

“The queen’s peace ends where her army ends,” Corbel said. “A lord who feeds his own has not broken faith.”

Braedyn looked up.

Seniro let the words settle. They had a finish unlike the rest of the man’s speech, a shape meant to be repeated by someone who could not remember the whole dispute.

“Whose army keeps Valdais off your stores?” he asked.

“Mine, if yours cannot.”

“And when yours cannot?”

Corbel said nothing.

Seniro had forty men outside and a larger force within a day’s ride. He could take the steward, seize the papers, put the lord beneath arrest until somebody higher decided whether urgency justified the act. His officers would support him. His father, on a different day, might have done the same.

Braedyn drew him aside on the pretext of checking the numbers in the old grant.

“Maresse is watching,” he said softly. “Lisle too. They will hear what happens here before they hear why.”

“They’re hearing it now from the people who taught him that speech.”

“Yes.”

Seniro looked back towards the hearth. Lady Corbel was folding the old paper. Her husband watched them as if their lowered voices proved he had been right to keep his men at home.

“Where is the new post?” Seniro asked.

“The lower lane,” Braedyn said. “A mile from here.”

They returned to the table. Seniro repeated that the drainage grievance would be heard. He asked Lady Corbel to send a copy of the account to Constano, and his brother to ensure it reached him. Then he asked Corbel to accompany him to the lower lane.

The baron refused.

“Very well,” Seniro said. “I will see it without you.”

The post was a hut, a barrier, and five men who had not expected the marshal to arrive from their lord’s house.

Two wagons waited behind the barrier. The first carried sacks of rye. The second held a broken bedstead and a woman’s household goods, lashed beneath an old sail. The woman sat beside the driver with a baby asleep against her, too tired even to look hopeful when she saw the soldiers.

The toll-sergeant had a broad leather belt and a sword whose scabbard showed little wear. He began explaining the emergency charge before Seniro had dismounted.

“Show me the authority.”

The man brought out a paper bearing Corbel’s seal.

“This is a levy on traffic using his landing,” Seniro said. “Where is the landing?”

“Beyond the bend.”

“Are these wagons going there?”

The sergeant did not answer.

The rye was going to a mill by the eastern road. The woman was going to her sister. Neither intended to use a boat. Both had been stopped because the lane offered a convenient place to stop them, and the seal on the paper looked enough like authority to make arguing dangerous.

Seniro told the men to lift the barrier.

The sergeant put a hand on his sword.

He did not draw it. Perhaps he had meant only to steady the scabbard. Seniro’s nearest captain caught his wrist before the distinction could matter and brought him against the hut wall hard enough to shake dust from the eaves.

“Alive,” Seniro said.

The captain eased his grip reluctantly.

They searched the hut. There was money, food taken in place of money, a girl’s cloak held because her father had promised to return with the charge, and three papers whose owners had apparently surrendered them in the hope of retrieving something more useful.

“Who keeps the account?” Braedyn asked.

The sergeant nodded at a younger man with ink on his sleeve.

Braedyn took him aside. His voice remained almost conversational. Seniro knew he would obtain more that way than anyone would obtain by making the man watch his officer struck again.

The marshal ordered the contents carried out. The woman’s bedstead went through the barrier. The rye wagon followed. He asked each driver to name what had been taken and where they could be found if it was recovered.

Then he told his men to burn the hut.

Braedyn turned sharply. “Wymund.”

“The hut. Not the bridge. Not the landing. This post.”

One captain was already pulling dry brush from beneath the wall. Another asked whether the sergeant should remain inside.

Seniro looked at him until the man lowered his eyes.

“He comes with us.”

Corbel arrived when the roof had begun to smoke.

He came without his wife, riding hard enough that his horse was blowing. For a moment he appeared unable to understand what he saw. Then he looked at Seniro with an expression the marshal had never seen on him before.

“My father would have been heard in this house.”

“So were you.”

“You heard me and did this.”

“Yes.”

The roof caught. Smoke moved low across the lane, making the horses sidestep. Seniro held his mount steady while his brother arranged the prisoner’s escort.

“This is royal road,” Seniro said. “You will not close it under a grant belonging to a ferry. Send the forty men.”

Corbel looked at the burning hut rather than at him.

“You will have your answer.”

They rode back through the harvested fields. The gleaners were gone. A pair of crows worked along a strip of overturned soil where a cart had cut the corner too closely.

Braedyn waited until they were clear of the hall’s riders.

“Father would have stayed to supper.”

“I know.”

“He might still have burned the post.”

“I know that too.”

His brother rode beside him for a while in silence.

“At Maresse’s,” he said finally, “they use the same words. At Lisle’s they say a household which feeds its people has kept the true peace.”

“Who brings them?”

“Different men.”

“Find one.”

“I am trying.”

Behind them, the toll-sergeant rode between two guards. He had asked twice whether he would be hanged. Seniro had answered twice that he would be heard. It was the same answer he had given Lady Corbel, and he disliked hearing both promises in his own voice as the light failed.

Chapter Seven: The War That Will Not Stand

The peat-stacks burned low in the rain. By morning only their centres were red, with steam drifting from the soaked turf piled around them. A woman stood beyond the blackened enclosure holding a sack she had intended to fill. Behind her, the hall’s cattle pressed against a wall for shelter. They had eaten the last dry hay before dawn.

Tylan dismounted and handed his reins to the nearest trooper.

“How much?”

“Most of the winter cut,” Wenna said. “They started it inside. The keeper’s men put out the top and thought they’d saved the rest.”

She had come down from the ridge before light. Water darkened her cloak from shoulders to hem; beneath it her boots were white with chalk. She carried no sword. A narrow knife rested high at her belt where she could reach it without opening the cloak.

“People?”

“One burned hand. No deaths here.”

“Here.”

“They took the relay horses at the next post. The keeper resisted.”

Tylan walked round the enclosure. The fire had been set where the roof kept the rain off the inner stacks, carefully, by somebody who knew peat. There was no reason for a traveller to enter that part of the yard. The raiders had come to burn it.

“How many horses?”

“Six. Two more in the hall stable. They took those too.”

“And the keeper?”

“His wife wants you to come before they move him.”

He nodded.

The hall owner came out with a folded cloth over his shoulder. His eyes were reddened by smoke and the effort of staying awake. He bowed, not deeply, in the manner of a man whose roof had received earls and soldiers long before this particular army arrived.

“Your men said it was safe.”

“What did they say?”

“That the pass was guarded.”

“It was.”

The keeper looked at the enclosure.

Tylan waited. There was nothing useful to say about a guard performing the duty assigned to it while the enemy chose a different road.

“Can you protect what is left?” the man asked.

“How much is left?”

The keeper took him into the barn. Eight carts of hay, perhaps nine if packed loosely. More could be brought down from two farms higher in the valley, but the farmers had their own animals and wanted payment in coin. The hall could not pay them until the travellers came, and travellers did not come when horses disappeared from the posts.

Tylan took a handful of hay and opened it against the light. It smelled sound. No mould in the lower layers. He put it back.

“I’ll leave twelve men here.”

“The pass?”

“Will have twelve fewer.”

The man considered that without showing relief. “They’ll say I drew them away.”

“Who will?”

“My neighbours at the upper hall.”

“They can speak to me.”

“Yes. You ride away afterwards.”

Tylan looked at him again. He had been in the hills long enough to know that an answer which settled an officer did not necessarily settle the person who would live with it.

“Send for them,” he said. “Before I go.”

They met in the hall’s long room, where fleeces hung from the beams and the smoke found its way slowly through a hole in the roof. The fire burned on a raised hearth of dark stones, each worn smooth along one edge. Cups had been set beside it, not on it. Tylan noticed where the men put their wet gloves and where they did not.

Wenna stood by the door. She knew three of the keepers well enough to ask after their families; she greeted the fourth more formally, and Tylan remembered which one.

No drawn steel under the roof. The rule had been explained to him on his first visit. He had left his sword with his horse. The men entering after him did the same without being reminded, except for one who carried a knife he evidently did not consider a weapon. Nobody challenged him.

The upper hall wanted its guard kept. The lower hall wanted more men. A herder whose grazing lay between them wanted everybody’s horses moved away from the water his sheep used.

Tylan listened until all three had repeated themselves.

“Which road did the raiders take?”

Nobody answered.

“Which road could they take with eight stolen horses?”

The herder rubbed his thumb along the seam of his cap. “Down by the old quarry.”

“Your sheep are there.”

“Not now.”

“Where are they?”

“On the far slope.”

“When did you move them?”

The man’s eyes shifted towards the fourth keeper. Wenna stepped away from the door.

“Yesterday,” she said. “Before the raid.”

Tylan looked at her. She gave a slight nod. She had seen the flock.

He could ask the herder what he had known, turn him into a witness against a neighbour, and perhaps learn one useful thing before ensuring that nobody in the room told him anything again. Instead he asked how much hay the quarry road could carry before the ground broke beneath the carts.

“Not carts,” the herder said. “Pack horses.”

“How many together?”

The man answered more readily.

They talked about fodder until the keepers began correcting one another. The upper hall had less than its owner claimed. The fourth keeper had a dry barn nobody else had mentioned, on a slope accessible from the drove-road. The herder could bring a flock past it without approaching the pass at all.

Tylan took out his paper.

He drew no map. A map would have given him the roads without the men who decided whether a stranger could use them. He wrote the names of the halls and, beside each, the person who could answer for its hay.

Twelve men here. Eight at the quarry road. Six moved from the pass to the dry barn. The watch at the pass remained, but it would no longer receive every soldier he could spare merely because a pass looked important on a survey.

“You are letting them go home,” the upper keeper said.

“If they want to.”

“They’ll take the horses with them.”

“Some. They’ll have to feed them.”

The man frowned. He had expected a threat, a pursuit, some act large enough to match the loss. Tylan understood the wish. It was what the earls expected too.

He finished the orders and asked Wenna which rider knew the quarry road in fog.

She gave him a name.

He went to the dry barn before visiting the relay house.

The road beneath it would admit two pack animals abreast if their loads were narrow. A cart would have to leave its wheels in the same ruts all the way up; there was nowhere to turn until it reached the yard. The barn stood on a stone footing, with the lower doors opening downhill. Whoever had built it had understood that a man ought not to have to lift hay twice.

Six troopers would sleep in the room above those doors. Tylan sent two round the back to look at the thatch, then took the corporal down to the lower road.

“From here you can see anyone coming from the hall. Where do men arrive if they don’t wish you to see them?”

The corporal pointed to the trees.

“And from there?”

“Through the ditch.”

“Walk it.”

The man returned with wet boots and a thorn caught in his sleeve. He had found a gap beneath the boundary wall, partly covered by brush. A person could crawl through it; a burning bundle could be pushed through without anyone entering at all.

“Close the gap. Keep one man outside after dark.”

“He’ll have no shelter.”

“Make him some.”

The owner had followed them down. He objected to taking timber from the lean-to for a soldier’s hut. His sons had built it that summer. Tylan asked where the sons were.

“With the earl.”

“Which?”

The man looked towards Wenna.

“Mine,” he said.

Tylan left it at that. There was a difference between requiring a man to obey and requiring him to name his sons before strangers. He arranged for an empty cart to be brought up from the lower hall. Its body could shelter the outside watch, and the owner’s lean-to remained intact.

The owner watched him write the order.

“My boys were called. They didn’t choose.”

“If they come home without weapons, tell the corporal.”

“So he can take them?”

“So he won’t shoot at them trying to cross his line.”

“What happens after?”

“They give their names. Their service is recorded. If there is an accusation against them, it is heard. If there isn’t, they work here.”

The man took time over that answer. Then he showed the corporal a place behind the barn where water could be drawn even when the upper trough froze.

The first attempt came before the cart arrived.

Tylan was at the next hall when a trooper brought word of riders on the slope above the barn. They had stopped where the old track divided. The sentry had counted nine, perhaps eleven; two had led spare animals. They had made no effort to conceal themselves.

The captain wanted to take a company after them.

“Where do they go if they see you coming?” Tylan asked.

“Back over the ridge.”

“And if you follow?”

The captain looked at Wenna. She shook her head.

“No ground for a formed troop beyond the first rise. They know the way between the cuts.”

Tylan sent the captain to secure the road below the barn. He himself rode only as far as the lower orchard, where he could watch the ridge through the bare upper branches. The men beside him grew restless. They could see enemy horse and had been ordered to stand where neither side could reach the other.

After a while one rider came halfway down. He shouted something about the queen’s courage. Nobody answered. He shouted again, gesturing with his sword towards the barn. Its new corporal stood by the lower doors with his crossbowmen, and did not move.

The man rode back uphill.

Wenna had gone round to the ditch. She returned with a bundle of tow, damp on the outside and dry within. Someone had pushed it under the brush beside the repaired wall. The riders on the ridge had kept the watch looking up while another man came along the lower ground.

“Gone?” Tylan asked.

“Before I reached him. He left that.”

They waited until the riders withdrew. It took most of the afternoon. The animals on the ridge had eaten nothing while they stood. The horses below the orchard had been allowed to graze, a few at a time, with their bridles slipped back over their necks. Tylan kept the captain there until the last enemy horse disappeared.

“We could have taken two,” the captain said.

“Perhaps.”

“We did nothing.”

Tylan looked towards the barn. A man was carrying hay out for the pack animals that had brought the watch’s food. The corporal had gone back to closing the gap more thoroughly.

“The hay is still there.”

“That won’t impress them.”

“They came for it.”

The captain looked at the tow in Wenna’s hand. At last he asked whether the outside sentry should have a second man. Tylan agreed, taking one from the indoor watch. Neither man was to remain outside all night. Cold men saw what they hoped was there, especially towards dawn.

When they left, the owner walked beside Tylan’s horse as far as the turning place.

“One of them was my youngest,” he said.

“On the ridge?”

“Yes.”

“Did he see you?”

“I don’t know.”

Tylan waited. The man rested one hand on the rough stone at the corner of his wall. He had more to say and could not bring himself to make a request of it.

“The corporal has his orders,” Tylan said. “If your son comes in, he is to be heard.”

The owner nodded. He went back towards the barn without waving to anyone on the ridge.

At the relay house, the keeper’s wife had washed her husband’s face. She had not covered the wound in his throat. He lay on the table where riders ordinarily ate, with one arm folded across his chest and the other straight beside him. The straight arm had stiffened before she could move it.

Their daughter stood against the wall. She was old enough to know what had happened and too young to have any task that would keep her from seeing it. Tylan asked whether there was someone she could stay with.

“She stays here,” the wife said.

“All right.”

“They asked him for the key. He gave it to them. Then he went after the bay because it’s ours, not one of the relays.”

Tylan looked through the open door. The stable stood empty except for two halters on pegs.

“Did you see who struck him?”

“Yes.”

“Would you know him again?”

“Yes.”

He did not ask her to describe the man in front of the child. Wenna remained when he went outside and took the account more quietly.

The riders who had been stationed there were waiting by the wall. Four men, two of them limping. They had been drawn out towards a signal fire on the ridge while the raiders came by the lower track. One kept repeating that the signal had been correct.

“I know,” Tylan said.

“We were told to answer it.”

“I know.”

He changed the signal arrangements. It took a few minutes to write and would take two days to carry to every post. Until then, some riders would still be answering a summons the enemy knew how to make.

That evening he put the replacement orders before Veralyn.

She read them in Anneth’s father’s hall, where the army had taken rooms beneath the western eaves. A lamp stood beside her hand. Shadowsteel hung from a peg near the door, above a pair of boots drying on their sides.

“Will they hold?” she asked.

“The halls will. The relay line will be slower.”

“How much?”

“Two days at least. More if they take the quarry post.”

She moved the lamp so the light fell across the second sheet.

“Food?”

“Eight days here. Twelve if Dunmere delivers what he promised.”

“And Hask?”

“Running out of places that will feed him without being paid.”

“Then let him run.”

She had once enjoyed making that sort of remark. He could usually hear it in her voice, the small pleasure of finding the sharp answer first. This time she was looking at the keeper’s name and seemed not to have noticed what she had said.

Tylan told her about the wife and child.

She asked how much the bay was worth. He gave the ordinary price of a sound relay horse. She wrote the amount beside the widow’s name, then added the cost of the lost fodder and the wages the house would no longer earn while it stood empty.

“Find the man,” she said.

“Wenna has his description.”

“Find him.”

He took the orders and left her with the lamp.

The forage party at Scarth Fold was late returning on the fourth day.

Rain had closed the upper track, and Tylan had allowed them to use the drove-road with an escort of twelve. The sheepfold stood beside it, four walls and an open gate, useful shelter for men waiting for a cart to be righted. The same walls made a company difficult to see until a rider came almost upon it.

Wenna brought the news herself. Three of the escort had reached her post. The others were inside the fold, with Hask’s riders outside it and the loaded animals caught between.

Tylan had eighteen men close enough to go at once. He sent a rider for the next company and took the eighteen.

The track fell towards the fold through wet bracken. He could hear shouting before he saw the walls. One of the pack horses stood loose beside the stream, its load sagging beneath its belly. It had dragged a broken rope far enough to tangle it around a root.

He saw the raiders’ mounts above the fold, tethered in a line beneath a low bank. Their riders were on foot at the gate. They expected relief along the drove-road and had put archers where the road narrowed.

Tylan left it.

The slope beneath the bank drained into the stream. It would be wet but firm where the stones showed. He sent six men along it to the tethered horses, with orders to cut the lines and drive the animals towards the road. The rest followed him down the higher edge.

He had drawn his sword before the first raider turned.

A man shouted his name. Tylan did not know which man. He saw a spear coming level and knocked its point aside with his shield, stepping inside its reach before its owner could pull it back. The man struck at him with the shaft. Tylan hit him under the jaw and went past him.

The next had a short sword and room to use it. Tylan let him begin his cut, moved left, and drove his own blade through the opening beneath the man’s lifted arm. He had to pull hard to free it. Rain ran down the grip beneath his fingers.

Someone fell against his knee. He shoved the body away and looked for the gate.

The men there had stopped trying to enter the fold. Two were climbing the wall. Another ran towards the horses and found them coming loose, kicking and turning under the shouts of the six troopers. He dropped his weapon before anyone reached him.

Tylan ordered the gate opened.

One of his own men inside did not recognise him at first. Blood and rain had made his face difficult to see. The man raised a spear, then lowered it so quickly he struck the stone beside the hinge.

“Out by companies,” Tylan said. “Wounded first. Keep the loads that can travel. Leave the rest.”

There were no companies. There were eleven frightened men and two drivers with bleeding heads. He gave the order again in smaller pieces until somebody began doing what he needed.

A raider who had reached the wall turned to fight. Tylan saw him hesitate, looking at the distance between them. The man chose the far side and dropped out of sight.

“After him?” a trooper asked.

“No. The horses.”

They brought out the animals, the wounded, and most of the forage. One sack had split and its contents were floating in the fold’s mud. Two dead men had to be laid across a spare pack saddle because nobody had brought a cart they could use.

Wenna arrived with the second company after the fighting was over. Tylan showed her where the archers had waited.

“I should have sent a rider ahead.”

“They’d have let him pass.”

“Two, then.”

“They knew what you wanted from this road.”

He looked at the spilled forage. She was right. He had protected the route often enough for the raiders to know its value and not often enough to make attacking it difficult.

A trooper behind him was describing the fight to someone who had arrived late. Tylan heard his own name and the word three.

“Two,” he said.

The man stopped.

“Two at the gate. Find out who the others are before you tell their families.”

They waited below the pass that night.

The hill sergeant had counted seven crows crossing it and refused to take the wounded through before morning.

“Seven,” he repeated. “The pass is bad.”

Tylan looked up and saw only a few black birds turning above the ridge, too distant to count reliably in the failing light.

“The road is open,” one of the southern captains said.

The sergeant said nothing.

Tylan asked whether there was water where they stood. There was. Shelter enough for the injured? A stone hut and the lee of the bank. Fodder? What they had just recovered.

“We camp here.”

The captain waited until the sergeant had gone.

“For birds?”

“For men who’ll watch the sky instead of their footing if I send them over now.”

They crossed in the morning. At the top, Wenna pointed to fresh horse-droppings beside the path. The riders had waited there during the night and gone before light.

Tylan wrote down where she found them.

At the queen’s camp, the keeper’s widow identified one of the prisoners taken at the fold. Veralyn heard her in the presence of two officers and allowed the man to answer. He denied striking the keeper, then admitted striking him but said he had not known the wound was mortal.

“You left him bleeding on his own doorstep,” she said.

“He was alive when we went.”

She gave the judgment without looking down. Tylan had seen her pause, in other hearings, over a man’s torn cuffs or the movement of his hands. This man had begun rubbing his thumb against a cut on the opposite palm. She did not seem to see it.

The judgment was lawful. Tylan signed as witness when the clerk asked him.

That night he moved the pickets back from the passage outside Anneth’s door. A guard walking there could see the queen’s room, but so could the man at the stair, and the second post served no purpose that justified the noise of boots beneath the lintel. He gave that reason when asked. It was true.

His sheet for the ten days lay open beside his supper.

Quarry hall: held. Scarth Fold: cleared. Upper relay: lost. South ridge relay: empty. Lower post: four men, no mounts. Food here: six days. Fit horses: fewer by thirty-one.

He added the time a letter would now take from Valora: twelve days, if the remaining posts held.

Then he went to look at the horses that had come back from the fold. One stood with its head over the stall door, waiting for company. Tylan let it nose his hand while he checked the swelling along its foreleg.

Part II: The Port Burns

Chapter Eight: The Limits of the Seals

The chancery bench had been polished by the sleeves of men who leaned across it to make an argument. Its middle shone almost yellow in the morning light; the ends remained dark. Jaennis sat behind the brighter part in a chair brought from her own rooms, with the delegated seals on a cushion at her right hand. Nobody had objected to the chair. Two men had objected to the cushion.

She let Pellam finish reading Corbel’s steward’s petition.

The steward wore mourning at his cuff, narrowly enough that it did not interfere with his work. His mistress’s father had died the previous month. Jaennis knew this because Ysabet Morne had sent flowers to the house and complained that the messenger had been made to wait outside until somebody could decide where the flowers belonged.

“My lord does not dispute the queen’s right to regulate the king’s roads,” the steward said.

“The queen’s roads,” Jaennis corrected.

He bowed. “The royal roads, Majesty.”

A careful man. She would have preferred a careless one this morning.

“The order does not name a road,” he continued. “It names a landing belonging to my lord under grant confirmed to his grandfather, with its approaches and the right of maintenance. His lordship has maintained them at his own cost through two floods and the present emergency.”

“His people have also demanded payment from carriers whose passage was already settled.”

“For the guard, Majesty.”

“The guard stands at the passage.”

“It does. A guard elsewhere would be of less use.”

A man at the back of the room lowered his head to conceal a smile. Jaennis noticed him and made no sign. If she punished everyone who enjoyed a competent answer, she would soon be hearing only poor ones.

The disputed order had gone out under her seal ten days earlier, after the widow’s petition reminded her how many people were waiting for offices to agree what had happened to them. It directed that carriers named under Oria’s accord should not be charged a second time at the licensed landings. She had intended it to keep the traffic moving. Corbel’s steward had brought enough parchment to prevent anything moving for a week.

Pellam laid the old grant beside the new order. He had warned her about the distinction before she signed. He did not look at her now as if he expected gratitude for being right.

“The land is held from Fenwood,” she said.

“Yes, Majesty.”

“And Fenwood holds under the crown.”

“In the accustomed manner.”

“You have made considerable shelter out of those words.”

“We live beneath them.”

The reply was almost too good. Jaennis wondered whose language it had been before the steward learned it. She had begun recognising a particular kind of precision in the barons’ petitions: each stopped just short of a defiance which could be punished without first having to be interpreted.

Pellam turned a page. The delegation from Veralyn permitted Jaennis to issue directions for the household, supply, and the execution of existing royal judgments. It did not permit her to decide a disputed grant in the reigning queen’s absence.

She could persist. There were people in the room who would support her because they had to, and others who would applaud privately while hoping she suffered the consequences. The steward would appeal. In the meantime the landing would remain an excellent place to stop a grain wagon.

She took the new order from beneath his hand.

“This will be amended. The established passage remains under the accord. The disputed charge goes before the proper hearing. Until then, no carrier is to be detained for challenging it.”

“His lordship will wish—”

“He may collect what he claims and answer for it afterwards. He may not keep the man as well as the man’s money. You understand the distinction.”

The steward bowed again. This time the movement was deeper.

“I do, Majesty.”

“So do the people who will witness it.”

She named two local officers. Neither belonged to Corbel’s household. Pellam wrote their names into the amended order.

When the petitioners had gone, Jaennis lifted the seals from the cushion. They were heavier than they looked. Veralyn had handed them to her with an ease which everyone in the room had mistaken for a lack of understanding of their weight.

“Was that graceful enough?” she asked.

Pellam put the old grant away. “For whom?”

“For the people who will tell me how gracefully I accepted the correction.”

“Yes.”

“How disappointing.”

He did not smile until she did.

In her dressing room the fire had been lit too high. The air smelled of warmed wood and the rosemary Joan put among the linen. Jaennis opened the little casement before sitting, and a strip of cold air moved across the floor to her ankles.

Joan began taking the pins from her hair. Margery occupied the chair beside the window with a letter in her lap. Ysabet stood at the table examining cloth sent for approval. A newcomer sat on the low stool near the hearth, trying to look as if she were not waiting to discover whether she had been invited to stay.

“Catrin Vey,” Joan said, meeting Jaennis’s eyes in the mirror. “Mistress Morne brought her.”

Catrin rose too quickly.

“Sit,” Jaennis said. “You’ll make Joan think I ought to stand too.”

The young woman sat. She was an adult, though she had the narrow, watchful face of someone whose confidence was often mistaken for youth. Her gown had been altered at the sides; the work was neat, and the fabric good enough to deserve the trouble.

“What has Ysabet promised you?” Jaennis asked.

“An introduction, Majesty.”

“Only that?”

“She is a careful woman.”

Ysabet looked up from the cloth. Catrin’s expression had not changed, but she was pressing one foot hard against the other beneath the stool.

Jaennis laughed. “Sometimes. What brings you to Valora?”

“My aunt sent me from Fairport. I keep her correspondence while her hand is bad. She thought I should meet the people whose invitations I answer.”

“Is the hand likely to improve?”

“When she discovers how much I’ve answered without consulting her.”

Margery put her letter down. That was a better sign of welcome than any compliment Jaennis could give.

The women began talking. News arrived without being announced as news: an officer had been sent home because his fever would not break; a sister had been asked to release rooms she had occupied for six years; Corbel’s wife was furious about a load of dye caught at her own husband’s landing.

Jaennis closed her eyes while Joan freed a pin that had caught.

“Her own dye?”

“For the household weaving. She had agreed the price before the roads became difficult. The merchant wants more now.”

“Because of the charge?”

“Because he knows she needs it before the cloth is finished.”

“Who told you?”

“Her sister.” Margery lifted the letter. “She writes most vigorously when inconvenienced.”

Jaennis held out a hand. Margery passed it to her without the envelope.

She read the relevant lines. The dispute was smaller than the steward’s: a household wanting its purchased goods, a merchant exploiting a delay, a wife who would have to explain why the winter cloth was not ready. It was also much nearer the person who could order the barrier opened.

“Does she want the merchant paid more?”

“She wants somebody to be wrong.”

“That is less costly.”

Joan put the freed pin in the dish. “Not always.”

Jaennis read the letter again. Then she asked Margery whether her sister would carry an answer which did not bear the royal seal.

The answer went that afternoon. It contained no threat. It offered two things Corbel’s wife could use: a reliable replacement supplier, if the present merchant persisted; and confirmation that the crown’s officers would not interrupt the household’s winter deliveries while the toll dispute was heard. In return, Jaennis asked that the same practical courtesy extend to the grain carriers waiting beside the dye.

She did not tell Corbel’s wife how to persuade her husband. The woman had lived with him for twenty years. If she could not find a way, a letter from Valora would not teach her.

Five days later Margery brought news that the loads had passed.

No amended grant had been required. Corbel’s steward would have found it difficult to explain the improvement at a public hearing, since his lord had conceded nothing in principle. Jaennis wrote to thank his wife and enclosed a sample of the cloth Ysabet had selected.

Joan watched her fold the letter.

“You enjoyed that.”

“I enjoy things arriving where they ought to.”

“Of course.”

Jaennis looked up. Joan watched her in the mirror until she became aware that she had enjoyed rather more than the arrival of grain.

Fayd came to report the return of the ferrymen after the morning audience.

Jaennis had arranged to receive him standing near the window, where they could speak without obliging the whole room to fall silent. He chose a position that left neither of them looking directly into the light. She noticed the small adjustment and made him wait while she finished speaking to an officer about bedding.

“The men are home,” he said when she turned to him. “All five.”

“The ferrymen you mentioned to Master Pellam?”

“Yes.”

“They had names when you asked for them. Use them in the report.”

“I have.”

He handed her the paper. She did not read it at once.

“Which road did they use?”

“The lower lane through Long Lea.”

“How fortunate. I understand it has recently become passable.”

“It had ceased to be obstructed.”

“Those are different things?”

“In a flooded valley, Majesty, regrettably often.”

She laughed. A gentleman near the door smiled uncertainly, recognising laughter without having heard the reason. Fayd did not turn towards him.

“Lady Corbel is pleased with the dye,” Jaennis said.

“I am glad.”

“Were you concerned?”

“I take an interest in anything which makes a household easier to live in. Men answer differently after a peaceful supper.”

“Then perhaps your first request should have been to her.”

“It appears yours was.”

She opened the paper. The men’s names were there, with the places to which they had returned. Two had been released too ill to travel without help; transport had been found for them. He had not concealed the difficulty in the larger success.

“This is useful,” she said.

“That was my intention.”

“And what do you want to do next?”

“Finish the arrangement with Maresse. His captain will meet a crown officer, but wants assurance that attending will not be taken as submission.”

“Has anyone said it would?”

“No. That is why assurance might be easy to give.”

She looked at him. He had made the small request sound so reasonable that refusing it would require her to invent an objection larger than the act.

“Put the proposed terms before Pellam. He will tell you what the seals can promise.”

“And what the household can?”

She let the silence remain long enough for him to hear it.

“The household,” she said, “will answer when it is asked.”

He bowed, accepting the limit without pretending not to have tested it.

After he left, she told Joan that one of the women should always be within hearing of his anteroom, openly sewing, waiting for a message, or exchanging the ordinary news which travelled through corridors. She wanted to know who stopped waiting at other doors.

“Not Catrin,” she added.

Joan looked at her.

“She has enough to learn without learning it from him first.”

The next day Fayd had his door moved fully open while he received callers. His clerk placed a second stool beside it. The woman Joan had sent was offered a cushion.

Jaennis found the report more irritating than she intended to admit.

Pellam’s notes about the released ferrymen arrived in a plain wrapper. Jaennis had asked for the examinations as well as Fayd’s report. Two men had needed help to get home. She wanted to know whether anybody had paid the people who carried them.

One account named the carrier; the other said only that a cart had been found. She sent it back with a request for the missing name and opened the letter which had come from the north.

Veralyn had written about a song.

The first page concerned the earls and the condition of the horses. The second began in the middle of a different thought, as if she had put one letter down and returned intending to write another. A woman in a northern household had supplied a verse she had never heard, and had informed her that the version sung at court was wrong in two lines and indecent in the wrong place.

Jaennis knew the court version. She had taught it to Veralyn herself.

She read the description again: a room with peat smoke at the door, a bowl of wine nobody should have tried to improve with honey, a singer who would not repeat the verse until Veralyn had listened to the words she was already singing incorrectly.

She could hear how Veralyn must have laughed. She could see the attention that followed, the change in the set of her shoulders when she found someone willing to surprise her.

For a moment Jaennis wished the letter had not arrived.

Then she was ashamed of the wish. Veralyn was alive, engaged with something besides a campaign that would not finish, writing to her because there were things she wanted her to know. Jaennis had asked for that. She could hardly complain that the answer included another woman’s voice.

She rang for the musician who kept the household’s songs. He came carrying a sheaf of pages tied with an old green ribbon.

“Do you know a northern version of this?”

He read the title and nodded.

“Which one, Majesty?”

“How many are there?”

“Enough to quarrel over.”

“Find me the one whose second verse concerns a woman waiting by a gate. Not the one with the priest.”

He thought for a moment. “I know who will know.”

“Then ask her.”

The pronoun had been a guess. His smile confirmed it.

A letter from her father lay beneath Veralyn’s. It smelled faintly of smoke and river damp when she unfolded it. He asked whether she was eating properly, told her the Fairport harbour captain had acquired a dog which obeyed every man on the quay except its owner, and enclosed a small drawing of the dog ignoring an order.

Conlin could draw remarkably well when he did not intend anyone to preserve the result.

Jaennis put it beside her mirror.

In her reply she told him she was eating, that the linen was finally being replaced, and that she had found a use for the lesson he had once given her about asking the person who needed a thing to arrive. She did not explain the whole dispute. He would understand enough, and ask for the part she had omitted if he thought it mattered.

Before sealing the letter she added that she missed him.

Then she went downstairs to the next audience.

Chapter Nine: Across the River

Matteo sent the children first and called it a visit.

There were four of them, with two nurses, a tutor, three chests, and a little dog that would not be persuaded to leave the boat. Constano watched a sailor pick up the dog and carry it ashore while its owner shouted instructions no one could possibly follow.

“The long warehouse,” he told the factor. “Upstairs. Have the rooms screened.”

“They aren’t rooms.”

“Then make them rooms.”

Matteo came on the next crossing.

“You’ve had news,” Constano said.

“I’ve had less news than I ought.”

He took Constano to the end of the quay, away from the children.

The northern road into Marcott had been closed by men claiming to protect a movement of troops. The movement had not passed. The men had remained. Two houses in the middle cantons had withdrawn guarantees on cargo already on the road, and a third had offered to renew them at a price that made clear they knew the first two had withdrawn.

“Adria?”

“His friends. There are more of them than there were.”

“Can you hold the road?”

“For a little while.”

“Can you get your people out?”

Matteo looked back at the boat.

“Some.”

Before the meeting, Constano crossed to Marcott.

Piero Tireno brought the boat himself. He had his mother’s long face and his uncle Matteo’s habit of waiting until a passenger was settled before announcing that the landing might be difficult. The tide was running against the river, and water struck the hull from two directions while they passed between the larger vessels.

“Your mother?” Constano asked.

“At the house. She told me not to let you walk from the quay.”

“Did you intend to?”

“No. She knows I didn’t. She wanted the pleasure of telling me.”

There was affection in the complaint. Constano watched him guide the boat between two lighters whose crews were arguing about which had the right to move first. Piero called a greeting to both and passed through the space they left while answering him.

At Marcott, the customs house cast a long shadow across the steps. The stones smelled of river weed and old fish oil. Above them, barrels waited beneath awnings striped by years of patched cloth. Constano had known this quay before Piero was born. He recognised particular warehouse doors by the colour used each time the owners could afford to paint them, and a baker’s shop by the place its customers sheltered from rain.

The shop was closed.

“Where’s the baker?” he asked.

“Gone inland to his daughter.”

“For a visit?”

Piero looked at him and said nothing.

Lucrezia received him in the room overlooking the water. It had been furnished by three generations of women who had disagreed about almost everything except the usefulness of a large table. Chairs of different ages stood around it. A red cloth covered the middle, leaving the edges bare where hands and dishes had worn the wood smooth.

She kissed him on both cheeks and told him he looked tired.

“So do you,” he said.

“I had hoped for manners.”

“You sent your son to ensure I arrived incapable of escaping them.”

She laughed and sat. Matteo stood by the window, holding a cup he had forgotten to drink from.

The two siblings were less alike than people claimed. Matteo could make a difficulty seem negotiable until the last possible moment. Lucrezia preferred to identify what could not be changed and finish arguing about the rest. Their father had left the principal affairs of the house to her for that reason. Matteo had spent years pretending to consider it an imposition on his sister rather than a relief to himself.

“The children are comfortable?” she asked.
