“They’ve discovered the echo in the warehouse stair.”

“Then they’re comfortable.”

“Dena may not agree.”

“She should stop them.”

“She does. They wait until she goes out.”

Piero smiled as he poured the wine. Lucrezia did not. She wanted the children safe enough to misbehave; having obtained that, she seemed uncertain what to do with her own relief.

They ate fish baked with onions and herbs, followed by small cakes whose spice reached Constano before he tasted them. Lucrezia still bought the same mixture she had served years earlier at a supper celebrating a boat nobody had expected to survive its first voyage. The boat was gone now, broken up after a long and profitable life. Most of the people at that supper remained names he could ask after. Today he did not ask, afraid of making Lucrezia begin another list of departures.

“The northern road is occupied,” she said when Piero had taken the dishes away. “Not closed by a patrol. Occupied.”

“Matteo told me.”

“He told you what he hoped it meant. I’m telling you what it does.”

She brought out three letters. One refused passage to a load whose owner had failed to obtain a guarantee from a middle-canton house. Another offered such a guarantee at a price which made the goods scarcely worth moving. The third concerned warehouse space already paid for several weeks in advance.

Constano remembered the earlier purchases. Men speculating on a rise in grain prices, he had thought. War always produced them. Their willingness to pay before taking possession had seemed evidence of confidence rather than preparation.

Now Lucrezia showed him that the stores stood along the same northern approach.

“They have places to put supplies before their companies arrive,” he said.

“Yes.”

“How many companies?”

“I don’t know. More than a dispute over duties requires.”

He looked towards the window. Fairport shone across the water in the afternoon light, its white walls clearer from here than from its own quays. He had chosen the bank from which he understood the danger least well and mistaken familiarity for a better view.

“If they come,” Lucrezia said, “will you receive our people?”

“Yes.”

“Not just these children. The lightermen’s households. The people whose names have stood beside ours when it was useful to have Tireno behind them.”

“How many?”

“More than you want me to say.”

“Say it.”

She did. He did not pretend the warehouse would hold them all.

“We’ll need another place ashore,” he said. “Somewhere boats can unload without blocking the grain quay.”

Piero came back in before Lucrezia answered. He had been listening from the passage and did not trouble to deny it.

“The inland cut,” he said. “The old timber quay.”

“It’s shallow.”

“For your barges. Not for my boats.”

Constano asked about the land behind it. There were yards, covered sheds, a road leading towards the north gate. Some belonged to men who would charge generously for finding themselves useful at short notice.

“Let them,” Lucrezia said. “Write down what they charge.”

“They may not take your promise here if Marcott is threatened.”

“Then they will take yours.”

He knew she was right. It was why they were in the room together.

He set a limit; other promises were already running on the same money. Lucrezia argued for more. Matteo finally put down his cup and proposed splitting the undertaking among three houses whose ships still used Fairport.

“They won’t like it,” Lucrezia said.

“They don’t have to like it. They have to prefer it to losing us.”

Constano looked at him. Matteo shrugged.

“I’m useful sometimes.”

They had the agreement ready before the tide changed. Piero took a copy for the boatmasters. Lucrezia kept one in the drawer beside her father’s seal. Constano put the third into the case Aldith had sent with him, between instructions for grain and a letter to his daughter which he had not yet finished.

At the door Lucrezia caught his sleeve.

“When the children ask when they’re coming home, don’t give them a day.”

“I won’t.”

“I keep nearly doing it.”

He covered her hand briefly with his own. Then Piero helped him down to the waiting chair, and they went back to the water.

The inland quay smelled of old bark and standing water.

Master Odo Fenn met them there with two men who had been clearing timber from the sheds. He was quay warden by appointment and carpenter by habit, and could not show Constano a building without pointing out the part someone had repaired badly.

“People can sleep here,” he said. “Not against that wall. The damp comes through below the sill.”

“How many?”

“Sleeping or living?”

“Arriving.”

Fenn looked at him. “Then as many as the floor will take. Living is where the trouble starts.”

They walked the quay slowly. Constano counted the changes in level, the places where a tired person carrying a child might not see the edge. He asked for ropes at those places and a lamp at each landing stair. Fenn wanted the lamps protected against men knocking them into dry timber. Captain Serrit, arriving from the harbour gate, said they would need a watch strong enough to keep the crowd moving away from the water.

“Without making them think they’re being driven back to it,” Constano said.

She nodded. “I’ll put people there who can speak before they shout.”

Behind them, Aldith was discussing the temporary kitchens with Dena. His secretary had served him for eleven years and had stopped asking leave to identify a difficulty which he would eventually have to answer. She wanted the food prepared away from the sleeping sheds. Dena wanted the cooks close enough to the well that carrying water did not consume half their day.

They found a place which satisfied neither entirely and both sufficiently.

Renn arrived with the two younger clerks, Joff and Tavin, carrying boards for the household names. Constano sent them to ask Fenn how the arrivals should be separated so that people from the same boat did not lose each other in the sheds. He had expected to be concerned principally with capacity. The more they prepared, the more the problem became finding a particular person again after they had stepped ashore.

Near sunset Piero brought a boat through the cut to test the depth. It grounded lightly on the outer turn and was poled free. He marked the safer line with two stakes.

“It’ll take us,” he said.

“Loaded?” Serrit asked.

“Not with everything people will want to bring.”

“Then tell them before they reach you.”

Piero laughed without pleasure. “I have been telling them for a week.”

Constano watched the young man tie up. There was river mud on his sleeves and a rope burn across one wrist. He had known him as a child who would rather be in a boat than at any family table. It had been an indulgence then. Now everyone depended on the hours he had spent avoiding other lessons.

“Take supper here,” Constano said.

“My mother is waiting.”

“Then take something across.”

Dena found bread and cold fish. Piero accepted them, ate half standing by the boat, and said he would tell his mother that the rest was all there had been.

The first sails off the headland were reported the following morning.

Harbour Master Dunstan stood in the countinghouse doorway with his cap in his hands, discussing them as part of the ordinary traffic. Fishing boats, he thought, waiting for weather. Two larger vessels had remained beyond easy sight from the lower quay. A trader entering late said they flew colours he recognised.

Constano asked whether anyone had gone out to speak to them.

“The launch is escorting Tireno’s boats across the bar,” Dunstan said. “They don’t all know the channel.”

“Can the tower see them?”

“In clear weather.”

“And in this?”

Beyond the window the river had become a grey sheet, its farther bank blurred by drizzle.

“We have extra men at the tower,” Dunstan said.

Constano asked that they remain.

They did for two nights. On the third, the refugee quay filled with families whose boats had arrived together after being held on the Marcott side. The watch there was too small to manage the crowd. A child fell between a lighter and the landing stair and was pulled out alive only because a boatman saw the hand above the water.

Serrit sent to the tower for the extra men.

She reported it afterwards. Constano approved the change, having just heard the child’s mother describe the stair. The ordinary tower watch remained. The launch would go out again when the boats had been brought through.

Dunstan’s dog slept under his chair while they discussed it. In the letter to Jaennis, Constano drew the animal ignoring its master’s boot and left out the uncertainty about the sails. She had enough difficulties which he could not improve by sending them upriver half understood.

The letter went with the next safe boat.

They met in the harbour office with the master of the watch, three captains, and the officer responsible for the seaward gate. Constano had asked for that officer particularly. He did not like conversations in which a necessary person was absent because someone had assumed the business did not concern him.

The officer was a short woman with grey at her temples and a scar above her lip. She listened to the river plan, then put two fingers on the chart.

“If you take these launches, I lose my outer watch.”

“For one tide,” the harbour master said.

“For one tide is when ships come.”

“We have the tower.”

“The tower can see. The launches can ask.”

Constano turned to the captains.

“Which boat can replace one?”

None of them answered at once. There were reasons. One was being repaired, one had men ashore, one drew too much water, one was under contract to carry a cargo whose owner would claim damages if it missed the tide.

“How much?” Constano asked.

The captain named the amount.

“Put it against me.”

The harbour master began to object.

“Against me,” Constano repeated. “Not against the harbour. Keep one launch out.”

The watch officer nodded. They still had people to bring across a river that was already taking too long to cross. She looked back towards the waiting households.

At sunset the road north of Marcott showed dust above the trees.

From Fairport it looked very small. A pale smear against the green, no more alarming than cattle moving on a dry track. Men came out on the roofs to look at it. By dark, the lights on Marcott’s northern gate had gone out.

The first boat arrived with a wounded man lying between sacks of wool. The second had too many passengers and a broken sweep. The third had no one at the tiller; a pilot from the landing jumped aboard as it struck the piles and kept it from turning broadside.

Constano stood where the people came ashore and sent them to the warehouse.

“Names first,” the factor said.

“Inside first. Names there.”

A woman carrying a basket asked him whether this was Fairport. He said it was. She asked whether the Tirenos had a house here. He pointed her toward the warehouse, and she thanked him with a courtesy so complete it frightened him.

Matteo’s boat had not returned.

Constano sent another man to look for it, then stopped himself sending a second man after the first.

Across the river something began to burn. The smoke showed red beneath and black above, and the water took the colour of it in long broken strips.

Chapter Ten: The Wrong Horizon

By midnight the river reflected fires from both sides of Marcott’s northern gate.

From Fairport the flames looked smaller than the distances they had already crossed. One roof caught, then another. The customs house remained dark. Constano watched from the upper window while Aldith read the names brought over in the latest boat. She had taken off her shoes to dry them beside the hearth; her stockings were stained with river mud above the ankle.

“No Piero?” he asked.

“He took the boat back.”

“Lucrezia?”

“Still at the house when the messenger left.”

He turned from the window. The room smelled of hot wax, wet wool, and the fish nobody had finished eating. Joff and Tavin were laying out the documents which might be needed if the countinghouse had to move its work inland. Aldith had told them to begin with the undertakings no other office held in full. Everything else could be recovered from somebody who had received a copy, provided that person and the copy survived.

It was sensible work. Constano had asked for it himself. Now he found he disliked seeing the room prepared for absence.

“Not all of them,” he said when Joff reached for another volume. “You won’t lift the case.”

The young clerk put it back and looked relieved to have a limit.

A bell sounded from the harbour cut, calling for help for a boat at the inner turn. Constano went downstairs.

At the inland quay the lamps had been lit exactly where Fenn placed them. Their covered flames showed the ropes along the edge and the wet boards of the landing stair. Beyond their light, boats waited in black water. Voices carried from one to another, questions about space, shouted names, the furious sound of someone being told that a chest could not be brought ashore until the people in front of it had moved.

Renn stood at the head of the stair repeating the same instruction.

“Stay with your household. Take your people to the far shed. Names there. Not here.”

It worked while somebody kept saying it. When he stopped to help a woman whose shoe had caught between two boards, the next arrivals began asking where they should go and the stair filled again.

Constano took his place until the shoe was freed.

A boat came in with Lucrezia standing near the bow, one hand on the shoulder of a boy who was not one of her family. Her cloak had been torn at the hem. She looked up, saw Constano, and did not waste breath greeting him.

“More behind us.”

“How many?”

“Piero knows.”

“Where is he?”

“Going back.”

He helped her ashore. She held his arm more tightly than balance alone required, then released it before he could ask if she was hurt.

“The upper street is taken,” she said. “They came through the hired stores. Men were waiting inside.”

The advance payments. The warehouses. Constano looked past her towards the water and understood how cheaply he had interpreted the first warning.

“Matteo?”

“Helping at the customs quay.”

“His wife?”

“Already inland. We haven’t heard which road she took.”

Lucrezia looked round the quay, measuring the people and the available shelter without needing to be asked to do it. Then she went to the first boatload that had stopped moving and found the person whose absence kept the others waiting.

They worked through the night.

Crane brought his barge into the cut near dawn, empty except for the men he had picked up from a stranded lighter. His hat was gone. There was a white strip down one side of his face where water had washed the soot away.

“They’ll take everything tied to the western bank,” he told Constano. “Get the last loads off now.”

“We need boats left to bring people.”

“You’ll have mine until it won’t float.”

Constano looked at the patched hull. It had carried grain north three times since the first argument beneath the warehouse awning. A man could grow attached to a thing by finding it where it had promised to be.

“Don’t leave it too late.”

Crane spat into the water. “I would prefer to be told which moment you mean.”

By first light the crowd had thinned enough for Constano to see the far end of the quay. Smoke lay along the western shore, hiding the roofs he had watched burn. Here, a woman was making broth over a protected fire. The smell reached him and reminded him he had not eaten since the previous afternoon.

He intended to sit for a moment. Then another boat came to the stair, and a child would not let go of its side.

The first warning came from the launch Constano had paid to keep outside the harbour.

It came in without its mast.

At first the men on the quay thought the crew had lowered it to pass beneath a spar or some wreckage carried out on the ebb. The boat was running badly, turning toward the piles and away again, and nobody at the tiller seemed able to hold it straight. Then the men saw the arrows in the gunwale.

Constano was helping a child out of a riverboat when the shouting began. He had hold of the little boy beneath the arms. The boy would not release the side of the boat, and his mother was telling him that the gentleman would not drop him, would not drop him, while the boat rose and fell and made all three of them liars.

“Let go,” Constano said.

The boy let go.

Constano lifted him onto the quay and nearly went down with him. Pain ran from his hip to his knee so sharply that for a moment he could hear nothing but his own breathing. He set the child on his feet against the warehouse wall.

A man at the end of the quay was pointing seaward.

Constano turned.

The launch struck the piles with a crack of wood. A sailor jumped for the ladder, caught it with one hand, and hung there until two men pulled him up. His face was wet and dark. He tried to speak and spat water instead.

“Ships,” he said.

Nobody asked where. They were all looking now.

Beyond the harbour arm, in the grey water under the morning cloud, three sails had separated from the merchant traffic waiting for the tide. Small boats moved beneath them. More than there should have been. More than the eye wanted to count.

The harbour bell began.

Captain Serrit met Constano at the foot of the sea gate’s stair.

She had no helmet. Somebody had handed her one that did not fit and she was carrying it by the strap while giving orders to a boy who kept trying to repeat them before she had finished.

“Tell him the outer chain. Not the river boom. Say it back.”

“The outer chain.”

“Now go.”

The boy ran.

“The tower saw them,” she told Constano. “They were under trading colours. They changed when the launch went out to ask.”

“How long?”

“Not long enough.”

She pushed past him toward the stair, stopped, and came back.

“The boats at the inland quay. Get people into them while you can.”

“They’re coming across full.”

“Then send them out full.”

“To where?”

She looked at him. For a moment the practical decision disappeared from her face and he saw the size of what she had been given to do.

“Anywhere that isn’t here.”

The chain did not rise.

From the sea gate Constano could see part of the outer arm and the tower at its end. Men were running along the wall towards it, stopping where a narrow stair joined the walk. Beyond them, small boats had reached the mole on the sheltered side. Their crews climbed where the stone had been built to let working men reach the water.

Dunstan appeared beside him, breathing hard.

“The tower’s cut off.”

“Can Serrit reach it?”

“She’s trying.”

The harbour master had brought a sword he carried badly and a cap he seemed unable to put down. His dog followed at his heels, frightened by the bell and looking for a place where the sound would be less loud.

Constano turned towards the inland cut.

“Hold the quay gates. Nobody leaves by the water without an officer directing it. We’ll lose people between the boats.”

Dunstan nodded and ran to send the order.

It was the sort of instruction Constano had given a dozen times during the night: prevent a crowd from reaching a dangerous place before the means to receive it existed. He had seen a child nearly drown because the stair was allowed to fill. Keeping people off the edge ought to help.

Then smoke rose between the inner store and the cut.

He could not tell at first which building burned. The wind pressed the smoke into the narrow lane, hiding the passage Fenn used to reach the refugee quay. Men came out of it coughing. One had wrapped his hands in cloth and was carrying a length of wood with flames running along its upper edge, trying to pull it away from the warehouse beside it.

A runner reached the gate.

“Master Fenn can’t get through. The timber yard’s caught. They’re asking to open the gates at the far end.”

Constano looked towards Dunstan, already too far away to call back.

“Open them.”

“The order was—”

“I gave it. I’m changing it. Open the gates and get the people out of the sheds.”

The runner hesitated long enough to make Constano seize his sleeve.

“Tell them Lord Constano says the first order is cancelled. Say that before anything else.”

The man ran.

Constano started after him and discovered that the pain from lifting the child had not passed. It had settled deeply into his hip, waiting for the moment he needed to move quickly. He took the shorter stair down from the gate and used the wall as well as his stick.

At the bottom, a woman was calling Piero’s name.

It was one of the older children from the Tireno warehouse, Livia. She had come to the quay looking for him because a younger child would not believe an account which did not include Piero bringing the next boat. Now she stood near the smoke with her hands held uselessly in front of her, unable to understand which direction had become dangerous.

Constano called her to him.

“He’ll come by the water if he can. You go to Lucrezia.”

“She said—”

“Go to her. Tell her where you were.”

Livia caught his sleeve. “You saw him?”

“Not since yesterday.”

“Then how do you know?”

He did not. He could see the harbour arm behind her, the boats crowding the inner water, the smoke obscuring the one quay Piero knew he would find his people at.

“I know your grandmother needs you where she can see you,” he said. “Come.”

He took her as far as the lane to the salt quay. Renn met them there and sent a warehouse man with the girl. Then the shouting changed beyond the chandlers’ stairs.

The first enemy boat struck below the chandlers’ stairs.

Constano did not see it arrive. He heard the shouting change. Until then people had been calling for things: a rope, a cart, a child, an officer. Now they were calling a warning, the same warning passed from mouth to mouth without the words surviving.

Men ran across the opening at the end of the street. Some carried weapons. One carried a door in front of him with another man holding the far end. Arrows struck it and remained there, quivering, so that the door seemed to grow branches as it moved.

Constano stepped back beneath the arch.

Renn, his factor, caught his elbow.

“This way.”

“The warehouse.”

“This way takes us there.”

They went through a cooper’s yard. A woman was rolling casks away from the fire against the outer wall. Nobody had yet told her to leave. She stopped them to ask which way the wind would carry it.

“Leave the casks,” Renn said.

“They’re paid for.”

“Leave them.”

Constano looked up. Smoke lay low above the roof, moving inland with the sea wind. There would be more behind it.

“Take the man with you,” he told her, pointing to a cooper standing beside a half-finished barrel. “He knows how to make others.”

She stared at him, then at the man, and took his arm.

They reached the long warehouse by the salt quay through its back court. The doors stood open. People were still arriving from Marcott, carrying the things they had been unwilling to leave an hour before. A man brought two chairs tied together. A woman carried a cage with no bird in it. The little dog from the children’s boat was barking upstairs.

Constano went to the loading floor.

“Listen to me.”

Nobody did.

He struck the end of his stick against the boards. The sound was sharp enough that the nearest people turned.

“Quiet.”

Renn shouted it after him. The word travelled farther than Constano’s voice had.

“There is an attack from the sea. We are leaving by the north road. Take food, water, and what you can carry. Leave the chests.”

A man asked who would guard them.

“Nobody.”

That was the answer that made them understand.

Matteo arrived as they were bringing the children down.

His left arm hung inside his coat. There was blood on the shoulder and none on his hand, which Constano hoped meant the wound had stopped. His face was grey beneath its river mud.

“The customs quay,” he said. “They took the customs quay.”

“Here or there?”

Matteo looked at him.

For a moment neither spoke.

Then Matteo said, “Both, apparently.”

He sat on the lowest stair. One of the children came down and tried to climb into his lap. He stopped her with his good hand, then caught her face and kissed the top of her head.

“Walk with your nurse,” he said. “Do exactly what she tells you.”

Constano crouched beside him. It hurt so much that he could not keep his face from showing it.

“Can you walk?”

“Yes.”

“Then we walk.”

“The boats?”

“Not enough. Not in time.”

Matteo leaned his head against the stair rail and closed his eyes.

“I brought them across.”

“Yes.”

“I told them—”

“I know.”

Upstairs something heavy struck the floor. A chest dragged, then fell. Somebody was trying to take it anyway.

Renn came down carrying the account case.

“Your books, my lord.”

“All of them?”

“What I could reach.”

Constano took it. The handle was familiar beneath his palm. He had used that case for nine years. He could have named the order of the books inside without opening it.

At the door a nurse was trying to carry the youngest child and a bundle. She could not manage both. The child had gone limp with exhaustion or fright and would not help her hold him.

Constano set the case beneath the stairs.

“Renn. Take the boy.”

“I can get someone for—”

“Take him now.”

Renn looked at the case, then bent and took the child.

Aldith came down the stairs as the last of the children reached the door.

She had a bundle tied in a sheet and another, smaller one under her arm. Joff followed carrying two water jars. Tavin had cut his forehead somewhere and wrapped it so badly that one eye was half covered.

“Leave the rest,” Constano told her.

“I have.”

“The case—”

“I saw.”

He took the bundle for bedding until she shifted its weight and he saw the square corners through the cloth.

“You can’t carry those all the way.”

“Then I’ll put them down when I can’t.”

She had worked for him long enough to know when a discussion would consume time neither possessed. She went past him before he could continue it.

Lucrezia stood in the loading doorway with Dena, dividing the people into groups that could keep track of one another. The nurses each took two children. The household servants followed the nurses. People with nobody of their own were attached to a group whose leader had room to remember another face.

A man kept going back for a bag. A woman refused to leave until somebody found her brother. Dena called the brother’s name, got no answer, and sent Joff to look behind the inner partitions.

He returned with an old porter who had fallen asleep sitting against a bale. The man could not understand why everyone was dressed for travel.

“Fire,” Dena said.

He looked up, smelled the smoke, and got to his feet without another question.

The boy who had slept beneath the stair appeared at the back door. Constano recognised the bundled blanket before the face. He had a basket of bread and was trying to decide whom to give it to.

Dena took the basket, broke a loaf, and put the larger piece back into his hand.

“Stay with me.”

He nodded.

Crane reached them from the cut. He had blood on his sleeve which he said was not his. The barge was burning at the stern. He had brought off the men he could reach and no longer had a boat to offer.

For a moment Constano had no answer. He had been keeping the barge in his mind as a last possibility even after telling Matteo there were not enough boats. Without it, the north road ceased to be a choice among bad choices and became the only direction left.

“Do you know the marsh roads?” he asked.

“Better from the water.”

“Better than I do.”

Crane took one of Joff’s jars. “Then we’ll discover whether that’s enough.”

They moved out together. At the end of the lane, Constano looked back and saw Dena closing the warehouse door. The repaired bolt worked smoothly. She drew it into place, stood for a moment with her hand resting against the wood, then turned and followed the boy.

The roof had not begun to burn. It looked absurdly possible that someone might return later and find everything where it had been left.

They did not reach the north gate by the first road they tried.

A cart had overturned across it. Behind the cart, people were pressing forward because they could hear fighting somewhere behind them and could not see the obstruction. A horse went down between the shafts. Men tried to lift it by the harness. The horse screamed once and began to kick.

Constano turned the party into a yard before the pressure reached them.

He counted the children. Four. He counted the nurses. Two. Matteo was beside him, breathing with his mouth open. Renn had the little boy against his shoulder.

The woman with the empty birdcage had followed them. So had others from the warehouse. Constano did not know how many.

“There is a passage through,” Renn said.

“Where?”

“Behind the dye vats. I bought rope here.”

“Go.”

They went single file between a wall and three vats full of dark water. The ground was slick. Constano put one hand against the wall and moved his stick a little way at a time. Behind him Matteo stumbled. A man caught him under the good arm and did not ask who he was.

The passage came out on a street parallel to the one they had left. Smoke ran along it at head height. A tile fell from somewhere above and broke beside Constano’s foot.

At the far end he could see the gate.

He could also see men coming through it toward them. They wore no colours he recognised.

Renn stopped.

The men stopped too.

For a moment the two groups looked at one another through the smoke. Then one of the men lifted his sword and pointed it toward a narrow stair against the wall.

“Up there. Get them up there.”

They were harbour men. Constano recognised a tarred jacket, a coil of rope, the long knife at a belt. One had tied a strip of blue cloth round his arm to distinguish himself from the men coming from the sea. In the smoke the blue looked black.

The stair led to the wall walk and a postern beyond the blocked gate.

Constano looked at the steps.

There were more than twenty.

“I’ll take him,” one of the harbour men said.

“No.”

“My lord—”

“No. Take the old woman.”

There was an old woman behind him. He had not seen her arrive. She was holding the birdcage, having taken it from the younger woman when they entered the passage.

Renn shifted the child onto one hip and gave Constano his free arm.

They climbed.

At the ninth step Constano had to stop. At the thirteenth he could not make his leg rise. Renn waited, holding the boy and his lord with the same fixed patience. Below them the harbour men had turned to face the street.

Constano reached down, took his trouser leg in his hand, and lifted the foot onto the next step.

He did it again.

Outside the walls there was a market garden.

They came down into rows of onions, crushing the green tops beneath their feet, and stood in the sunlight as though sunlight belonged to another country. The smoke went over them. Behind the wall the bell still rang, more slowly now, with pauses between the strokes.

The gardener came out of his house carrying a hoe. He looked at the people among his onions and began to shout. Then he saw the children.

He put down the hoe.

“There’s a cart,” he said.

It was a small cart with one high side and one low one, built to take produce to the morning market. They put Matteo in it with the youngest child. Constano tried to keep walking until his leg gave beneath him and Renn caught him before he fell.

There was room beside Matteo if they turned the baskets sideways.

They had no horse. The gardener and two of the warehouse men took the shafts.

Constano looked back once.

From outside the north wall he could not see the harbour or the long warehouse or the rope-maker’s house. He could see smoke rising from all three places. He could not tell which smoke belonged to which.

The case was beneath the stairs.

He knew exactly what was in it.

Then the cart moved, and he put his hand against the little boy’s head to keep it from striking the side.

They stopped beyond the last gardens, where the road rose onto a salt-marsh dyke.

The cart could not take everyone. They changed the people pushing it, gave the water jars to those with free hands, and let the children sit in turn beside Matteo. Lucrezia walked with one hand on the high side. She had not asked about Piero again. Each time a boat appeared on the narrow water below, she turned towards it before anyone else noticed.

From the dyke Constano could see farther into the harbour than he had from the garden. Small craft moved between the burning quays and the larger ships. Men were loading them. He could not distinguish what they carried, but he recognised the rhythm of cargo being passed hand to hand.

It was work he had watched all his life.

Renn offered him water. He drank, then gave the jar to the youngest child, holding it while the boy tried to take too much at once.

Aldith sat on the bank with the sheet-wrapped bundle between her feet. He asked what she had saved.

“Three books. The current undertakings. Your seal. The papers from Marcott.”

“Not the earlier accounts?”

“No.”

“The copies from the northern stores?”

“No.”

He nodded. There was no reason to make her list what remained beneath the stairs.

Tavin began unwinding the cloth from his forehead. Joff helped him. The cut was shallow, though the blood had made it look worse. Dena tore a cleaner strip from the hem of her shift and told them how to bind it so he could see.

Constano counted the clerks. Aldith, Renn, Joff, Tavin. Four. He counted the children again. Four. Two nurses. Matteo. Lucrezia. Crane. Other people whose names he had not yet asked, walking because they had followed somebody who seemed to know a way out.

He had no orders from the north and no place prepared to receive them.

Crane pointed along the dyke.

“There’s a ferry beyond the reed beds. They may still be taking people.”

“May?” Lucrezia asked.

“If they are, we want to arrive before they stop.”

They began moving again. Constano sat upright in the cart so the children could lean against him without putting weight on Matteo’s wounded arm. The wheel struck a stone. He caught the little boy’s head once more, less quickly this time, and kept his hand there until the road grew smoother.

Behind them the harbour bell ceased.

Chapter Eleven: The Barge That Did Not Come

Seniro waited until midday before he allowed the granary doors to be opened.

The men had been told to expect grain from Fairport. The boat was late. Boats were late for many reasons, and he had refused to let a rumour become a ration order until someone brought him something better than fear.

At midday a pilot came in on a horse without a saddle.

The horse belonged to someone else. The pilot admitted this before saying anything about Fairport, as though he hoped dealing with the smaller offence first would make the rest easier to hear.

Seniro had the animal taken to water.

“Now.”

The pilot had been upriver when the first refugees caught him. He had turned back far enough to see the smoke and no farther. The sea gate had fallen. Men were landing at the lower quays. Marcott had been taken from the north. The houses still holding its southern streets were trying to get people across.

“Constano?”

“I don’t know.”

“The long warehouse?”

“I don’t know.”

“Whose ships?”

“I saw sails. I couldn’t read them.”

Seniro asked him to sit down. Then he went outside because he did not want the man to watch what he did with his face.

Avren found him beside the granary wall.

“We can send horse.”

“How many?”

“Thirty.”

“And when they get there?”

Avren said nothing.

Seniro pressed his thumb into the old scar across the base of his other hand. It had opened under the gauntlet that morning. He had not noticed until now.

“My brother is there,” he said.

“Yes.”

“And thirty horse cannot take a harbour.”

“No.”

“Then we do something they can do.”

They sent twelve riders south by the inland road with spare mounts and orders to find the people coming out. The rest were sent to the crossings ahead of the refugees, where a frightened village might close a gate that an army could not afford to stop and open.

Seniro wrote to the queen. He wrote what the pilot had seen and what he had only been told. He did not write that Constano was dead. He did not write that Constano was alive.

The sheet looked intolerably empty without an answer to that question.

He sealed it and sent two copies by different roads.

By evening the enemy had found out as well.

Their patrols pressed harder at the western approaches, and a village that had been sending milk stopped sending it. The headman came himself to explain. There were men in his yard. He did not know whose. They had told him which road his milk should take.

Seniro could have sent soldiers to the village. He had soldiers. Every one of them was standing somewhere for a reason.

He asked the headman whether the men had harmed anybody.

“Not yet.”

“Then tell them you came here and I would not give you an escort.”

The headman stared at him.

“Tell them exactly that. They may let you keep your cows.”

“And the milk?”

“Feed your children before they take it.”

When the man had gone, Avren said, “They’ll call that yielding.”

“Yes.”

“We could—”

“Bring the men from the eastern watch to take the village? Then who keeps the road from Fairport open?”

Avren looked south.

They opened the granary. Seniro stood by the doors while the measures were filled. He knew how many days the grain should last. He did not know how many people would be eating it by morning.

At dusk one of the twelve riders returned with a child in front of his saddle and an old man walking beside him. Behind them came others on foot. None had seen Constano.

Seniro went to meet them anyway.

By morning the refugees had filled the ground below the store.

They slept beneath carts, along the lee of the bank, in a disused cowshed whose owner wanted to know who would pay for the straw. The smell of people unable to wash reached Seniro before he came down from his tent. He had ordered another latrine cut farther from the water. Men who had spent the night digging it were now standing in the food line beside the people for whom they had dug.

Avren brought him the proposed ration.

Seniro read it and handed it back.

“The wounded first.”

“They’re already first.”

“Then the children.”

“They’re second.”

He looked up. Avren’s face was grey with fatigue and patient enough to make the marshal ashamed of having required him to explain his own work.

“Good,” Seniro said. “Use it.”

Braedyn arrived with a steward from the lower depots. The man had ridden ahead of a withdrawal already under way and wanted an escort which Seniro had no time to assemble from anywhere except the ground he was holding.

“Who authorised the movement?”

“My lord, the road behind us is closing.”

“That wasn’t the question.”

“I did.”

The steward’s voice shook, but he kept his eyes up. Seniro recognised the courage required to claim an order in front of a man who might find it easier to punish initiative than to admit that nobody had been available to give permission.

“How many wagons?”

He gave the count. More than Seniro could afford to lose. Food, spare bowstrings, bandages, tools for the bridges, the ordinary things which became irreplaceable when the place providing them burned.

“And the people?”

“Households from the depots. Whoever was there.”

Seniro spread the map on a barrel. The road from the depots divided below the mills at Ashford. One branch came through ground now watched by Valdais’s forward men. The other followed a raised lane past a drainage cut too narrow for two carts to pass. If the head of the convoy was stopped there, the whole movement would back into the enemy’s reach.

To hold the junction, he would have to strip the mill line.

“Could they take the eastern road?” Braedyn asked.

“Empty carts could.”

“Unload?”

“Into what?”

His brother looked at the people sleeping beneath the wagons and said nothing.

Hob Carrow came while they were considering it. He had a tear in his sleeve held together with black thread. Ralf Pennick followed carrying bread which he divided without asking, putting the larger piece into Carrow’s hand while listening to the steward describe the blocked road.

They shared a tent because their companies often worked the same ground. Men who disliked the explanation generally discovered that both companies could hear very well.

Carrow studied the lane.

“We can keep it open until dark.”

“With whose men?” Seniro asked.

“Mine at the junction. Pennick’s along the cut.”

“And the mills?”

The two captains looked at each other. Pennick answered.

“We bring out whoever will come. Leave a small watch to make it look held until the convoy’s past.”

Valdais would see through it. The question was how soon, and whether he would pay men to force an answer before the day had already been lost.

Seniro sent the captains to the junction.

He went to the mills himself.

Ista was taking the smaller wheel apart when he arrived.

She had come back after the first withdrawal, against his expectation and without asking whether he considered the return sensible. The miller’s household had taken shelter with relations north of the stream. Ista and her father had returned by a route the enemy did not yet use, bringing two men who knew the machinery. They had replaced enough floor to reach the gearing and resumed grinding in small quantities.

Now they had stopped again.

“Word came from the lower mill,” she said. “Men on the bank. Different colours from yours.”

“You need to leave.”

“So you said before.”

“This time they may burn it.”

She looked at the wheel rather than at him. Her father sat on an upturned bin with his coat across his knees. He had taken his boots off to dry them beside a little stove. The scene was so domestic that for a moment Seniro had to remind himself of the road outside.

“We can take these,” Ista said, touching the iron fittings. “Not the wheel.”

“Take them.”

“Will we be allowed back?”

“If we hold the ground.”

“And if you don’t?”

He had no answer different from the one he had given at the first bridge. She saw it before he spoke.

“Then help me lift this.”

They worked together. The fitting was heavier than it looked and came free suddenly, throwing both of them off balance. Seniro caught it against his chest. Ista steadied the lower end and laughed once, sharply, from the effort.

“You pull too hard.”

“I’ve been told.”

“By people carrying the other end?”

“Occasionally.”

Her father put his boots on while they wrapped the iron. He did not look at the marshal. That hurt more than an accusation would have, though Seniro knew the man had not arranged his grief for the convenience of making a lord feel it.

They loaded what they could onto a handcart. A soldier brought the boy with the hammer to help. His mother and the goats were already north of the line, the soldier said. The boy said nothing until Ista asked him to take the lighter end of the last bundle.

Seniro left six men at the mill, with orders to withdraw by the orchard path when Carrow’s signal came. They knew the risk. He did not disguise it as an honour.

One asked how long they should wait if no signal arrived.

“Until the shadow reaches that post. Then you go.”

He pointed to the place. The man nodded, relieved to have something he could see.

The convoy took longer than the steward promised.

It always did. A wagon broke a wheel, a driver attempted to turn because he had heard the northern lane was closed, two families lost each other and refused to move until someone went back to look. Pennick’s men cleared the wheel with timber taken from a field gate. Carrow sent the wandering driver to stand beside him while another man took the reins.

Seniro watched the wagons pass the junction. Grain. Tools. Bedding. A woman nursing a child beneath a cloth while the cart jolted over stones. The storehouse labourers brought their own children on top of the loads they had packed. Behind them came people whose only possessions fitted inside the garments they wore.

Smoke appeared over Ashford before the last wagons reached the cut.

Not much at first. A dark streak above the trees, then a second beside it. The wind brought the smell towards them. Seniro could distinguish burning grain from timber by now. He wished he could not.

Avren came to stand beside him.

“The watch?”

“No word yet.”

The last cart passed. Pennick’s rear section withdrew along the lane, pausing twice to face men who had begun testing the far end. Carrow counted them as they reached the junction. One was being supported by two others. Another had lost his helmet and looked much younger without it.

Four of the six from the mill came in after dusk.

The other two had been cut off before the signal reached them. Their corporal said they were alive when he last saw them, fighting beside the sluice. Seniro asked which two and had the names written down. The corporal kept describing where he had stood, as if the exact arrangement might prove that leaving had been necessary.

“I gave you the order,” Seniro said. “You obeyed it.”

“Yes, my lord.”

The man did not look absolved. Seniro had not expected him to.

They fed the company beside the granary. Ralf Pennick found Hob Carrow sitting on a sack with one boot off, staring at a blister opened across his heel. He knelt, took the boot, and examined the inside seam before calling for a knife to cut the offending stitch. Neither noticed Seniro watching. He left them to it.

The herald arrived under a white cloth the next afternoon.

His horse was well groomed. His coat had been brushed before he came, and the letter he carried showed no sign of the roads between them. Valdais apparently still had enough time and servants to make courtesy arrive in the condition intended.

Seniro received him outside the granary, where the smell of smoke remained in the sacks.

The letter addressed him by his proper command and named his family without error. A separate sheet listed wounded taken at Ashford. Both missing men were on it. One was marked as gravely hurt.

Braedyn stood beside him while he read the letter aloud.

To the Marshal of the Belworth, with the regard due to his office.

The men named on the enclosed sheet were received alive after the fighting at the mills. They have been given such care as our surgeons can provide. I propose their exchange with wounded of like number and standing held by your officers, the conditions for those owing ransom to be settled by our heralds before either party moves them. Men whose condition will not permit travel may remain under the same undertaking until it does.

The northern road from Kettling will soon be required for the movement of my army. I would regret obliging your men to defend each of its buildings separately when the outcome of such a division is already plain. A withdrawal made in good order need not be taken as want of courage.

I trust that Lord Constano has escaped the disturbances at the ports without injury. Please convey my respects when communication with him is restored.

Valdais.

Braedyn looked towards the herald. The man’s expression remained composed, offering no indication of what his master had expected the last paragraph to do.

Seniro read the names again. Spelled correctly. The regiment of each man, where known, set beside him. Somebody had asked questions while the prisoners could still answer them.

“Tell His Grace I accept an exchange in principle,” he said. “My surgeon will identify the men fit to move. The heralds may agree a place.”

“And the road, my lord?”

“Tell him I have read the letter.”

The herald bowed. Seniro ordered food for him and a fresh horse if his own required rest. Courtesy would not become cheaper merely because he disliked the use Valdais made of it.

His reply took three attempts. The first said too much about the mills. The second said too little about the wounded. The third accepted the exchange, named an officer to arrange it, and made no statement about Constano which he could not support.

He sealed it and gave it to the herald.

After the man had gone, a rider arrived from the north with reports from two crossings and a request for instructions about grain released under somebody else’s authority.

“Conlin’s report?” Seniro asked.

The rider shook his head.

He had asked the question in the ordinary way, expecting the answer which had accompanied the work for most of his adult life. For a moment he could not decide which of the papers in front of him should be opened first.

Braedyn pulled a chair beside the table and sat down.

“Start with the crossings,” he said.

Seniro did.

Chapter Twelve: The Price of Turning South

The first account said Fairport had fallen. The second said it was burning. The third, written by a man who had left before the first, assured Veralyn that the harbour remained secure.

She put them in the order of their dates.

The light through the tent wall was yellow. Outside, the last cartload of hay stood under a cloth darkened by rain; water dripped from the corners onto the wheels. A groom had turned a saddle upside down to dry. In the twelve days since the earliest letter had been written, she had taken two empty halls and sent six men south to be buried.

Tylan stood across the table, waiting. A strap had broken on his breastplate and someone had repaired it with red leather. It looked frivolous, like a ribbon tied to a workhorse.

“How far?” she asked.

“For which men?”

“All of them.”

“Eleven days if the roads hold. More for the train.”

“Eight?”

“We can send mounted men ahead.”

“I need the army.”

“Then we need to get it there.”

He laid down the sheet he had brought beside Seniro’s letter, where her attention remained. He had written my brother once and crossed it out for the name. She could see the words beneath the stroke.

“Has Constano been found?”

“No later news.”

“Does Jaennis know?”

“The roads to Valora are shorter.”

A man outside shouted for someone to stop moving the cook’s wood. The ordinary noise offended her. She wanted everybody to understand what had happened, and knew that if everybody stopped to understand it they would not eat.

“I could ride with the household.”

“You could.”

“And leave you to bring them.”

“Yes.”

She imagined arriving at Seniro’s headquarters with sixty swords and a horse too tired to carry her another mile. Men would have to feed her household. They would have to find her a room. Somewhere beyond the next ridge Valdais would still possess the men who had taken Fairport.

Tylan moved one of the letters away from a damp ring on the table.

“I would prefer to bring you something you could use.”

She took his sheet.

There were three hundred and eleven prisoners distributed among the halls. Many had surrendered on assurances that their cases would be heard before the queen left. Seventy were labourers who had followed their lords’ summons; their reeves had yet to come and identify them. Thirty-eight were ill. Beside the number of guards Tylan had drawn a line extending all the way to the bottom of the page.

“What is that?”

“Men who stay if we leave the prisoners.”

“And if we take them?”

“They eat on the road. We slow to the sickest. At every bridge we separate armed men from men who would like to be armed.”

“Bring the reeves today.”

“Some live two days away.”

“Then send. Hear the men whose reeves are here. No one is to confess to a rising to get out of a shed sooner.”

He nodded and marked the margin.

She had expected one large decision. Instead he gave her the names of men with swollen feet, a grain agreement that expired at the end of the week, and twenty-three horses that could not be ridden at all. Each required someone to stay behind or something to travel more slowly.

They went to the horse lines before dinner.

The ground had been churned into stiff ridges that held yesterday’s rain. A piebald with a split heel stood with his forefoot curled under him. The farrier was cutting a pad for him out of an old saddlecloth. Beside the next rope a man held up a grey’s lip while an assistant tipped medicine between its teeth. The grey rolled one white eye towards Veralyn.

“Will he walk?” she asked.

“Today.”

“South?”

The farrier stopped cutting.

“Majesty, I haven’t seen the road south.”

Tylan took her a little farther, where the man would not have to contradict her again. He pointed out the teams. Some had come from farms; the owners had sent their strongest animals because the summons specified fit horses. They had hauled guns and grain across the flint. Now the same farms wanted them for the late harvest.

“If we take the sound ones from every team,” he said, “we can get the heavy wagons down. The others stay with the loads we leave.”

“Who buys replacement teams?”

“The owners will ask us.”

“And the men whose horses die on the road?”

“The same.”

She watched a groom undo a knot with his teeth. His fingers were bandaged. Behind him the first rank of horses turned together towards a boy carrying an empty feed basket.

“Write down whose they are.”

“We have.”

“Then give each owner a copy. I don’t want them coming to Valora and being told the man who knew has died.”

That was a thing she could do immediately. The farrier resumed his cutting when they moved away.

The remount merchant was waiting outside Tylan’s tent when they returned from the horse lines.

He had brought the order for the next stage folded inside a wooden case. The wood had kept it dry; the corners of every other paper Veralyn had handled that morning were beginning to soften. He opened the case with a care that made her dislike him before he spoke.

“I have received an instruction from my partner, Majesty.”

“Where is he?”

“South of the ridge. With the animals.”

“How many?”

“Forty-seven sound. Six more under examination.”

“Then why aren’t they on the road?”

He looked at Tylan.

“Tell me,” Veralyn said.

“The payment was to be honoured at Fairport. My partner asks where it will be honoured now.”

“By the crown.”

“Yes, Majesty. At which place?”

It was the same answer she had demanded about food for the wounded: a place, a person, something that existed beyond the assurance. She looked at Tylan, who had begun finding the purchase agreement among his papers.

“The first stage was paid,” he said.

“And performed.”

“The animals were to come north before the second payment fell due.”

“They have come north. They are at the station named.”

“Where they are now remaining.”

“Yes, my lord.”

Tylan found the agreement and turned it so the queen could read the disputed sentence. The merchant had no right to hold the horses. Veralyn could have them taken under the warrant, and perhaps recover damages later for delaying the march. He knew this. He stood very still while she read.

“Does your partner believe Fairport’s loss makes my word worthless?”

“No, Majesty.”

“What does he believe?”

“That the people who supplied him must be paid before they part with another horse.”

“How many of these belong to him?”

“Eleven. The rest have been bought from farmers and small breeders.”

“And paid for?”

“In part.”

She set the agreement down. She could take forty-seven horses and leave the next forty-seven where they stood, owned by men who would conceal them before they answered another royal inquiry.

“What money have we?” she asked Tylan.

He told her. Some was reserved for the men’s pay, some for the grain at the next two halts. The grain was already in barns. That did not mean its owners would allow hungry soldiers to empty them without the agreed payment.

“Pay the eleven in full. Pay another third on the rest. The balance is to be honoured at Valora. I will give my own warrant.”

The merchant bowed. “I can carry that.”

“Will your partner release them?”

“I believe so.”

“Find out. By tonight.”

He took the papers and left. Tylan watched her draw the new warrant towards her.

“We’ll need to tell the captains why part of their pay is delayed.”

“Tell them their horses cannot wait for Fairport to be rebuilt.”

“They know that. They’ll want to know how long.”

She stopped writing.

Three days, she nearly said. It would sound reasonable and might prove true. It might also become the day a sergeant had promised his wife he would send money, or a captain who had paid his men himself discovered that he had no coin left for food.

“I don’t know.”

“I’ll tell them.”

“No. Bring them to me.”

They came before the afternoon meal. She explained the payment in the order in which she had made it, named the number of animals, and told the captains which portion of the men’s pay must wait. One asked whether the household would be paid on time.

“The same portion waits,” she said.

His face changed. He had expected to be rebuked for asking.

“Your own household, Majesty?”

“Yes.”

“And when we reach the southern army?”

“I will have an answer from Valora by then. If I haven’t, you will hear that from me as well.”

There was no cheer. She had asked them to wait for money already earned. The captains took the order away, and soon afterward she heard raised voices from the lines where they repeated it.

The merchant’s partner agreed before dark. The first horses would reach the camp on the next day. Tylan marked the number on his sheet and crossed out a longer estimate of the time they would have to remain.

Veralyn watched the mark dry. She had bought a day with money belonging to people who would ride through it.

The next morning, at the prisoner shed, a guard asked whether the queen wished the men brought out. She went in instead. The smell made her breathe through her mouth. Light entered through gaps below the roof; underneath one of them a prisoner was sewing the sole back onto a shoe, turning it to catch the light with every stitch.

A man stood up too quickly and caught the beam with his head. Several others rose after him. She told them to sit.

“Who here has a reeve at the camp?”

Four men answered. The fifth kept his hand raised until she looked at him.

“My reeve says he won’t come.”

“Why?”

“I was dismissed before the summons. I went in my brother’s place.”

“Where is your brother?”

“Dead, Majesty.”

She asked his name and parish, then had the guard write them down. It was possible that he had invented the brother. It was possible that all five men were exactly what their captains had called them. She had two days in which to discover which men could safely be sent home, and a clerk who was trying to record five parishes with a pen that had split.

She left the shed angry with Dunmere. It was easier than being angry with the man who might have invented a dead brother.

The earl’s envoy arrived while they were eating. Dunmere had sent him to propose changes to the agreement already signed. Renald’s forty riders, the envoy explained, could not serve under a captain empowered to requisition their own tenants’ horses. The joint garrison required a fresh instrument. Until then the forty would remain at their present station.

Veralyn set down her spoon.

“His eldest son is already travelling to Valora.”

“With his wife, as agreed.”

“And Renald?”

“Must have an order he can honour.”

The envoy had eaten before coming. She could smell anise on his breath. He offered condolences for the disturbances in the south.

“Fairport has been attacked,” she said. “You may call it that.”

“My lord has received conflicting accounts.”

“So have I. He has chosen an excellent day to discover an objection.”

Tylan drew the pass survey between their plates. At the eastern end he had marked two watering places and the village from which the joint garrison would buy its grain.

“Your forty use their own mounts,” he said. “Our captain requisitions none without the joint clerk recording the owner and the reason. Complaints go to both signatories. That is already in the agreement.”

“The rate of payment—”

“Last market before the rising. Weekly.”

“Which market?”

Veralyn looked at him. He met her gaze. The omission was real; the lowland market paid less for grain than the pass villages did, and she could not ride south leaving two armed companies to settle it over somebody’s barn.

“The nearest open market on the day before the rising,” she said. “Put its name in. Renald arrives within three days.”

“Five would allow—”

“Three.”

The envoy asked for a room in which to consider the wording. She gave him the vacant clerk’s tent and watched him cross the wet grass, lifting his skirts to keep the hem clean.

“He knows I have to go.”

“Yes,” Tylan said.

“He will discover another omission tomorrow.”

“Then we should give Renald a reason to come before his father writes again.”

“What reason?”

“He has been asking who will command the western patrols.”

She studied the survey. The western patrols would have the harder riding and the earlier warning of any new rising. They would also know where Dunmere’s men went.

“No independent arrests.”

“Agreed.”

“And he reports to Bucktree.”

Tylan turned the survey towards himself and wrote beside the village name.

The envoy returned before the watch changed. He accepted the market and the three days; Renald would command a mixed patrol under Bucktree’s orders. Veralyn signed on the dry corner of the table. Afterward she could remember the anise more clearly than the man’s face.

That evening she went to Anneth’s house.

The steward was mending a shutter at the back. Anneth stood over a pan of onions, moving them away from the heat with a wooden spoon. There was flour on one sleeve and a clean place on the table where someone had lifted a bowl. Veralyn could hear the old man testing the shutter, opening and closing it until the latch caught.

“Will you eat?” Anneth asked.

“I have eaten.”

“Will you eat again?”

Veralyn sat down.

They ate from the same pan because Anneth had not expected company. The onions were sweet at the edges. Veralyn asked about the grey, and Anneth said he had bitten the farrier and was therefore recovering.

“Come south with me,” Veralyn said.

Anneth’s spoon stopped above the pan.

“To Valora?”

“Wherever I must go first. There would be a room. Your father could come, if he wished. I could find him work suited to him.”

“My father has work.”

“I meant—”

“I know what you meant.”

The shutter clicked again. Anneth set down the spoon and wiped a small crescent of onion from the edge of her plate.

“No.”

Veralyn had already thought of horses, an escort, a room with a door nobody would open without knocking. She had not thought beyond those arrangements.

“Is it your father?”

“He is part of it. There are the tenants whose rents he receives, and the household, and my own work.”

“Someone could do that work.”

“Yes. Someone could.”

Anneth glanced towards the hearth-shelf. The stones there were pale in the firelight; a cup had been placed at the far end, beyond the reach of the heat. She looked back at Veralyn.

“I want to stay here.”

The queen felt a quick, unreasonable impulse to ask what was lacking in the offer. She could improve it. She could offer something that would make leaving easier. Anneth sat with both hands on the table, waiting, and Veralyn heard how the question would sound.

“Very well,” she said.

Anneth’s shoulders eased. That hurt more than the refusal.

“I’m sorry.”

“Don’t be.”

“I didn’t mean to make you unhappy.”

“You haven’t been very successful at that until now.”

Anneth smiled a little. Veralyn put her hand on the table beside hers, without taking it. After a moment Anneth touched her wrist.

They stayed until the pan was cold. The steward came in, saw them, and asked his daughter whether the queen would require a candle. Anneth said they could manage. He took the pan away with him.

Later, Veralyn lay awake listening to the wind move the repaired shutter. Shadowsteel was outside the door. She had left it there herself.

Before dawn she returned to camp and wrote to Jaennis. She began with the campaign and stopped after two lines. On another sheet she wrote:

I have no reliable word of your father. Seniro is looking. I will send what I learn, even if it is only that we have learned nothing.

She sat with the pen suspended. I am coming was what she wanted to write. It was also what Jaennis would read as a promise with a day attached to it.

I am making the northern road safe enough to leave. Tylan is preparing the army. I wish you were here, although you would hate the tent, and the cook would hate the questions you would ask him.

She signed before she could turn the letter back into a report.

Outside, the guard changed. A cart went past carrying the prisoners who could not yet walk. The man with the mended shoe followed it, helping to push at the wheel where the ground dipped. His reeve had come in the night. Veralyn watched until the cart reached firmer ground, then folded the letter and called for wax.

Chapter Thirteen: A Court After the News

Jaennis knew that her father was probably wearing the wrong shoes.

It was the first thing she thought after Pellam told her. She listened to him distinguish a report from a confirmation, and pictured the pair with the stiff backs that rubbed above Conlin’s heel. He wore them when he expected to sit all day. If he had been obliged to walk, he would have been bleeding before he reached the gate.

Pellam stopped speaking.

“Say it again.”

He did, with the same care. Fairport had been taken by an assault from the sea. The people arriving north spoke of fire and killing. There was no confirmed account of Lord Constano Fenwood.

“Who last saw him?”

“A harbour servant. Before the attack.”

“How long before?”

“The servant left at first light. I have sent to ask him precisely.”

First light. Her father would have been eating something while reading, holding it away from the page so that crumbs did not fall into the fold. She had teased him about doing that. Once, when she was younger, she had put crumbs into the fold herself and laughed when he discovered them.

“Thank you,” she said.

Pellam remained by the table.

“There is an audience at noon.”

“Yes.”

“Shall I cancel it?”

“No. Give me half an hour.”

He went out. Joan closed the inner door behind him, then returned and sat on the floor beside Jaennis’s chair.

“I don’t need—”

“I know.”

Joan did not take her hand. After a while Jaennis put it on her shoulder herself.

The noon audience involved a dispute over a roof. Two religious houses claimed the income of a property whose roof had fallen in. Neither claimed responsibility for the repair. Jaennis heard them through and asked where the tenants were sleeping.

“Temporarily with relations, Majesty.”

“Then we have time to find out which of you has been receiving their rents. Pay the repair from that account.”

“There are questions of title.”

“There is rain.”

Ysolt, standing behind the petitioners, looked briefly at the ceiling. Jaennis knew she had been too sharp. She did not recall the order.

By afternoon the upper room was full of folded linen. Margery had arrived with a seamstress and several bags that looked charitable until one opened them. Half contained gowns unsuitable for anyone who had just fled a burning city.

“Send these back,” Jaennis said.

“They were offered kindly.”

“Then return them kindly. Ask for shifts and blankets.”

Margery lifted a silk bodice embroidered with small golden insects. “Even the beetles?”

“Especially the beetles.”

Joan laughed. The sound struck Jaennis as almost indecent, and then she wanted to hear it again.

They spent an hour sorting. A young wife whose name Jaennis habitually confused with her sister’s turned out to know how many strips could be cut from an old sheet for binding wounds. One of the serving women knew a carrier whose sister lived north of Fairport. Ysolt took the name. Jaennis asked that the carrier be paid for any journey made on her behalf, whether he brought news or not.

She did not want men manufacturing a dead father or a living one because there was a reward attached to the answer.

By the next hour there were three lists on the table and no agreement about which should be used first.

Joan had the guild families. Some had relations already in Valora; others had been told that the guild would find rooms and had reached the city to discover that the rooms were still occupied. Margery had the officers’ households, including two women whose husbands were with the army and had been sending money through Fairport. Catrin had copied the names of children travelling without anyone able to say where their parents were.

“Those first,” Catrin said.

“Where?” Joan asked.

“Here.”

“In which rooms?”

“There must be rooms.”

Joan looked at Jaennis. She did not answer on her behalf. Jaennis had a sudden, unworthy wish that she would: make the beds appear, as she had made a pleasant gathering out of women whose husbands objected to one another.

“The old sewing room,” she said.

“It has no hearth,” Joan replied.

“The room above it.”

“Two of your attendants sleep there.”

“They can share.”

“They already do.”

Margery set her list down. “The officers’ wives need money, not beds. Most are staying with people who can afford to feed them, though they may be unpleasant about it. We could begin with advances on their husbands’ pay.”

“Who authorises the advances?” Joan asked.

“I can ask my husband.”

“Then ask him before you offer them.”

Margery’s expression cooled. “I said I could ask.”

Jaennis put one hand flat on the table. The silk bodice with the gold insects lay beside it, absurd among the names. She had enjoyed having these women come to her with things they knew. Now they had come with people who could not all be put in the room at once.

“Catrin, how many children?”

“Eleven on this list.”

“Where are they now?”

“At the south gate. In the chapel room.”

“With whom?”

“A widow who came with the boat. She says one is her neighbour’s child. The others followed.”

“Has she said she cannot stay?”

“No.”

“Then we send food and bedding there tonight. Someone who can take the names carefully goes with it. We don’t bring children into this house and then have their families searching at the gate.”

Catrin looked down at her list. “I thought it would be safer.”

“It may be. We find out.”

Joan offered to send the woman who had identified the carrier’s parish. Margery wrote a note to her husband, changing the wording twice before folding it. Jaennis asked for the number of guild families likely to lose their present rooms within the next two days. There were seven. Three had small children, two had a sick adult, one was a married pair, and the last entry gave only a man’s name.

“Why has he a room to himself?”

“His wife died on the road,” Joan said. “He won’t let anyone take her things away.”

“Does he need the whole room for them?”

The words were out before Jaennis heard them. Catrin looked up. Joan’s face remained composed.

“I will ask what can be done,” Joan said.

“No. Ask whether someone he knows can stay with him.”

She drew the list towards her and put a small mark beside the name. She did not know whether the man needed company, but she could at least stop treating his wife’s clothes as space somebody else ought to occupy.

They found the beds by giving up other uses. A room reserved for visiting household officers could hold one family. Two women already lodging near the palace would take sisters and their children if the household supplied meal. The guild-master in Valora had an empty warehouse room he had not volunteered because he expected cargo; Jaennis offered to hire it until the cargo came and asked him to say when that would be.

By evening six of the seven families had a place promised for the next week. The seventh still required a room suitable for a man who could not climb stairs. Jaennis had three messages sent to people whose houses she knew well enough to ask about the ground floor.

She could not remember having spoken so long without anyone offering a compliment.

When the first baskets went out, Catrin helped carry them to the door. Jaennis followed her into the passage. She had meant to thank her for the list. Instead she noticed the dark half-circles beneath the younger woman’s eyes and asked where she was sleeping.

“With my cousin. In the little room over the kitchen.”

“Have you been sleeping?”

“Some.”

“There is a chamber here, beside the dressing room. Use it while we find out where your aunt is.”

Catrin hesitated.

“It has a fire,” Jaennis added. “You needn’t be useful to earn it.”

The woman looked towards the stairs, then back at her. Jaennis could see how much she wanted to accept and found the knowledge pleasing, even now, with the displaced families’ names still in her hand.

“Thank you,” Catrin said.

“I’ll have your things fetched.”

“I can fetch them.”

“Then take someone to help.”

She let Catrin choose the person. When Joan came to ask which chamber she meant, Jaennis named it without looking towards the door from her own room that opened onto the same passage.

Fayd came before supper. He brought no assurance of Constano’s safety, for which she was grateful until she realised he might understand exactly how grateful she would be.

“Maresse has admitted the displaced people to the meadow,” he said. “His captain is keeping them there until somebody finds room farther north. Corbel will let households with named sponsors pass north. I am trying to remove that condition.”

“By asking him?”

“By finding someone who can feed them beyond his boundary.”

“And then?”

“Then he can tell his tenants he has not admitted another village to eat their seed.”

She leaned back. “You make these men sound very reasonable.”

“They are reasonable about certain things. It is useful to know which.”

“Do you know whether my father is alive?”

“No.”

“Then do not let anyone imply that you do.”

He bowed his head. “I won’t.”

She believed that answer. It was an uncomfortable thing to believe him about.

He asked leave to speak with Pellam about a broader undertaking. Three lords, he said, might be persuaded to make their roads available to the queen. Not merely to refugees. He had brought the names, the passages they controlled, and the terms under which each captain currently held them.

“Put it before Master Pellam. I want the roads checked by someone who has travelled them.”

“Of course.”

“Someone we choose.”

His smile was small and perfectly civil. “That will take longer.”

“Then you have an interest in being helpful.”

After he left, she returned to the upper room. Margery had stayed. She was sitting on the rug with her shoes off, picking the gold beetles from the bodice.

“The thread can be sold,” she said, before Jaennis could object.

Jaennis sat beside her. For several minutes they worked in silence. Her hands steadied with the small task.

“Will the queen come here?” Margery asked.

“When she can.”

“I should like to meet her properly.”

“You’ve met her.”

“I curtsied at her. She was speaking to a man about a horse.”

“That happens.”

“What does she like?”

Jaennis looked at her, at the bent head and the dark lashes and the clever mouth that was not pretending the question innocent. Desire came to her unexpectedly, through the weariness. She felt it and wanted Veralyn’s hand on her waist at the same moment.

“People who answer her,” she said. “A little recklessly.”

“I can do that.”

“Yes. I rather think you can.”

Veralyn’s letter arrived the following evening. Jaennis read the first paragraph standing. At the mention of the tent and the cook she sat on the bed and began to cry, because it sounded so much like her that for a moment the distance had become unbearable.

The carrier’s sister sent back a description of a cart.

It came on the reverse of a request for soap, in a hand Ysolt could read more readily than Jaennis. A gentleman with a twisted foot had been seen at a mill, travelling with children and a wounded Vhasian. He had worn one cut shoe and borrowed a second stick. There had been clerks with him, or men who looked like clerks, and a woman who insisted on keeping a wrapped bundle dry.

“When?” Jaennis asked.

“Four days before this was sent.”

“So six days ago.”

“Yes.”

“Did she speak to him?”

“She spoke to the miller’s wife.”

Jaennis read the description again. The wounded Vhasian could be Matteo. Half the clerks in Fairport would have tried to save papers. Her father’s foot was a better mark than his coat, but wounded men borrowed sticks every day.

“Ask what he called the children. Ask whether he wore his ring. Ask whether the walking stick had a silver cap.”

“It says he paid for grain.”

“That is like him. It is also like anyone who wants grain.”

Ysolt took the paper back. She did not congratulate her on being sensible.

Jaennis sent a carriage as far as the last village the household could reach without an escort. She sent clean shoes with it, in two sizes, and hated herself for the larger pair. A physician went with the driver. The driver asked where he was to wait if the village had been emptied. Ysolt made Jaennis answer that before he left.

In the upper room Catrin was standing over a sack of bedding. She had cut the stitching and was lifting blankets out one at a time, turning each over before laying it on the table. Joan stood beside her with a letter.

“What’s happened?” Jaennis asked.

Catrin kept working.

“My aunt’s house. The roof went. They could see it from the water.”

“Your aunt?”

“With her daughter. They got across to a boat.”

“Then we must send for them.”

“They’ve gone inland. Her daughter has friends there.”

Catrin set down a blanket embroidered with faded red flowers. She flattened it with both hands, although it was already flat.

“She had the room ready for me,” she said. “When I left to come here she said she would keep it exactly as it was. As if I were going to forget where I lived.”

Jaennis looked at Joan, who shook her head slightly. No useful letter needed dictating. No official had failed to do something she could now order him to do.

She sat on the bench and took the next blanket from the sack. Catrin let her. They worked until the table was full, and then Joan brought bread and cheese. Catrin ate standing up before either of them could invite her to sit.

Valdais’s terms arrived under a safe conduct issued for the exchange of wounded.

The envoy wore travelling black and a plain gold cross at his throat. His gloves were good enough to have survived the journey without showing it. He waited while Pellam checked the seal, then laid a second document beside the first.

“An undertaking concerning the river trade, Majesty.”

Jaennis read it without inviting him to sit.

Valdais proposed to guarantee passage at specified crossings, protect merchant vessels whose masters paid the published duties, and restore the property of those who accepted his protection within a fixed term. The wording concerning Fairport appeared halfway down the second sheet. Its exclusive right to receive certain cargoes would lapse during the emergency.

“For how long?” she asked.

“Until secure trade can be restored.”

“Whose finding restores it?”

“His Grace is willing to discuss the form of supervision.”

“He is already collecting the duties?”

“At crossings for which he is responsible.”

She passed the document to Pellam. On the table between them lay a list of household purchases delayed by the loss of Fairport. Oil for the lamps. Dried fruit. A chest of good wool that Margery had expected before Reaping.
