Valdais’s ships and northern allies had interrupted the route; now his envoy offered to make it reliable for people who would pay him to do so.

“Does he require an oath to his title?”

“To his protection of the river.”

“Carefully phrased.”

“Carefully intended, Majesty.”

The envoy did not smile. Jaennis found that she preferred him to men who expected admiration for being evasive.

“I cannot bind the queen to this. You may leave it for her.”

“His Grace would value your recommendation.”

“He may value the queen’s answer when she has given one.”

After he had gone, Pellam marked the clause about Fairport and another concerning warehouses on the western bank. Fayd, summoned from the anteroom, read both standing at the window.

“He offers no claim to the crown,” Pellam said.

“He can collect from a cargo without wearing one,” Jaennis said. “Where does he put the money?”

Fayd turned the sheet. “Part pays the cantons whose men took Marcott. Part maintains his force. There will be promises concerning the rest.”

“Promises we have not seen.”

“No.”

She had a servant bring the map used for Seniro’s dispatches. It was too large for the table. Pellam moved the ink and the untouched fruit while she unrolled it.

“Show me your three lords.”

Fayd touched Corbel’s lands, Maresse’s approaches, then the road held by Lisle. His finger paused at each boundary. He gave the names of their captains and distinguished the men he had spoken with from those whose answers had come through intermediaries.

“If they make their roads available,” Jaennis asked, “does that drive Valdais back?”

“It permits an army to try.”

“Where?”

“At Hollin, if the marshal can hold him there.”

“And if Seniro cannot?”

“Then we will need another agreement.”

Pellam drew his chair closer. Jaennis laid her finger on Hollin and looked back along the roads Fayd had shown her.

“If Lisle stands aside a day late, whose horsemen will be behind him?”

“Valdais’s, possibly.”

“And the queen will be riding towards them.”

Jaennis took her hand from the map. “Then Seniro must know the day you promise, and whose promise it is. Master Pellam, send the road names to Seniro with the conditions. No statement that the lords have joined us. They haven’t.”

“Will you include the envoy’s terms?”

“Every word.”

Fayd pointed to a narrow road behind Hollin.

“The marshal may be able to withdraw his stores along this approach.”

“May?”

“I have an account from a carrier.”

“How old?”

“Eight days.”

Jaennis moved his hand gently away from the map.

“Joan has a woman whose brother went through four days ago. He says the culvert has fallen in. You can send foot, perhaps. You cannot send loaded wagons until someone repairs it.”

Fayd looked at her, then at the road.

“May I speak to him?”

“You may send your questions. He is working and I have promised he will not lose another morning.”

The admission interested him more than if she had produced the man from behind a curtain. He asked for a sheet and wrote three questions. She added one about where timber could be bought. Pellam attached them to the dispatch so Seniro would know what had been asked and what remained unanswered.

They worked until the servants came to lay supper. Jaennis had places set for Pellam and Fayd. It was easier to continue than to summon them the next day to reconstruct what each had said. Fayd declined wine while he was reading and accepted it afterward. She observed the distinction without deciding what to make of it.

“Twice a week,” she said when the papers were cleared. “You will bring the replies to these questions. Master Pellam will bring the letters that have come by other roads.”

“And if there are no replies?” Fayd asked.

“Then we shall have a short supper.”

He smiled. “I have found those quite uncommon here.”

“Do not mistake the cook’s generosity for mine.”

The following morning Ysolt brought the miller’s answer. The man had called Matteo by his childhood name when the wound was being dressed. He had a silver-capped cane, and the ring had left a pale place when he removed it to wash his hands. The miller’s wife had asked his secretary who he was. The secretary had said Lord Constano Fenwood.

Jaennis put the paper down before trying to speak.

“Send to the carriage. Send to Seniro. Say when they saw him. We don’t know where he is now.”

Ysolt’s hand rested briefly on her shoulder.

Fayd brought his first account of Constano’s escape the next afternoon. Jaennis thanked him and compared the locations. His witness had seen the party earlier, beside a drainage channel south of the mill. Together the two accounts gave them several miles of her father’s journey. The carriage was still waiting farther north.

She left its driver there. She wanted to order him south along every possible road until he found her father, but that would leave no carriage at the place her father had been told to reach.

Part III: The Return

Chapter Fourteen: A Road with Three Masters

The horse belonged to the woman, the saddle belonged to the carrier, and the man holding the bridle said both belonged to his lord until somebody paid him.

When they had left Vhasia, Valdais was reaching Sennet. Elyas had heard where the duke was buying grain and imagined coming home in time to warn someone. Since then the duke had crossed, Marcott had fallen, and Fairport had burned. The last news had reached them at an inn where the keeper refused to accept a Fairport draft for their supper.

Elyas heard the argument over the horse before he saw any of the people in it. Their wagon had stopped behind two others at the bend above the landing, and Alric had gone forward to discover why. He came back with the expression he used when he had asked a simple question and been given an answer requiring a different education.

“There is a toll.”

“There was a toll at the top of the hill.”

“This is another one.”

“On the ferry?”

“On approaching it, apparently.”

Pell shifted beneath the wagon cover. One of the guards told him to keep his hands where they could be seen. The clerk obeyed without speaking. He had learned to make no unnecessary movement when the guards were bored.

Elyas dismounted.

“Stay here,” he told Alric.

“I had intended to.”

Lyra was already looking down the road, past the blocked wagons. Gyllan’s staff lay along her saddle beneath two leather loops. She had wrapped its worn grip to keep the rain off it, then unwound the wrapping three days later and put it away.

“Can you see the landing?” Elyas asked.

“No. I can see the men who can.”

There were two of them on the rise beside the road. They wore boiled leather and carried bows with the strings under their coats. Neither was watching the argument. They were watching the wagons.

Elyas went forward on foot.

The woman had one hand on the saddle and the other against the horse’s neck. The carrier stood beside her with a broken strap in his fist. Opposite them, a man with a green strip tied round his upper arm held the bridle close to the bit.

He saw Elyas coming and straightened.

“Highness.”

That was something, at least.

“Whose men?” Elyas asked.

“Lord Maresse’s.”

“Where is your captain?”

“Down at the ferry.”

“Fetch him.”

The man looked toward the rise. One of the bowmen turned away and went down the bank.

The woman did not let go of the saddle.

“What have you paid?” Elyas asked her.

She brought a folded paper out of the front of her dress, but the carrier answered first.

“Two coppers a wheel at the high gate. A copper a head for beasts. Passage at the landing was to be what it was last autumn, by the agreement at Tarlington.”

“That was before the emergency,” the man with the bridle said.

“What emergency?”

“The present one.”

The carrier held up the broken strap.

“They took the mare off the wagon. She’s in foal.”

“I can see that.”

“They said if we couldn’t pay—”

“Let me hear the captain.”

The man came up from the landing with a piece of bread in his hand. He was a broad, grey-bearded fellow whose left boot had been split at the instep and laced back together. Elyas knew him. He had ridden behind Lord Maresse at a hunt three years earlier and spent most of the day finding a child who had gone after the dogs.

“Hew,” Elyas said.

“Highness. We heard you were coming home.”

“We have been coming home for a long time.”

“So I see.”

He looked past Elyas toward the wagon, then at the sword on his hip. He finished his bread before speaking again.

“The lord has ordered a road contribution.”

“The contribution has been paid.”

“At the high gate. This is for the landing guard.”

“Whose landing?”

“His lordship’s bank.”

Elyas held out his hand for the woman’s paper. She gave it to him reluctantly, as though having to let go of it was another thing the men might charge for.

Sister Oria’s mark stood at the foot. Above it a clerk had recorded the carrier, the number of animals, the number of wagons, and the freight. Two sacks of dried beans had been crossed out and entered again in a different hand.

“This is under the accord.”

“The accord covers the passage. We’re not charging for passage.”

“You are holding her horse.”

“Pending the road contribution.”

Elyas looked up at him.

Hew rubbed a crumb out of his beard.

“I’m sorry, Highness.”

“Then give it back.”

“That isn’t what I meant.”

Behind them, a child in one of the wagons began to cry. Somebody told it to be quiet, softly at first, then with an edge that made the crying worse.

Elyas returned the paper to the woman.

“How much?”

“Six silvers for the two wagons,” Hew said.

The carrier made a noise.

“For a landing guard?”

“There have been attacks.”

“By the landing guard?”

Hew’s expression closed. Elyas wished he had not said it. It had been easy and it had pleased the carrier, and neither was useful.

He drew Hew a little way up the road.

“You know whose protection that paper carries.”

“I know who signed it.”

“Then honour it.”

“My lord didn’t agree to feed a garrison on what he was getting last autumn. He agreed the pilots could use the landing. Now the pilots want guards and the duke’s officers want guards and everybody wants my men to stay here while they go somewhere else.”

“The duke’s officers can answer for the cost.”

“Which duke?” Hew asked.

Elyas stopped.

It had not been said cruelly. That was the worst of it. The man wanted to know which person would answer a letter.

“Constano,” Elyas said.

“From Fairport?”

He had not known Constano was in Fairport.

“From wherever he is.”

Hew looked at him with a tired patience that made him feel younger than any insult could have done.

He paid the six silvers.

He called it an advance against the proper settlement of the dispute, and had Hew say that he understood. Hew said it, and took the coins, and ordered the horse returned. The woman caught the mare by the halter and held her face against its neck for a moment before helping the carrier put the harness back on.

Elyas asked for the payment to be acknowledged in writing.

Hew sent for the landing clerk.

It took most of an hour. During that time a third wagon came down the road and was stopped behind theirs. Its driver had no paper with Oria’s mark. He watched Elyas with the steady, hopeful attention of a man who had just seen a rich person do something useful.

Elyas went to find out what he carried.

By the time they reached the ferry, he had paid another four silvers, undertaken to send an answer from Tarlington, and promised the woman with the mare that nobody would take it on the return journey.

Lyra waited until they were on the water.

“How will you do that?”

“What?”

“Stop them taking her horse when she comes back.”

“I’ll speak to Maresse.”

“Before she comes back?”

He looked toward the woman. She was standing at the head of the mare on the other ferry, holding the bridle herself now. The carrier had put a folded sack over the animal’s back against the wind.

“I’ll write tonight.”

Lyra nodded. Her silence left him considering what a letter would accomplish at the landing.

The river smelled of rain coming from farther north. He had known that smell before he had known what it meant. Beyond the ferry’s square bow the opposite landing rose between willows, and above it the road ran toward Tarlington through fields that should have been standing gold.

One of them had been cut green. The sheaves lay where they had fallen, turning black.

“Who did that?” he asked the pilot.

“Men who wanted fodder.”

“Whose?”

The pilot put his weight against the sweep.

“They didn’t leave their names.”

They did not reach the posting house until after dark.

The road on the eastern bank had been churned by traffic going both ways, with the wheel ruts so deep that the wagon’s underside scraped the crown between them. Twice they unloaded Pell and the document case. The prisoner stood in the rain while the men pushed. Nobody thought to offer him a cloak until Lyra took one from the bundle behind her saddle and held it out to a guard.

“He can put it on with his hands tied.”

The guard looked towards Elyas.

“Yes,” Elyas said.

It was a small thing to require permission for. That did not make him comfortable with how readily he had allowed the habit to grow.

A mile farther on, a boy ran out of a field and caught his rein.

The horse reared sideways. Elyas hauled it round before it struck the child. One of the guards drew his sword.

“Put that away. Let go of the rein.”

The boy held on, staring up at him. He was perhaps twelve. His feet were bare and blue with cold.

“My mother’s in the house.”

“What house?”

“She’s in the house.”

Alric dismounted and came forward slowly, keeping his hands visible. The boy flinched when he touched his shoulder, but allowed himself to be led clear of the horse.

“Show me,” Alric said.

The house stood beyond the cut field. Its door had been taken off its hinges and laid across two barrels to make a table. There was no one at the table now. A handful of dice lay in a puddle on the wood, together with a crust of bread that had swollen in the rain.

Inside, a woman was sitting on the floor beside an overturned chest. She had not been assaulted. Elyas understood how quickly he had made that assumption only when she raised her head and he saw the fury in her face.

“They’ve taken my husband.”

“Who?”

“The men who slept here.”

“What colours?”

“I don’t know colours. One had a bird. One had yellow sleeves. They said they needed somebody to show them the road.”

He looked at the boy. Another guide taken. There must have been dozens of them in a country whose paths changed every time someone opened a drain.

“When did they leave?”

“Yesterday.”

“Which way?”

She pointed east. Alric, standing behind him, made a small movement of the head. Their own road went north. They had a prisoner, evidence, horses growing thin, and no means of identifying a company from a sleeve and a badly remembered badge.

The woman saw the exchange.

“You’re not going to look.”

“I can send word.”

“To whom?”

He nearly said Braedyn. It was the answer he had carried through childhood: take the difficulty to the man who would know who to summon. Instead he asked for her husband’s name.

“Roth. He works the lower strips. Everybody knows him.”

Elyas wrote it in the small book he kept inside his coat. The woman watched without moving. It was the second time that day he had put a person’s trouble on paper because he could not remove it from their life.

Behind the chest, a dog began to growl.

The woman put out a hand.

“Quiet.”

It was a thin brown dog with one damaged ear. It had been protecting a sack. When she drew the sack out, Elyas saw the grain scattered beneath it, trodden into the dirt floor. The soldiers had taken most of the contents and cut the bottom to see whether anything was hidden there. Grain had spilled while she carried what remained indoors.

Alric knelt and began picking it from the earth.

“You can’t eat that,” one of the guards said from the doorway.

The woman looked at him. He found something to do outside.

They righted the chest. Lyra came in with a blanket and a packet of dried peas. The boy followed her, carrying a wooden bowl. No one asked him to; he simply crouched beside Alric and began separating the cleaner grains from the dirt.

Elyas went outside and found the door’s hinges beside the threshold. The pins were gone. He could probably make replacements from wood, if he had the right knife and a little time. He took the door from the barrels and discovered how heavy it was.

“Highness.”

The senior guard had followed him.

“We should not remain here.”

“Help me lift this.”

“The road is exposed.”

“Then put a man on it.”

“We haven’t men to spare.”

Elyas looked at the drawn, disapproving face. The guard was right. A few months earlier that would have settled it: a man who understood roads would have explained the danger, and Elyas would have accepted being taken somewhere safer. Now the correctness of the warning felt like only one part of the decision.

“Two men at the wagon. One on the rise. You help me with the door.”

The man obeyed with no visible enthusiasm.

It took longer than Elyas expected. His first wooden pin split. His second was too thick and had to be shaved down a little at a time while he crouched beneath the lintel. Rain ran off his hair into his collar. The woman came to watch. After several minutes she brought him her husband’s awl, which made the work easier.

“Keep it straight,” she said when he began forcing the second hinge.

“I am trying.”

“Try more to the left.”

The guard made a sound that might have been a cough.

They got the door hung badly enough to scrape but well enough to close. The woman opened it and shut it twice. She asked again where he would send her husband’s name.

“To the officers at Tarlington. And to the people commanding on the eastern road.”

“Will they answer?”

“I’ll ask them to answer you here.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

“No.”

He gave her the name of the posting house ahead and said that a message left there could be forwarded to him. That much he knew how to arrange. When he turned away, she took his sleeve.

“If he comes back while you’re asking, I’ll send word.”

“Thank you.”

“I don’t want anybody going to trouble after it’s done.”

He looked at her hand, chapped and white around the nails. “I don’t think that would be much trouble.”

“It’s somebody’s.”

At the wagon, Pell was standing with the borrowed cloak round his shoulders. A guard had taken him behind the hedge to relieve himself and was now inspecting the cords on his wrists. Pell looked at Elyas and then away.

“You had a chance to run,” Elyas said quietly.

“Into what?”

“The field.”

The prisoner looked towards the house. Warm light showed through a crack in the ill-hung door.

“I can hear what people think soldiers do with strangers found in fields. I would prefer the hearing you promised me.”

“You’ll have it.”

“I know you mean that now.”

There was nothing insolent in his voice. Elyas would almost have preferred insolence. He helped him onto the wagon step and waited while the guard arranged the blanket beneath him.

They resumed the road. Alric rode beside Elyas until the house disappeared behind the trees.

“You cut your hand,” he said.

Elyas looked down. A bright line crossed the heel of his palm.

“Only a scratch.”

“I was going to suggest wrapping it before the rein rubs it open. I wasn’t proposing to carry you.”

Elyas laughed and accepted the cloth.

By the time they saw the posting house lights, he was rehearsing the questions he would ask Braedyn about the delay: whether there had been something obvious he could have done for the woman, something a person familiar with the valley’s households would have known.

He was still thinking about that when the keeper came out to meet them.

At the posting house, Elyas found a letter waiting for him.

He recognised Mara’s hand on the outside. There were two other names on the cover, places at which somebody had been told to send it on if he had already passed. Both had been crossed through.

He took it into the yard because there was better light there.

She had written that Braedyn had died in the early morning. That he had not been alone. That Constano had been with him, and Sister Oria, and the physician. She had written the names carefully, as though she knew he would ask and wanted him to have the answer before there was anybody to ask.

He read the first half of the page twice.

Then he read the rest.

The funeral had been held. The household was well. His room had not been given to anybody. She did not know whether he would want it when he returned, but had made sure of it anyway.

At the bottom she had written: I am angry that you were not here. I know that is not fair. Come home.

He sat on the mounting block.

His father’s letter was in the inner pocket of his coat. Not the announcement of his death; the last letter Oswyn had written to him, telling him that he was glad the road had been passable and asking whether he had enough money. Elyas had written an answer in a room in the west and rewritten it because it had sounded too much like an account of expenses.

The answer was still in the same pocket.

Alric came out of the posting house with a cup, saw him, and stopped.

“Is it—”

“Yes.”

Alric set the cup beside him on the block and went back inside.

Elyas folded Mara’s letter along its existing creases. He had thought about what he would say to Braedyn all the way home. There had been things he would explain and things he would not try to defend. He had become quite good, in his head, at having that conversation.

Across the yard, a groom was washing mud off a horse’s legs. He lifted each hoof and ran his hand down it, careful of the tender place above the heel. The horse shifted. The groom spoke to it without looking up.

Elyas sat until the tea was cold. Then he went inside to write to Lord Maresse.

Chapter Fifteen: North Without the Office

The gardener’s name was Peren. He had intended to recover his cart at the first village.

At the first village the well was fouled and the house nearest it had been pulled apart for timber. At the second, armed men told them that the road ahead was closed. Peren asked who had closed it. The men said Lord Corbel, though none wore his badge.

At the third, Matteo Tireno began to shake.

Constano made them stop beneath a shed with half its thatch missing. Matteo said he was cold. It was afternoon, and warm enough that flies had gathered at the edge of the bandage on his arm.

“We need clean water,” Constano said.

“We’ve been needing that,” Peren replied.

“Yes.”

The gardener looked at him, expecting anger. When none came, he took the bucket and went to ask again.

Renn helped Matteo out of his coat. The wound was deeper than it had looked in the smoke. One of the nurses, a broad woman called Sella, sent the children outside and began to soak the cloth free.

“You’ve done this before?” Constano asked.

“I’ve had children before.”

“That isn’t the same.”

“No. But I’m here.”

He let her work.

His own foot had swollen until the shoe would not come off. Renn cut the fastening. The skin beneath the heel had broken. Constano stared at it with disproportionate resentment. He had lived with the foot all his life; it seemed gratuitous that it should choose this moment to become a new problem.

“We’ll have to leave the cart,” Peren said when he returned. “Axle’s splitting. I’ve bound it twice.”

“Can it be repaired?”

“With wood, an auger, and someone who isn’t trying to keep twenty people walking.”

“I’ll ask.”

The gardener’s mouth tightened. “People are less obliging up here.”

Constano went to the mill using his cane and a length of broken rake handle. There was a bench outside. He was grateful for it before he reached it, and humiliated by how visibly grateful he was when he sat.

The miller’s wife came to the door. She looked at his good coat, his ruined shoe, and the rake handle.

“We have nothing to spare.”

“I want to buy it.”

“With what?”

He put his few coins on the bench between them and kept his name to himself for the moment.

“We need grain, and the use of tools. There are children. One wounded man.”

“There are children here.”

“Yes.”

“The lord’s men came yesterday. Took four sacks. Left a paper.”

“May I see it?”

She laughed in his face.

“Are you going to give me another?”

He had been about to offer exactly that.

She went inside. He thought she had dismissed him until she returned with the paper, held by a corner as if it were dirty. The paper acknowledged four measures, not four sacks. Its ink was fresh; the countermark was smeared.

He explained the difference. She called her husband. The man came out white with anger, then bent over the paper with a finger resting on the disputed word.

“I told her he was counting sacks.”

“He may have been,” Constano said. “This doesn’t say so.”

“Can you make them pay?”

“Not from here.”

“Then what use is it?”

“I can write down what was taken, and you can get the carter who loaded it to put his mark beside yours. Keep this with it. If somebody does pay, you’ll have something better than an argument about a smudge.”

The miller considered that. It was less than either of them wanted. He brought out a stool for his wife and, finally, the ink.

They bought enough grain for a meal, and borrowed the tools. Peren repaired the axle. In return Renn helped lift a stone from the millrace that the miller’s son had been unable to shift alone. By dusk everyone was exhausted, and they had moved nowhere.

Constano wrote three letters. One to Seniro, one to Jaennis, one to the last agent he knew to be collecting grain upriver. He gave his location as precisely as he could, including the miller’s name and the bend in the road. He asked where he should go.

The carrier who agreed to take them would not travel until morning. He was going north, away from Seniro’s last known position. That made the letter to his brother slower, but the southern road had soldiers on it and the carrier would not change his mind.

“Will that be soon enough?” Renn asked after the man left.

“I don’t know.”

Matteo slept badly. Sometimes he spoke as if they were still at Fairport, asking whether a boat had come in. Once he asked for the tutor.

No one had seen the tutor since the warehouse.

Sella took a spare piece of paper and began a list of the people they had not found. The tutor’s name came first. Constano watched her sound the syllables before writing them. It took her a long time.

“Did you see him go out?” he asked Renn.

“No, my lord.”

“Did anyone?”

They asked the nurses, Peren, the older children. One child remembered the tutor taking books upstairs before the alarm. Another said he had seen him on the quay. Neither could be certain when.

Constano had ordered everyone out. He remembered saying it. He had been proud, for a little while, of knowing what to abandon.

He folded the paper and put it inside his coat with the letters he could not yet send. Then he went to sit with Matteo until the shaking stopped.

The next morning the miller’s wife brought a pair of shears and cut the back from Constano’s shoe. She did it on the step, with his foot resting on a folded sack. The leather was good. She ran her thumb over it before cutting, with the reluctance of someone about to spoil a thing she could not afford to replace.

“It was rubbing before we left,” he told her.

“Should have said.”

“To whom?”

“To whoever made you put it on.”

He laughed, and the movement hurt his heel. She looked up, offended, until he said that he had chosen it himself.

Lucrezia took the children down to the stream to wash. She had found a piece of soap in the pocket of a nurse’s coat and divided it with a knife, keeping half dry in a scrap of linen. In Marcott she had possessed a room whose windows opened above a garden of clipped bay. He remembered sitting there while servants brought water cooled in clay jars. Now she was explaining to Livia why the soap must not be allowed to float away.

The child listened without argument. Constano would have preferred an argument.

Aldith came to the step with one of the books she had carried from Fairport. Its cover had warped where water entered beneath the wrapping. She had put clean straw between the damp pages. A few could no longer be separated without taking the ink from one to the next.

“We have the inland advances,” she said. “And most of the outstanding shipments. The wages are in the other volume.”

“Let me see.”

She held it for him. He could not sit comfortably enough to support it on his knees.

One agent’s name ran into the next where the ink had spread. Beyond that, in Aldith’s neat hand, were payments for grain that should now have been approaching Seniro’s army. The goods belonged to the queen. The men handling them had been paid by Constano’s office. If they heard that Fairport had fallen, they might wait for another order. If they did not, they might deliver the grain to a storehouse already abandoned.

He dictated messages for the agents whose names remained legible. Aldith asked which seal she was to use.

“The small one.”

“That tells them it is you. It doesn’t tell the armed men on the road.”

“No.”

She closed the book halfway, keeping one finger between the pages.

“Then say they are to inquire after the marshal’s current stores before proceeding. Otherwise they may obey exactly.”

He changed the wording. She opened the book again.

They used the last clean paper by noon. Tavin went to ask at the mill and came back with the blank ends cut from two old contracts. The miller wanted the written portions preserved. Constano wrote very small.

On the third day Matteo could sit up. Sella would not let him put on his coat. He objected that he could not receive visitors in his shirt; she asked which visitors he was expecting.

His sister came to stand in the doorway.

“Piero would know the cuts through the reed beds,” Matteo said.

“He would.”

“He may be using them.”

“He may.”

Lucrezia sat beside him. She had repeated those words to the children until they had ceased to reassure anyone. Constano left them alone and went outside to find Renn.

The gardener had repaired the axle. He wanted his cart back.

Constano had expected it, and still felt abandoned. He could pay for the timber and the days lost, although doing so would leave almost no coin for the road. He could offer to buy the cart, except that Peren needed it to carry produce when there was produce to carry again.

“We have one man who cannot walk,” he said.

“I’ve seen him.”

“And the children.”

“I’ve seen them too.”

Peren stood with his hands at his sides. One was swollen across the knuckles from working the auger. He had meant to bring vegetables to market and take his empty cart home. The market was gone.

“How far is your house?” Constano asked.

“Half a day that way.”

“Is anyone there?”

“My sister. Unless she left.”

“Could we send someone to find out?”

“And leave me here wondering why they hadn’t come back?”

Constano bowed his head. “No. Of course.”

They settled it by hiring a handcart from the mill and giving Peren the money for its return. Two of Constano’s clerks would help pull it; Crane said he had hauled heavier loads against a contrary tide, though he looked closely at its wheels before making the claim. Matteo could ride. The children would have to walk.

Peren helped transfer their belongings. At the last moment he took a small bag of dried peas from beneath his seat and put it among the bedding.

“You didn’t buy those,” he said when Constano reached for his purse.

The gardener drove away before noon. Livia watched the cart until the willows hid it, then asked whether they were going the wrong way.

“We’re going north,” Lucrezia said.

“So is he.”

“For a little while.”

The child accepted that. Constano wished he knew the roads well enough to offer anything more definite.

They moved at the pace of Matteo’s breathing. At each rut the men slowed the cart, and sometimes stopped to pack earth beneath a wheel before letting it down. Dena walked beside the smallest child, telling him the names of birds. When she did not know one she described it instead. The boy who had slept in her warehouse carried the bag of peas; no one had asked him to, and he refused to give it up when Crane offered.

They reached the next village after dark.

There was room in the bakehouse once the ovens cooled. The baker would take payment for the floor and for the bread his family had intended to eat the next morning. Constano asked about the price. It was high. The man showed him the wood piled against the wall and explained how far his sons had gone to find it. One had not yet returned.

Constano paid.

In the warmth, the smallest child began crying. He had been quiet all afternoon, obedient through each halt. Now he could not be persuaded to sit, eat, or tell anyone what hurt. Lucrezia took him outside. They could hear him through the open door until he fell asleep against her shoulder.

Aldith unwrapped the books. Renn spread their coats beneath Matteo. Joff put a bowl under a slow drip in the roof and looked absurdly pleased when it caught the first drop.

Constano sat against the warm brick and tried to remember whether the last order he had sent from Fairport instructed the grain factors to await confirmation. He could remember signing it. He remembered a loose thread at the edge of the desk cloth, and pushing it beneath the book with his thumb. The sentence itself would not come.

He asked Aldith.

“You changed it,” she said. “They were to proceed.”

“Good.”

“To the old stores.”

“Yes.”

She sat beside him, holding the small book against her knees. Neither suggested writing again. They had already written, and had found no one willing to carry the letters towards the army.

The bridge beyond the village was closed in the morning. Nobody could say who had ordered it. The baker said the water had risen under the southern footing; a carter said soldiers had removed the planks; a woman buying bread said both were true, because the soldiers had taken them after the water loosened them.

Crane went to look. He returned with wet trousers and the information that walking people could cross by the bank, but the handcart could not. The nearest usable passage lay a long way east, through a place whose name the villagers pronounced three different ways.

They remained in the bakehouse. Each morning the baker needed it clear before firing the ovens. They moved bedding, children, Matteo, and the wrapped books into the yard, then waited for the heat to subside before carrying them back. By the second day the children knew where each bundle went and could complete the removal before the baker asked.

Constano sat on a bench beneath the eaves while the others worked. He could see where they had left space around his foot so nobody would strike it while passing. He hated that they had needed to think of it and was grateful every time they remembered.

On the third day a messenger came from one of the northern agents.

The man had no answer from Seniro. He had been carrying a packet towards Fairport when the roads filled with people coming the other way. At the mill he had heard of Constano and followed the slow account of their progress from one village to the next.

“I thought I might overtake you,” he said.

“You have.”

He put the packet down. The outside bore a destination which had been useful before the attack and was now an address in a burned street. Aldith spread a cloth beneath it to keep the damp from entering her rescued book.

The agent had grain under contract at three stores. He needed to know which loads should move first and whether the second advance would be paid as agreed. He had heard rumours of an attack, followed by assurances that the river remained under Fenwood protection. The assurances had arrived later and been written earlier.

Constano sorted them by their dates. He had done this often enough at a desk in Fairport. Here the papers fluttered whenever someone opened the bakehouse door, and he had to weigh their corners with cups.

“Can you reach him again?”

“Yes, my lord. I came by that road.”

“And the marshal?”

The messenger shook his head. “The last post sent me on to a granary. There was nobody there who would receive letters.”

“Empty?”

“Men there. They said the office had gone.”

Constano asked the name of the granary. It was the place he had directed Halven’s powder lighter to reach. Seniro had moved again since then. Of course he had. The river line had not stayed still while Constano tried to get a cart over one damaged bridge.

Aldith opened the relevant pages. They knew what had been purchased, what had been paid, and which carriers were due to move it. They did not know where an army could now receive it.

“We can send them north of the last stores,” Renn said.

“Which north road?”

“The inland one.”

“With whose escort?”

Renn stopped. He had been trying to offer something because the silence at the table was becoming difficult.

Constano thanked him and asked the messenger how long the agent could hold the grain. A few days without expense. Longer if someone paid the carts standing idle. The owners had already begun unloading one company of wagons to accept other work.

For the better part of an hour they discussed a movement none of them could see. The baker’s wife came out, looked at the papers occupying her cups, and fetched different ones for the people waiting to eat. Constano heard her tell Lucrezia not to worry; she had enough cups. It was the kind of lie households told to save a guest from knowing the cost of accommodating him.

He wrote a shorter instruction than the one he had first intended. The agent was to retain the grain already paid for, release the unused transport rather than bind men indefinitely, and send riders to discover where the marshal’s stores now stood. No laden convoy was to move on the assurance of a place-name written before the sack.

“How does he pay the riders?” Aldith asked.

“From the reserve held for the second advance.”

“That pays for information instead of grain.”

“Yes.”

“And when he knows?”

“He writes to whoever can answer him.”

She looked at him for a moment before recording the instruction. He had not named himself. It felt less like generosity than an admission of where he was sitting.

The messenger ate before leaving. Constano asked him whether he needed money for the road. He did. The sum left enough for two more days in the bakehouse, provided they bought no meat and the cart required nothing.

Joff offered to go with him.

“I could find the marshal’s office. I know the men who receive the shipments.”

“Do you know the roads?”

“Some.”

“Do you have a horse?”

“I could walk.”

Constano looked at the young clerk’s boots. They had been repaired with cord after the sole came loose. He had helped pull Matteo for miles without complaint and wanted now to do something by which the office might become an office again.

“If I send you alone, I lose a man who can read the contracts and gain another person whose whereabouts I cannot discover.”

Joff looked down. “Yes, my lord.”

“I know why you offered.”

“I thought it would be useful.”

“It would, if you could get there.”

He asked the messenger to take Joff’s letter to his family instead. The young man had not written one. Aldith gave him a piece of paper and moved her stool so he could use the table. He wrote until the messenger had finished eating, then sealed it with the plain wax they used to close the food jar.

After the man left, Constano dictated a duplicate of his own order. If another route opened, he wanted the instruction ready. Aldith wrote slowly where the ink thinned, pausing to shake the bottle before each fresh line.

In the yard the children had found a game which required stepping only on the pale stones between the paving. Livia could do it in seven steps. The smallest child always needed more and accused her of moving the stones while he looked away. Matteo watched from the doorway, laughing quietly when Lucrezia took a turn and failed at the same place as the boy.

Constano put the paper aside to watch too. For several minutes nobody asked him where they should go.

On the fourth morning a woman selling onions told them there was a physician waiting with a carriage on the northern road. She had heard he was looking for a merchant whose family would pay to have him brought home.

Constano asked where. She named a village he did not know. Renn thought it lay west of their route; Crane thought east. The woman had come from neither direction.

They went on to the next bridge, where someone might be able to tell them.

Chapter Sixteen: The People Who Stayed

Mara was in the stable when they arrived.

For a moment Elyas thought somebody had sent her to meet him. Then he saw the bucket, the groom holding a horse’s head, and the sleeve she had rolled above her elbow. The horse had a cut along its shoulder. She was washing it with the concentrated annoyance she brought to injuries somebody ought to have prevented.

She heard the wagon and looked round.

He had imagined running to her. He had imagined what he would say first. In the yard, with his legs stiff from riding and three guards waiting to be told where to put a prisoner, he said her name and stopped.

She handed the cloth to the groom.

“You took your time.”

“Yes.”

She came across the yard and put both hands against his face. He closed his eyes before she kissed him. When she drew away, one wet thumb remained beneath his jaw.

“You’ve got thinner.”

“So everyone keeps telling me.”

“Perhaps listen.”

He laughed, and found that laughing hurt him in a place he could not have named.

Mara looked past him at the wagon. Alric stood beside it with the document case under his arm. Lyra had dismounted and was loosening the loops that held Gyllan’s staff.

Mara’s hands fell.

“No,” she said.

Elyas had written. He had written the letter twice and sent it with a carrier whose route went through a place that had since changed hands. He had assumed it had arrived because he had no other means of bearing what he had put into it.

“No,” Mara said again, more quietly.

Lyra came toward them carrying the staff herself.

The house had changed very little.

That offended him more than it should have done. The same crack ran up the plaster beside the west stair. The same boy who had been too small to carry a tray properly was still too small, though somebody continued to make him do it. A woman in the passage told Elyas it was good to see him and immediately asked whether he had brought news of the northern army.

The people from Fairport occupied the rooms nearest the yard. He had passed a woman sleeping upright beside a sack of clothes and mistaken her for someone waiting to be received. Mara told him she had arrived two nights before and still woke whenever anyone moved the sack.

“Conlin?” he asked.

“Seen north of the port. We haven’t had a letter from him.”

“And the people with him?”

“Some were Tirenos. That is all we know.”

He had brought witnesses who could tell the court what had happened in the west. Here he could not discover which of his own people had escaped a town a few days downriver. He asked Mara who was keeping the accounts of those coming in, and went to read them.

Master Pellam came from Valora three days later to receive the western evidence.

During those three days Elyas went to the grave, wrote to Harald, wrote again to Maresse, and tried to discover what had happened to the company Corbel owed Seniro. He was told that all the matters were receiving attention.

Mara took him to the study on the second evening.

“I thought you ought to see it before somebody asks you about it.”

“About what?”

She opened the door.

The board stood where it had stood when he was a child. He had watched Braedyn and Riarhin play there, had understood perhaps one move in four, and had tried to look as though he understood the others. The dust lay lightly on the edge nearest the window.

“Constano left it like this when he went to Fairport,” Mara said.

“No.”

“Somebody will have to clean in here.”

“Not yet.”

“I didn’t mean you had to decide.”

He stood in the doorway. She waited beside him without putting a hand on his arm.

After a little while he said, “I thought he’d tell me whether I’d done it properly.”

“The journey?”

“All of it.”

“What would you have told him?”

He began to answer and could not find a place to begin.

Mara closed the door gently.

“Tell me some of it,” she said. “After supper. You can start where you like.”

At supper Mara sat where she could see the door. Elyas remembered that she had done so before he left, but he had never asked why. Now a servant came to her twice during the meal, once about a woman waiting at the side entrance and once about a jug which had not been returned from the sickroom. She answered quietly and continued eating after the servant went away.

“Are you still looking after the room?” he asked.

“People are still ill.”

“I meant his.”

“I know. No. The room at the back, where the men from the road have been put.”

She broke a piece of bread and pushed the rest towards him. The crust had been brushed with egg. A little yellow glaze shone in the fold where it had split during baking, the same bread he had eaten here as a boy. He had forgotten it could still taste of anything after everything else had changed.

“How long have you been doing that?”

“Since the first wagon came. There wasn’t room at the infirmary.”

“Who asked you?”

“Nobody. A man was being sick over a clean blanket, and I fetched a basin.”

She smiled at his expression.

“It improved from there.”

He wanted to ask whether she was being paid. He wanted to ask who had given her the authority to admit people, and then disliked himself for wanting to discover who had permitted work which she was plainly capable of doing.

“Will you show me?”

“After you eat.”

They went by the servants’ stair. The stone at the turn had been hollowed by generations of feet, and Mara stepped over the hollow without looking. Elyas, following her, nearly put his heel into it. She caught his wrist.

“You’ve been away too long.”

“Yes.”

She kept hold of him as they climbed.

The room at the back had two windows and beds that did not match. One was a narrow household bedstead, another a pallet raised on boards, a third made from a door balanced across low trestles. Fresh rushes lay beneath them. The smell was of vinegar, damp linen, and bodies kept indoors too long.

A man nearest the window was asleep with his mouth open. The woman beside him was mending a shirt. She rose when Elyas entered, then saw Mara’s hand and stopped halfway, uncertain whether she had been told to sit or merely to be quiet.

“Please stay,” Elyas said.

Mara went to the far bed. The man there had a swelling beneath one knee and had been trying to hide it from the physician because he wanted to return to work. He showed it to her reluctantly.

“You’ve walked on it.”

“To the window.”

“You’ve walked downstairs.”

“Only once.”

“Then tell the physician once. If he thinks the swelling came while you were lying still, he’ll try to cure the wrong thing.”

The man looked away. She adjusted the folded cloth beneath his calf, asked whether he had eaten, and gave him water.

Elyas stood where he would not block the light. He had watched officers do less useful work with considerably more ceremony.

In the passage afterwards he asked how she knew the man had gone downstairs.

“He had mud on his stocking. The corridor was swept this morning.”

“Do you notice everything?”

“No. I notice the things I’m expected to wash.”

He laughed. She leaned against the wall for a moment, looking tired now that no one needed her to do anything.

“Was it like that with him?”

She knew who he meant.

“Sometimes. He didn’t hide that he’d been walking. He told everyone the physician was opposed to exercise because he disliked being made unnecessary.”

“That sounds like him.”

“It did. That was what made the bad days difficult. He could sound exactly like himself and still not be well enough to stand.”

They went to her room. It was small, with a chest at the foot of the bed and a table beneath the window. A bowl held water she had drawn that morning. Beside it were two folded cloths, one clean and one waiting to be washed. She moved the used one before he could sit down.

He had begun the journey home imagining this room as a place where he would be received. Now he saw where she had lived while he was absent: the repaired latch, a new shelf, a book with slips of paper marking passages she wanted to find again. He asked about the book.

“The physician lent it.”

“Do you understand it?”

“Some. I ask about the rest.”

He turned it carefully. The illustrations were old and the language formal, but the margins contained notes in her hand, questions waiting to be answered.

“Tell me about the last week,” he said.

She took the book from him and set it down.

“Are you sure?”

“Yes.”

She did not begin with the end. She told him about the window, which Braedyn wanted open even when the room grew cold; about the dispute over whether broth counted as supper; about a morning when the old duke insisted that someone had taken a particular pen and sent three people looking for it before finding it beneath his own pillow.

Elyas smiled, then stopped smiling when she told him why the pen had been there. Braedyn had wanted to write without asking someone to sit him up. He had waited until the room emptied, tried, and failed.

“Did he ask for me?”

“Yes.”

“What did you say?”

“That we’d sent word. That you were coming home.”

He looked down at his hands.

“I didn’t know.”

“I know.”

“I thought there would be time.”

“So did I. I was here and thought it.”

She sat beside him. He had expected her to absolve him or accuse him, perhaps because he had spent so much of the journey supplying both sides himself. Instead she spoke about the hour before dawn when the house had become quiet and she had wanted, absurdly, to wake someone who knew what to do next.

“Constano came,” she said. “He was already awake. I don’t think he’d gone to bed.”

“Were you frightened?”

“Yes. Afterwards more than during.”

He took her hand. It was warm and smelled faintly of the soap from the sickroom.

“I should have asked you sooner.”

“You’ve only just come back.”

“Still.”

She let him keep the hand without answering. After a while she asked about Gyllan, and he told her as much as he could without making what had happened to him another account of Elyas’s own difficulties.

The lamp burned low. Someone passed the door carrying a tray, the cups making a small regular sound against one another. Mara rested her head against his shoulder. He put his arm around her and discovered that he had wanted to do that all through the room upstairs, while she was working and he had been unable to find a useful place for himself.

They kissed without haste. When she drew him closer, he stopped trying to think of the next thing he ought to say.

In the morning he went to the practice yard.

The straw at the foot of the training post had been replaced. A new cut crossed the old scar where Rekedim’s blade had once struck too deeply during a demonstration. Elyas remembered the movement better than the explanation. He set his feet and tried it with a wooden sword.

It was wrong. He knew before the wood struck.

He tried again.

“Your shoulder,” a voice said behind him.

Alric stood beneath the arch carrying the document case. He had not intended to instruct anyone; he looked almost annoyed to have spoken.

“What about it?”

“You raise it before you move. He used to tell you.”

“I remember.”

“Apparently not all the time.”

Elyas lowered the sword. “Will you show me?”

“No.”

“Afraid I’ll beat you?”

“Afraid you’ll expect me to keep doing it.”

He came into the yard anyway and set the case where it would remain dry. They practised slowly, with more pauses than strokes. Alric could identify the mistake without always being able to correct it himself. Twice they stopped to reconstruct something Rekedim had said. The third time Elyas laughed at the solemnity with which they were remembering a man who would have become impatient after the first repetition.

“He would tell us to stop talking.”

“He would have told you. I was always quiet.”

“That is not how I remember it.”

“No. Your memory favours you.”

They put the swords away before either was properly tired.

Alric picked up the case.

“Will the man coming from Valora know what these are?”

“He has been told.”

“That isn’t the same.”

“No.”

Alric ran a thumb along the edge of the fastening. He had repaired it on the road with a strip of different-coloured leather, and the new piece was already darkening from use.

“I want to go with them.”

“To Valora?”

“Until they’re taken properly. Until somebody knows which sheets can be opened without tearing.”

“Of course.”

“I don’t want a ceremony.”

“I wasn’t offering one.”

“People keep telling me we’ve done well. They haven’t looked at any of it.”

Elyas understood that better than he wanted to. He had returned with an achievement which required the dead to receive it, and everyone still alive seemed to have work of their own.

“I’ll stay for the taking of the evidence,” he said. “If you want me there.”

“Yes.”

It was the most direct request Alric had made since they reached the house. Elyas was glad he had not spoiled it by trying to give the answer more importance than it needed.

They went inside together. At the stair, a servant stopped Alric and asked whether the box belonged in the strongroom. He looked to Elyas before answering.

“Ask him,” Elyas told the servant. “He’s the person who knows.”

Pellam had brought two clerks and the authority to receive evidence for the crown. He wore an old grey coat whose cuffs had been turned twice. The seal on his commission had been protected with a folded scrap of cloth, stitched to the paper at one corner.

He read Elyas’s covering statement before looking at the books.

“Which of these men is Pell?”

They brought the clerk in.

Pell had been shaved that morning. The fresh skin above his collar looked painfully bare. He stood beside the table with his hands apart where the guard could see them.

“Do you understand what you are being asked to attest?” Pellam asked.

“Yes.”

“Tell me.”

Pell did. He began too quickly, caught himself, and began again. Pellam listened without interrupting.

When the guards took him out, Alric opened the case. Each bundle had been wrapped separately. There were sheets so damaged they had to be lifted on the cloth beneath them. Pellam asked before touching anything.

Lyra sat by the window. She had said almost nothing all morning.

At the second witness, she leaned forward.

“Where did he wait?”

Pellam looked at her.

“The witness. Before coming in.”

“In the passage.”

“With Pell?”

The clerk nearest the door looked up from his pen.

“They were both by the stair.”

Pellam sat back.

“What was said?” he asked the guard.

“Nothing about the evidence.”

“What was said?”

The guard hesitated.

Lyra looked at the witness, who was staring at his own hands. Elyas recognised a phrase the man had used: Pell had said it often on the road. Perhaps the witness had always used it too. He could not tell whether Lyra had noticed the phrase or something else, but Pellam was asking the guard to be exact.

Pellam sent the witness to another room and had the guard describe the conversation. Then he marked the account already taken and began a new sheet.

Afterward, while the clerks packed the books, he came to the window.

“Will you travel with these to Valora?”

“I was going west again.”

“When?”

She looked down at the staff against her chair.

“I haven’t arranged it.”

“I can use someone who notices where people wait.”

“Isn’t that the guard’s work?”

“It ought to be. Today it wasn’t.”

She looked at him properly then.

He waited for her answer, pen poised above the page.

“I need to write first,” she said.

“I have paper.”

Elyas told Mara he wanted to marry her while she was taking a stone out of her shoe.

They were on the path above the river. He had meant to wait until they reached the bench beneath the old beech, where he had worked out the words, but she had stopped and sat on the low wall and put her foot in his lap.

He took the shoe from her.

“There.” She pointed. “Under the lining.”

He worked the little stone free with his thumbnail. It was pale and sharp, no larger than a grain of barley.

“I want to marry you.”

She looked up.

He held the stone between finger and thumb.

“That wasn’t how I meant to say it.”

“How did you mean to say it?”

“Better.”

“Try this way first.”

He put the shoe down.

“I thought about it on the road. Before we came back. Before I knew about my father.”

“Why does that matter?”

“Because I don’t want you to think I’m asking because they’re dead.”

“Are you asking?”

“Yes.”

She drew her foot out of his lap and put it on the grass.

For a little while he heard only the river. A boat was coming downstream under a square sail, moving faster than it looked. The man at the stern had taken off his hat.

“Where would we live?” she asked.

“I don’t know yet.”

“What would I do?”

“Whatever you—”

She looked at him.

He stopped.

“What you chose,” he said more carefully. “If we could arrange it.”

“And when your brother says no?”

“He isn’t marrying you.”

“No. He’ll only decide where you’re allowed to bring me.”

“I won’t let him do that.”

“You haven’t asked him to do anything else yet.”

He looked at the shoe between them on the wall. There was a worn place at the heel where the stitching would soon need attention.

“I don’t want to keep coming home to you as though you belong to a room I used to have.”

Her face changed then. She took his hand, the one still holding the stone, and opened it.

“That is better,” she said.

“Is it an answer?”

“It is something I can answer. Let me think about it.”

He nodded.

She put the shoe on and stood. When they continued along the path, she kept hold of his hand.

Chapter Seventeen: A Man with an Agreement

The chancery’s morning light reached the upper shelves before it reached the desks. Lyra had learned to begin with the papers she could lift to the window and leave the large books until the sun moved. Nobody had told her to do this. On her first day she had lit a candle, and the woman at the next table had silently brought her a dish to catch the wax before it reached the writing.

Two weeks later, the dish was still beside her elbow.

The woman was called Nell. She copied faster than Lyra, spoke less while working, and knew which draught would lift an unsecured sheet before the door had fully opened. She had a husband employed by a tailor near the river and came to work with thread clinging to her cuffs, which she picked off while waiting for ink to dry.

“That’s the wrong witness,” she said one morning.

Lyra looked at the name. “It is the name on the covering paper.”

“Yes. Wrong covering paper.”

The two men had similar names and accounts from the same road. One had described a cart breaking near a bridge; the other had borrowed part of that description when asked where his own party stopped. Lyra had been comparing the two for an hour, growing increasingly certain she had found a deception more elaborate than anyone had suspected.

Nell put the correct sheet beside her hand.

“Pellam will want to know how they were put together.”

“I put them together.”

“Then he’ll know whom to ask.”

Lyra felt heat rise behind her ears. She had been useful at Tarlington. The usefulness had brought her here. It was unpleasant to discover how quickly she had begun depending on being the person who noticed mistakes made by other people.

She took both accounts to Pellam.

He listened, asked which parts she had already marked, and told her to correct the covering papers in a way that left the original arrangement visible.

“Not a clean copy?”

“Then the next person would wonder why your earlier note referred to something no longer there.”

She returned to the desk. Nell had shifted the candle out of the growing strip of sunlight.

“Does he always make you keep the mistakes?” Lyra asked.

“When somebody else may need to understand them.”

She spoke without consolation. Lyra found that easier to accept than kindness would have been.

At midday the clerks ate on a bench in the passage because the hearing room was occupied. The cold from the flagged floor travelled through the soles of Lyra’s boots. Above the bench, a painted border of gold leaves ran the length of the wall, far beyond anybody’s reach. She wondered who had chosen to spend money keeping the ceiling bright while the people below it ate with their coats on.

Nell gave her half an apple.

“You’re not used to this.”

“To apples?”

“To sitting still after you’ve worked all morning.”

Lyra looked down at her hands. She had been folding and unfolding the scrap around her bread.

“I used to have other things to do.”

“You still do.”

“Different things.”

Nell nodded towards Gyllan’s staff, propped in the corner where Lyra could see it from her desk. “Is that yours?”

“Yes.”

She had answered too quickly. Nell did not ask again.

A messenger came through carrying a roll wrapped in blue cloth. Two men who had been waiting outside the hearing room stood, followed him with their eyes, and sat again. One made an impatient gesture towards the door at the far end of the passage.

“Who’s in there?” Lyra asked.

“Someone expected.”

“Who?”

Nell finished her apple. “That is what people have been asking all morning.”

The boy had been told to say he was well treated.

Lyra knew this because he said it before anybody asked. He stood beside the long table in Master Pellam’s room with his cap pressed flat between his hands and a new coat over clothes that were not new at all. The coat smelled of lavender. His boots smelled of marsh water.

“I was well treated.”

Pellam put his pen down.

“What is your name?”

“Tomas, master.”

“Which Tomas?”

The boy gave his father’s name and the name of his parish.

“Sit down.”

“I can stand.”

“I can see that. Sit down anyway.”

He sat on the edge of the stool. Fayd remained by the door.

Lyra had recognised him in the passage before Pellam introduced them. He wore a brown coat she might have passed in any street, with good seams and no ornament. He looked at her once, remembered, and inclined his head.

“I’m glad you reached home.”

“This isn’t my home.”

“No,” he said. “Of course.”

He did not ask after Gyllan. She wondered who had told him.

Now Pellam asked the boy where he had been taken and what work he had done. Tomas had guided a company through the reed beds, then stayed because the officer needed him to find the way back. The officer had been wounded. Another man had taken command. By then the boy had lost his pass and nobody wanted responsibility for letting him go.

“Who found you?”

“The marshal’s men. They came and asked.”

“And Master Fayd?”

“They said I couldn’t go by Long Lea. Then he came.”

Fayd moved away from the door.

“The road captain had orders to stop unattached men of levy age. We obtained an exception.”

“How old are you?” Pellam asked Tomas.

“Fifteen.”

The clerk looked at Fayd.

“They begin young in the valley,” Fayd said.

Pellam asked the boy whether he was hungry. He was. Lyra took him downstairs to the kitchen, where Joan found a bowl and did not ask him to give his account again before filling it.

When Lyra returned, Fayd had gone.

“Is that what he brought?” she asked.

“The boy? Among other things.”

“What other things?”

“Permission for three grain wagons to pass Long Lea. Twelve carters released by Lisle. Confirmation of the names of two prisoners. An offer of a meeting.”

“With Corbel?”

Pellam looked at her.

“I read the covering letters,” she said.

“Yes. I asked you to.”

He moved the papers into a new pile.

“Do you think the boy was badly treated?”

“I don’t know.”

“You sounded as though you did.”

“He had been told what to say.”

“Perhaps he had. That doesn’t tell us whether it was true.”

She looked at the empty stool.

Gyllan would have said something like that. She disliked the thought so immediately that Pellam noticed.

“What?”

“Nothing.”

“Then find out what you can. Leave the rest unfilled.”

Ysolt Harrow found her in the gallery with the three wagon permissions.

“You’re the girl from the west.”

“Lyra.”

“Yes. I’m Ysolt. Move your elbow.”

Lyra moved it. Ysolt opened a little cupboard behind her and took out a jar of salve.

“Are you hurt?”

“Old. The distinction matters less every winter.”

She rubbed a little into the joint of her thumb and put the jar away.

“Who signed those?”

“Corbel’s steward.”

“Who receives them?”

“The road captain.”

“Who brought them?”

“Master Fayd.”

“Who will they ask next time?”

Lyra looked down at the papers.

Ysolt flexed her hand.

“He may have done a useful thing,” Lyra said.

“He has. The boy is eating, isn’t he?”

“Yes.”

“Then don’t deprive him of it because you don’t like the man who brought him. But keep the captain’s name.”

“Why?”

“Because next time somebody ought to be able to ask the captain.”

Ysolt went down the gallery toward the queen’s rooms. Lyra watched a servant open a door for her before she reached it. There had been no knock, no announcement, no question about whether she was expected.

She copied the captain’s name into the margin of the household’s working book.

Then she went to see Tomas again. This time she asked who had given him the coat.

“The man who kept me,” he said.

“Why?”

“He said I couldn’t go before the queen dressed like a ditch boy.”

“Did you see the queen?”

“No.”

He looked down at the sleeve, rubbing the good cloth between his fingers.

“Do I have to give it back?”

“No,” Lyra said, and then remembered what Pellam had told her. “I’ll ask.”

The next morning Pellam sent her to see two of the released carters.

“They are staying at the west yard until their families arrive. Take Nell. Ask what happened from the time they were stopped to the time they left.”

“Do you expect them to disagree with Fayd?”

“I expect them to know things he wasn’t there to see.”

They went after the first hearing. Valora’s streets were crowded with harvest traffic, wagons coming in with roots and wool, empty baskets going back towards the fields. A woman outside a baker’s shop was selling pieces of hot pastry too small to count as pies. Nell bought two and gave Lyra the larger without comment.

The west yard belonged to a carrier who rented stalls by the night. Its gateway was narrow, with stone posts scarred by wheels. One of the men they sought was mending a shoe beneath an awning. The other lay on a pallet in the loft, feverish enough to find the ladder an unreasonable obstacle between himself and the street.

They spoke to the man beneath the awning first. The nail of his left thumb had gone black. He braced the shoe against his knee to drive the awl through, but the damaged thumb would not hold the leather steady; each time it slipped he stopped, breathed through his nose, and began again. Lyra waited for him to finish the hole before giving her name.

He had been stopped because his employer owed a disputed charge. The wagon had been taken, then returned without its team. When he protested, he was kept with eleven others until the steward could decide whose promise should answer for whose cargo. He had been given food. He had also been struck when he tried to leave.

“By the captain?” Lyra asked.

“By one of the guards.”

“Did the captain know?”

“He was standing there.”

She wrote that down.

“And Master Fayd?”

“He came with a letter. Not the first day. Later.”

“What did he say?”

“To me? Asked where I wanted to go.”

“What did you tell him?”

“Home.”

The man returned to his shoe. He had told the account often enough to resent telling it again. Nell asked whether his family knew he had arrived. They did not; his village lay beyond a road still held against travellers.

“We can send word,” she said.

Lyra looked at her. Nell ignored the look and asked for the person’s name and a description of the house.

The second carter could give fewer details. He remembered the room where they had been kept, the bad water, the day someone brought clean straw. He had scarcely seen Fayd. He did remember that the men guarding them changed their manner after the letter arrived, and that one apologised for the inconvenience as though the preceding days had been caused by weather.

“Did you believe him?” Lyra asked.

The man looked at her from the pallet.

“I wanted him to stop talking and open the door.”

She could not improve on that answer.

Outside, Nell unfolded the note about the family.

“There is a woman in the linen room from that parish. Her brother brings hens to market. He can carry it.”

“You knew that when you offered?”

“I knew there would be someone to ask.”

“What if there wasn’t?”

“Then I would tell him I couldn’t do it.”

Lyra thought of the coat she had promised Tomas he could keep. There were promises made because one had the power to fulfil them and others because one intended to try. She was not yet good at telling people which kind she was making.

They reached the palace as the afternoon audience ended. Ysolt was in the gallery, speaking to a woman whose husband had been waiting all morning outside a supply officer’s room. The husband was no longer there.

“Where did he go?” Lyra asked when the woman had left.

“To Master Fayd.”

“Can he help?”

“Perhaps. Watch the door.”

Two petitioners went through while they stood there. One carried a bundle of papers. The other carried a child’s shoe, holding it as carefully as if it were a valuable object rather than a means of describing somebody missing.

“Should we stop them?” Lyra asked.

“On what grounds?”

“They may have to begin again.”

“They are already beginning again. The other officer sent them here.”

Ysolt adjusted the fastening at her throat. She was dressed for an audience, in cloth whose quality appeared only when one stood close enough to see the weaving. Her hands moved stiffly, but she refused Lyra’s offered help without looking offended by it.

“Count tomorrow,” she said. “Tell Pellam what kinds of requests they bring. Don’t tell him they’re all being deceived unless you know that they are.”

Lyra stayed in the gallery after the older woman went in.

Fayd came out a few minutes later with Jaennis. The dowager had asked him something about a man who wished his detained wagons treated as royal cargo. Fayd replied that the wagons had developed a remarkable loyalty after being stopped.

Jaennis laughed.

“And before?” she asked.

“Before, Majesty, they were private enough to evade the royal charge.”

“Then return them to the condition in which they began. Private wagons, private payment, no decoration from us.”

“That was my proposed answer.”

“How fortunate for the man that he waited long enough to have it twice.”

The gentlemen behind them smiled at the queen’s laughter. Lyra was not certain they had heard what was funny. Fayd had. He bowed slightly, and Jaennis turned towards the next petitioner as if the exchange had been no more than an efficient way to finish a piece of work.

Ysolt reappeared beside Lyra.

“She doesn’t laugh at people she hasn’t placed,” she said.

“Has she placed him?”

“Ask me after she has heard what you know of him.”

Lyra went back to Pellam’s room.

Her report on the carters took two pages. She removed three sentences which described what she thought the men must have felt and replaced them with what they had said. Then she added a shorter note about the petitioners: whose room they had left, what they carried, how long they waited outside Fayd’s door.

Pellam read both.

“You’ve made no recommendation.”

“I don’t know what should be done.”

“Then this is enough for today.”

The words should have relieved her. Instead she found herself thinking about the man with the child’s shoe, still waiting when she left the gallery. She asked whether she could go back and take his name.

Pellam gave her a clean sheet.

That evening, Nell’s acquaintance found a carrier for the message to the carter’s family. Lyra went to tell him before the west yard closed its gate. He was still mending the shoe. When she gave the news, he set it down and asked her to repeat the route the message would take.

She did, slowly enough that he could remember it.

Chapter Eighteen: A Squire and a Brother

At Harwick the yard had been swept since the duel. New sand filled the hollow beneath the stair, and the post against which Cedric had rested while the physician examined him had been painted. Elyas noticed both changes before he noticed the man waiting to receive him.

Edran came forward with his hand extended.

“My father is inside.”

“How is your brother?”

“Better.”

It was an answer with a boundary. Elyas accepted it.

They climbed the same stair they had used before. From the landing he could hear the river traffic, men calling between boats below the bluff. Aeryl had kept his wharf busy. Whatever else the war had done to the house, it had not made the family indifferent to the arrival of cargo.

The baron received him in a smaller room than the one used for their earlier meeting. There was no company to witness the greeting, only a steward at a side table and an older woman seated near the window. She rose when Elyas entered, with a gravity which made him bow before Aeryl introduced her.

“Berent’s aunt.”

“Your Highness.”

She had a sewing bag at her feet and a finger wrapped where a needle had gone too deep. Elyas liked her immediately and distrusted the ease of the judgment; people who loved a boy did not necessarily welcome the man who would take him into a war.

Aeryl offered him a chair.

“The request was made properly,” he said. “I have no objection to the boy’s advancement.”

“I am grateful.”

“You may be grateful when you’ve discovered whether he can keep a saddle dry. At present you have an opinion of him formed in a hall.”

“He kept his composure when others didn’t.”

“Yes. He had less at stake.”

Edran looked towards the window. Elyas let the remark pass. They would get nowhere by deciding who had behaved best at the last meeting.

The baron had set out the terms with more care than the boy’s employment seemed to require. Keep, clothing, training, responsibility for horses and equipment; the right of his aunt to receive word of an illness; the understanding that ordinary mistakes in learning would not be punished as dishonesty.

Elyas read that clause twice.

“Has there been a difficulty?”

“Not here,” the aunt said.

Aeryl did not look offended. “A boy hears what happens elsewhere.”

“So does an aunt,” she added.

Elyas laid the paper down.

“He will be taught before he is expected to know. If he is ill, you will be told. If he wishes to return, he may tell me.”

“And you will tell me,” she said.

“Yes.”

She nodded once. He had the impression that she had come expecting to be politely ignored and was now deciding whether this unexpectedly direct conversation could be trusted.

Aeryl signed the release. Edran witnessed it. The steward made the second copy while they spoke about the road, carefully avoiding every subject which might reopen the quarrel that had brought Elyas there before.

When they went out, Edran walked with him as far as the gate.

“He’ll ask you about the duel.”

“Berent?”

“He read the terms. He thinks that means he understood what happened.”

“What should I tell him?”

“The part he didn’t see.”

Elyas looked at the new sand beneath the stair.

“I don’t know if I understand all of that myself.”

“Then you have a useful beginning.”

He said it without reproach, which made it more difficult to dismiss. They shook hands again. Edran stood at the gate until Elyas had gone down the road towards the lodging house.

Berent’s aunt had packed too much.

Elyas knew because the boy was trying to carry all of it at once from the lodging house to the yard, and had the expression of someone determined that being unable to see over his baggage would not diminish the dignity of his arrival.

“Put it down,” Elyas said.

“I can manage, Highness.”

“I’d like to see you while we speak.”

The bundles went down. Berent straightened his jacket. The sash was gone, but the habit of correcting something on his shoulder remained.

“Baron Aeryl has given his leave.”

“So I see.”

Elyas held the letter. Aeryl had written that the boy was diligent, literate, and unlikely to disgrace a household if properly supervised. There was no mention of why the baron had become willing to part with him. Elyas suspected Mara knew.

“Do you want this?”

“Yes, Highness.”

“You may find it less pleasant than announcing visitors.”

“I didn’t find announcing visitors very pleasant.”

“You were good at it.”

“My aunt says that isn’t the same thing.”

“A formidable woman.”

“Yes, Highness.”

Elyas folded the letter.

“You will keep my kit, look to my horse with the grooms, learn the work you’re given, and tell me when you don’t know how to do it. Especially then.”

“Yes.”

“You’ll learn arms. You won’t be expected to know them before someone has taught you.”

Berent’s shoulders loosened a little.

“And if I give you two orders that cannot both be done, tell me.”

The boy considered that.

“In front of people?”

“If necessary.”

“My aunt will like you.”

“I am trying to earn her approval.”

Berent’s first ride in his service was not beside Elyas.

The prince had promised to send Maresse’s captain the amended assurance for the carrier’s mare. Pellam had prepared it before they left Valora, and Elyas had put it among the papers he meant to deliver himself. Now the route to Istovar would take them east of the landing, and a household carrier was going there with letters.

“You’ll go with him,” Elyas told the boy. “Give this to Hew. Ask whether the woman has returned. If she has, find out whether the mare passed freely.”

“And if it didn’t?”

“Bring me the answer.”

“Shouldn’t I have money?”

Elyas considered him. Berent had watched enough of their earlier journey to know that an assurance and a payment could produce different results.

He gave him a small purse, told him exactly what was in it, and said he might use it to release the mare if necessary. Otherwise he was to bring it back.

The boy repeated the instructions. He had polished his boots that morning and was trying not to look pleased at being sent on something that could be described as a mission.

They met again two evenings later at a posting house on the Istovar road.

Berent returned the paper unopened. Hew had been called away to a dispute farther down the river. The woman had passed through, according to the landing clerk, but the clerk could not say whether the mare had been with her. He had been more interested in telling them about Corbel’s burned post on the lower lane.

“I saw it,” Berent said. “On the way back.”

“What did you see?”

“Four black posts. The roof on the ground. People were taking nails out of it.”

“Did you speak to them?”

“One woman said she wished they’d burned it before she paid.”

He put the purse on the table. It was lighter by one copper.

“I bought bread.”

“All right.”

“I was supposed to bring it back.”

“You were supposed to eat too.”

“You didn’t say that.”

“No. I should have.”

Berent looked uncertain whether he had escaped a rebuke or failed some subtler examination. Elyas told him to sit and asked what the road had been like.
