The boy described a washed-out ditch, a team stuck behind a fallen tree, the woman taking nails from the hut. He had noticed more than Elyas expected and understood less than he thought.

“Was burning it lawful?” he asked at last.

“I wasn’t there.”

“Lord Seniro was.”

“Yes.”

“Would he have done it if it wasn’t?”

Elyas almost gave the easy answer, the one which would allow Berent to sleep knowing that men entrusted with command always understood what command permitted. Then he remembered Edran’s warning.

“He may have believed the need justified it. That doesn’t mean no one can question him afterwards.”

“Who can?”

“The people who have to live with it. The queen. His own brother, if the accounts make no sense. They will not all ask in the same way.”

“Could I ask you?”

“Yes.”

“About Harwick?”

Elyas put down his cup.

“Yes.”

Berent had remembered the duel exactly as a boy in a formal place might: the terms, the first blood, the fact that the argument had ended without anyone dying. He wanted to know how Elyas had known it would happen that way.

“I didn’t.”

“But you agreed.”

“I thought giving the anger a form would keep it from going farther. I was fortunate that it did.”

The boy frowned. “Baron Aeryl signed.”

“He did.”

“Then it was right.”

“The terms were kept. Whether I should have agreed before trying another way is a different question.”

Berent looked down at the purse. Elyas could almost see him attempting to arrange the two kinds of correctness beside each other.

“Did Mara think you should?”

“No.”

“Did you ask her?”

“No.”

The silence that followed was uncomfortable enough to be useful.

Elyas pushed the bread towards him.

“Keep asking,” he said. “You don’t have to find the whole answer tonight.”

They reached Charel under a low sky, with the outer yards full of wagons whose teams had already been taken elsewhere. Men were shifting the wagons by hand to clear an approach for visitors. Berent helped push one before the porter could tell him he was not expected to.

In the passage outside the business room hung Oswyn’s portrait.

Elyas had seen it before. He had been shorter then, and the light usually struck the painted eyes from above. Now he stood almost level with the face and understood why older people sometimes paused before greeting him. He recognised the angle of the head and the broad shoulders; even in the painting, Oswyn seemed to expect the person addressed to listen.

He looked at it and found it looking back.

A servant carrying towels stopped beside him.

“Would you like it moved to your room, Highness?”

“No.”

The answer came too sharply. The servant lowered his eyes.

“Thank you,” Elyas added. “It belongs here.”

Berent waited by the window, pretending to study the courtyard. Elyas was grateful for the pretence.

Harald received them at Charel in a room Elyas had never used as a child.

It had been his father’s business room. He had known the passage outside it very well, the little window at the end, the place where one could hear voices through the door without making out words. Inside, the walls were lined with pale wood. A clock stood on a shelf above the hearth and ticked far too loudly.

Harald rose when Elyas entered. They embraced awkwardly, each waiting for the other to decide how long to hold on.

“You should have written that you were coming.”

“I did.”

“When?”

“From Tarlington.”

“It hasn’t come.”

Elyas took off his gloves.

Harald looked past him at Berent.

“My squire.”

“Whose boy?”

“Ailsean. He served at Harwick.”

“Have we paid for him?”

“No.”

“I only meant—”

“I know.”

Harald asked a servant to show Berent where the horses were being kept. When the door closed, the brothers stood facing one another with the table between them.

“He asked after you,” Harald said.

Elyas looked down at his gloves.

“When?”

“Toward the end. Several times.”

“What did you tell him?”

“That we had no new letter.”

Harald said it gently, and Elyas knew that he had intended no accusation. He sat before his brother could say anything else.

They spoke about the lower passage, the garrisons, and the levy. Harald brought out papers already read so often that their corners had gone soft. Each answer led to another thing that needed to be established first.

“Corbel is refusing service,” Elyas said.

“Corbel doesn’t owe me service.”

“He owes Seniro. The grain road comes through land you can protect.”

“For how long?”

“Long enough to get the convoy through.”

“And after that?”

“We decide after that.”

Harald looked unhappy.

“That is precisely what Father disliked.”

“Father decided things before he knew everything.”

“Father had been doing it for fifty years.”

“And how will you begin?”

The clock ticked.

Harald gathered the papers into a neater pile. He had always done that when they argued, even as a boy: put something straight that had not caused the disagreement.

“I have children,” he said.

“I know.”

“I cannot put this place at hazard because the Fenwoods have committed themselves beyond what they can support.”

“They raised me.”

“They held you.”

Elyas stood.

Harald closed his eyes for a moment.

“That was badly said.”

“Yes.”

“I meant that our father had obligations too.”

“Yes.”

Neither moved.

At last Harald said he would consider an escort if Elyas sent the precise route and the number of days required. Elyas told him those had been in the letter that had not arrived. Harald asked him to write them again.

He did.

At supper Harald’s eldest daughter asked Elyas whether he had killed a man in the west.

The question emptied the table of conversation. Her younger brother stopped trying to break a walnut with the handle of his knife. Harald told her that was not a thing to ask over food.

“I didn’t ask how,” she said.

“Enough.”

Elyas put down his cup. Across from him Berent had become still, waiting to learn which part of the journey could be told in this house.

“Yes,” Elyas said.

The girl looked at him with disappointment rather than excitement. She had perhaps expected a denial which would permit her to ask another question.

“Did you know him?”

“Not well.”

“Why did you do it?”

Harald began to speak. Elyas raised a hand before he could stop her again.

“We were fighting. I wanted to live.”

She considered that. “Were you afraid?”

“Yes.”

The younger child looked up from the walnut. “Berent says you won a duel.”

“That was another occasion.”

“Were you afraid then?”

“Yes.”

“But you won.”

Elyas looked at Berent, whose ears had gone red. The boy had given the children the account he knew, and it had become something they wished their uncle to perform again at the table.

“I had good help before it began,” Elyas said. “And I was fortunate.”

The girl returned to her food. Her brother asked whether the walnut could be opened by somebody stronger. Harald took it from him, wrapped it in a cloth, and cracked it with the flat side of a small serving weight. The nut inside was whole. He divided it between the children with the practised care of someone who knew which would complain about the smaller piece.

Elyas watched his hands. He had spent the afternoon seeing only his brother’s inability to give an order. Here were two people for whom Harald had been answering questions every day while Elyas was elsewhere.

After supper the girl showed him a painted box their grandfather had given her. One hinge had pulled loose. She wanted to know whether the people who repaired armour could repair this as well.

“Probably,” Elyas said.

“Father says the house carpenter will do it.”

“Then he should.”

“He keeps being sent to work on the wagons.”

She opened and closed the lid carefully. Inside lay a child’s collection of small, important things: a ribbon, a smooth pebble, two buttons which did not match. There was room for more. Oswyn had chosen something she could continue filling after he was gone, without intending anything of the kind.

Elyas asked whether he might keep it until morning. She looked at him uncertainly.

“I won’t take anything out.”

“I know. I don’t want it mended differently.”

“I’ll show the carpenter. You can tell him what must stay the same.”

That was acceptable. She put the box on a side table and went to find her brother.

Harald had remained near the fire. He watched the children leave before speaking.

“She thinks everything can be repaired if she finds the right person.”

“Most of her things probably can.”

“For now.”

Elyas looked towards him. The firelight made his brother’s face older than it had looked at dinner. He had begun to resemble Oswyn in repose, despite all the people who insisted Elyas had inherited the likeness.

“I won’t leave your house undefended,” Elyas said.

“You don’t command it.”

“I meant I won’t ask you to.”

Harald nodded. It was not agreement about the escort. For the moment, neither tried to turn it into one.

They stood together until a servant came to ask whether the children might be put to bed. Harald said yes, then went himself when the younger one called from the stair. Elyas took the box to the carpenter’s room. The man examined the hinge, said it required two small pins, and asked whether it could wait until the wagon axle was secure.

“It can,” Elyas said.

Outside, Berent was waiting with his cloak.

“I didn’t mean to boast about the duel.”

“You were telling them what you saw.”

“I left out the bad parts.”

“Which?”

“The waiting. How you couldn’t know what he would do.”

Elyas put on the cloak. “Next time tell those as well.”

“They won’t want to hear them.”

“They may need to.”

The boy looked back towards the house. One of the children had appeared at an upper window in a nightshirt, watching them cross the court. Berent lifted a hand and received an answering wave.

Elyas let him finish waving before they went to the stable. At the door he told the groom where the little box would be in the morning, so it would not be lost among the work required for the road.

That night Berent could not get a buckle to hold.

The tongue kept slipping beneath the leather because the hole had stretched. He was trying to repair it with a piece of wire found in the stable. Elyas sat beside him and watched the wire bend.

“That won’t last.”

“No, Highness.”

“Then why keep doing it?”

“It might last until morning.”

Elyas looked at him.

The boy looked back, uncertain what he had done wrong.

“Good,” Elyas said. “Make it last until morning. We’ll find leather then.”

He went upstairs to write the route for his brother a third time, because the second version had used the name of a crossing Harald knew by another name, and no order could be issued until they were certain they meant the same place.

Chapter Nineteen: The Inconvenient Acquaintance

Elyas arrived at Valora while Fayd was waiting to be thanked for another passage opened through the valley.

Lyra saw them recognise one another across the outer chamber. Fayd rose. Elyas stopped just inside the door, one glove half drawn from his hand.

“Highness.”

“Master Fayd.”

Neither seemed surprised enough for the people watching them.

Jaennis came from the inner room before either spoke again. She kissed Elyas on both cheeks, asked whether he had eaten, and took his arm.

“You know one another.”

“Yes,” Elyas said.

“Good. That saves an introduction.”

“It may require another kind.”

Jaennis looked at him. The smile remained, but its use changed.

“Come inside.”

Lyra was there because Pellam had asked her to bring the account of the western evidence. She had expected to put it on the table and leave. Jaennis asked her to remain.

Elyas described the bargain at Pont-de-Tithe. He did not improve their reasons for making it. Fayd had known where Orvain was. They had needed the information. In exchange they had obtained the account book that allowed him to remove his superior.

“Within the Vigil,” Jaennis said.

“Yes.”

She turned to Fayd.

“Is that accurate?”

“Substantially.”

“Which part isn’t?”

“The book did not remove him by itself. Other people had to agree that it warranted removal.”

“Did they?”

“Yes.”

Elyas leaned forward.

“You understand what they want.”

“I understand several things they want,” Jaennis said. “One of which is now a hearing with me.”

Fayd had not sat down. Lyra watched him while the other two spoke. He seemed prepared to remain standing indefinitely. There was a frayed place at the edge of his cuff that he had turned inward.

“Do you still serve them?” Jaennis asked.

“I have not forgotten the people who trust me.”

“That isn’t an answer.”

“It is the part of the answer useful to Your Majesty. If I had abandoned my contacts, I could offer you a very clean allegiance and nothing to do with it.”

“You could offer loyalty,” Elyas said.

“To which of the arrangements presently in dispute?”

“To the queen.”

Fayd looked at him.

“Which one?”

The room went still.

Jaennis laughed softly.

“Be careful, Master Fayd. I enjoy precision until it becomes expensive.”

He bowed his head.

“I am offering three things within my power. Passage for the grain. Return of the named prisoners. A meeting with men who have so far refused the crown’s officers. I have done the first. The second can be checked. The third requires authority I do not possess.”

“And afterward?”

“Afterward you will know whether I can be useful a fourth time.”

Jaennis gave him authority to arrange the meeting, not to make terms.

Pellam wrote the distinction down. Fayd read it and asked for one alteration: safe conduct should extend to the barons’ messengers as well as the barons themselves. Jaennis agreed. Elyas asked that the meeting’s record be kept by someone from the chancery. Fayd agreed to that too.

When he had gone, Elyas remained standing beside the table.

“You know what he is.”

“I know more than I did before you came in.”

“And you’re sending him back to them.”

“Yes. Because they will receive him.”

Lyra thought of the boy in the new coat. Of the captain’s name she had copied into the margin. She had wanted this conversation to make something simpler. Instead, every fact she knew had been admitted and the decision had still been made.

In the passage, Ysolt was waiting for the queen.

“What did you learn?” she asked Lyra after Elyas had gone.

“That she knows.”

“Yes.”

“That he knows she knows.”

“Usually the people in these rooms do.”

“Then why does it feel as though something has been missed?”

Ysolt looked toward the closed door.

“Because knowing what a man has done doesn’t tell you what you’ll be willing to let him do next.”

Lyra took the record back to Pellam. He had left a space for the authority granted and a separate space for the terms expressly withheld.

She filled both.

Pellam disagreed with her about one phrase.

“He said the Vigil’s contacts,” he told her.

“He said his contacts.”

“Are you certain?”

Lyra looked at what she had written. She could remember Fayd standing, the cuff turned inward, Elyas leaning forward when he spoke of loyalty. The exact arrangement of words was less secure. She had heard the distinction and believed it mattered. Belief was not a means of hearing it again.

“Certain enough to ask the secretary who was taking it down.”

Pellam sent for the rough notes.

The secretary came with sand still clinging to one page. He had abbreviated most names and left gaps where two people spoke together. At the relevant line he had written my. Lyra felt a small, unworthy relief at being right and tried not to let it enter her voice.

Pellam corrected his version.

“He is claiming something,” she said.

“Yes.”

“That they belong to him.”

“Or that he can answer for the people he brings. We need the next thing he does before deciding which part of that was important.”

The secretary collected his notes. Lyra watched him go, carrying the page on which their certainty depended. She had imagined, before coming to Valora, that official accounts existed more solidly than speech. Now she knew how often they began as a man trying to keep up while someone else interrupted.

Elyas found her in the passage before he left.

“Did I make that worse?” he asked.

“Which part?”

“Her willingness to hear him.”

“I think she’d already decided to.”

“Yes.”

He looked towards the outer chamber, where Fayd had been received with the men waiting to thank him. Berent stood beside the door holding Elyas’s gloves. The boy was watching the people pass through with the effort of someone trying to learn which departures mattered.

“Don’t turn everything he does into proof,” Elyas said.

“Proof of what?”

“Whatever you’re most afraid he’ll do. I kept doing that on the road. Sometimes I was right. Sometimes I stopped looking.”

She was surprised by the admission. He seemed to regret making it only because he had no further answer to give her.

“What will you do?” she asked.

“Go where I can be useful. Ask him what he promises when he promises it. Try to remember he has kept a promise to us already.”

“And the Vigil?”

“I’ll remember that too.”

He took the gloves from Berent. Before putting them on, he asked whether she needed anything sent from Tarlington.

For a moment she could think only of things which could not be sent. Then she remembered the warmer cloak left in her room.

“I’ll ask Mara to find it,” he said.

“I can write.”

“So can I.”

He smiled, and for a moment they were simply people who had travelled together and knew what the other had forgotten to pack.

The revised safe-conduct was ready the following afternoon.

Jaennis asked for it to be brought to her smaller chamber rather than the formal audience room. Lyra carried the chancery copy and sat at the side table, where the light fell directly across the page. Two women had been speaking with the queen when she entered. One was Margery Ashcombe, whose gown she recognised before her face. The other was Catrin, carrying a bundle of letters tied with green thread.

They did not hurry to leave when Fayd was announced. Jaennis finished the matter with them first.

“Not through the main gate,” she told Catrin. “Use the service entrance by the old kitchens. The officer there has been given the names.”

“And if he isn’t there?”

“Ask for Joan.”

Catrin nodded. Margery put a hand briefly on her arm as they left, a movement too familiar to be merely assistance with the door. Lyra looked down before either could feel observed.

Fayd signed for the instrument. He read the limits, asked whether Maresse’s captain could bring a clerk of his own, and accepted the requirement that both versions of the meeting be compared before either was sent onward.

“Easier while they’re in the same room,” Jaennis said.

“Most disagreements are.”

“Not the interesting ones.”

He smiled and folded the document.

Lyra thought the business finished. She had begun ruling the line beneath the entry when Fayd thanked Jaennis for the supper held two nights earlier in the blue dressing room.

Her pen stopped.

It was a small private supper, she knew, because Nell had mentioned the kitchen’s confusion over how many places had actually been required. No formal list of guests had been made.

Jaennis rested her hand on the arm of her chair.

“You weren’t there.”

“No.”

“Then your thanks are unusually generous.”

“One of the women you received has a sister whose husband was among the men released. She wanted someone to know that she had been treated kindly here.”

“That is an innocent account of being informed.”

“It is the account I was given.”

“By whom?”

Fayd named a person Lyra did not know. Jaennis did.

A brief silence followed, during which the clock in the next room struck the quarter. Lyra kept her eyes on the paper and understood that lowering one’s gaze did not make the ears disappear.

“You could have let me believe nobody noticed,” Jaennis said.

“I would be a poor servant not to know which households look to you. A worse one if I made their trust an embarrassment.”

“Yet you have told me.”

“So that you needn’t discover later that I knew.”

Jaennis considered him. The interval was long enough that Lyra wondered whether she should ask to leave, and short enough that doing so would announce a concern neither of the others had acknowledged.

Then the queen said, “Your courier is spending too freely at the east gate.”

Fayd’s expression did not alter.

“Which courier?”

“The man with the grey gloves. He bought his wife a length of cloth she had been pricing for two months. I was pleased for her until I learned who had begun paying him.”

“Who?”

“A factor who has acted for Merrow.”

Lyra put the pen down very carefully.

“What does he buy?” Fayd asked.

“Copies. Not everything, I think. Enough to know whom you are meeting and what you expect to obtain.”

“Have you spoken to the courier?”

“No.”

“May I ask why?”

“Because then he would stop taking the money before we knew how often it came. And because he is your man, which you have lately been at pains to make clear.”

Fayd looked at her. For the first time in the exchange, Lyra could not mistake his attention for ordinary courtesy.

“Thank you,” he said.

“Those thanks I will accept.”

She gave him the name of the shop where the cloth had been bought and the day on which payment changed hands. He repeated neither aloud. Lyra could see him fixing the details in memory; she could not see what he intended to do with them.

“The factor may have other employers,” he said.

“Then you have something to find out.”

“I do.”

Jaennis rose. The meeting was over because she had made it so.

Fayd bowed and went out without looking towards Lyra’s table. She remained seated until the queen asked whether the entry was complete.

“The safe-conduct, Majesty? Yes.”

“Good. Take it to Master Pellam.”

Lyra gathered the papers. At the door she nearly asked whether to include the conversation about the courier. Jaennis looked up, waiting. Lyra wished her a good day and went out.

She took the question to Pellam instead.

He heard her in a room with the door closed. She gave the words she remembered, identified the places where she was summarising, and said when she had stopped writing.

“Should I have continued?”

“Would either of them have stopped you?”

“I don’t know.”

“Then we cannot answer the question that way.”

He gave her a separate sheet.

“Write what concerns the courier. Keep it distinct from the formal meeting. If there’s an examination later, you’ll be asked what you heard, not what you thought it meant.”

“And the supper?”

“Did anyone allege wrongdoing?”

“No.”

“Then don’t invent an allegation by attaching private hospitality to an account of sold information.”

She wrote the courier’s name and the shop. She did not write a conclusion about Fayd or Jaennis. The pleasure they had taken in understanding one another had been as visible as their caution, but she had no place to enter it which would make it useful evidence rather than her own discomfort.

Two days later the courier ceased appearing at Fayd’s door.

Nobody announced a dismissal. His wife came to the palace with a message and was received by Tarr, the clerk who had begun managing Fayd’s growing correspondence. When she left, she still carried the new cloth folded beneath her arm.

Lyra saw her from the gallery and looked away. She could not know whether the woman had known where the money came from. It was tempting, after being trusted with a secret, to believe every detail near it had become legible.

Tarr sent her a request that afternoon for the correct spelling of a village named differently in two reports. He had attached both versions and left the uncertainty marked rather than choosing the one written in the better hand.

She went to Nell, who knew a carrier from that district.

While she waited for the answer, three more petitioners arrived outside Fayd’s room. One had originally come to ask about a missing soldier. Another wanted to know whether a baron’s assurance of passage could be relied upon. The third had a matter which ordinarily belonged to the supply office, but the officer there had sent him to the person who could obtain an answer from the lord withholding the carts.

Lyra wrote the three kinds of request in her own book. Then she found the correct village name and took it to Tarr.

He thanked her, corrected both reports, and asked whether she would like to see how he kept them together.

She said yes.

Chapter Twenty: Whose People

By the time Elyas reached Maresse’s landing, the people waiting to cross had eaten the hedge.

Not all of it. The blackthorn remained, with garments spread over it to dry. But the nettles beneath had been cut, and someone had taken the tender leaves from the hedge mustard. A pot stood over a small fire. The smell of boiled greens came to him above the river mud.

Hew was at the barrier, wearing the same boots. Since the sack he had brought most of his watch across to the eastern landing; the western shore had become too uncertain for more than a small guard.

“Your Highness.”

“You remember me.”

“I’ve been hoping you remembered us.”

Elyas dismounted. Berent took his reins without being asked, then discovered that two horses did not necessarily wish to stand on the same side of a boy. He worked them round. Elyas left him to it.

Jaennis’s letter was inside his coat. It directed officers and servants of the crown to assist the passage of people displaced from Fairport and Marcott. Pellam had been exact about what it did not do: it gave Elyas no command over Maresse’s household, no right to seize food, and no money beyond the purse he carried. Jaennis had offered him more money. He had asked for two wagons instead.

Those were a day behind him now, coming by the better road.

Hew read the letter slowly. Behind him a woman was arguing with a ferryman about a stool.

“We’re not taking goods,” Hew said. “People first.”

“What have you been charging them?”

“Nothing since yesterday.”

Elyas looked at him.

“They haven’t got anything. And the boatmen won’t work for nothing, so I’ve been paying them.”

“Maresse’s money?”

“My company’s pay. I’ll make that plain when his lordship asks where it went.”

The woman at the ferry lifted her skirt above her ankle. Her leg was wrapped in dirty linen from heel to knee. The stool was how she moved when she could not stand. Hew closed his eyes briefly, then called to the ferryman to take it.

“How many?” Elyas asked.

“Crossed? Waiting? Died here?”

“Waiting.”

“I don’t know anymore.”

The road beyond the landing climbed towards the uplands. Elyas could see a second barrier there, this one built from a farm cart. Maresse’s men stood beside it with spears. The refugees had come up the river’s eastern bank. Some wanted boats to relations on the western shore; most wanted the northern road, away from the smoke. Both routes passed beneath Hew’s watch.

“Why are those people stopped?”

“Long Lea won’t admit them without a receiving household. My lord won’t have them wandering on the estate. We sent two hundred north yesterday. Half came back.”

“Then open the road and let me deal with Corbel.”

“And where do they sleep tonight?”

He had been ready to be angry with the captain. It was less satisfying to discover that the man had already asked the questions he was arriving to ask.

A child crept under the farm cart. A soldier caught the back of his shirt and returned him, gently enough, to a woman sitting on a sack. The child began to cry with an exhausted, open-mouthed effort that produced almost no sound.

Elyas took Hew aside.

“I have two wagons coming. Meal, blankets, salt. The meadow above the bend—whose is it?”

“Maresse’s tenant. It’s cut already.”

“Fetch him. I’ll pay for the use of it until the wagons reach us. Two nights after that, if necessary. I want water carried up, and a place downhill of the camp for people to relieve themselves. Away from the stream.”

“Three nights won’t mend the valley.”

“No. But I can pay for three nights.”

Hew folded the letter along its original creases and handed it back.

“Put your name on the hiring.”

“I intended to.”

“I know. Put it where the tenant can see it.”

They found him in the crowd, furious because people had been cutting stakes out of his young willows. His name was Restin. Elyas let him finish describing the damage before making the offer. Restin wanted compensation for all the willows, including some apparently cut the previous winter. Hew disputed those. They argued for several minutes over a sum smaller than the price of Elyas’s gloves.

Berent stood beside them, holding the horses and listening.

“Put them with the captain’s mounts,” Elyas told him. “Then count the households as they go into the meadow. Not just the people. Find out who’s missing someone.”

“What shall I do with the names?”

“Write them down.”

“And then?”

Elyas looked at the people by the barrier. Several were watching the boy.

“Then we may be able to tell someone where they are. Don’t promise more than that.”

By evening the pot had something in it besides leaves. A farmer had brought peas for sale and accepted a written undertaking for half the price. Restin stood over the cook to make certain that his household’s share was measured before the rest went into the water.

The mare Elyas remembered was tethered near the fire.

He recognised the white scar above her eye before he recognised her owner. The woman had a different cloak, too large for her, with a burned hole in the shoulder.

“You got her back,” he said.

“For a while.”

The mare’s flanks were hollow. No foal walked beside her.

“I told them you said.”

“Did they listen?”

“Him.” She nodded at Hew. “The others said you hadn’t said it to them.”

“I should have written it.”

“Yes.”

She had no use for an apology. He could see that, and gave it anyway. Then he asked her name and wrote it, and the mare’s markings, on a separate paper. Hew witnessed it. She folded the paper and put it away for the journey north.

Berent brought his first list after dark. Halfway down the page was an empty space.

“Why that?”

“She couldn’t say her husband’s name. She kept trying.”

“Leave room, then.”

The boy sat down on the ground beside Elyas’s chair and continued copying. His hand was cramped; he shook it between entries. Beyond the lamplight, Hew was dividing the last of the peas among his men.

The wagons arrived the next day with Mara beside the leading driver.

By then the meadow had become a small settlement without any of the things which made a settlement bearable. Smoke from the cooking fires lay close to the ground. Wet blankets hung between poles cut from Restin’s hedge. Somebody had dug a channel to carry rain away from a row of sleepers and directed it into the place where the next row kept their bundles.

Elyas saw the wagons before he saw who rode on them. He had been expecting meal and blankets. The sight of Mara made him forget, briefly, what he had intended to do with either.

She climbed down while the driver was still speaking to Hew.

“Where are they unloading?”

He kissed her before answering. She held him for a moment, then looked past his shoulder at the meadow.

“Not there,” she said. “You’ll block the path to the water.”

He had been about to point there. He changed the direction of his hand.

“You’ve brought help.”

“Nessa. Two people from the household. A woman who knows more about cooking for a crowd than I do.”

Nessa stood beside the second wagon, looking at the fires. Elyas recognised the mark on her hand before he remembered her name. She had washed Braedyn’s body. Mara had told him that on the evening in her room.

“She wanted to come?” he asked quietly.

“She said there was work.”

Nessa came over and asked where the sick were being kept. When he said they had no separate place, she looked at the meadow again and chose one. A little higher, sheltered by a bank, within reach of water without putting the people who needed carrying in the path of every bucket.

They spent the morning moving shelters.

Mara took names differently from Berent. He had been asking each household for the person who could answer for it and writing that name first. She asked who was travelling together, who needed to stay near someone else, and whether anybody among them knew a household farther north.

The answers did not always follow kinship. A woman refused to leave the old neighbour whose daughter had gone missing. Two apprentices wanted to remain with a master they complained about continuously. A boy called a boatman his uncle until the man explained that they had met on the quay and he had not known what else to tell the people refusing to let an unattached child through.

Berent came to Elyas with the last case.

“Should I cross out uncle?”

“Ask them what they want written.”

“The boy wants uncle.”

“And the man?”

“He said it would do until they found his mother.”

“Then write that.”

The boy looked relieved. He had expected to be told that accuracy required taking something useful away from somebody.

At midday Maresse’s steward arrived with orders about the camp.

His name was Etien. He had a good horse and a practical coat, stained below the knee from inspecting fields. He greeted Elyas correctly, acknowledged the queen’s letter, and asked when the meadow would be cleared.

“When there is somewhere for them to go.”

“There are places beyond Long Lea.”

“Which have refused them.”

“Then someone must prevail upon those places. This ground belongs to a tenant with obligations of his own.”

“Restin is being paid.”

“For damage already done. The longer people remain, the more there will be.”

Mara stood close enough to hear, holding a bowl from which a little girl had just finished eating. Etien glanced at her once and continued addressing Elyas.

“My lord has agreed to receive a crown officer. He has not undertaken to maintain another parish.”

“No one says he has.”

“His people are beginning to say it. Their seed is here. Their animals winter on the hay being asked of them. A lord has a duty to the people placed under him before he makes promises to strangers.”

He said it sincerely, not as a convenient phrase borrowed for the occasion. Elyas had heard Oswyn speak in the same terms about households whose names he could recite without looking at a page. That duty had meant something. It still did.

Mara set the bowl down.

“How many farms have a spare hearth-room?” she asked.

Etien looked at her, then at Elyas.

“She asked you,” Elyas said.

The steward answered reluctantly. Some had rooms, some had outbuildings which could shelter people until the cold grew worse. None could feed additional mouths indefinitely.

“They won’t have to,” Mara said. “Tarlington sends grain. Not a cart into the meadow for everyone to quarrel over. A quantity to each household for the people it takes.”

“Tarlington has agreed?”

She looked at Elyas. He understood that the answer had just reached the person who would have to give it.

“I will answer for the request,” he said.

“That isn’t an agreement.”

“No. It is my word that I will obtain one or tell the receiving households why I have failed before their stores are eaten.”

Etien shook his head. “You ask them to begin on that.”

“I ask them to begin with the grain already here. Then with the next load if it arrives. No one is being promised a winter’s food out of a wagon which hasn’t left.”

Mara watched him. He had almost given the larger promise. She knew it, and the steadiness of her attention helped him stop where his authority stopped.

Etien asked for the quantities. Nessa supplied them more readily than either Elyas or Mara. She had supervised the packing and knew how much had been opened since morning.

They began with six farms.

Berent carried the first list with one of Maresse’s men.

Elyas went with the second party, leaving Mara to arrange the sick and the families waiting for places. The road rose through fields where the stubble had been grazed down to pale points. At each gate the soldier called the tenant’s name before entering. Some came willingly. Others waited until they could see how many people stood behind the horsemen.

At the third farm, a woman blocked the door.

“My daughter sleeps there.”

“She can share,” the soldier said.

Elyas raised a hand before the woman answered.

“How old is your daughter?”

“Fourteen.”

“Then no man will be put in her room.”

The soldier shifted in his saddle. “We have to put them somewhere.”

“We do. This isn’t the place for that household.”

They exchanged the proposed lodging for a woman with two small children. The farmer’s wife was still displeased, but no longer frightened in the same way. She asked whether the children were ill. Elyas said they had been seen by Nessa and appeared well; he would not promise more than anyone knew.

The next farm accepted an old cooper because the owner had three barrels needing repair. The cooper objected to being bargained into work before he had slept. They settled on lodging first and discussion of the barrels later. Elyas found himself smiling at the argument, then remembered the circumstances which made it possible and let the smile go.

At the last house, a man asked who would bear the responsibility if one of the newcomers stole.

“The person who steals,” Elyas said.

“And if I can’t prove it?”

“Then you have an accusation to be heard, as you would against your neighbour.”

“They aren’t my neighbours.”

“They’ll be sleeping here.”

The man considered that for a long time. Then he moved a stool from the doorway and let the family enter.

On the way back, the soldier who had accompanied Elyas said his lord would dislike the arrangement.

“Will he dislike the grain?”

“No.”

“Then begin with that when you tell him.”

The man almost smiled. “That’s what Master Etien said.”

Mara had not eaten when Elyas returned.

She was sitting beside a woman whose baby would not settle, holding the child while its mother drank broth. Nessa stood behind her, correcting the way she supported the little head. Mara adjusted her arm without protesting.

He brought bread and cheese and put them where she could reach them.

“Six farms,” he said.

“How many people?”

He told her. Fewer than he had hoped. More than would have gone if he had remained in the meadow arguing about a baron’s duty.

“Berent got four,” she said. “One wants the grain delivered before the family comes. I said that was fair.”

“Did you have enough to send?”

“Yes. I sent it.”

He nodded.

The baby had fallen asleep. Mara returned it gently, waited until the mother had settled its weight, and came to sit beside him on an empty sack.

“You promised Tarlington grain,” she said.

“So did you.”

“I promised what was on the wagons.”

He looked at the camp. The difference was larger than he liked.

“I’ll write tonight.”

“To whom?”

“The household steward. Seniro, so he knows what I’ve undertaken.”

“He may want it for the army.”

“He will.”

She tore the bread and gave him half.

“Tell him what it keeps off his roads,” she said. “Not just what it feeds.”

He wrote after dark, with Berent asleep nearby and Mara reading the household lists by the same lamp. He named what he had promised, the amount needed for the next deliveries, and the date on which the food already sent would be gone.

At the bottom he put his own name.

The answer, when it came, would have to find him among the people he had committed it to feeding.

Part IV: Who Can Give an Answer

Chapter Twenty-One: The Promised Marriage

Mara came back from the northern farms beside the driver of an empty grain wagon. The meadow had begun to show strips of bare ground where the tents had stood. Under the willows, leaves lay in the wheel ruts and stuck to the wet spokes.

Elyas was holding a man by the collar to prevent him striking another man with a ladle.

“Put it down,” he said.

“He took twice.”

“He took for his mother. Put it down.”

The man dropped the ladle into the pot. Elyas released him and turned towards the wagon.

Mara had already begun unloading.

“Did they take the families?” he asked.

“Four. The last two stay here until there’s room.”

She handed him a basket. Inside were jars wrapped in straw, a roll of clean cloth, and a pair of his boots.

“Oria sent those. The soles are mended.”

He kissed her. She accepted the kiss, then took the basket back before he tipped it.

“The brewer’s widow has given us horse rugs,” she said. “Good ones. We can cut the damaged edges away.”

“Somebody will object to sleeping under them.”

“Then you can set an example.”

They worked until evening. The first households had begun moving north under guarantees from villages beyond Long Lea. Nobody could tell Elyas who had obtained all the guarantees. Two came through Fayd’s messengers, one through a priest, and another through a woman who had walked ahead to her sister’s house and returned with her brother-in-law and his cart.

Mara watched Berent help an old man spell a place-name. Later she asked whether the boy had eaten.

“I told him to.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

Elyas found him beside the horse lines, asleep with an untouched bowl in his lap. He woke him before the flies finished his supper.

When he returned, Mara was sitting on the riverbank. She had taken her shoes off. He sat beside her, leaving his on because he was not certain he could get them back over his blisters.

“I’ve been thinking,” she said.

He waited.

“I don’t want to live at your brother’s house.”

“All right.”

“Don’t say that so quickly.”

“I don’t want to live there either.”

“That’s different. If you’re angry, you can go somewhere else and still be his brother. I’d stay the woman who made you angry with him.”

“He manages that himself.”

“Elyas.”

He drew a reed between his fingers and laid it down whole.

“All right. I hear you.”

“I want a household where I’m allowed to understand what things cost. Where people don’t wait until you’ve come home to answer a question I’ve asked them.”

“That sounds reasonable.”

“It will sound less reasonable when I’ve dismissed someone you like.”

“Then tell me why.”

“And listen when I do.”

He looked at her. She was watching the boats, not him. He had asked her to marry him in the warmth of getting her back. She had spent the days since imagining rooms in which he would not be present to protect the promise.

“Yes,” he said.

She rubbed a mark from the top of one foot.

“I want to keep doing something useful. I don’t mean being praised because I handed a bandage to somebody.”

“What do you want to do?”

“I don’t know all of it yet. I’d like time to find out without someone deciding that means I ought to embroider.”

“You embroider badly.”

“I know. It should be a warning to everyone.”

He laughed. She turned towards him then, and the seriousness in her face made him stop.

“I want to marry you. I am afraid of what comes with it. Both are true.”

He took her hand.

“I can’t tell you there won’t be talk.”

“I’ve heard talk.”

“Or that I won’t make it worse.”

“I’ve met you.”

This time they both laughed. He kissed the inside of her wrist, where there was a small raw patch from carrying a basket by its handle.

“Shall I tell them?”

“Tell whom?”

“Anyone who asks.”

“And the ones who won’t ask, but will make my life difficult?”

“Them first.”

She leaned against him. Across the water a boatman was hammering a peg into a loose plank. Every fourth blow sounded different.

The next morning Elyas wrote to Harald. He announced his intention to marry Mara and her acceptance of him. The date and household arrangements would follow when the immediate work allowed.

He showed her the letter before sealing it.

“You’ve made it sound like a levy,” she said.

“I was trying not to give him anything to argue with.”

“He’ll manage.”

She gave it back. “Send it.”

At breakfast Restin asked whether the lady would be travelling with the northbound wagons.

“Mara is arranging the remaining lodgings,” Elyas said. “And when we can make our own arrangements, she and I are to be married.”

The tenant stopped cutting his bread. Hew looked up from the company accounts. One of the servants smiled and then hid it, as if she had done something presumptuous.

Mara reached for the butter.

“Would you pass that, Captain?”

Hew passed it. After a moment he congratulated them both.

Berent was grinning. Elyas kicked his ankle lightly under the table.

“What?” the boy said. “I didn’t say anything.”

“No. Keep practising.”

The morning after the announcement, the woman responsible for the guest beds asked Elyas which linen should be set aside for Mara.

“Mara knows what she needs,” he said.

“Yes, my lord. I wondered what you wished.”

“For you to ask her.”

The woman curtsied and went away. He thought the matter settled until he found Mara standing beside the folded sheets with an expression he had learned to approach cautiously.

“She asked you,” she said.

“I sent her to you.”

“Yes. She told me.”

He waited. Two women were working at the next table. Neither looked towards them, which required more attention than looking would have done.

“I wanted the ordinary linen,” Mara said. “She says you wouldn’t wish me to have it.”

“I didn’t say that.”

“I know.”

He turned towards the guest mistress. Mara caught his sleeve, lightly enough that he could have ignored it.

“Let me.”

The woman came when called. She was older than Mara, with hands reddened from years of hot water, and looked frightened by the pause in the work around her.

“I asked for these,” Mara said, touching the plain folded sheets. “Is there a reason they cannot be used?”

“Only that they are kept for the lesser rooms, my lady.”

“Do they fit the bed?”

“Yes.”

“Then I would like them.”

“As you wish.”

“And when I ask about something I don’t know, please tell me the reason. You needn’t find someone else to decide first.”

The woman curtsied again. She took the sheets away, holding them stiffly against her apron.

Elyas waited until the work at the other table resumed.

“She was trying to be respectful.”

“Yes.”

“You sound angry.”

“I am trying not to be. She doesn’t know which of us will punish her if she chooses wrongly.”

“Neither of us would.”

“She doesn’t know that.”

He looked towards the door. The guest mistress had spent her life reading wishes which people of his rank did not bother to state. He had thought a single instruction would release her from the habit. Mara had seen the difficulty and still had to sleep in the room while the woman decided whether she believed her.

“What should I do?”

“When she asks you again, send her to me again. Don’t begin by saying I have misunderstood what she meant.”

He nodded. She let go of his sleeve.

Later Joan’s first letter came. It had travelled with a household messenger and a packet of thread. Mara read it while Elyas repaired the strap on his sword belt, stopping once to turn a page back.

“Bad news?”

“Advice.”

“From Joan?”

“She says I should have someone with me when I receive household officers. Someone who will remain in the room, instead of being sent for wine.”

“Because of what they might say?”

“Because of what they might say afterward.”

He put down the belt.

Mara kept reading. “She also says I should decide which servants belong to our household before your family sends me people who believe they still belong to theirs.”

“That sounds like Joan.”

“She is being kind.”

“I know.”

“She says people who approve of nursing a duke may speak differently of a woman who works among soldiers.”

Elyas glanced up. The letter had been written after the relief convoy left Tarlington, before the announcement reached Valora. Joan had known Mara was here and had chosen her warning carefully.

“Have people been saying that?” he asked.

“Probably. Joan doesn’t name anyone.”

“If she does—”

“What will you do?”

He imagined an interview, an apology compelled in front of witnesses, the offender afterward describing what sort of woman required a prince to protect her. He had begun with an action that would satisfy him and reached a result Mara would have to inhabit.

“I don’t know yet.”

She lowered the letter.

“Thank you.”

She seemed to find something useful in his reluctant answer. She folded the page and reached for the packet of thread. The colours were ordinary: brown, grey, undyed linen. Joan had chosen things that would mend working clothes.

“I don’t want to spend the rest of my life arranging that nobody can talk,” Mara said.

“I don’t think we’d manage it.”

“Nor do I.”

She threaded a needle, missed the eye, tried again. Elyas held the thread steady until it passed through. The task was small enough that neither had to decide what it meant before doing it.

During the following week a reply came from Harald. He had received the declaration with surprise and hoped his brother had considered the standing of their house. He asked that no irrevocable arrangements be made before a proper discussion.

Mara read it in a careful imitation of Harald’s measured voice. She drew out proper so precisely that Elyas laughed before he could stop himself.

“That’s cruel.”

“I’ve heard him speak.”

“He doesn’t always sound like that.”

She reread the next sentence. He laughed again, and after a moment she did too. The letter lay between them on the bench, unchanged by the laughter.

He answered it before bed. They would discuss the household arrangements. Mara sat beside him while he wrote, mending the sleeve she would wear in the morning.

Gisla arrived two mornings later to collect cloth and take six recovered men back to their companies.

She was a small woman with a strong nose and a burn on one wrist. Her wagon had been scrubbed so often that the wood showed pale wherever a hand could reach. She asked Mara for linen, boiled thread, and two people able to carry a full basin without stumbling. Mara said she could provide the first two and asked what the third would be doing.

“Washing.”

“Wounds?”

“Everything. Wounds when they’ve learned how.”

Elyas was at the stable door, trying to persuade a new gelding to accept a bridle. He heard Mara ask whether Gisla needed someone who would stay.

“Most people offer for a day,” Gisla said.

“I know.”

“You’ve other work here.”

“Nessa can run the stores. She’s doing it now. Restin has agreed to bring the grain lists to her.”

“Have you asked her?”

“Yes.”

The gelding lifted its head just as Elyas raised the bit. He lowered his hand and began again.

Gisla told Mara to come after the midday meal. She would dress a man’s leg in the little room behind the stable, where the light was better. Mara could watch and make her decision afterward.

When the nurse left, Elyas crossed the yard.

“Are you going with her?”

“If she’ll have me.”

“You’ve never—”

“I know.”

He stopped. The barn doors stood open behind her. Nessa was supervising two men who were moving the food sacks onto boards, away from the damp wall. One man objected to moving them twice; she told him to do it once in the right place and she would stop asking.

“I was going to say you haven’t seen a field station,” he said.

“No. That’s why I’m going to look.”

He nodded. It was difficult to find a useful thing to offer when what he wanted was for her to remain where he could find her.

At midday he rode out with Berent. They had to find a farmer whose cart had been taken under a promise made in Elyas’s name. He had made no such promise. By the time they returned, the farmer had a written order to recover his cart and Elyas had agreed to provide another, which he did not yet possess.

Mara was behind the stable, washing a basin.

Her sleeves were wet almost to the shoulder. She had tied her hair back twice; strands had escaped at both temples. Gisla sat on the step beside the patient, waiting for him to finish drinking before they lifted him into the wagon.

“Would you take the other side?” Mara asked Elyas.

He did. The man held his breath as they moved him. Elyas felt the effort in the arm around his shoulder and told him when they reached the step, then when they reached the wheel. Gisla supported the injured leg. Once he was settled, the man shut his eyes without thanking them.

“Was that the one?” Elyas asked quietly.

Mara nodded.

“How was it?”

“I had to go outside.”

“You came back.”

“She told me to. I wasn’t much use for a while.”

He could see she did not want reassurance he was unqualified to give. He picked up the empty basin and asked where the clean water was.

That evening she told him she was going. Gisla would keep her at the station until she could do the work safely; she would begin with washing, feeding, and helping men who could not get up. Nessa would send her news of the families. She had written to Oria herself.

“Braedyn will object,” Elyas said.

“Your uncle has already objected.”

“When?”

“This afternoon. He said people would suppose you had sent me there.”

“What did you say?”

“That they should ask me.”

Elyas sat down on the bench. He had liked his uncle since childhood, and still did; the objection sounded exactly like him, kindly meant and difficult to escape.

“What did he say then?”

“He wanted to know who would answer for my welfare.”

“And Gisla?”

“Said she had enough patients.”

Mara laughed, then pressed the heels of her hands against her eyes. He let her rest for a moment before asking whether she had eaten. She had, this time. The fact pleased him more than it should have.

Joan wrote again in the morning post. She congratulated them, then named a woman near Tarlington who could find a cook without first consulting Harald’s steward. There were two more names, for linen and for shoes, with a warning that the second shoemaker charged for his shopfront. Mara read those lines twice and put the letter away carefully.

She left with Gisla after breakfast. Elyas held the horse while she mounted. The borrowed saddle was too wide; he adjusted the stirrup, then stood back while she tested it.

“Write when you know where you are,” he said.

“I will.”

“Not just where you think you’re going.”

“I’ve seen your letters.”

She bent to kiss him. Gisla waited beside the gate with the wagon. She had put a folded blanket where Mara would sit when the horse needed rest.

After they had gone, Berent came across the yard carrying the bridle Elyas had left on the fence.

“Are we going too?”

“Tomorrow.”

“Towards the station?”

“Towards the army.”

The boy looked down at the bridle and began straightening its twisted rein. Elyas took it from him before he could make the knot tighter.

“First we find the farmer a cart.”

Chapter Twenty-Two: The Ground Seniro Leaves

The orchard house at Hollin had belonged to Seniro’s aunt before it belonged to him. He remembered being sick beneath the stairs after eating unripe fruit. His father had told everyone it was wine.

Now the stairs were stacked with sacks. Beyond the open windows, the last apples hung above grass yellowed by fallen leaves. Men had tied horses to the espaliers. Every time an animal pulled back, a branch scraped against the glass.

Avren was explaining why the house could not be held.

“I know,” Seniro said.

“I thought you did.”

“Then why are you explaining it?”

“Because you keep looking at the windows.”

He turned from them to the map. Ashford lay behind the enemy now. The destroyed mill bridge had delayed Valdais until his men found another passage; the abandoned lanes beyond it had brought him to the Ashbrook meadows. Here the ground narrowed again. Hollin Bridge crossed the brook between two stretches of raised road. South and west of it, most of the meadow would not support loaded wagons after rain.

Four routes mattered. Hollin’s main causeway carried the road across the brook. The Upper Causeway ran along the northern edge of the wet ground, joining Lisle’s lands to the roads behind Valdais. Maresse’s approaches led in from the east, out of sight of the bridge. Farther west, the Kettling link carried supplies towards the crossing by which Valdais kept contact with the river.

Corbel’s barns stood near that link. Seniro had bought grain there before the rising. Now the grain fed the men coming towards his aunt’s windows.

“Eight wagons here,” Avren said. “Six at the northern store. The rest are away.”

“Leave the house after the exchange. Clear the northern store first.”

“And the bridge?”

“Keep it standing.”

Avren looked at the marks around its abutment.

“The mason has already opened the work.”

“Let him finish. Put the supports in and keep the tools with the guard. We may need to drop it. We may need to cross it.”

“Which is more likely?”

“I’ll tell you when the queen tells me where she is.”

He put the letter from Jaennis beside the map. His brother had been alive at a mill in the fens. The account was already several days old. Someone had sent a carriage north of him. Seniro had read the description of the cut shoe three times and had decided not to read it a fourth until he had done something useful.

The exchange began at noon, when both sides could see the entire length of Hollin Bridge.

Men carried the worst wounded on doors taken from empty houses. Others walked between escorts, with a strip of white cloth tied above the elbow so nervous bowmen would know what they were. A clerk stood beside each end of the bridge. Every man stopped long enough to have his name called before proceeding.

Dame Ysabeau de Corvaunt brought the first group herself. She wore a riding cloak over her harness and had covered her horse’s ears against the flies. One of the injured men beside her had a splinted leg. When his crutch caught between the bridge stones, she leaned down and freed it with the butt of her whip.

Seniro went out to meet her.

“You got your company away at Ashford,” he said.

“Most of it.”

“Before my men came out of the millrace.”

“I had seen your shields move.”

“The officer who sent them in had not.”

“He has been informed.”

She dismounted. The horse shook its head, rattling the bit, and stood quietly when she put a hand on its nose.

“His Grace asks after your brother.”

“Alive. Last heard of north of Fairport.”

“I am glad.”

She said it before she could have calculated whether gladness suited the interview. Seniro found himself inclined to believe her.

“Does your commander know who came ashore there?”

“He knows whom he paid.”

“And the others?”

“I was not there.”

She did not offer an explanation on behalf of men she had never seen. Behind her, the clerk called a name and waited. The soldier on the litter tried to answer, failed, and lifted a hand. Both clerks marked their papers.

Ysabeau watched him go past.

“Your man has been asking for his brother,” she said. “We couldn’t identify the company. He called it the red company.”

“There are three.”

“We found that out.”

Seniro sent an attendant to inquire among his wounded. He asked whether the dame wished to sit while the exchange continued. She said she had been sitting since dawn and preferred to stand.

“Your horse was hit at Ashford.”

“The saddle. We were fortunate.”

“Good animal.”

“His name is Bastian.”

“After someone?”

“My companion’s father. She says the resemblance is poor. Her father had better manners at table.”

Seniro smiled. The horse investigated the cuff of his coat and found nothing worth eating.

“Valdais knows his name,” Ysabeau said. “Before I joined him he knew my canton, my previous commander, and what Bastian had cost. I assumed someone had prepared him for the interview. He remembered six weeks later.”

“Does he remember what his promises cost?”

“He asks before he makes them.”

“Some of our merchants would dispute that.”

“Then I expect he will hear from them.”

She had the serene conviction of an officer who had received her own pay on time. Seniro knew men who felt the same about him, and other men who would have laughed at them.

The next litter carried a young Vhasian whose face was hidden beneath a hood. His hands were strapped to the sides to prevent him striking the dressings over his eyes. Ysabeau stepped aside to let him pass without being made to turn. When he began asking where they were, she answered him by name.

“Across now. You’re across.”

He quieted at her voice.

“Your lord offered rank for rank,” Seniro said after the litter had passed. “You sent a sergeant in place of a captain.”

“The captain died last night. We have returned his effects with the next party. His Grace accepts a sergeant in return.”

“No money?”

“He had the rates for each rank before we crossed the Belworth. I don’t think the difference will inconvenience him.”

Seniro looked west along the road. A wagon was waiting beyond the Vhasian guard, its driver leaning against the wheel. It carried fodder, covered against the damp. Valdais had brought fodder to an exchange of wounded because the same road would carry it on to his horse lines.

“He wants this bridge intact too,” Seniro said.

“It would be useful.”

“For the country?”

“For moving wagons.”

He laughed, unexpectedly. She seemed pleased, though she did not join him.

“Where next?” he asked.

“You know I cannot tell you that.”

“Valora?”

“Your capital has many admirable buildings. I have been given no orders concerning any of them.”

The barns, the roads, the tolls. Jaennis’s copy of the river terms had said as much. Hearing it here, with the fodder wagon beyond the wounded, made Seniro see how little Valdais needed a siege. Men would pay him to let their goods move long before they believed he had won the war.

Ysabeau asked which of the queen’s officers was Vayle.

“Not here,” Seniro said.

“I know that.”

“What have you heard?”

“Enough to wish to recognise him.”

“He’s large.”

“So are several of your officers.”

“He listens longer.”

She considered that description. “I will try to notice.”

The exchange ended with a dispute about a man who had given two names. Seniro’s clerk believed he was a deserter. Ysabeau’s clerk believed he was the man on his paper. They brought him forward, frightened and angry, and Seniro discovered that one name belonged to the place he had served and the other to the place he was born.

They let him cross.

Ysabeau mounted without using the offered stool. At the far end she turned to see the last litter safely past the guard, then rode away beside it.

Ralf Pennick met Seniro in the house yard.

“Hob has the northern store clear. He wants another half hour for the teams.”

“He has it.”

“He’ll ask for more.”

“Then tell him he hasn’t.”

Ralf’s smile was tired. Across the yard, Hob Carrow was helping a driver back a wagon between the gateposts. He stood close to the wheel, palm raised, talking to the man as quietly as if they were unharnessing after a day’s ploughing. When the wagon cleared, he looked first towards Ralf. Ralf held up three fingers for the remaining loads. Hob nodded and went to the next.

Seniro’s aunt’s former housekeeper came out of the kitchen carrying a large copper pan. She had refused to leave when he first sent the household north. She said the men would ruin the kitchen.

“They have,” she said now. “There are knife marks in the table.”

“Take it up with Avren.”

“Don’t you put this on him. He asked for a board.”

Seniro took the pan. It was heavier than he expected.

“What else?”

“The preserving jars.”

“We can’t take all of them.”

“They have food in them.”

They left a chest of hangings and loaded the jars. The housekeeper stood on the wagon to make sure the first basket did not strike the second. A groom brought her a blanket. She accepted it without acknowledging that she would be cold.

Seniro went upstairs once, to the room where he had slept as a boy. Its mattress had been carried down for the wounded. The bare frame showed the notch he and Constano had made while trying to carve their names into a place nobody would find. They had been interrupted before they finished the first letter.

He ran a finger over it, then shut the door and returned to the yard.

The first skirmishers appeared near the orchard at dusk. An arrow struck the wall beside the kitchen door. Hob sent the last wagon through and began drawing back his men by sections. Ralf’s archers held the lane while one group crossed the yard; the next group stopped to cover them in turn.

A soldier fell at the well. Two men dragged him behind its stone lip and could not move farther without exposing themselves. Seniro took six shields down to them.

The wounded man’s jaw was broken. Blood made bubbles at the corner of his mouth. They carried him as far as the cart track, where a handbarrow waited. He tried to say something. Seniro bent close and understood none of it.

“I’m listening,” he said.

Hob caught Ralf by the back of his coat when he stepped into the lane to call his last archers in. A bolt passed through the space where his chest had been. Ralf ducked, swore at him, and called again from behind the gatepost.

They crossed Hollin Bridge after dark. Water moved beneath the stones with a steady sound. The mason waited at the farther abutment, beside the tools and the men who would bring it down if ordered. Seniro told him to keep waiting.

On the other side of the brook the orchard house showed three lights. For a moment he imagined his aunt moving from room to room, displeased with the guests. Then a shutter was torn away, and the window filled with men.

“Alive?” he asked when the barrow reached him.

The bearers shook their heads.

“Take him up anyway.”

He waited for the last archers, then sent a relief to watch the mined bridge. Hob and Ralf went up the causeway together. Neither had enough breath left to resume the quarrel.

At the northern camp a rider brought the queen’s message. She intended to come south with the army. Tylan had given her an estimate; she had copied it in her own hand and added that he was still settling the roads behind her.

Eight days, if they could march as intended.

Seniro sent the rider to a fire and read the letter again by the light of a stable lantern. Then he called for Avren and moved the remaining stores farther from Hollin Bridge.

Chapter Twenty-Three: The People Fayd Can Bring

The captain who was to certify the opening of the Wrenford road owed Lord Lisle eighty silver marks.

Lyra learned this because he wanted to know whether royal service would prevent collection. He asked Pellam while she was sharpening a pen, apparently assuming that a woman occupied with a knife could not also hear.

Pellam said that depended on whom he owed and under what terms.

“Lord Lisle,” the captain said.

The knife slipped. Lyra cut too deeply and split the nib.

Pellam glanced at the ruined pen. “Thank you. We will need the terms of that obligation.”

After the captain went out, Lyra said, “He can’t be the witness.”

“He can witness a road being opened.”

“But if Lisle tells him what to say—”

“Then he has a reason to obey. Yes.”

Pellam put the captain’s account beside Fayd’s proposal.

“Find me a carter who uses that road.”

“Any carter?”

“One who expects to be paid for telling us where he drove, rather than where he intends to drive next.”

It took two days. Ysolt knew a woman who arranged the household’s deliveries, who knew three carriers, of whom two were absent and the third disliked Valora enough to have to be paid for coming inside its walls.

His name was Joss. He smelled strongly of onions. He had been through Wrenford four days earlier and been turned back with a load of empty barrels because he would not pay the new road charge.

“Would you go again?” Pellam asked.

“With whose barrels?”

“Mine, if necessary.”

Joss considered him. “You don’t look like a man with barrels.”

They sent him with six, hired from a cooper. Two of the queen’s own servants went along, carrying letters for a village beyond the disputed road. Neither was introduced to Lisle’s captain as an inspector. Both were told to observe the barrier, the men, and anything demanded of the carter.

Fayd approved the arrangement when he heard of it.

“I should have thought of that,” he said.

Lyra could not tell whether he meant it. She was beginning to dislike how much time that question could consume.

He spread his map on Pellam’s desk. Beside each house he had written the name of the person who answered its letters. Beneath Long Lea he had written two names, one crossed out. Beside Maresse’s landing were Hew’s name and another in smaller writing.

“Three lords,” he said. “Corbel, Maresse, Lisle. Not the whole valley. I cannot offer you the whole valley.”

“What can you offer?” Jaennis asked.

She had come without announcement and was still wearing the plain gown in which she had been sorting clothing. Joan stood behind her with a basket, looking impatient to be relieved of it.

“Corbel’s barns beside the Kettling link,” Fayd said. “Maresse’s approaches from the east. Lisle’s section of the Upper Causeway, reached through Wrenford. Maresse can admit the queen’s men. Lisle can prevent Valdais moving reserves across them. Corbel can deny him the grain on which he expects to withdraw.”

“Why would they?”

“Because he is bringing other men’s soldiers into their houses. Because their quarrels with Fenwood have not become less expensive since they began. Because some of them expected a restoration of old rights and are receiving requisitions.”

“You helped them expect it,” Elyas would have said. Lyra heard his voice distinctly in her mind.

Jaennis said it more softly.

“And what did your friends promise them?”

Fayd put his hand flat on the map.

“Some promised more than they could perform.”

“Were you one of them?”

“I have made promises I could not perform. I am trying not to add to them.”

He did not look at Lyra. She wished he had. She wanted some sign that her presence troubled him.

Pellam began asking about quantities. Fayd could give the stock at Corbel’s barns as of six days before, not as of that morning. He knew Lisle’s captain could bring forty foot; he did not know whether Lisle would send all of them. Maresse’s men would move if Hew recognised the command and the promised pardon covered acts already reported to Fenwood’s officers.

“Not acts done after the agreement,” Jaennis said.

“No.”

“Not anything they may choose to call a military necessity.”

“We will need to name the exclusions.”

“We will.”

Joss returned the next evening with five barrels. One had rolled off the wagon and broken, which he reported before reporting anything else. The road had been open. Lisle’s captain had waved him through. No money had been demanded. One royal servant confirmed the account; the other had stayed overnight with the letters’ recipients and came back the following morning by a different way.

The promised act was real.

Lyra had expected the finding to make her feel better or worse. Instead it left the larger question exactly where it had been.

“Put your concern in the covering note,” Pellam said when he found her looking at the map.

“Which concern?”

“The one you’ve been trying not to repeat.”

She took a clean sheet.

The passages have been tested as set out below. The test establishes that Lord Lisle’s captain performed this undertaking. It does not establish that future reports can safely be obtained through one intermediary. The crown should keep the names of captains and carriers and retain the means to write to them directly.

Pellam read it.

“Good. Now tell me which report you mean by ‘as set out below.’”

She found the date, the carter’s name, and the two servants’ names. Precision took longer than indignation.

Seniro’s request arrived with a strip of filthy linen tied inside the covering.

Lyra held it by the cleanest corner. The cloth had been white once. Someone had knotted one end, as if to fasten it to a branch, and trodden the rest into wet earth. Beneath it lay a short note in an officer’s hand.

We can mark the firm way. We cannot make the marks visible in the dark with brown cloth.

The officer was Ralf Pennick. Seniro’s longer letter followed. It named the approach to Hollin, described the drainage cuts beside it, and asked for linen, rope, iron pins, and authority to take planks from a cattle shed whose owner had refused him twice.

The planks were on Maresse’s land. The owner had been told by Maresse’s steward that lending them to Fenwood might prejudice an outstanding claim for their use during the last levy.

Lyra read that sentence again. She had spent the morning helping establish that a road was open. An open road with a broken culvert would still stop a wagon. Beside the clerk’s account of a successful test lay a dirty strip of cloth which had failed to tell a man where he could put his foot.

She took both to Pellam.

“Is this part of the undertaking?” she asked.

“It ought to be.”

“Who decides?”

“The people making it.”

“Then they should see this before it goes north.”

He looked at her, then at the cloth she was still holding away from her skirt.

“Take the letter. Leave that on my desk.”

“Why?”

“Because if you carry it into supper someone will take it away to be washed.”

Jaennis and Fayd were in the small room beside the gallery. Their meal had been interrupted before the fish was uncovered. Joan stood at the sideboard, pouring wine into two cups; she filled a third when Lyra came in, then set it aside while Jaennis read the letter.

“Forty lengths,” the dowager said. “Does he mean forty pieces or forty lengths of cloth?”

“Pieces long enough to see from horseback,” Lyra said. “The officer enclosed one.”

“How long?”

She showed the distance between her hands. Joan went to the linen cupboard and returned with a worn sheet. She folded it twice, looked at the cloth remaining between the folds, and said four sheets would give the requested number with enough left for mistakes.

“Take six,” Jaennis said.

“The relief room has asked for eight.”

“Then take six from ours.”

Joan set the sheet down. The choice had been made so quickly that Lyra nearly missed it. Nothing would be withheld from the relief room; somebody in the dowager’s household would sleep between older linen until the next purchase.

Fayd was looking at the planks.

“Maresse will sign a general permission for work required by the movement.”

“That won’t answer the owner,” Lyra said.

Both looked at her.

She felt the heat rise in her face. “The letter says he is afraid of losing the earlier claim. He isn’t asking whether this use is required.”

Fayd reread the sentence. “Then the old claim must remain open, irrespective of what he lends now.”

“Or sells,” Jaennis said. “Who will return planks after an army has driven over them?”

“They may still be usable.”

“Then the queen can own usable planks. State the payment.”

Fayd asked Joan for a writing surface. She brought a small board and moved the bread basket to make room. He drafted a separate instruction while Jaennis read the rest of Seniro’s letter.

“Why does he want the materials at the eastern junction?” she asked.

“The road to the bridge is narrower,” Lyra said. “He says the wounded must come back along it.”

“I was asking Master Fayd.”

Lyra stopped.

Jaennis looked at him. “Your account of the movement has wagons waiting by the bridge.”

“Until the advance companies are across.”

“And if wounded men come back before then?”

He put his pen down and found the relevant clause among the papers. Lyra could read the sequence upside down: stores, soldiers, then the returning wounded. Seniro’s letter described men being carried back while the advance was still crossing.

“We keep the wagons at the eastern junction,” Fayd said. “A guard sends them forward when the marshal asks.”

“Whose guard?”

“That remains to be assigned.”

“Then leave a space. Don’t write the guard as if one will grow there.”

He made the correction. Lyra wondered whether she would have noticed the assumption if Seniro had not sent them the dirty cloth.

Jaennis pushed the paper towards her.

“Read this against his request.”

She did. Fayd had covered the materials, the payment, the earlier claim, and the place of delivery. The owner was to receive a copy before anything was taken. Lyra could not tell whether the sum would be accepted; she said so. Jaennis ordered an alternative purchase authorised at another yard, with the same limit, so that one refusal would not hold the whole movement.

“Two messages?” Fayd asked.

“One to the man who must supply it. One to Seniro, who must know what we have promised.”

“I can carry both.”

“Your people carry the first. The marshal’s rider takes the second. He has brought us something useful and deserves an answer.”

Fayd looked at Lyra. “Will you copy the amended clause?”

“Yes.”

She reached for the papers, then stopped. “And the name of the officer who is to receive the timber?”

“Pennick,” he said.

She put it in full.

On her way out Joan gave her the cup of wine. It was no longer cool. Lyra drank it standing in the passage, careful not to wet the clean copy. Inside the room Jaennis asked whether the fish could still be eaten. Fayd answered that he hoped so, because he had intended to.

The letter went back with Seniro’s rider before the supper dishes were cleared. The linen followed in the morning with the ropes. Lyra kept a copy of the requested dimensions beside the tested road report, where the next reader would see what the passage needed as well as whose men had agreed to open it.

Tarr brought the corrected road report to Lyra’s desk himself.

He had ruled a line through the old estimate of the culvert’s strength and entered the date of the newer account beside it. Beneath that he had written the name of the timber seller and how far his yard lay from the damaged road.

“Master Fayd asked that you see this before it goes,” he said.

“Why me?”

“You wrote the covering note.”

She checked the names against her copy. One was wrong. The carrier’s brother was the man who sold timber; the carrier himself had no yard. Tarr leaned over her shoulder to compare them, then stood back when he realised he was keeping the light from the page.

“I’ll correct it.”

“Does the marshal have the earlier copy?”

“Yes.”

“Then he’ll need the correction too.”

Tarr made a mark on the outside of the fold. He looked tired. There was a brown stain on his cuff that could have been ink or yesterday’s supper.

“How many copies do you send?” she asked.

“As many as must agree.”

“That sounds like a great many.”

“It is more than I expected when I accepted the work.”

He sat at the end of her desk to make the correction. She gave him a sharper pen. For a few minutes they worked without speaking, two people making sure a man on a distant road asked the right person for timber.

When he left, Nell leaned across the space between their tables.

“Are you coming tonight?”

Lyra had forgotten.

The Lantern gathering was in the tailor’s yard, behind the rooms where Nell’s husband worked. She had been invited a week before, when it had seemed likely that she would have finished something by then. The road report lay open beneath her hand. There would be another report tomorrow.

“Yes,” she said.

Nell looked pleased, then warned her that the yard became cold after the lamps were lit.

Lyra nearly turned back at the gate. Everyone inside appeared to know where to put a cloak and whom to greet first. She stood with Gyllan’s staff in one hand and the other gripping a bottle she had bought because she had been told not to bring food.

Nell’s husband took the bottle and pointed her towards a bench.

“She said you’d come armed.”

“It helps me walk.”

“I didn’t mean the stick. That’s very strong wine.”

He went to find water for it. Nell came out of the workshop carrying a tray of little cakes, two already missing. Behind her, a boy was trying to hide the evidence on his sleeve.
