There were coloured scraps tied above the lamps. Light shone through the thinner pieces and left the heavy ones dark. Someone had stretched a cloth over the wet end of the bench. Lyra sat where she was told and received a plate before she could offer to help.

The woman beside her was making a child’s cap. She had brought it because she could sew and talk at the same time, and wanted it finished before the child grew another head, which she said he seemed determined to do. Lyra held the thread while she measured it.

No one asked about Fayd.

A man who worked on the palace roofs knew she came from the west and wanted to know whether the rain there was as persistent as people said. She told him about a day when the water had come through the seams of their packs. It was a poor account, with no ending except that they had been wet. He accepted it as sufficient evidence and began describing a roof he had been sent to mend in weather that made mending it impossible.

Later they sang. Lyra did not know the first song. The second she knew badly, and Nell supplied the missing line without stopping. By the third she had moved her staff underneath the bench so a dancing couple would not catch a heel on it.

Walking back, she realised she could not give Pellam the names of half the people she had met. There was no reason he would need them.

She slept until Nell knocked in the morning. The road report was still where she had left it. She finished the corrected copy before the light reached the upper shelves.

The following night she finally finished the letter to Amru. She had begun it three times since Tarlington. The first had explained too much of the road; the second too little of Gyllan. Neither had said clearly that she was staying.

This one did.

I meant to return for the hearing. I am asking you to tell me what can be done in writing, because I have accepted work here for the present. I will not pretend that this answers every question you were going to ask me.

She described the work without making it sound grand. Then she wrote about Gyllan, and had to put the pen down.

His whetstone lay beside the lamp. She turned it once in her hand and set it back. She did not need to sharpen anything.

In the morning she paid for the letter’s passage herself. The clerk gave her a written acknowledgement. She kept it in the same book as the names of the men Fayd could reach.

Chapter Twenty-Four: What Holds When She Leaves

The sheep were coming down a week early.

Tylan watched them from the track above the hall, a pale moving line between the black banks of heather. Dogs worked along the edges. Behind the flock, two men carried a hurdle with a lame ewe laid across it, her head moving with each uneven step. Nobody hurried them. The animals had all day to reach the lower pasture, and the people walking behind them intended to arrive with the flock they had started with.

He asked the oldest herder why they were moving now.

“Snow.”

“The pass is clear.”

“Today.”

The man had a face darkened by wind and a little white scar where the corner of his mouth had split. He gave no further account of the weather. Tylan could ask about birds, grass, the direction of the wind at night, and perhaps receive something he could put before a captain as evidence. He had already watched the man move his flock through two changes of weather the army had failed to anticipate.

“How long?”

The herder held up a hand, then folded two fingers.

Tylan looked towards the high ridge. There was no cloud on it yet. He called the rider waiting below and moved the meeting of the hall-keepers forward by a day.

Dunmere had sent his second son to the meeting with the authority to agree positions, but not expenditure. His eldest was on the road to Valora with his wife. Tylan had seen the departure: two good horses, a covered cart, three servants, and a man trying to make his leave-taking look like the beginning of a visit.

The second son was called Renald. He was younger than Orren and more readily annoyed, but he knew the country and answered a question about forage without asking a steward what the question meant. Tylan considered that useful enough to begin with.

The hall-keepers gathered around the central hearth. The flat stones on its shelf had been dusted. Somebody had placed one upright between the others. Tylan noticed it because a cup had been put beside it rather than in the space where it would ordinarily stand.

Wenna remained near the door, speaking quietly to the men arriving last. She had been able to persuade two keepers to attend who would not have answered a royal summons. Neither acknowledged the persuasion. Both found a place from which they could see the way out.

“The queen is going south,” Tylan said.

No one looked surprised. News of the port had reached even the high pastures by now, altered in the telling until some households believed the whole coast burned. What interested the men in this room was how many soldiers would remain between them and Hask.

“Bucktree keeps the pass,” Tylan continued. “Two hundred foot. The mixed mounted company. Each hall will have a named watch, and the keepers will answer for the ground between the marks we agree today.”

“To whom?” asked the man from Quarry Hall.

“To Bucktree for the watch. To your own hall for the truce beneath its roof.”

“And when his men forget the difference?”

“You send to him. He comes.”

“Will he?”

Bucktree stood at the far side of the hearth. His left eye watered in the smoke. He wiped it with a cloth and said he would come.

The keeper looked at him for a while, then nodded. Tylan had chosen Bucktree for his willingness to do a dull task correctly for the fiftieth time, however he was received.

They argued over the marks. The royal surveys divided the country by streams, but the streams were poor boundaries for people who crossed them daily to move stock. The cairns on the ridges were older and better understood. One belonged to a dead household, another to a place where two drove-roads met, a third had been moved after a slope gave way and was now disputed by men who agreed perfectly about where it stood.

Tylan wrote down what they settled. The keepers watched him without asking to see the page. They would know if a patrol crossed the wrong cairn.

Renald objected when the line included grazing his father claimed.

“It doesn’t transfer the grazing,” Tylan said. “It tells a captain where he is expected to go when somebody burns it.”

“My father will want that stated.”

“It is stated.”

He turned the sheet. Renald read it, nodded, and asked about the next ridge.

Grain was harder. The halls could shelter men, but not feed them indefinitely. Tylan offered a regular allotment for each post, delivered from stores established before the main army departed. The keepers wanted the first allotment before they accepted the men. He agreed.

The herders wanted payment for sheep already taken by Hask’s riders.

“How many?”

Each man had a number. Some had marks cut in a stick. One had brought an ear, dried and folded into a scrap of cloth, to show the particular cut by which his flock was known.

Tylan asked Wenna which claims she could confirm. She named two. He authorised payment on those and sent riders to check the others. When the herders saw money change hands for a sheep no longer alive, they began telling him where the missing animals had been driven.

By the end of the afternoon he had two roads by which Hask could still reach the low country and three places where those roads could be watched without a garrison living in a bog. He did not close the road into Hask’s own valley. A man driven home with hungry horses would cause less damage than the same man made desperate on somebody else’s grazing.

The keepers ate with the captains that evening. It was necessary to discover whether they could do that before asking them to live together through winter.

Dunmere arrived late, his cloak stiff with dried mud. He listened to Renald’s account and raised no objection to the positions. Then he asked Tylan, in the pause before the next dish came, whom the north was being asked to hold for after the queen.

“Her heir,” he said. “If we are making arrangements for her line, we ought to know the line.”

Tylan put down his spoon.

“Not a question for this hall.”

“It will be a question for somebody.”

“Yes.”

The earl looked at him. Tylan knew what could be said and did not say it. After a moment the keeper brought more bread, and the conversation moved to grain which existed and could be divided.

In the morning Bucktree went to inspect the posts he had agreed to hold. Tylan rode with him as far as High Fold, then let him take the lead.

The hall stood below a shoulder of ground which hid it from the upper road. A stream ran behind its stable, narrow enough to step over in summer, wider now, with brown water shouldering between the stones. The hay doors faced downhill. The upper room would hold six men if two slept in the passage and nobody expected to undress.

The keeper’s son came out to take the horses. He was tall enough to reach Tylan’s bridle without stretching, though his wrists were still thin above his gloves. One glove had been mended with leather of a different colour.

“Petter,” the keeper called from the doorway. “The back stalls. Keep them away from ours.”

The boy nodded and began leading the horses round the building. Bucktree stopped him to ask which track he used for the pass.

“The drove-road, unless the crossing is up.”

“And then?”

“Over the shoulder. But you can’t bring a horse all the way.”

“Show my sergeant after we’ve eaten.”

The boy looked towards his father before answering. The keeper nodded. Petter led the horses away, pleased to have been given something beyond carrying buckets.

Inside, the keeper’s wife was measuring grain into a cooking pot. She listened while Bucktree explained the watch, then asked whether the soldiers would grind their own meal.

“If that is the arrangement,” he said.

“It had better be. I feed this household already.”

“They will.”

“And mend their clothes?”

“They will.”

“And if they expect my girls to do it?”

“You tell the sergeant. If he expects it too, you tell me.”

She set down the measure. Tylan thought she would ask how she was to reach him with a sergeant between them. Instead she called a younger woman from the back room and made Bucktree repeat the answer in front of her.

The captain did, without impatience.

Tylan went outside while they chose a place for the flour. He found Petter checking the pony he meant to ride with the sergeant. A stone had lodged at the edge of one shoe. The boy held the hoof between his knees and worked at it with a small blunt tool, keeping his own face turned away from the animal’s flank.

“Lift it a little less,” Tylan said.

Petter lowered the leg. The pony stopped pulling.

“It’s an awkward angle for him.”

“I thought he was being difficult.”

“He may be that as well.”

The stone came free. Petter let the foot down and rubbed the pony’s neck. He had a quick, private way of doing it, the animal and the boy familiar enough that neither had to demand attention.

“How far can he run?” Tylan asked.

“Far enough to the post.”

“With someone chasing you?”

The boy looked up.

“You’re showing the sergeant a road. You aren’t being appointed a messenger.”

“I know.”

“Tell the keeper if you see riders you don’t know. He decides what follows.”

Petter’s pleasure diminished. He nodded anyway and checked the other forefoot.

At the back of the stable, Tylan examined the stream. There was a small crossing made of flat stones, one tilted by the current. The boy said the pony knew it. Tylan asked the keeper to have it reset before night. He had begun to understand how many separate things a captain meant when he promised to come quickly: a horse fed, a man awake, a stone kept flat enough to cross in darkness.

They tested the signal after dinner. Petter took the sergeant along the drove-road. Another soldier remained at the hall with the signal cloth. From the lower bend Tylan could see it raised against the wall, but not when the wind caught the edge and folded it around the pole.

“A heavier bottom,” he told Bucktree.

“We’ll sew in a strip.”

“Today.”

The captain looked at him. Tylan heard the unnecessary sharpness of the word. Bucktree had not needed urging on any of the dull tasks so far.

“Before we leave,” Bucktree said.

He turned to the keeper’s wife and asked for a needle. He would sew it himself. She watched him attempt the first stitch, sighed, and told her daughter to show him how to hold the cloth. The younger woman took the needle only long enough to demonstrate, then gave it back.

When the sergeant returned, he had walked the last part of the route with Petter leading the pony. The crossing stone had shifted again. They had marked a wider approach which would let a rider dismount on firm ground before reaching the water.

Bucktree had both routes described to the entire watch. The keeper listened from the door. His wife brought bowls and made them move the cloth out of the way before it was stained.

On the ridge above the hall, Tylan stopped and looked back. The signal hung straight now. It could be seen from the turn towards the post and from nowhere else on the upper track.

“Will you keep changing it?” Bucktree asked.

“If it needs changing.”

“After you go.”

Tylan looked at him.

The captain’s eye was watering again. He wiped it and waited. Tylan shifted his weight in the saddle. He had been sitting crooked to look back at the hall; the stirrup leather had pressed a seam into the inside of his knee.

“What do you want changed?”

“The upper watch.”

“Which part?”

“Where it sleeps.”

Tylan looked towards the next rise. He had chosen the ruined hut himself. It stood within sight of the cairn and kept the watch above the last bend. A man coming uphill would be seen before he could see the men waiting for him.

“The lower shelter loses the bend,” he said.

“It has a roof.”

“So will the hut. There are men working on it.”

“The lower one has water.”

“Below the hut. Fifty paces.”

Bucktree put his cloth away. “Come and walk them.”

They left the horses below the rise. Tylan climbed with one hand against the bank where the path narrowed. Loose flint turned beneath his heel. At the top, two soldiers were lifting a length of thatch onto new battens; another held the ladder steady with his shoulder. The hut would be usable before dark. Through its open doorway Tylan could see the cairn and the pale edge of the track beyond it. He had been right about the sightline.

Bucktree picked up the empty water pail beside the door and took it down to the spring. Tylan followed.

The water ran out between two slabs and collected in a shallow hollow. Somebody had wedged a scrap of wood beneath the lip to carry it clear of the mud. Bucktree filled the pail, waited until its slopping had settled, and offered it to him.

Tylan took it. He knew what the captain was doing and disliked being made to do the rest.

On the climb, the pail struck his leg when he turned towards the bank. He held it farther out. That drew his shoulder downhill; he put his free hand against the rock and felt the wetness on it. There would be ice here before there was snow on the road.

He reached the hut without spilling much. His fingers were stiff where the handle had rested. He set the pail inside the door and opened his hand slowly.

“A rope along the bank,” he said.

“Yes,” Bucktree answered. “If we keep them here.”

The men on the roof had stopped working. Tylan could feel them waiting for the next instruction. A rope would help. So would steps cut in the softer ground, a covered vessel, another man awake to carry it. He could make the hut serve. He had six men to put in it, and the upper road still needed watching while they slept and carried water.

“What are your hours at the cairn?”

Bucktree gave them. Two men would climb before first light, relieved through the day. At night the watch would move to the lower bend, where the horses could not pass without coming close. They would lose warning of men on foot. The captain said that before Tylan could.

“And if the foot come first?”

“I have the hall’s dog at the lower turn. The keeper agreed.”

“A dog isn’t a watch.”

“No. It will wake one.”

Tylan went to the doorway again. From here he could see almost the whole approach. In the lower shelter he would see less and have men who had slept under a sound roof. He wanted another evening on the ground before deciding. Bucktree had already spent one there, with the sergeant who would have to keep it through winter.

“Make your change,” he said.

The captain turned to the soldiers. They were to finish the roof. The upper hut would still shelter the daytime watch in bad weather. Tylan had been about to say the same thing and shut his mouth on it.

On the way down, the sergeant asked whether the change should be entered on the sheet sent to the queen. He addressed Tylan.

“Captain Bucktree gives the positions now.”

The sergeant looked to Bucktree, who told him what to write. Tylan walked ahead of them to the horses. He had left his reins looped over a branch too thin to bear a frightened pull; he moved them to the trunk before looking up at the hut again.

At the next post he let Bucktree ask the first questions. When the captain began with the men rather than the road, Tylan waited to hear the answers.

The arrangement was tested two nights later.

Petter, the High Fold keeper’s son, reached Bucktree’s post before midnight. He had run most of the last mile because his pony had gone lame at the crossing. Raiders were taking the hall’s hay. The watch had barred the stable doors and could not keep them shut much longer.

Bucktree came, as he had said he would.

He sent word to Tylan while his men were saddling. By the time Tylan reached the ridge, the company was already descending towards the hall. Wenna met him at the turn and said the attackers had begun to withdraw. They had expected a guard they could frighten, not men arriving from the pass before the hay was loaded.

A knot of riders appeared above the hall, crossing the open ground towards the high track. Tylan brought his horse round. His escort turned with him, the nearest man already gathering his reins for the climb.

Below, Bucktree’s signal cloth rose beside the stable. His mounted men were going downhill, away from the escaping riders. Tylan could send his own escort after them. He counted the horses he could see against the sky and put a heel against his mount’s side.

Then he drew it back. The captain’s men were circling towards the hay doors, where the raiders still had a cart. From up here the riders on the ridge were the easiest part of the attack to see.

“Follow the captain,” Tylan told his escort. “Keep clear of his men.”

His horse resisted the turn downhill. He had to take it across the slope before it would follow, losing sight of the riders while he did. When he looked again, they had passed the crest. He could still have sent someone after them. He rode down to the hall.

Bucktree recovered the hay and took four prisoners. Two raiders escaped towards the high ground. The keeper’s wife brought water for the soldiers and insisted that the men posted in the stable eat first. They had held the doors for nearly an hour.

Petter wanted to fetch his pony. His father told him to wait until daylight. He argued, was silenced, and went to help the men move the spilled fodder beneath cover.

Tylan remembered seeing him there, thin shoulders under an adult’s coat, carrying an armful too large to see over. Later, when the body came in, he found himself returning to that image as if there had been an instruction he ought to have given then.

The boy had left by the short track after the household settled. One of the escaped raiders met him near the crossing. Perhaps the man wanted the pony. Perhaps he thought a messenger was still carrying word of the attack. There was no witness to say. A herder found Petter beside the path with the rein in his hand.

Bucktree stood outside the hall while the keeper’s wife washed her son. He had taken off his gloves and was rolling them together so tightly that the leather creaked.

“He was told to stay.”

Tylan looked at him.

“I know that isn’t an answer,” the captain said.

“No.”

Inside, the father was speaking to someone in a voice too low to carry. The mother made no sound at all. Tylan had heard grief take enough forms to know that waiting for a cry would not make it more recognisable.

He asked where the boy had fallen. Wenna took him to the place.

The pony stood on three sound feet beneath a thorn bush. It allowed her to approach, tired and hungry rather than frightened. She untied it and led it back towards the hall.

Tylan marked the place on his sheet beside the report of the relief. Then he added Petter’s name. There was no position to put after it.

The horses were being examined in the lower camp when he returned.

Lanterns hung from poles between the lines. A farrier lifted each foot while a clerk noted the condition of the animal; beside them, grooms led the rejected mounts into a separate enclosure. The horses disliked the separation and called to the others. Men answered them without thinking, soothing sounds repeated as they checked teeth and rubbed a hand down a leg.

Tylan walked the lines until his own lamp burned low.

The weakest were obvious. The more difficult animals were still fit enough to work if the pace remained ordinary and the fodder sound. A forced march south would not remain ordinary. Grain would arrive late at one halt, water would be scarce at another, a driver would keep an exhausted team moving because the wagon behind could not pass.

He knew the numbers for the planned stages. He had the farriers’ marks. He made two estimates, one for leaving within three days, another for staying a week to rest and feed the transport animals before moving.

The first allowed for four hundred horses lost before the southern army was ready to fight.

He checked the figure again. Some would be left alive with farmers or at posts. Some would be killed when an injury made movement impossible. Some would simply stop along a road where there was nobody left to take them in. He could not identify each animal in advance, but he knew the conditions which produced the loss.

Anneth found him beside the remount enclosure.

“Your groom said you were still here.”

“Did the queen send you?”

“No.”

She had brought a covered bowl. He took it and discovered he was hungry. The food was still warm.

“How many?” she asked.

He looked at her.

“The horses. People have been saying numbers all day.”

“Four hundred, if we go now.”

“And if you wait?”

“Fewer.”

She stood beside the fence, looking at a bay whose neck had been rubbed bare by its harness. The animal put its nose towards her. She let it smell her hand.

“She’ll pay it,” Anneth said.

He did not ask what she meant. The same question lay in the two estimates folded inside his coat.

“See that she eats on the road.”

“I always have.”

Anneth smiled then, briefly. “I thought you might.”

They stood together while he finished the food. From the upper camp came the sound of someone hammering a wheel rim into place. The work went on after dark because leaving required more preparation than arriving.

“Will you write to her?” he asked.

“If I have something to say.”

He handed back the empty bowl.

“She’ll read it.”

“I know.”

He watched her go up the path towards the hall. There was nothing she had asked him to tell Veralyn, and he was grateful not to have been given something he could deliver badly.

His handover sheet for Bucktree was finished before dawn. Hall by hall, pass by pass: who held it, with how many men, and how long the food would last. The country would not fit on the page. The duties did.

When he took it outside to read in better light, the high cairns were white with the first snow.

Chapter Twenty-Five: Enough of the North

Renald Dunmere arrived with thirty-nine mounted men and a pack mule. Mud had dried white above their horses’ knees. One rider carried a second saddle across his lap, and the mule bore a pair of boots tied together at the ankle.

“Forty,” Veralyn said.

“One broke a leg on the road, Majesty.”

“His or the horse’s?”

“His. He’s with a healer below the pass. I can give you her name.”

Renald was trying not to look frightened. He had his father’s narrow face, but none of the ease with which the earl allowed other people to wait. Veralyn decided she preferred the missing rider to a perfectly counted company that had left an injured man in a ditch.

“Give it to the clerk. He counts when he arrives fit for duty.”

“Yes, Majesty.”

“And you count as yourself, not as the missing man.”

“I understood that.”

They signed in the pass house because it had the only table large enough. Arlan Bucktree had spent two nights inspecting the walls and brought a list of repairs longer than the agreement itself. His left eye watered when the wind came through the shutters. He wiped it with a clean corner of the cloth he kept inside his sleeve.

“You’ll have the timber,” Tylan told him.

“From where?”

“The earl’s estate.”

Renald opened his mouth, then shut it. The instrument provided timber for necessary repairs. Someone in his father’s household had evidently believed that phrase would remain on paper.

Veralyn signed last. Her name took less time than any of the arguments.

Two hundred royal foot would remain with Bucktree, together with the mixed mounted watch. The halls would keep their grain under their own men and supply agreed quantities to the fixed posts. Patrols would meet at named places. They would not pursue every report of horsemen into the hills. The other earls had not submitted; Hask remained somewhere beyond the northern ridge. They would find a defended pass and a neighbour with a son inside it, instead of an army following them from supper to supper.

Orren, the eldest son, was already on the road to Valora with his wife. Veralyn had received the captain’s confirmation. She had read the woman’s name twice before putting the letter away.

There was one appointment left. The proposed reeve for the nearest supply village stood beside the door with his cap held against his thigh. Bucktree recommended him because he had kept grain moving during the raids. Renald recommended another man, related to the old steward.

“Who lives there?” Veralyn asked.

“Both, Majesty,” Bucktree said.

“Who will still be there when the garrison leaves?”

“The man I’ve named has a holding. The other serves where Lord Dunmere appoints him.”

She looked at Renald. He did not contest it.

“Then the man with the holding. Complaints about grain go through the joint clerk. He is not to decide them himself.”

The new reeve bowed. Behind him, a woman waiting with a basket of food made a small movement with her mouth. Relief, perhaps. Veralyn would not know unless she stopped the room to ask.

Outside, the column was forming below the ridge.

It was less than she had brought north. Men had died; others would stay. The sick horses had been distributed among the halls, with their owners’ names tied to the halters. She could see one standing beneath a lean-to, its bandaged leg held clear of the mud. Its rider was kneeling beside the stirrup of a borrowed mount, adjusting leather that had been cut for someone taller.

“How many horses?” she asked Tylan.

“If we keep the pace you want, perhaps four hundred by the time we reach Seniro. Dead or unable to work. More if the southern roads have gone under.”

“Four hundred.”

“We can reduce that by slowing the train.”

“How much?”

“A day. Possibly two.”

She looked down at the line. There were wagons carrying bread baked before dawn, wagons carrying men who would walk when their blisters closed, and wagons carrying the tools without which the guns could not cross a ditch. She had ordered them reduced twice. They still filled the road to the first bend.

“Seniro has already waited.”

“He has.”

“Then we pay it.”

Tylan did not answer immediately. He picked a loose grain of flint from the ledge and let it fall.

“The team masters will need to know before the first halt.”

“Tell them. No man is to be punished for reporting an animal unfit. I want to know while there’s still a chance to change it.”

A soldier below them touched two fingers to the stone at the foot of the gate, then to the end of Veralyn’s scabbard as he passed. His companion did the same. The third man reached for it and drew back when he saw her watching.

“Does it trouble you?” she asked.

“No, Majesty.”

“Then don’t hold up the road.”

He touched the chape lightly and went on. She shifted the sword so it would not strike the gate as she turned. Tylan was watching the wagon nearest the bend.

“If Dunmere breaks it,” she said, “we’ll have left good men in his reach.”

“Yes.”

“If he keeps it, he’ll tell everyone he made me leave.”

“He may do that either way.”

She touched the red repair on his armour.

“Will that hold?”

“It has so far.”

“You could have had it replaced.”

“I forgot it was there.”

She smiled. For a moment she could see him younger, returning a practice sword to the rack with the same unhurried certainty that it would be there when he needed it again.

Anneth was waiting at the lower gate with the grey.

The horse still favoured the injured leg. She had brought him only as far as the level track and would lead him home slowly. He put his nose into her coat, searching for the food she always seemed to have concealed somewhere.

“I’ve paid the farrier,” Veralyn said.

“I know.”

“He says another week before you ride him.”

“I know that too.”

Veralyn looked at the halter, then at Anneth. She had prepared something to say while coming down the hill. None of it would improve this.

Anneth kissed her beside the horse, in full sight of the men mending a harness. It was a brief kiss, and had nothing of petition in it.

“Come back when you don’t need a guide.”

Veralyn touched the grey’s neck. His coat was warm beneath her hand.

“I’d like to.”

Anneth stepped back to let the first cart pass. By the time Veralyn mounted, she was leading the horse towards the house. She turned once and lifted her hand. Veralyn answered, then rode to the head of the column.

The march south was dust where the road rose and mud where it fell. Men argued over places at wells. A road that looked level on the map climbed for most of the afternoon.

At the first halt Veralyn found a wagon overloaded with private baggage. Its owner was a knight who had argued vigorously for speed. She had his chest unloaded. He watched two shirts, a silver cup, a book of devotional readings, and a small carved stool laid on the verge.

“The stool stays,” she said, looking at the driver, whose knee had swollen. “He can use it when he rests. Choose what else you can carry on your horse.”

The knight chose the cup and the book. She waited while he tied them behind his saddle. When the wagon moved again, the driver put the stool beneath his good foot to climb into his seat.

The second day cost a team at a ford. One animal fell between the submerged stones and could not rise while still harnessed; the other pulled until it injured itself. Veralyn came back along the road in time to see a groom holding a cloth over the fallen horse’s eyes.

The men had already unloaded the wagon. They needed a new team. Beyond them, three carts stood in water while their drivers waited for someone to decide whether they could turn.

She ordered them backed out one by one. The man with the cloth asked her to move farther up the bank.

She did, and heard the shot from there.

At supper the team master brought the day’s losses. He stood beside her until she read them; perhaps he feared she would let the paper disappear among the letters. She gave it back with instructions for the remaining horses. Then she ate the bread Tylan had left beside her cup.

The proposal from Valora reached them on the southern road the next evening.

Veralyn read Lyra’s covering note twice. She remembered the young woman at Tarlington: watchful, unwilling to claim more than she knew. The hand on the page had been careful rather than elegant. Beneath it were the carter’s name, the witnesses, and the date on which Lisle’s captain had let the test wagon through Wrenford.

Tylan read the reports beneath it. He traced the Upper Causeway with a blunt finger, then asked the messenger whether the broken culvert was on the road marked in the margin or the one below it.

“The lower, my lord. They sent a separate correction.”

“Good.”

He found it and laid the two accounts side by side.

“Maresse can admit us from the east,” he said. “Lisle shuts the lateral road behind their forward companies. Corbel denies them the nearest grain. None of that works if the others move a day late.”

“And if they all perform?”

“We can force Valdais to choose between his position at Hollin and his way back to Kettling.”

“And Fayd?”

“No terms for himself.”

“There will be.”

“Yes.”

She called for writing materials. The messenger ate while she composed the answer.

Let him meet me on the southern road. Bring the instruments, the names of the captains, and his own terms. I will hear them together.

After he rode away, she walked through the camp to where the men were singing over their supper. The song was badly suited to a queen’s presence. They faltered when they saw her.

“That’s not the next verse,” she said.

A man with a split lip supplied it. She sang the answer. Farther down the line another man joined in, then the cook, who had no tune and knew every word.

Tylan found her there and gave her a bowl. She took it and made room for him on the fallen tree.

Chapter Twenty-Six: The Welcome

The lamp on Jaennis’s worktable had begun to smoke. She trimmed it herself, holding the blackened thread in the snuffer until the flame steadied. Beyond the casement the garden paths were dark, but the windows of the old wing remained lit. Fayd’s clerks had acquired another room there during the week. Nobody had asked her for it; the keeper of the rooms had found space for people who were plainly working late.

Joan brought supper on two trays.

“Is he here?” Jaennis asked.

“In the passage. Finishing a letter.”

“Then leave the food. He may discover that letters are less interesting when dinner is cold.”

Joan put the jug between the trays and went out smiling.

Fayd entered a moment later with ink on the side of one finger. He apologised, sat when invited, and moved a folded petition far enough from the bread to keep crumbs from the writing. Jaennis saw the gesture and thought of her father. It made her briefly less inclined to enjoy the evening.

The latest report placed Constano on a fen road, alive, accompanied by the Tirenos and the people he had brought out of Fairport. The miller’s wife who had identified him had described the turned foot and the silver-capped cane. His secretary had given the name Constano Fenwood. A second account placed Matteo Tireno with him. Neither had been able to say where the company meant to go next.

Fayd had sent riders by two routes. Joan had written to three women whose husbands worked the peat boats. Jaennis had received replies from two of the women and neither rider.

“Anything?” she asked.

“Not since midday.”

She nodded and uncovered the first dish. Eel in green sauce, bread still warm enough to tear rather than cut, a bowl of beans with onion. Ordinary food, prepared well. She had stopped allowing cooks to furnish these working suppers as though a petition became easier to resolve when placed beside an ornamental bird.

Fayd looked at the first page.

“Lisle’s second condition.”

“Is still his second condition. He has merely written it in a different hand.”

“He wants the guarantee extended to his captains.”

“He wants the guarantee extended to anything his captains can persuade a witness to call an order.”

“Yes.”

“Then write the acts it covers. If he wants us to pardon a burning, let him name the burning.”

Fayd took a clean sheet. “He will object.”

“He has practised enough to do it efficiently.”

They worked while they ate. The new proposal had grown from three local arrangements into a way of moving an army. Every road now carried more than traffic: a baron wanted his men protected, a wife wanted an old injury heard, a captain wanted to know whether obeying one letter would get him hanged under another.

Jaennis had spent the morning listening to people describe these as technical difficulties. At supper she could call them what they were without first explaining why they mattered.

Fayd read a paragraph aloud, stopped halfway through, and crossed out a phrase before she could interrupt.

“You were about to say—”

“Yes.”

“It’s gone.”

“Good.”

He passed the revision across. She made one change and returned it. For nearly an hour neither had to repeat a question.

Then he reached Corbel’s submission.

Jaennis read the proposed assurance and laid it beside her plate.

“No.”

Fayd waited.

“You’ve given him a hearing and left him nothing he can take home.”

“The hearing determines his claim. We cannot settle it in advance.”

“I didn’t say settle it. I said give him something he can take home.”

He looked at the paper again.

“His wife still has her grandfather’s account of the drainage,” Jaennis said. “Not in the estate room. With the family papers. If you tell her the matter will be heard after the war, she will tell him your grandfather said that to mine.”

“My grandfather was not involved.”

“She won’t care.”

She took his pen and added the means by which the hearing would be constituted: named assessors, one nominated by Corbel’s household, one by Fenwood, a third agreed by the crown; the old watercourses to be inspected before winter flooding obscured them; the family’s account to be received with the surviving Fenwood surveys.

“That can begin before the campaign ends,” she said. “The judgment can wait. The looking cannot.”

Fayd read the addition. “You know her.”

“I know what she needs to be able to say to him.”

“And what does he need to say to his men?”

“That he obtained something by turning which he hadn’t obtained by refusing. Give him the truth. It’s cheaper to maintain.”

Fayd leaned back. For a moment she thought he would offer an elegant disagreement. Instead he drew the paper towards him and copied the addition into the formal terms.

She liked him better for doing the work without making her admire his acceptance of being corrected.

The jug was nearly empty when they finished the road conditions. Jaennis rang for more wine, then changed her mind and asked for the bottle recovered from the Fairport stores. Its cork had been stained by river water; the wine inside remained good. Her father would have made some dry remark about the care given to a bottle while half the quay burned. He would also have poured it.

“I hear you write to Ecktoria,” she said.

Fayd’s hand paused beside his cup.

“Frequently.”

“The letter in the passage was not one of the frequent ones.”

“No.”

He answered plainly enough that she could either leave it there or make the question intrusive. She poured the wine.

“A friend?”

“Yes. A map-printer. We kept house together for some years.”

“Does he still print maps?”

“When people pay for them. When they don’t, he corrects the maps they have already bought and sends complaints about the errors.”

“Does that help?”

“Not financially.”

She laughed. He smiled, with less calculation than she had heard in his account of any baron.

“What’s his name?”

“Aurel Dace.”

“I shall remember it if I need somebody to tell me my roads are wrong.”

“He will tell you whether you need it or not.”

The letter had not gone through his clerks. She had noticed because everything else he sent out did. Now she understood the exception and found herself disinclined to make it useful.

They returned to the proposal.

“What are they swearing to?” she asked.

“The queen.”

“They have already sworn to the queen.”

“They swore the forms their houses had always sworn. They need to undertake acts this queen has asked of them.”

“And when she dies?”

“Her successor receives the office and the agreements.”

“How comfortably you move from a person to an office when it suits you.”

“Would you prefer allegiance to a house which no longer answers?”

“I would prefer a house capable of answering when one person cannot. My father is alive in a village you haven’t reached. Fenwood has not ceased to exist.”

“No.”

“Then don’t make absence into proof that the old arrangements were foolish.”

He put his cup down.

“I don’t think they were foolish. I think too many depended on a man knowing another man’s father.”

“Some of those men have wives.”

“I have learned that.”

“And daughters.”

“That too.”

She held his eyes for a moment, then looked back at the road names. The argument could continue all night without either of them admitting the person whose future made it worth having. She had enjoyed the hour enough to resent how easily he could give her another of the same kind.

“The company meets tomorrow,” she said. “You should come.”

“I’m honoured.”

“It isn’t an honour you need to refuse.”

“I am afraid I must.”

She had expected hesitation, perhaps a question about who would be present. The refusal was complete, courteous, and without an excuse she could remove.

“You dislike music?”

“No.”

“The people?”

“I don’t know them well enough to earn that judgment.”

“Then come and improve your position.”

He smiled slightly. “My position would change. I’m not certain it would improve.”

She understood him then. Everyone admitted to that room had been chosen by her. Its freedoms existed because she permitted them; its obligations could be left unspoken because the people accepting them already knew whose hospitality they enjoyed. He had no intention of becoming one of her people while negotiating the means to establish his own.

“Very well,” she said.

He let her invitation stand. She appreciated the tact.

When he left, he took the road proposal and left the bottle. Jaennis remained at the table a few minutes longer, looking at the two empty plates. She had a father to find, households to feed, and a queen moving south through bad weather. Fayd would receive no more of the evening merely because he had made the part already spent on him pleasant.

She rang for the musician.

The song was ready except for the second verse.

The musician laid two versions beside the lamp. In one, the woman at the gate waited for a lover. In the other, she had already let the lover in and was keeping someone else out. Veralyn’s letter had remembered enough of the refrain to make the choice clear.

“That one,” Jaennis said.

He looked relieved. “I thought so, Majesty.”

“Then why bring both?”

“The northern singer was insistent that each had its proper occasion.”

“She sounds formidable.”

“She charged for both.”

Jaennis asked him to play it softly. Without the court’s familiar embellishments the tune was more direct, the turn at the end of the second line arriving sooner than she expected. She could imagine Veralyn losing the words there and laughing at herself, then trying again because somebody had declined to rescue her.

She asked for a clean copy, in a hand Veralyn could read by poor light. No decoration. No dedication for anyone else to repeat.

When the musician had gone, she wrote a note to accompany it.

I have found the verse you omitted. I am told this is the version appropriate to your circumstances, though nobody would explain exactly which circumstances they meant. Come home with enough voice left to sing it to me.

She crossed out home and wrote here, then restored the first word on a new sheet.

The copy and the letter went into an oiled covering. She sealed it herself.

The next evening she tried the room as it would be used for the welcome.

Joan had moved the chairs so that the place beside Veralyn would face the hearth rather than the door. Margery tested it, sitting with a cup in her hand while the servants passed behind her. Ysabet inspected the linen without being asked. Catrin stood at the window, reading a letter which had arrived from the people sheltering her aunt after the loss of their Fairport house.

“There’s a room for her if she comes,” Jaennis said.

Catrin folded the letter. “She won’t yet. She thinks somebody must go back and see what’s left.”

“Then we’ll send somebody who can travel safely.”

“She doesn’t believe anyone else will recognise what matters.”

Jaennis understood that too well to argue.

They tasted the wine. The musicians played the first part of the song. A shoemaker arrived with Veralyn’s old dancing shoes, their soles replaced and the worn fastening strengthened. Jaennis had refused his suggestion that a new pair would be more suitable. Veralyn knew these shoes. For one evening she wanted something to fit without being worked at.

Catrin watched her turn the shoes in her hands.

“You know her very well.”

“Yes.”

“Does she like meeting people?”

“When they’re worth meeting.”

The answer had been too quick. Catrin looked towards the chair by the hearth.

“Is that meant for me?”

“It could be.”

“I don’t want it.”

Margery stopped speaking to the musician. Joan continued folding a cloth, slowly enough that the movement could not be mistaken for indifference.

Jaennis put the shoes down.

“You needn’t decide tonight.”

“I have decided.”

Catrin’s hands were steady on the folded letter. The rest of her was held very still. Jaennis could see what the refusal cost her and felt, beneath the first sting, an unwelcome awareness that she had counted on that cost making it less likely.

“Is it the queen?”

“No. It’s being placed.”

The room had become too attentive. Jaennis wanted everybody to resume speaking and knew she could not order that without making it worse.

“Where would you prefer to sit?”

Catrin looked at the window, then at Joan’s ordinary chair.

“Beside Joan. If she doesn’t mind.”

“I don’t mind,” Joan said.

“Then there.”

The decision was made. Catrin let out a breath and looked away before anyone could treat it as gratitude.

A little later, when the musicians were discussing their order, Jaennis moved Margery’s cup to the place by the hearth. Margery saw her do it. She lifted the cup, tasted the wine, and sat down.

No further instruction was necessary.

Margery remained when the others went to hear the last song tried in the gallery. She had taken one shoe off and rested her foot on the hearth fender, just outside the heat. Jaennis sat in the chair opposite, pleased to have her there and uncertain whether the pleasure was being returned in quite the way she wished.

“You needn’t tell me how to make her laugh,” Margery said.

“I hadn’t intended to.”

“You’ve been telling me for weeks.”

Jaennis looked down at the cup in her hands. “Have I?”

“The cook. The songs. Which questions she’ll answer and which make her impatient. I could conduct a small examination on the subject.”

“Would you pass?”

“I’d prefer to meet the woman.”

The answer was lightly made. Jaennis could have laughed and let it go. She wanted to know whether Margery was teasing her or declining an instruction, and disliked that the distinction mattered so much.

“She will ask what you want,” Jaennis said.

“Then I hope I shall know.”

“And if you don’t?”

“I could tell her that.”

Margery put the cup on the floor and bent to unfasten the other shoe. Jaennis watched her hands. The pear clasp at her shoulder had come loose; when she straightened, it hung at an angle. Jaennis crossed to put it right.

“Stay still.”

“I am.”

“You’re laughing.”

“That doesn’t move my shoulder.”

It did. Jaennis had to catch the cloth with both hands and found herself smiling despite the irritation. Margery looked up at her, close enough that the lamplight no longer softened the small lines at the corners of her eyes.

“What do you want?” she asked.

Jaennis could have answered readily an hour earlier. Veralyn home, her father found, an evening in which the people she wanted did not have to leave by different doors. Standing over Margery, holding the loosened clasp, she discovered that she did not want to give an answer which would make any of those desires seem smaller.

“You know.”

“I know some.”

Jaennis finished the fastening. She kept one hand on Margery’s shoulder after it was secure.

“I want her to be happy here.”

“Yes.”

“And I want to be here when she is.”

Margery’s expression changed a little. She reached up and covered Jaennis’s hand with her own.

“Then be here.”

“You make it sound easy.”

“I haven’t said it will be.”

Jaennis bent and kissed her. There was no question in the first kiss. In the second she felt Margery’s fingers close around her wrist, asking her to stop moving away before either had decided it was over.

The musician struck a false note in the gallery. A woman’s laughter followed, then the same phrase played again correctly. Jaennis found she was listening for footsteps approaching the door, even as she resented the habit.

Margery let her go.

“If I sit beside her,” she said, “I shall answer her myself.”

“I would expect nothing else.”

“No answers prepared for me afterward either.”

Jaennis stood back. “You expect a great deal of interference.”

“I’ve watched you arrange a room.”

She could be offended. Margery had left the choice open with enough affection that taking offence would be revealing. Jaennis went to the window and touched the cold fastening. Below, a servant carried a tray across the courtyard, holding a cloth over it against the first drops of rain.

“You may tell me when I’m doing it,” she said.

“I just have.”

“Yes.”

She turned. Margery had not put her shoes back on. The knowledge eased something Jaennis had been holding too tightly.

Joan returned with the musicians’ questions and found them discussing the order of the songs. She listened, settled the matter in three sentences, and asked whether Catrin’s things should be moved to another chamber before the queen came.

“Why?” Jaennis asked.

“She thought you might want the passage clear.”

“She has a room. It remains hers for as long as we agreed.”

Joan nodded. Jaennis could not tell whether she had expected that answer or had come to make sure of it.

“Tell her I meant what I said about the chair as well.”

“I think she knows.”

“Then don’t tell her.”

Joan’s mouth softened. She gathered the musicians’ papers, leaving the evening’s questions answered without quite leaving Jaennis comfortable about how they had arisen.

Margery put on her shoes. Her husband had sent no inquiry tonight; he was away with the garrison stores and would not return before morning. She said this plainly, without turning it into an invitation Jaennis would have to pretend not to understand.

Jaennis asked whether she wished to stay for supper.

“Yes,” Margery said. “If you mean supper.”

“And afterward?”

“We’ll have eaten. We can decide then.”

Jaennis rang for another place. When the servant came, she ordered what they would both like and left the rest of the night unarranged.

The rider came before the room was put away. Veralyn was two days from the appointed meeting on the southern road. Fayd’s proposal had reached her. She would hear its author in person before the army moved into the valley.

Jaennis sent for him at once. There was practical work to finish before he left.

While Joan arranged the message, she took the dancing shoes upstairs and set them beneath the chair in Veralyn’s room. The fire had not yet been lit. She opened the shutters, looked at the cold bed, and asked that the room be aired in the morning.

Chapter Twenty-Seven: A Command Harald Cannot Give

Harald had come as far as the riverward hall with three secretaries and no troops.

There were troops available. Elyas passed them exercising in the meadow below the house, their spears moving in uneven ranks. Their captain said he had orders to remain ready. He did not know for what.

Inside, Harald was arguing over a map that showed the lower passage twice under different names.

“That is the old name,” Elyas said.

His brother looked up.

“I know.”

“Then this is one crossing. You haven’t two places to defend.”

“I said I know.”

The secretary holding the map carefully lowered it.

Elyas had ridden ahead of the royal column with a proposed commission bearing Veralyn’s seal. She had summoned him on the road, heard his account of Maresse’s landing, and asked whether Hew would open the lane to men under his command. He had said Hew would want to see the order. She had approved of that answer more than of anything else he said.

She had heard him in a room above a stable, with rain dripping from his cloak onto the boards. Tylan stood at the window, examining a lane too narrow for the wagons already in it. The queen’s supper had gone untouched beside a pile of names.

“Fayd says they will turn,” she had said.

“He says he can make them.”

“Is that a distinction?”

“It means they may be waiting for something he hasn’t told us.”

“He has given his conditions. I shall hear them.”

She held out the proposed commission. Elyas read it standing. It was broader than the escort duty he had expected, narrow in its duration, and particular about whose orders he must obey once the forces joined Seniro. He found his own name among passages and companies not yet available to anyone who served the crown.

“Do you want my brother’s men?”

“I want every man who can be spared without leaving another road open behind us. Your brother must say what he has.”

“He will ask who commands them.”

“You do, under the marshal.”

“He may not like that.”

Veralyn looked up. “Do you?”

He had expected a question about Harald’s loyalty. He looked again at the paper. A few months earlier it would have seemed a fine thing to be named in such terms, given a defined place in a royal movement. Now he could see the men who would ask which ditch they must stand in, and the families who would write when one did not return.

“I think I can do it.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

“No.”

She waited.

“I would like to know there will be men enough to do what it says.”

“So would I. Tylan?”

He left the window and laid the current estimate beside the commission. It contained fewer men than the list Fayd had sent. Elyas pointed to the difference.

“Some are promised twice,” Tylan said. “Guarding the approach and furnishing the moving company. They can do either.”

“Then the promise is false.”

“The number is false if we use it that way.”

Veralyn took the larger list and set it aside.

“Use his.”

Elyas read the lower figure. He had heard himself ask for certainty and been given a smaller, more credible difficulty. He asked what he was to do if the turned men came under attack from Fenwood’s companies before their orders reached them.

“Keep them alive long enough for the orders to be read,” the queen said.

“And if there is an accusation?”

“Bring it to Seniro.”

“If he knows it is true?”

She watched him more closely then. “What are you asking?”

“At Maresse’s landing, men who took grain called it a lawful charge. The people who lost it called it theft. Some died after the taking. If the agreement covers those men, somebody will stand before the marshal with a name and expect him to act.”

“Yes.”

“Will he be allowed to?”

“Under the agreement I make. That is what an agreement means.”

Elyas looked at Tylan. He offered no rescue from the answer.

“You want me to protect them,” Elyas said.

“If they perform what I require, yes.”

“What do we tell the families?”

“The truth. Their case has not vanished because we require the road. The remedy may be money when they want a hanging.”

“They may not accept it.”

“No.”

It was easy to admire a queen for refusing to make a false promise. Less easy to hear her make a promise he disliked and require him to keep it.

She stood, took the plate from beside the papers, and carried it to the little table near the window. The bread had stiffened. She broke a piece anyway.

“You know Fayd,” she said. “You know some of these men. That is why I need you at the approaches. An officer who believes every enemy has become a friend will get us killed. An officer who refuses to believe an order has changed will do the same.”

“I am not refusing.”

“I know. I am telling you what I am asking.”

He read the commission a final time. A drop from his cloak had reached the lowest corner. He moved the paper and dried the boards with his sleeve, an awkward little act under the attention of the queen.

“When does it take effect?”

“When the passage is authorised. Until then you may seek your brother’s company and prepare your men. You undertake nothing on Maresse’s behalf.”

“And Berent?”

Veralyn frowned. “What about him?”

“He is my squire. I won’t put him into the fighting to prove he is one.”

“Then don’t.”

He almost laughed. He had expected another question, perhaps a comment about boys who served among soldiers. She had given him the responsibility without the comfort of somebody else deciding its daily limits.

Tylan walked him to the stair. Below, Berent was feeding scraps of hay to the remounts, keeping his fingers flat as the grooms had shown him.

“Give the boy things he can finish,” Tylan said.

“I try.”

“And tell him where to stop.”

Elyas watched one of the horses search Berent’s empty hand. The boy laughed and turned to find more hay.

“I’ll remember.”

He had brought the commission away still smelling of the stable, with the queen’s provisional authority and none of the lords yet bound to perform it. On the ride to Harald he had rehearsed how to explain that uncertainty without letting it become another reason to wait.

Now he put the commission before Harald.

His brother read the first lines without sitting down. Then he sat.

“She gives you authority over the passage.”

“Over the escort and movement of the forces assigned to it. For this campaign.”

“Here.” Harald put his finger on a phrase. “And over such guards as are placed at its approaches.”

“Yes.”

“Our approaches.”

“Some are yours. Some are Maresse’s. Seniro holds the far end.”

“Seniro holds Fenwood’s land. Maresse holds his own. You arrive with a royal paper and expect me to congratulate you for taking responsibility from everyone else.”

“I expect you to tell your captain whether his men may accompany me.”

“And if I say no?”

“I use the men the queen gives me.”

The secretary had become very interested in the map’s lower corner. Elyas wished they were alone and knew it would make the conversation worse. Alone, they would stop speaking about men and roads altogether.

Harald dismissed the secretaries anyway.

When the door closed, he said, “You have always had somewhere else to go.”

Elyas looked at him.

“Fenwood. The west. Now the queen. Somebody whose judgment you can borrow when you dislike mine.”

“I’ve been asking for your judgment for weeks.”

“You ask for agreement.”

“I ask whether the road will be held, and you tell me there are questions.”

“There are questions. Father could answer them because he remembered which promises had been made and to whom. Every man who enters this room tells me Father promised him something. Half of them are lying. The other half think a conversation fifteen years ago ought to empty the stores today.”

For the first time Elyas could hear the fear beneath the annoyance. Harald would have to live with the decision after the man who advised it had gone away.

“You could ask for help.”

“From you?”

“Yes.”

“You’d ride away.”

He could not honestly deny that. His horse was already saddled below the window.

“I can do this,” he said, touching the commission. “A defined command, with men and orders. I can’t stand beside your chair until you feel ready to give one.”

Harald rose and walked to the window. In the meadow, the practising troops had stopped while their captain corrected the front rank.

“And Mara?”

“What about her?”

“Is that another defined undertaking?”

Elyas waited until he was certain his voice would remain level.

“I sent you the letter because you’re my brother.”

“You should have spoken to me first.”

“I did speak to the person whose answer mattered first.”

Harald turned. “You know what people will say.”

“Yes.”

“You know what alliances you are giving up.”

“I know there are people you would rather I married.”

“People who would bring something to the house.”

Elyas stood so quickly that his chair struck the wall.

Harald stopped. He had the grace, or perhaps the caution, to look away.

“She has brought a great deal to my life,” Elyas said. “You don’t have to like the marriage. Don’t speak of her as if she arrives empty-handed.”

He took the commission from the table.

“I will serve under this. Tell the queen whether you can spare the company. Tell her today. If you cannot, we will make the plan without it.”

He reached the door before Harald spoke.

“Leave me a copy.”

Elyas turned back.

“So I know what you’ve undertaken,” his brother said.

They summoned the secretary. It took an hour to make and check the copy. Neither brother left while it was done.

Outside, Berent had found a saddle girth beginning to split. He showed Elyas the worn stitching and the replacement he had borrowed from the stable.

“Borrowed from whom?”

“The head groom. I gave him my name.”

“Good.”

“Are they coming?” The boy nodded towards the men in the meadow.

“I don’t know.”

“Should we wait?”

“No.”

Berent looked back at the house. “Was he angry?”

“Yes.”

“Because you wouldn’t do what he wanted?”

“Partly.”

They mounted. Elyas adjusted the new girth, then made himself add the rest.

“He has reasons to be afraid. That doesn’t make the road any less necessary.”

Berent took this in without answering. At the gate he looked down at the borrowed leather and repeated the groom’s name under his breath, making certain he would remember whose it was.

The company caught them at the second halt.

Elyas heard the uneven pace of the horses before he saw the banner. The captain from the meadow rode in front. Behind him came men in household colours, two carrying spare spear shafts on their shoulders because the baggage cart had been left behind.

“His lordship’s order,” the captain said.

He handed over a folded paper. Harald had specified the company, its equipment, the stores from which it was to be supplied, and the campaign for which it was released. Beneath the secretary’s formal conclusion he had written a line himself.

Return the men to me when the queen releases them.

Elyas read it twice.

“He said you would need this as well.”

The captain held out a small purse. It contained enough coin to buy food before they reached Seniro, and no more. Elyas felt a sudden shame at how much he had expected the gesture to be inadequate.

“Did he review you before you left?”

“Yes, my lord.”

“What did he say?”

“To keep the banner dry.”

The captain glanced behind him at the man carrying it. The cloth was wrapped against the weather. Elyas had watched Oswyn inspect troops beneath the same colours, asking after the men whose names he knew and leaving the others to his officers. Harald would know fewer names. He had sent them anyway.

“Give me yours,” Elyas said to the captain.

“Edrick Vale.”

“And your sergeants.”

They made the introductions beside the road. Elyas took longer over them than was convenient. One sergeant had served Oswyn for twenty years. Another had been a tenant’s son when Elyas went west and now had two children of his own. A young spearman wore a helmet that had been padded so thickly it rode high above his ears; the strap would not hold it straight.

Elyas called Berent over and had him bring leather from the saddlebag. Together they shortened the strap. The spearman stood patiently while they worked, staring past Elyas as though at an inspection.

“You can tell me if it pinches,” Elyas said.

“It pinches, my lord.”

They began again.

At the next halt he wrote to Harald. He acknowledged the company, gave its strength as received, and said the captain had brought it in good order. He added that he had received the money. Then he sat with the pen in his hand, listening to Berent count nosebags on the other side of the hedge.

He wrote that he was glad they were his own house’s men.

That evening the captain asked where his company would stand. Elyas showed him the commission. They would escort the movement through Maresse’s approaches, then hold the eastern mouth of the causeway under Seniro’s orders until the queen required them elsewhere. The captain asked whether they were expected to fight beside Maresse’s men.

“Yes.”

“Some of ours have families on the lower road.”

“So do some of theirs.”

“I meant families harmed by them.”

“I know.”

Elyas folded the commission and put it away. He had been about to answer too quickly, with something that would sound generous only because nobody had burned his cart.

“Tell me the names before we arrive. I’ll ask the marshal where they can serve without being put beside the men concerned. I can’t promise every request. I can make sure he hears them.”

The captain considered that and nodded.

Berent brought the mare in from the grass. She had stepped on her rein and dragged it through mud; he was trying to clean it with his sleeve before Elyas noticed.

“Use the cloth,” Elyas said.

“I thought we needed it for the tack.”

“We do. That’s tack.”

The boy laughed, embarrassed. Elyas sat beside him while he cleaned it. They were both tired enough that the small mistake seemed funnier each time one of them looked up.

Before sleeping, Elyas checked the young spearman’s helmet. It sat straight now. The man was asleep beneath Harald’s wrapped banner, with his hands tucked under his arms.

Chapter Twenty-Eight: The Queen’s Bargain

Fayd arrived with a small chest and no retinue worth noticing.

Veralyn had expected more display. She distrusted the pleasure she took in its absence. Men could dress modestly for an audience as deliberately as they could dress in cloth of gold.

The room had been a dairyman’s parlour. Pale rectangles on the wall showed where shelves had been removed to make space for the officers; the table still smelled faintly of scalded milk. A cracked window had been covered with oiled cloth. Every time the wind moved it, the flame beside the papers leaned towards Fayd.

He bowed. She left him standing while she finished reading the instrument he had brought.

“Maresse wants indemnity for tolls taken since the first rising.”

“Within the stated limit, Majesty.”

“And anything above that limit?”

“Restitution.”

“From him.”

“Yes.”

“Not from my treasury because he’s found his loyalty.”

“No.”

She looked at the next page.

“Corbel wants the disputed levy heard before a court that does not consist entirely of Fenwoods.”

“He does.”

“And Corbel wants payment for grain his men prevented us from buying last month.”

“At the earlier price.”

Tylan, seated at the end of the table, asked how much grain remained. Fayd gave the latest count and the name of the man who made it. A queen’s officer had witnessed the weighing that morning.

Veralyn set the papers down.

“You have been very busy among the people who made my cousin’s defence difficult.”

“I have.”

“Were you as busy when they began?”

“I supported their resistance to certain demands.”

“Elyas says you serve the Vigil.”

“I have served it for a long time.”

“Have?”

Fayd met her eyes.

“I have not ceased to believe that the lands once joined under Utara could possess something better than their present quarrels.”

“And you thought those quarrels needed encouragement.”

“I thought certain loyalties were preventing other possibilities.”

Tylan moved in his chair. Veralyn kept looking at Fayd.

“Now those loyalties are keeping Valdais from your friends’ granaries.”

“Yes, Majesty.”

It was an answer without ornament. She almost preferred an excuse; she would have known what to do with it.

“Your price.”

“A permanent office under the crown. Correspondence, intelligence, the employment of informants, and negotiation under your authority. Its own clerks and a continuing allowance. Direct access to you.”

“After the campaign.”

“Especially after the campaign.”

“Why should I put you in charge of that?”

“Because the people who answer my letters will still exist when your army goes home. Someone will be writing to them.”

“And if I find you writing against me?”

“Then you will dismiss me or try me, according to what you find. I am asking for an enduring office, not immunity.”

She stood and went to the open door. Beyond it, a saddler was working in the sun with a strip of leather held between his knees. He had a dozen small repairs laid out beside him. Each would take less time than one of these sentences. Each mattered to somebody who would be riding into a battle she had not yet made possible.

Jaennis’s private letter was in her pocket. Hear him. Do not let his usefulness persuade you that nobody else can learn the names.

Lyra had said much the same thing more formally.

Veralyn turned back.

“Tylan.”

He had already opened the map.

“Lisle’s men on the Upper Causeway,” he said. “How do ours recognise them?”

“His captain comes to Seniro with the countersealed order. His men carry their own badges. They are not to change them for ours.”

“Good. We know what ours look like. Who stands surety?”

“Lisle’s nephew comes with the first party.”

“And if the captain comes without the forty?”

“He receives pardon for the men and acts covered, on performing the passage. The remaining levy stays owed.”

“That is law. I asked what we do on the road.”

Fayd paused.

“Require him to announce his numbers before he enters it.”

“We will count them,” Tylan said. “If he has fewer, Seniro uses his men to hold the junction and keeps our reserve together. No piecing companies out to make the agreement look complete.”

They went through the other undertakings. Maresse would admit the royal column through the eastern approaches. Lisle would close the Upper Causeway to Valdais’s troops. Corbel would make the barns available to the queen, or destroy their grain rather than let the enemy take it away. None of those promises closed Kettling. Valdais could still withdraw towards it. What he would lose was food for his forward companies and the lateral road by which he could reinforce them, while a royal reserve threatened his way back to the river.

The map had been made before the wet weather. Tylan asked for a length of cord and laid it along the Ashbrook. He used the queen’s knife for Hollin Bridge, a folded cloth for the barns, and three little heaps of salt for the places where the raised roads met.

“Which is Lisle?” she asked.

He put a spoon across the northern road.

“That closes here. Until it closes, Valdais can move men from the Kettling link across our front. We cannot treat it as done because someone has promised it.”

Fayd drew the spoon a finger’s breadth west.

“The captain’s men hold this section.”

“Then that is where we need the report from.”

“And Corbel?” Veralyn asked.

Tylan put a fingertip on the cloth.

“His men are already near the barns. They can take them if the guard is small enough. If it isn’t, fire from the eastern sheds. The wind has been in that quarter for three days.”

“Has anyone told the households nearby?”

“Corbel’s steward is to clear them. That is in the undertaking,” Fayd said.

“Does the grain feed those households?”

“Some of it.”

“Then say where they are to receive food afterward.”

“There is a provision for loss.”

“I asked where they eat.”

Fayd took the document back. For the first time he needed to confer with his clerk. They found the name of a store beyond the planned fighting, then checked that its owner had agreed to admit the villagers. He had agreed to take his sister’s household. The promise extended no farther.

Veralyn waited while another order was written.

The saddler outside had finished one repair. She could hear him testing the leather, pulling it hard enough that the stool creaked beneath him. Her own gloves lay beside the map. The seam at the thumb had opened. She had meant to ask him to mend it before he went.

“How many of the forty can actually hold that road?” she asked.

Tylan looked at the spoon. “Against a mounted patrol, enough. Against a company determined to get through, not for long. We put our men behind them.”

“Then what do the forty give us?”

“Time to reach the junction without fighting the people who own it.”

It was a smaller answer than the word turning had led her to imagine. She preferred it. She could see the men now: Lisle’s men at their own barrier, royal foot coming up behind them, an enemy officer receiving news that the road he meant to use had become uncertain.

“And if Corbel fails?”

“Valdais keeps his food. We can still make his forward position dangerous.”

“If Lisle fails?”

“We keep the reserve east of the bridge. Seniro falls back by the causeway. We do not send horse into the wet ground to rescue the plan.”

“If Maresse fails?”

“We never get that far.”

Fayd stood with his hands resting on the back of his chair. He had brought three undertakings whose clauses fitted one another. Tylan had made them into three men who might be late, frightened, or dead before they obeyed.

Veralyn put the knife back beside her plate.

“You hear what I’m buying,” she told Fayd.

“Yes, Majesty.”

“An opening. I still have to fight through it.”

“Yes.”

“And afterward you keep the office even if the fight goes badly.”

“If the undertakings have been performed.”

She let him hear the silence that followed. Outside, someone called for the saddler to bring his tools to the next wagon. He answered that he had work unfinished here.

“I will put my name to that,” she said at last. “I won’t promise it privately and make you ask Jaennis to remind me.”

Fayd’s gaze moved briefly to Shadowsteel, leaning against her chair. When he looked up again his expression had not changed.

“The warrant should be clear,” he said.

“So should your reports. Elyas distrusts you. Lyra distrusts how people must reach you. Neither has asked me to pretend the carters are still imprisoned. I expect the same accuracy from you when someone you dislike does useful work.”

“You shall have it.”

“We’ll discover that.”

She asked the clerk to read the pardon exclusions aloud before they returned to the appointment. The clerk read every one. Fayd corrected a place-name. Tylan changed the hour at which the road captain must announce his numbers. Veralyn listened without hurrying them, though the light had shifted halfway across the floor while they spoke.

“Maresse’s passage is tested first,” Veralyn said.

Fayd inclined his head.

“Six grain wagons,” she said. “Under an officer I appoint. They pass, unload at Seniro’s northern store, and the officer returns. Then we move the reserve.”

“The store has been emptied,” Tylan said.

“So much the better. We’ll know whose grain is in it.”

She sat again and dictated the office’s terms to her clerk. Master of the Queen’s Correspondence. An allowance specified in money, not a vague claim on whatever could be found. Two clerks initially, more only by warrant. Correspondence received in the office to be copied and registered, with confidential reports retained under seal. Private sources might remain private; orders issued in the queen’s name could not.

Fayd asked that no minister be able to prevent his reports reaching her.

“You may put a sealed report into my hands,” she said. “You may not pretend an instruction is mine because you spoke to me alone.”

He accepted that.

The appointment would take effect upon the undertakings being performed. Its permanence was written plainly. She would not defeat him afterwards with an argument about what she had meant by gratitude.

“You understand,” she said when the clerk had finished, “that I will be choosing which advice to take.”

“I am counting on it.”

That answer pleased her least of all.

She signed anyway.

The wagons went through the following morning. Veralyn waited the entire day for the officer’s return. By evening rain had begun, light and persistent, making the road shine between the campfires.

He returned after dark with mud on his boots and Seniro’s acknowledgement inside an oiled wrap. Hew had passed the convoy. Lisle’s men had opened the Wrenford barrier for the agreed convoy. The grain was at the store.

Seniro had added six words beneath the count.

Bring soldiers before they change their minds.

Veralyn handed the paper to Tylan.

“Wake the captains.”

Part V: The Lords Who Turn

Chapter Twenty-Nine: The Answer Sent Too Late

The first answer Constano received was not addressed to him.

It was a notice nailed to the village hall, ordering able men to report for road service. The seal belonged to Lisle. The notice had weathered at the corners, but someone had put a fresh nail through it that morning.

The handcart stood beneath the hall’s lean-to. Matteo could walk beside it now, for short stretches, and the children took turns riding when the ground was level. The mill from which they had hired it lay three slow days behind them. Jaennis’s carriage had moved to meet them; they had learned that from a woman who had heard it from a carrier, and could not discover where it had moved to.

Constano could walk to the hall door and back. Anything farther cost him a night without sleep. His heel had begun to close, but the foot was swollen through the instep. Sella had found a loose slipper and tied it in place with strips cut from his ruined stocking.

Crane read the notice over his shoulder.

“They’ll want the cart.”

“They may.”

“And men to pull it.”

Constano watched Tavin and Joff move a bench into the sunlight for Matteo. They had become good at lifting together. At Fairport he had known which one made fewer errors in figures; now he knew which shoulder each preferred beneath a load.

“Tell whoever comes to speak to me.”

Three men arrived with a list. They wanted the cart and two adults. There was no officer. The man with the warrant had been a quarry worker before Lisle’s steward sent for him, and still carried a mason’s hammer in his belt.

“Whose soldiers use the road?” Constano asked.

“Ours.”

“Whose are ours?”

“Lord Lisle’s.”

It was the answer to a different question. Constano asked how much road needed repairing.

A washout beyond the quarry, the man said. Two wagon lengths, perhaps three. They had been ordered to carry broken stone and fill it.

“The ditch is blocked,” Crane said from the doorway. “We came past it yesterday. You’ll need to clear that first.”

“You’re a road man?”

“Barges. Water behaves much the same beside them.”

They settled on the handcart for one day, Crane to direct the clearing, and Renn to help. The cart would return before dark. Matteo and the children would remain. The warrant-holder wrote the condition and offered his paper for a witness.

Constano signed.

The man stared at the name.

“Lord Fenwood?”

“One of them.”

“We were told you were dead.”

“Please correct whoever told you.”

The man promised to bring word to his captain. Constano thanked him without allowing the promise to become an expectation.

An Ecktorian factor came to the hall while the road party was away.

He had come for the grain merchant who owned the building. Aldith recognised his seal on the bag and spoke to him before he could leave. By noon he was sitting opposite Constano at a borrowed table, with one of the rescued books between them.
