His name was Orven. He had dealt with Constano’s office twice without meeting him. He named the consignments correctly and knew which agent had delayed payment on the second. When he learned that Constano was alive, he looked relieved for the papers in his own bag before he remembered to express relief for the man.

“I have offers on several of the outstanding notes,” he said. “My principals are buying.”

“At what rate?”

He gave it. Roughly a third of what had been promised.

“And if the goods have already been delivered?”

“The same. There is uncertainty about who can honour the original terms.”

“I can honour them.”

The factor looked at the slipper, then carefully back at the book.

“With respect, my lord, I have come from a town where three men assured me you could no longer do anything.”

Constano closed the cover. Aldith had done her best with the water damage. One corner had remained permanently raised.

“You would buy from the men who supplied the army.”

“Or from their widows. Or their partners. Men who need to pay their own suppliers before the winter trade.”

“And present the whole amount when the queen pays.”

“If she pays. My principals accept the risk that she does not.”

It was an ordinary offer made in extraordinary circumstances. Constano had arranged transactions much like it when a ship failed to arrive and someone needed money before the dispute could be settled. He could not make this factor a villain without condemning several men he had dined with comfortably for years.

“Do you expect my endorsement?”

“No. I thought you should know what is being offered.”

“Then tell your principals I remain alive, and that the obligations of my office remain recognised by me.”

“I will.”

“Will it improve the offer?”

“A little, perhaps.”

After the factor left, Aldith opened the book again. They marked the names of suppliers most likely to sell. There was no money to send them. Knowing who would be hurt first did not keep the hurt from reaching them.

Lucrezia came to the door before they finished.

“The little one has a fever.”

The physician lived half a mile away. He came only after Tavin paid him, and then stayed longer than the payment could have justified.

He examined the child in the light beside the door, listening while Sella described what he had eaten and how long he had been sleeping. He asked for clean water and watched the child drink. No one could tell him whether there had been sickness among the people they had passed. Sella could tell him where the water had come from at every stop.

“Keep him here tonight,” he said.

“We had intended to move if the cart returned,” Lucrezia said.

“Tomorrow, if he is better.”

“And if he isn’t?”

“Then I will come again.”

Constano waited until Lucrezia turned away before asking what they owed. The physician named a sum. They had enough for this visit and almost enough for another.

He offered his ring as security for the remainder.

The physician did not take it.

“Your secretary has told me who you are.”

“That is not a guarantee of ready money.”

“No. It tells me where to send the bill.”

Constano thanked him. He kept the ring in his hand until the man had gone, then put it back on. The skin beneath it was soft and pale, unchanged by the road.

Renn returned near sunset with mud above his knees and a sack of meal given for the work. The handcart was sound. The ditch, he said, had taken the water as though someone had pulled a plug from a bath. Crane had made them clear twice the length ordered, and then the quarry men had insisted on showing him where it discharged.

Constano listened to the whole account before asking about the captain.

“Gone, my lord. They said he had orders for the Upper Causeway.”

“Against whom?”

“They didn’t say.”

He wrote to Seniro.

Lisle is taking men and carts on the northern lanes. Do not assume passage through his land. I have received no answer to my earlier letters. Matteo is living. His sister, the four children, and both nurses are with us. Aldith and the clerks also. There are others missing; I cannot give you an honest total.

He included the grain contracts and the names of agents still awaiting orders. He left out the quantities he could neither remember nor recover from Aldith’s damaged book.

Matteo came inside while he folded the letter.

“I should have brought my wife across sooner.”

Constano had not known what to say the first time Matteo said it. He did not know now. She had been visiting relations beyond Marcott. She might have taken another road. They had exhausted the reasonable possibilities by repeating them.

“We’ll keep asking.”

“That isn’t the same.”

“No.”

They sat together until Lucrezia came to say the child was sleeping and had kept the water down. Matteo went to see him. Constano asked Sella to read the letter back. He was tired enough not to trust what he thought he had written.

When she reached the warning about Lisle, he told her to leave it as it was.

It was the best advice he had.

A reply reached him the following day, carried from one of his grain agents through people employed by Fayd.

The agent had delivered two loads to the army. He had held a third because the destination had changed. A letter under royal authority had redirected it, naming both the old agreement and the person empowered to receive the goods. The agent wanted to know whether Constano confirmed that instruction.

Constano checked the date. It had been issued six days before he wrote his warning about Lisle.

Aldith read it over his shoulder.

“They have the grain.”

“Yes.”

“That is what we wanted.”

“Yes.”

He dictated confirmation. The messenger said he could take a second letter if it was short. Constano wrote to his daughter, in his own hand, while the man ate in the yard.

He told her where they were. He described the foot briefly, knowing she would understand what he omitted, and said they were trying to find the carriage. He asked after Seniro and the queen. At the end he added that he had kept the little drawing of Dunstan’s dog inside his book. He hoped the dog had escaped the quay.

The child was well enough to sit up by noon. The physician came again, took the remaining money without counting it in front of Lucrezia, and said they might travel the next morning if he continued to improve.

That evening a rider came from Jaennis’s carriage. He had asked at every bridge between its new waiting place and the mill. He found Constano because the quarry man had corrected someone who said Lord Fenwood was dead.

The carriage could reach them in two days. The rider had no news of the army beyond a rumour that the queen was coming south. Constano gave him a letter to carry back, then watched him turn onto the road with the easy speed of a man on a sound horse.

He went inside and told the others. Livia asked whether they would be home by the next evening. Lucrezia explained that they were going to a different house.

Constano sat beside Matteo until the child stopped asking which one.

Chapter Thirty: The Men Seniro Must Admit

Lisle sent twenty-eight men, two hours late.

Seniro counted them twice, in case anger had made him careless. Their boots were pale with quarry dust; the last four carried picks instead of spears. The captain stood at the foot of the embankment holding the queen’s countersealed order. Beside him a young man in a good coat tried to look as if he had chosen to be there.

“The nephew?” Seniro asked.

“Yes, my lord.”

“Name?”

“Edren.”

“Can you read, Edren?”

“Yes, my lord.”

“Then read me the number on that paper.”

The young man glanced at his captain.

“Forty.”

Seniro waited.

“Twelve are clearing the washout above the quarry,” the captain said. “The work took longer than we expected.”

“Did you send notice?”

“A rider. This morning.”

“By which road?”

The captain pointed. The road he meant lay beyond the orchard house, in ground the enemy had taken two days before.

Seniro swore. Edren went red.

“The rider didn’t know,” the captain said.

“Neither, apparently, did you.”

Avren took the order and checked the seals. Below them, Hollin Bridge remained standing. The enemy held the western approach but had not crossed in strength. They knew about the work under the abutment; they had watched the mason’s men, and Seniro had made little effort to conceal the watchers who waited with tools on his side.

The eastern approaches from Maresse’s land joined the queen’s road behind the embankment. Royal troops were coming along them in small, tired companies, each stopping to be counted and told where to go. Farther north, Lisle’s section of the Upper Causeway still stood open. His twenty-eight men had come to receive their orders before returning to close it.

Seniro sent Avren to give them thirty royal foot as agreed. He did not divide the mounted reserve to make up the missing twelve. Tylan had been exact about that.

A shout rose from the bank.

One of Lisle’s company had been recognised.

“That one!” A Fenwood soldier came down towards them. “He was at the cart burning.”

The accused man backed into his fellows. The captain put out an arm.

Seniro stepped between them.

“Name him.”

“Daven. My sister’s husband drove that cart.”

“Was your sister’s husband in it?”

“He died the next morning.”

Men stopped unloading the wagon behind Seniro. Daven said nothing. He was young, but not a boy. A healing burn ran along one side of his neck.

“Is he named in the undertaking?” Seniro asked the captain.

“Yes.”

“Is that act?”

“The seizure of the grain carts at the lower lane. Before the agreement.”

“Read it.”

The captain unfolded the list. He found the place and read the names of the wagons and the men. The seizure had been made by men from two estates. Lisle had undertaken restitution as a condition of the pardon. The dead carter’s family was to receive payment through the crown, drawn from Lisle’s revenues, with the amount fixed by a hearing.

The Fenwood soldier stared at Seniro.

“You’re letting him in?”

Seniro wished Veralyn were beside him. She had given the order; he wanted her to see the face of the man who must obey it.

“I am keeping the queen’s terms.”

“My sister has three children.”

“Give Avren their names. The hearing must include them.”

“And he goes back to his supper?”

The soldier pointed at Daven. Daven looked down.

Seniro could not make the provision sound sufficient. He told the soldier to lower his spear. For a moment the man did not move. Then he drove its butt into the mud and walked away, leaving it upright between them.

“Your company stays together,” Seniro told Lisle’s captain. “You take no prisoners into your own keeping, and you enter none of our billets. If another accusation is made, it comes to Avren. If one of your men answers it with a knife, the pardon will not cover what he does today.”

The captain nodded.

Edren remained beside the paper, pale now rather than red.

“You stay with the clerks,” Seniro told him. “You’ll be fed. No one is going to hurt you to make twelve men appear.”

The young man tried to thank him and could not finish. Seniro sent him uphill with a guard who knew how to keep his mouth shut.

Hew arrived before noon with Elyas and the leading Istovar company. He brought back the empty teams from the test convoy to help move the reserve’s baggage. Seniro found that more reassuring than the additional signatures he carried.

“Maresse?”

“Holding the eastern approaches. Against anyone who isn’t named in the queen’s warrant.”

“Does that include Valdais?”

“It does now.”

Seniro took the list and looked at Elyas.

“Your brother sent them.”

“He did.”

“Good. They’ll hold the eastern mouth when we move. Keep them out of the bridge guard until their captain has seen the ground.”

Elyas nodded. He had brought a boy who stood near the horses with the strained attentiveness of someone trying to remember six instructions at once.

“Ailsean’s?” Seniro asked.

“Berent.”

“Show him the marked road before you use him to carry a message.”

“I will.”

“Today. In daylight.”

Elyas took the boy down the embankment himself.

The route was marked with stakes and strips of white cloth. Seniro had made the men space them closely where the road passed a drainage cut. From horseback the ditch looked like firm grass. In the dark a rider could follow it for twenty paces before discovering the difference with his front legs.

Hob and Ralf had tested the route with a loaded wagon. Ralf brought the result: one culvert safe for men and horses, doubtful for the heaviest carts. They had put planks across it, but the supports would need watching.

“Keep the heavy train back,” Seniro said. “A broken axle there holds everyone.”

“And the wounded?”

“Through first when they come. No baggage to block them.”

Hob glanced at Ralf’s torn cuff. Ralf saw the look and pulled it down over the scrape on his wrist. Seniro pretended to be absorbed in the map until they had finished the small argument without words.

The last of the timber had arrived that morning, on a wagon whose driver insisted on finding Pennick before he unloaded. Ralf had been under the culvert. By the time they brought him out, the driver had been threatened by two officers and had shown both the instruction naming the man who must receive his load.

“He did right,” Seniro said when Ralf told him.

“I told them.”

“Before or after they threatened him?”

“After. I was under the road.”

The marshal went down to see the work. The planks were sound oak, wider than the ones already laid across the stonework. A carpenter knelt on the near edge, driving an iron pin through a bored hole. He had put his shoes beside him to keep their soles dry; his stockings were dark with water. Below his knees the brook ran through the culvert in a narrow brown rush.

Seniro crouched beside him. The repair left scarcely room for a cart’s wheels. Beyond either edge, the grass grew out over empty space. A wagon going through in poor light would need a man walking ahead of each wheel.

“Give him a place to stand,” he told Ralf.

“The foot can use the bank.”

“The man guiding the wheel cannot watch it from the bank.”

Ralf looked down, then called for two of the shorter pieces. They would make a footing outside the wheel track. The carpenter laid his hammer down while they were brought. He said the pegs would have to be checked after the first gun passed. Seniro told him that no gun was using this route until he said it could.

The white strips had been cut from good linen. One hanging beside the repair still had part of a hem at its end. A soldier was tying a second strip to the next stake, making the knot so large that it left little cloth to show.

“Loosen it,” Ralf said. “That’s to be seen.”

“Wind’ll take it.”

“Then I’ll send you after it.”

Seniro watched the man untie the knot. He had asked for whatever old cloth the court could spare. Someone had understood what it was for.

At the eastern junction the wagon guard had put a pole across the road between two carts. He lifted it for Seniro, then dropped it in front of the team coming behind him. The driver began arguing at once. His load was bread; men at the bridge had sent for it.

“Who sent?” the guard asked.

“The men who want to eat.”

It was an answer Seniro might have given himself. He stopped to hear the rest.

The driver could name his recipient, a sergeant at the eastern end of the bridge. The guard sent a boy to fetch him. While they waited, a cart came from the bridge carrying a wounded mason. He sat propped against folded sacks, holding his hand up so that blood did not run into his sleeve. The bread wagon had left enough room for him to pass. Its driver fell silent until the cart had gone by.

The sergeant arrived at a run. The guard raised the pole, admitted the bread, and told the next team to wait.

Seniro asked his name and learned which company had supplied him. Ralf wrote both on the answer to the court. The timber had been delivered and used; the route was marked; the guard was in place. Seniro added that men still had to guide the heavier wheels. He did not want the next request for timber refused because somebody remembered that Hollin had already been repaired.

“Send that with the next rider,” he said. “They ought to know it reached us.”

Ralf folded the answer against his thigh. The white strip beside him lifted in the wind and fell across the road again.

Corbel’s messenger came at midafternoon. The barns still held grain. A Vhasian guard had been reinforced that morning. Corbel’s captain could no longer promise to take them intact.

“Can he burn them?” Seniro asked.

“He has the men in the eastern sheds.”

“And the households?”

“Cleared to the store named in the queen’s order.”

“All of them?”

“I saw the last cart leave.”

Seniro wrote the fact for Veralyn and added that he had not seen the cart himself. He sent the messenger back with the hour for action, then sent another man by a different route to watch for the smoke.

Near evening a prisoner was brought from the lane south of Hollin. He had been collecting the carts that supplied the enemy’s forward companies. He wore no armour, carried a short sword he had not drawn, and was more frightened for his mule than for himself.

Avren wanted to know where Valdais had placed his reserve. The man did not know. He knew where he had been told to bring oats.

“Ask him that,” Seniro said.

They gave him a cup of water and let him point to the map. He indicated the Kettling link, then a yard on the nearer side of the junction. One load for the horses waiting there, another held back for animals returning from the forward position.

“Returning when?”

“When ordered.”

“By whom?”

“His Grace’s quartermaster.”

He had the instruction in his pouch. It named the yard and required the returning horse to be fed before the grain moved farther forward. At the bottom a short addition in another hand forbade wagons being left in the junction. Drivers waiting for orders were to pull clear and leave the road open.

Avren read the addition aloud.

No driver is to wait in the junction for leave to proceed. If the yard is full, hold him beyond the turn. An empty road here is worth more than a full yard of grain. Officers who require a wagon will send for it; they will not send the driver back to ask what was meant.

“Who gave you this?” Seniro asked.

“The quartermaster.”

“And the writing below?”

“His Grace came through while we were loading. There were two wagons stopped in the road.”

“Did he move them?”

“He asked whose they were. The officer said they were ours.”

The prisoner glanced towards Avren. He had already discovered how little useful meaning the word had.

“And then?” Seniro asked.

“He said he could see that. He wanted the man who would move them.”

“Was there one?”

“Two drivers. They were waiting to be paid for the last journey. One wouldn’t harness until he had the money.”

“What did Valdais do?”

“Paid him.”

“Out of his own purse?”

“I didn’t see whose purse. The man was paid. They moved the carts.”

The prisoner rubbed the rim of his cup. Seniro waited, thinking there might be more.

“He took the officer’s name,” the man said. “Afterward. He didn’t keep us there while he did it.”

Avren folded the instruction. “A considerate commander.”

The prisoner looked up quickly, uncertain whether he was expected to agree.

“A commander who wanted a clear road,” Seniro said. “Let him finish his water.”

He took the paper from Avren. The last word had been written smaller to fit before the edge. Valdais had not called for a fresh sheet when the first proved inconvenient. He had left an order a carter could understand, found the money required to make it happen, and gone on to whatever needed doing next.

Seniro knew how much difference such a visit could make. He disliked seeing evidence that the man across the brook knew it too.

Seniro had seen that hand on the letter offering the exchange.

Valdais had prepared a route out before he knew whether he would need it.

“He means to go,” Avren said.

“He means to be able to go.”

“Does it change ours?”

“It tells us which road he cannot afford to lose.”

Seniro sent the instruction to Tylan with the location of the yard. He let the prisoner feed his mule under guard. The man had answered what he knew; there was no sense rewarding that by killing the animal he had asked after at every pause.

The twelve road workers did not come. Lisle’s captain sent word that they had been kept near the quarry, waiting on another order from the estate. He had taken the Upper Causeway with the twenty-eight who had answered. Seniro told Avren to record the number actually there.

The queen’s main force came in after the lamps were lit. There was no sudden wall of friendly banners. Men arrived thirsty and had to be placed. Carts stuck. A wheel slid off the reinforced culvert, and twenty men spent half an hour lifting a load they could not afford to spill.

Seniro went to meet Veralyn at the road. She dismounted before anyone brought a stool and put one hand briefly on his arm.

“Your brother?”

“Alive when last seen.”

“Jaennis has sent for him.”

“I know.”

She looked towards the dark line of trees beyond the brook. He had expected questions about the ground. Instead she asked where the wounded would be brought.

He showed her the marked road and the shelter above it. Then they went to find Tylan and hear which companies had actually arrived.

Chapter Thirty-One: The Southern Battle

The frost lay white on the raised road and nowhere in the water beside it. From the eastern end of Hollin Bridge, Veralyn could see the pale strip vanish into mist beyond the brook. The orchard trees stood above it, dark at the roots, with a few leaves showing copper where the first light reached them. Someone had hung a broken harness over the parapet. It was stiff enough to keep the shape of the horse.

Royal foot had crossed before dawn. They held the western end under a low bank, close enough that the men waiting on her side could hear them coughing. Valdais’s forward guard had withdrawn towards the orchard wall after a short fight. Three wounded men had come back over the bridge. The fourth remained beneath a shield on the far side until there was room to carry him.

“Can the guns cross?” Veralyn asked.

“After the foot,” Tylan said. “One at a time. The mason says the bridge will bear them if no one has touched the supports.”

“And has anyone?”

“Seniro’s guard says no.”

She looked down at the opened work near the abutment. A man stood beside it with a mallet and a length of rope. He had orders to leave it alone unless the marshal told him otherwise. A bridge that could be dropped quickly could also fall at an inconvenient moment.

Tylan gave her the count of men present. She read it and put the paper inside her gauntlet. The extra companies she would have liked were not going to emerge because she asked again.

Seniro would send the main attack along the causeway and against the orchard rise, fixing Valdais’s leading troops west of the brook. Maresse’s approaches had brought the royal army to this side without a second battle. Now Lisle must close the Upper Causeway, north of the orchard, and Corbel must deny the barns farther west. Tylan’s mounted reserve could then move along the northern road to threaten the Kettling link behind the enemy.

Veralyn could see the bridge and the first wall. The rest would reach her by messenger.

“Where will you wait?” she asked Tylan.

“Here until the companies are over. Then at the eastern junction, where I can hear from Lisle.”

“And if he hasn’t closed it?”

“We hold the horse. You have room to come back.”

She nodded. A man behind her repeated an order incorrectly and was brought forward to hear it again. That took time. Everything took time.

The first arrows came while the third company was crossing.

They struck the parapet and the road beyond it. One man bent as though picking something up and stayed bent, holding his thigh. His sergeant pushed the next file past him before turning back to help. Veralyn watched the gap close. She wanted to tell them to move faster. The men ahead had nowhere to go until the company at the far end advanced.

Seniro’s horn sounded. The men under the bank rose and moved towards the orchard.

Enemy riders came out of a lane between the trees, trying to catch them before they formed. Archers stood up behind the low boundary wall. Their first flight broke the riders’ order; the second made them turn into the rough ground beside the road, where the horses lost speed. Seniro sent his own horse from behind the bank.

Veralyn’s mount leaned into the bit at the movement. She held it. Her captain looked towards her, waiting to discover whether he was commanding a company or accompanying the queen into a charge.

“Keep the bridge clear,” she told him. “No one pursues beyond the first trees.”

He saluted and went.

The collision was hidden by the bank. She heard it before she saw the first riderless horse. A royal banner advanced twenty paces, stopped, and advanced again. Behind it, the infantry spread into the field and made room for the company coming over the bridge.

By full daylight the western end was secure.

Veralyn crossed with her household. In the middle she passed two men carrying the wounded soldier they had left beneath the shield. He was conscious now. He turned his face away from the cold wind off the brook, and she recognised the childishness of the movement before she remembered how young he was.

On the western side, the orchard rose gently above a strip of open ground. Valdais’s infantry held its wall and the lanes through it. Their banners were steady. The house behind them had lost two shutters, exposing black squares beneath the pale eaves. Seniro stood beside the first gun while the crew unhitched its team.

“Your aunt’s house?” she asked.

“Mine now.”

“I’m sorry.”

“So is the housekeeper. Stay left of the gun when it fires.”

He moved her horse by the rein, gently but without waiting for permission. She let him.

The first shot struck the orchard wall and lifted a short cloud of pale dust. When it settled, the wall still stood. The crew loaded again. Men who had been talking stopped while the rammer went down the barrel.

Her first attack gained a corner beside the lower lane and no more.

A shield came back with someone on it. An officer knelt to vomit beside a tree, wiped his mouth, and ran uphill again. A gunner discovered that the wheel chock had shifted and shouted for men to stop leaning on it. Veralyn had expected noise to make the battle difficult to understand. The separate, ordinary voices were worse. Each asked for something that mattered, and she could not hear them all.

A rider from the north reached Tylan on the eastern bank. She saw the two horses stand together, then the rider go back the way he had come. Tylan crossed to her.

“Lisle has men at the barrier. He hasn’t closed it.”

“Why?”

“A Vhasian supply party is there. His captain says he needs to let it pass before he declares against them.”

“Which way is it going?”

“Towards Valdais.”

She looked at the steady banners above the wall. Another supply party would not decide this by itself. Another delay might.

“Tell him to close it behind them. If he waits for an empty road, he’ll still be waiting tonight.”

Tylan sent the order. He remained beside her while Seniro brought up the second body of foot.

“Do we have word from Corbel?” she asked.

“Not yet.”

At the orchard, enemy men were moving out of the house yard towards the north. Veralyn could not tell whether they were a reserve or men being sent to keep the causeway open. Either way, her first attack had not held enough of them in place.

“Send the second body against the garden,” she said.

Seniro looked towards the low wall on the left, where a section had fallen in. Beyond it lay open beds and the back of the house. Men crossing the exposed ground would be seen from both upper windows.

“I can cover them with the archers until they reach it.”

“Then do that.”

“And afterward?”

“We take the wall.”

He gave the order. Tylan said nothing. She had wanted one of them to tell her the choice was obvious. Both knew that it wasn’t.

The second attack moved across the open ground in a ragged line. A man fell and another stopped; the officer behind them struck the second man’s shoulder with the flat of his sword and pointed him on. Veralyn could see the officer shouting, but could not hear the words.

She rode forward with the supporting horse, far enough to be seen from the wall. The queen’s banner drew arrows. That was part of its work. She had known it when she chose to carry it forward; knowing did not prevent her ducking when a shaft passed close to her face.

The royal foot reached the gap. Men climbed over one another’s shoulders to enter. For a few breaths the banner stayed upright inside the garden. Then a knot of enemy infantry came through from the yard and struck their exposed side.

The banner tipped. Men turned to catch it or defend it, and the space they left widened.

“Now,” Veralyn told the horse captain.

This charge she joined.

They had perhaps forty paces to gather speed. Her horse struck the uneven ground hard enough to jar her teeth. She cut at a spear shaft, felt the shock up her arm, and was carried past the man before she knew whether she had harmed him. Another reached for her rein. She struck with the pommel and saw his mouth open. Then the horse shouldered through the gap beside the wall and she was among her own foot, shouting for them to hold the garden.

For several breaths she could see only the men within reach and the narrow space in which her horse might turn. A hand caught her bridle. She raised Shadowsteel before recognising her captain.

“Back, Majesty. We have it.”

“Then keep it.”

“We will. Back.”

She went. Her horse slipped at the broken wall, recovered, and carried her down to the lower ground. She had to make herself loosen her grip on the sword.

Tylan met her by the gun.

“Your cheek.”

She touched it. There was blood on her glove. She had not felt the cut.

“Do we hold?”

“The garden. The house is still theirs.”

West of the orchard, a dark line began to rise above the trees. At first she thought it was mist lifted by the sun. Then the top spread, brown against the pale sky.

“The barns?”

Tylan watched it for several breaths before answering.

“Where they should be.”

The messenger came soon after. Corbel’s men had fired the eastern sheds. The main granary roof was burning. Valdais’s guard had tried to put it out, then withdrawn towards the Kettling link. The households had been cleared. Two of Corbel’s men were missing.

Veralyn asked the rider whether he had seen the main roof burn. He had.

“Good. Water your horse.”

He went, looking relieved that he would not have to repeat the account immediately.

The smoke thickened. Some of Valdais’s men could see it from the house; she watched heads turn at the upper windows. A new company came down towards the yard. He was strengthening the part of the line she had already attacked.

A second rider reached her from the eastern bank. His horse’s chest was white with sweat. He said the Upper Causeway was closed and enemy wagons were pulling back towards Kettling. Lisle’s captain had sent him from the barrier.

“Did you see them turn?” Tylan asked.

“From the rise, my lord.”

“Which wagons?”

“The covered ones. Going west.”

“How many?”

“Six. More behind the trees.”

Tylan took the paper. There was no signature on it, only the captain’s mark and the hour. He asked whether the rider had come directly. The man had stopped to find a way around a cart stalled on the eastern approach.

Veralyn looked at the smoke, the orchard, the horse waiting beyond the brook. The moment in which Valdais could be made to leave was here. She could feel its shortness as plainly as the rein in her hand.

“The Kettling link,” she said.

Tylan looked up.

“Take the horse there. Close the junction behind his forward line.”

“If Lisle is still admitting—”

“He has closed it.”

“We have his message. We don’t have our officer’s.”

She asked for the distance from Lisle’s barrier to the junction.

Tylan gave it. Then he gave the time the horse would need, allowing for the lane where the companies must pass one behind another. He did not make the answer longer to discourage her. There was room in it for a counterorder if one went soon enough.

“And a confirming rider?”

“If ours left with the first, he may be here shortly.”

“If he didn’t?”

“I don’t know.”

Across the brook, a groom walked between the waiting companies with a bucket. Men lowered their horses’ heads towards it, one at a time. The little pause between animals seemed intolerable to her. She could see what those riders could do at the junction, before the enemy had time to prepare it, with smoke from his food rising behind him. The same men would be less useful there half an hour later. They would still be alive on this bank.

Tylan followed her gaze.

“We can send the leading troop to the barrier. The rest wait until it reports.”

“And give him time to form against it.”

“Possibly.”

She had asked for alternatives. He had given her one that preserved the horses, protected the army’s retreat, and depended on less than the report claimed. She could state every reason to prefer it. None made it feel like the thing to do.

Her hand lay on the pommel. The steel was cold through the glove. She took the hand away and rested it on her thigh.

“The whole force,” she said.

Tylan looked from her to the waiting companies. Somewhere near the garden a man was calling the same name again and again. She had heard him for several minutes without attending to the words. Now the call stopped.

She straightened in the saddle. The junction lay beyond the smoke, out of sight; she found she could picture it more clearly than the garden in front of her. When Tylan turned back, she was ready with the order.

The rider stood silent between them. Veralyn could hear the horse drawing air through its nostrils. She had wanted reports checked by people she chose; she had made that a condition of the whole undertaking. Now the smoke was rising and men were dying in the garden while the confirming rider remained somewhere on a road she could not see.

“Take the last mounted companies,” she said. “Go by the Upper Causeway. If his barrier is open, close it. The junction is the target.”

“Majesty, that leaves you the foot already committed and your household.”

“I know.”

“If the house counterattacks—”

“Seniro holds the bridge. I hold the garden. You take the junction.”

Tylan studied her for a moment. She did not feel hurried now. The separate demands that had filled the morning had become one direction, and she found herself impatient that it should require so many words.

“By your order,” he said.

“By mine.”

He mounted and rode back over Hollin Bridge. She watched him reach the eastern junction, where the waiting companies turned north behind him. The empty ground they left showed how much she had kept unused until now.

Seniro came to her from the guns.

“Vayle?”

“The Kettling link.”

“All the horse?”

“Yes.”

He looked across the brook. Then he sent Avren to reinforce the garden with men drawn from the gun guard.

The correction arrived before any word came back from Tylan.

Lisle had let the six wagons through. They were empty supply carts being sent back for grain, not evidence of a withdrawal. His captain had barred the road behind them, then opened it again when an armed escort challenged him. The royal officer had found him arguing over the order and had insisted he lower the barrier. It was closed now. The time on the new report was later than the time at which Veralyn had sent her reserve.

She read it without moving.

“Send this after Vayle,” she said.

The officer waited. She realised he expected another order: a recall, a change of target, something to acknowledge that the facts had changed beneath the first decision.

“The same target,” she said. “Tell him the road was open longer than we believed.”

The rider left.

At the house, a trumpet sounded. Men emerged from the side gate and came down against the garden in close ranks. Veralyn counted the files until those nearest the wall hid the others. More were still coming through the gate.

Seniro’s men met them at the broken wall. A spear rose above the heads, stayed upright for a moment, then fell towards the garden. She could no longer see the patch of bare earth where her captain had stood. The royal banner moved backwards.

“Bring ours forward,” she said.

She rode as far as the gun. Its crew had stopped loading. Men of both sides were too close together beyond the muzzle; the gunner stood with the rammer held against his chest, watching the wall. One of the archers pushed past him to find a clear shot.

“Not from there,” Seniro shouted.

The archer moved. Veralyn’s horse flinched as the bowstring snapped beside it. She held the rein low and felt a hard ache run from her thumb to her elbow. She had hurt the hand in the charge without noticing when.

A man came from the garden holding his helmet by its strap. He had lost the padding beneath it and there was blood in his hair.

“The captain asks for men at the gap.”

“Avren has sent them,” Seniro said.

“He asks for more.”

Seniro looked towards the bridge. Veralyn followed his gaze. Men stood at its western end, facing the bank and the low ground beyond. She had watched them arrive; she knew their number. Withdrawing them would leave the crossing open to anyone who could get round the garden.

“Who holds your right?” Seniro asked.

The messenger named an officer. Seniro sent him back with an order to shorten the line against the wall and hold the gap. The man looked at Veralyn before leaving. She nodded. He put the helmet on without its padding and ran uphill.

There were no men following him.

The royal banner dipped again. She rose in the stirrups to see beneath the branches. Her horse took the movement for permission and stepped forward. She checked it hard enough to make it toss its head.

Beside her, the gunner had laid his rammer down. He and another man were turning a wheel, trying to draw the gun back towards the path behind them. The men who had helped bring it up were fighting at the wall.

“Leave it,” she said. “Keep the path for men coming back.”

The gunner looked at the wheel, then at the narrow space beside it. He fetched his powder box and carried it out of the way. She watched him set it behind a tree, carefully, as though putting something away before leaving a room.

A shaft struck the banner staff. The bearer shifted his hands below the splintered wood. She asked whether he could hold it. He nodded without looking at her.

From the gap came a shout she could understand: a man’s name, then an order to get up. Other voices took it up. She could see backs now, men in the garden leaning into those in front. An enemy helmet showed between two royal shields and vanished. The line stopped moving towards her.

She waited for it to move the other way.

The bearer slid his lower hand farther down the staff. He had pressed the broken part against his shoulder; small splinters caught in the wool of his coat. A groom approached leading a spare mount. He reached towards her rein, then stopped when he saw she was staying mounted. She had not summoned him. Someone had sent him in case she must leave quickly.

“Behind the gun,” she told him.

He led his spare horse there. She could hear the animal pulling grass while the men above them fought.

The enemy began to come out of the gap backwards. One fell, and the man behind caught him under the arms and dragged him over the fallen stones. The others closed around them. They reached the side gate in a body, carrying two men between them. Nobody from the garden followed.

The royal foot stood bent over their weapons. One man sat down on the wall and had to be lifted by his sergeant when the next order came. The messenger with the ill-fitting helmet appeared at the gap, spoke to someone below him, and disappeared into the garden again.

Veralyn let her heels settle. Her knees hurt. She had been standing in the stirrups longer than she knew.

“Water for them,” she said.

Seniro pointed. Men were already carrying vessels uphill. She watched a bucket pass over the wall, disappear, and return empty. The second took longer. When it came back it was handed to a different man.

No messenger came from the north.

She looked at the watch rider waiting below the bridge and raised her hand. He began to climb towards her before she had decided what to ask. Halfway up he had to give way to a litter. One of its bearers had the poles too high, tipping the wounded man towards the other end. They stopped to change their grip. The rider waited behind them.

“Any word from Vayle?” she asked when he reached her.

“None, Majesty.”

“Is there a man at the eastern junction?”

“Yes, Majesty. Two.”

She could send him to ask the same question there. He would cross the bridge, stop the men watching the northern road, and return with an answer which would already be old. She had sent the correction. There was nothing new to send him.

“Stay where you can see them.”

He saluted and went back down. She watched him all the way, including the place where he moved aside for another litter.

Smoke from the barns crossed behind the house. The shadow of the orchard had moved down the slope. The fire still burned where she had seen it before giving the order. It told her nothing more now than it had then.

She tried to reckon how far Tylan could have ridden. She knew the distance and the narrow place where his companies must pass in single file. She did not know whether a cart stood across it, or how long Lisle’s men had taken to lift their own barrier. She reached the junction in her reckoning, put enemy horse there, and had to begin again.

At the gun, the crew waited for a target. Seniro stood beside them, looking towards the house. Veralyn nearly asked him how long it had been. Instead she eased the rein out of the crease beneath her thumb and made herself look at the gate.

Then the enemy banner above the yard moved west.

It remained upright. The men beneath it withdrew by groups, those nearest the garden fighting hard to cover the others. A trumpet sounded twice beyond the orchard. An answering call came from the direction of Kettling.

“Is he going?” she asked.

Seniro watched through narrowed eyes.

“He’s trying to.”

The message from Tylan reached them on foot. His runner had left a horse with the reserve because there was no clear way back for it. Lisle’s road was shut. The royal horse had reached the eastern arm of the junction and driven off the guard at the fodder yard. Valdais’s leading companies could no longer use the Upper Causeway. They were falling back by the southern bend of the Kettling link, under the cover of his heavy horse.

Tylan could threaten the crossing road. He could not take Kettling with the men he had.

Veralyn read the last sentence twice.

“Pursuit?” her captain asked.

“Keep the companies together. Take the house and the road beyond it. No pursuit into the crossing.”

He went with the order. She dismounted. Her knees shook when she put weight on them.

Seniro handed her a cloth.

“We’ve made him leave Hollin.”

She looked at the orchard. Men were coming out carrying other men. The cheer began behind her, spread over the bridge, and returned from the companies still waiting on the eastern road.

“Yes,” she said.

Valdais’s note came before dark, under a flag sent to arrange the recovery of wounded left between the lines.

You hold Hollin. I acknowledge the field.

My parties will collect our wounded under the terms observed at the bridge.

Your men may do the same beyond the southern lane until the second morning bell.

After that hour I cannot guarantee their safety.

Veralyn read it to Seniro and asked whether the terms could be carried out. He said they could. She accepted them in writing and sent guides to keep the searching parties away from the mined abutment.

Tylan returned after the lamps were lit. There was a new cut across the red repair on his armour. He gave the report standing, with one hand resting on the table while she asked her questions.

“Your men?”

“Still coming in. I will have the names by morning.”

“The junction?”

“Held. Ralf has foot there now. The horse are east of it.”

“Lisle?”

“Performed. Late.”

She looked at him, then at the unsigned message beside the correction. He had brought both back.

“I sent you before we had the second report.”

“Yes.”

He did not say what might have happened. She knew enough to imagine it, and too little to know which possibility he was remembering.

“Eat before you go out again,” she said.

“I was about to ask you the same.”

They sat. The cook brought something hot in a pot too large for the table. Veralyn moved the papers to make room. Shadowsteel leaned beside her chair, its blade clean in the lamplight. Someone had wiped the scabbard as well. Her glove still smelled of the man who had reached for her rein.

She set the glove on the floor and took the bowl Tylan passed her.

Chapter Thirty-Two: What Turning Costs

The man in the ditch wore Lisle’s colours and shouted that he had surrendered.

The two men above him wore Fenwood’s. One held a spear by the middle, ready to thrust down. Elyas reached them at a run and struck the shaft aside with his shield.

“He’s ours.”

“He was theirs yesterday.”

“He’s ours today. Back to the bank.”

The man with the spear recognised him and stepped away. His companion took longer. Behind them, the eastern approach shook beneath a gun team trying to turn without putting a wheel off the reinforced culvert.

Elyas hauled the man from the ditch. It was Daven, accused at the previous day’s muster. He had lost his weapon and was bleeding from a cut above his ear.

“Where is your captain?”

“On the upper road. He sent me to find Avren.”

“Then find him. Along the stakes.”

Daven stared at him.

“Go.”

He went, stumbling once before finding firm ground.

Elyas turned to the Fenwood men. “Names.”

They gave them. He made Hew, who had come up behind him, hear both.

“Back to your section. We settle this when no one is trying to kill us from the other direction.”

The first enemy attack had come along the lane south of the brook. Valdais’s riders had found the edge of Maresse’s approach and tried to cut behind Hollin Bridge. Elyas held the narrowing where the lane met the raised road. If they lost it, wounded men could no longer be carried out and Seniro’s main force would have an enemy at its back.

The companies holding it had been assembled as they arrived: Maresse’s men, royal foot, the Istovar company Harald had sent, and two of Seniro’s wounded who refused to remain uphill. Edrick Vale had spent the first hour making sure his own men knew the badges beside them. It had not prevented the quarrel in the ditch.

Beyond the narrowing stood the field station, screened from the lane by a fold in the ground. Elyas had ridden past it at first light. The shelter was an open-sided cattle shed, with clean straw laid over hurdles and water heating in a black pot. Mara stood beside Gisla, holding a lamp close while the nurse cut a sleeve away from a man’s arm.

She had looked up as Elyas passed. He lifted his hand. Then her patient moved, and she turned back to hold him while Elyas rode on.

He could not see the shelter from here. That was one reason Gisla had chosen it.

Hew had placed the spearmen behind a low retaining wall. Beyond it, the lane narrowed between the ditch and a steep bank. On horseback it looked like an invitation to charge. Standing beside the wall, Elyas could see how little room there would be to turn when the front stopped.

“How many?” he asked.

“Horse gathering beyond the pasture. Foot with them.”

“Coming together?”

“Not yet.”

Elyas put the strongest spears at the narrowing and kept the others back from the wall, where they would not present a neat line to archers. Edrick went along his company correcting the grip of a man who had never faced horse outside an exercise ground. He did it quietly, without making the others watch.

Berent appeared with the remount.

“I didn’t ask for it.”

“Your horse is lame.”

Elyas looked towards the tree where he had left it. The animal held a foreleg clear of the ground. He had been too occupied to notice when it began.

“Good. Keep that one behind the bank.”

“Do you need—”

“A message to Avren. By the white stakes. Tell him horse are coming up the southern lane. We hold the narrowing, but he must send men to the ditch if he wants this approach kept. Say exactly that. Make him repeat it.”

Berent’s mouth tightened.

“By the stakes,” Elyas said again. “No shorter way through the meadow.”

“I heard.”

“Say it back.”

The boy did. Elyas nodded and let him go.

The first riders advanced at a walk, shielding bowmen behind them. Elyas recognised the movement too late to prevent the first flight. A spear beside him dropped as its owner folded against the wall. Hew dragged the man back by his collar.

“Shields up. Don’t answer yet.”

They had fewer archers than Elyas wanted. He put them behind the bend in the wall and waited until the riders came closer. The bowmen loosed again. A horse shook its head violently and turned sideways, forcing the man behind it to stop. Hew’s archers shot through the gap.

The enemy commander sent foot towards the ditch. He was trying to turn the narrowing instead of filling it with dead horses. Elyas took a section down to meet them and found the water deeper than it looked. His boots sank above the ankle. Across from him a man with a short axe was having the same difficulty.

They fought awkwardly, breathlessly, the mud holding their legs. Elyas caught the axe on his shield and shoved forward. The man fell against the bank. Someone behind Elyas thrust past his shoulder. He did not see who.

A hand seized his cloak from behind. He turned too quickly and nearly fell. It was Edrick, pointing to the lane.

The horse were moving.

Elyas climbed out with mud pulling at his boots. The first rider struck the spears and swerved. The second tried to follow the opening and hit the retaining wall hard enough to dislodge stones. Then men and animals jammed in the narrowing, everyone behind pressing on those ahead.

Hew went down.

Elyas reached him before the next horse did. He pulled at the captain’s armour and could not move him. Edrick seized Hew’s arm. Together they dragged him through a gap in the wall while the spears closed behind them.

Hew’s leg lay at an angle Elyas disliked.

“Stay there.”

“Hadn’t intended to dance.”

“Can you feel your foot?”

“Yes. I would rather not.”

A man came through the gap after them with blood running from his nose. Elyas pushed him towards the captain and told him to keep Hew from being stepped on. Then he returned to the wall.

For a few moments he could see no more than the men immediately in front of him. A spear shaft broke. Someone shouted for room that did not exist. His shield had become heavy, the leather behind it slippery. He changed his grip and found he could not close two fingers fully.

He had fought in the west and learned things there that kept him alive now. None helped him see whether Avren was coming.

The relief reached the ditch without banners raised high enough to warn the enemy. Hob led it along the marked route. His men struck the flanking party as it began to climb the bank. The enemy commander turned to look; the men in the narrowing pushed forward while his attention was elsewhere.

The riders withdrew. Some went quickly. Others stayed long enough to help a man on foot reach a spare horse. They had lost the ground at the wall, but had not become a frightened crowd.

Hob came up from the ditch with mud to his thighs.

“Avren sent two sections. Ralf has the northern junction.”

“Berent found him?”

“He did. Gisla has your boy.”

Elyas felt the words in his stomach.

“What happened?”

“His horse stumbled at the broken fence. He came off. Delivered the message, then someone saw he was shaking. She’s looking at him.”

“Can he walk?”

“He walked to the station.”

Elyas looked towards the fold in the ground. He could ride there in a few minutes. He could find out for himself whether the boy’s head had struck something, whether he was trying to be brave, whether walked meant two men had carried most of him.

At the wall, Edrick called for another dozen spears. The enemy foot had stopped retreating.

“Tell me if you hear anything else,” Elyas said.

He stayed.

The noise changed in the afternoon.

A trumpet sounded beyond the orchard, then another farther west. Elyas could not interpret the calls, but he could see wagons moving along the distant road. They were going towards Kettling. Smoke from Corbel’s barns hung behind them, dark enough to hide the farther trees.

“Going,” a soldier said.

“Watch them.”

The lane ahead stood almost clear. Elyas had a fresh horse. He could see a chance to ride into the withdrawing baggage, take prisoners, perhaps reach someone whose capture would give the victory a face.

Then heavy horse appeared at the southern bend.

The woman commanding them wore a pale surcoat. She placed her first rank across the road, facing Elyas’s position, and sent another troop to cover the wagons farther back. When a team stopped, she rode to it and had two riders dismount to cut the damaged harness away. The wagon moved. Her first rank did not.

“Corvaunt,” Hob said.

“Seniro’s dame?”

“The one from the exchange.”

Elyas watched her return to the line. She had left him enough open ground to make pursuit tempting and too little to carry it through before meeting formed horse.

He put his foot on a fallen stone and shouted for the companies to gather.

“We hold here. No pursuit beyond the pasture.”

An officer began to protest. Elyas pointed to the road behind them, where wounded men were coming from the bridge in blankets.

“Those are the people you leave if you go.”

The officer looked and went back to his men.

Lisle’s company brought prisoners down from the upper road. The two Fenwood men who had threatened Daven were still at the wall. Elyas had Edrick send an escort with the prisoners, and told him to keep the escort in place until the royal guard took their names. No one was to be passed from company to company without someone knowing where he had gone.

Berent returned while they were lifting Hew onto a barrow.

There was mud on his face and a clean strip of linen around his wrist. He walked carefully, testing his right foot before putting his whole weight on it. Elyas went to him too fast and stopped too close.

“Your head?”

“I didn’t hit it.”

“Gisla said that?”

“She asked me things. Mara washed my wrist. They said I could come back if I walked.”

“You followed the stakes?”

“They stopped at the broken fence. I went along the bank. The horse caught his foot going down.”

Elyas looked towards the route. He had shown it to the boy in daylight. The fence had been intact then.

“All right.”

“Avren repeated the message.”

“I noticed.”

Berent tried to smile. His eyes moved to Hew’s leg, and the smile failed.

Elyas let him stand without explaining that the captain was alive. The boy could see that. Hew was asking for the barrow to be shifted so his ankle would stop striking the side.

They carried him up with two soldiers. Berent took none of the weight; Gisla had been clear about the wrist. He walked beside them, holding the captain’s loose boot and watching the wheel ruts.

At the station, Mara pointed them towards a clear place beneath the shelter. Her apron had been changed since morning. The clean one was already stained. She asked Hew his name, although she knew it, then which leg hurt and whether he had lost consciousness. Gisla came over while he was answering.

Elyas waited for Mara to look up. When she did, he said he would be at the road.

She nodded. Her hands were occupied holding the split cloth away from Hew’s swollen knee. He went back before either of them could try to make a conversation fit there.

Near dusk he found the queen beyond the orchard.

She had left her horse with an attendant. Men were searching the field under the truce agreed with Valdais. Those who could walk were helped towards the road; those who could not waited beside small lamps brought from the house. The light caught the wet places in the grass and made it difficult to see where the ground was firm.

Veralyn stopped beside a dead man wearing the colours of a middle canton. She crouched, took a loose stone from the edge of the path, and placed it beside his shoulder.

Elyas waited. He thought she might be marking the position for the burial party. She moved to a royal soldier a few paces farther on and did the same, then another man whose coat had been cut away so thoroughly that Elyas could not tell which side he had served.

There was a little blood on her bare hand. A stone had opened the skin below her thumb. She wiped it against her skirt and bent for another.

“Majesty.”

She looked up.

“They’re bringing a lamp.”

“I can see.”

He went to the edge of the path and took a stone himself. When he approached the nearest body she glanced at his hand, then at the place where he meant to put it.

“That man was still breathing when we came,” she said.

Elyas knelt quickly. The man was breathing now, shallowly, through his mouth. He called for bearers and stayed until they had lifted him. When he turned back, Veralyn stood farther along the row.

He could not tell whether she had counted them. He did not ask.

“Seniro has the house,” he said. “The wounded are coming through.”

“Yes.”

“And the road is held.”

“I know.”

She looked towards the darkening smoke beyond the trees.

“It cost what it cost,” she said.

He had no answer to that. The words sounded tired, and exact in a way he could not account for. He took the cloth her attendant offered and held it while she bound her hand. She thanked him, returned to her horse, and let the man help her mount.

At the bridge a herald was rehearsing the proclamation for the companies. He gave the queen’s name and titles, then named the victory at Hollin. He began again when he stumbled over one of the titles. Men carrying a wounded archer waited for him to move out of their way.

Elyas took the herald by the elbow and shifted him to the parapet.

“From there,” he said.

The herald began again. Beyond him, Berent was walking a recovered horse towards the station, slowly enough that the animal’s lame leg did not have to hurry.

Chapter Thirty-Three: The Useful Office

The victory notices had been pasted to the chancery gate before the rooms inside were warm enough to work in without gloves. Lyra passed beneath them carrying two accounts of the same company of soldiers. In one, Lisle had furnished his whole undertaking. In the other, nearly a third had remained on the estate.

The first came from the Correspondence. The second had arrived with a letter about winter cloth.

She put them side by side on Pellam’s table.

Nell had already lit the lamp. Its light made a small yellow circle on the wood; beyond it the room remained grey. Two new clerks occupied desks brought in after the army’s return. There was scarcely space to carry a bundle between them without someone drawing in his chair.

“Which is right?” Pellam asked.

“The household letter, I think.”

“Why?”

“Lisle’s steward’s wife says she was told to keep feeding the men until they were called. She names the houses where they slept and the people supplying them. Some had worked on the quarry road, then returned to their billets. They had never been sent on to the captain.”

“And the other account?”

“It counts the company named in his first promise, then says the captain arrived. It doesn’t count who arrived with him.”

Pellam read both again.

“Who can confirm the feeding?”

“Two suppliers. One has already asked to be paid.”

“Ask the other.”

They sent a clerk rather than a question routed through the same household. By midday they had confirmation of the supplies and a separate statement from the man who had taken the bread to the billets. He remembered the last delivery because the soldiers had complained that victory elsewhere ought to mean meat for them.

Lyra took the papers to Tarr.

The Correspondence had acquired cupboards with locks. She recognised one from an unused room off the gallery; somebody had repaired its loose hinge and put a clean label beneath the keyhole. Tarr sat beside it with his sleeves rolled back, sorting reports into bundles whose arrangement she had learned well enough to follow without asking.

He listened to her account and held out a hand for the first report.

“This should have been separated.”

“From the promise?”

“Yes. The clerk copied the undertaking beside the arrival and allowed one to answer for the other.”

“Did nobody ask Seniro for the number?”

“We have his number. It arrived later.”

Tarr found the sheet. There it was, in the marshal’s blunt hand, the smaller figure unmistakable.

Lyra looked at him.

“It should have been joined before the honours list went forward,” he said.

“Yes.”

She had been in the chancery when the reports arrived and knew how much work there had been. Without the household letter, the error would have gone forward with the list.

Fayd entered while Tarr was attaching the correction.

Lyra had seen him tired before. Today the tiredness showed in the time he took to remove his gloves. He listened, read the two reports, and asked whose letter had first raised the discrepancy.

“Lady Lisle’s household,” Lyra said.

“Through the dowager?”

“Yes.”

“Then Her Majesty has saved us an unnecessary honour.”

He gave the corrected page back to Tarr.

“Lisle performed enough to receive the pardon,” he said. “That isn’t the same as furnishing everything he promised. Keep both statements.”

Lyra waited for a question about Jaennis. Fayd asked whether the suppliers had been paid for feeding the men kept at home.

“Not yet,” she said.

“Then make certain the claim follows Lisle. It doesn’t belong in the queen’s supply.”

It was the correct instruction. She wrote it down and remained uneasy.

The instruments came before the council two days later.

Lyra stood at the side table with Pellam’s register open. She had been told which documents would be needed and where the copies lay. There was nothing difficult about the task until people began asking questions in an order different from the one she had prepared.

Veralyn listened to the account of the lords’ performance without interruption. The cut on her cheek had closed. Beneath it, a faint line of new skin caught the light when she turned her head. She seemed less interested in the room than on the occasions Lyra remembered from Tarlington, or perhaps more exact about which part of it deserved her attention.

“Maresse?” she asked.

“Performed the passage, Majesty. His captain held it under the Prince’s command.”

“Corbel?”

“The stores denied as agreed. The barns burned before Valdais could recover them.”

“Lisle?”

“The road closed. Fewer men than promised, arriving late.”

Veralyn looked at Fayd.

“Enough to do the thing?”

“Yes.”

“Not enough to claim the rest.”

“No, Majesty.”

The corrected honour was set aside. The pardon remained. Lyra watched the two decisions enter separate lines and wondered how often people later reading a single name would understand the difference.

Then the appointment was read.

Master of the Queen’s Correspondence. Continuing authority to receive intelligence, maintain correspondents, employ messengers and confidential agents, and negotiate within warrants issued by the crown. A fixed allowance, staff, rooms, and direct access to the reigning queen. Orders in her name were to be registered. Reports which could not safely be made public were to be kept under seal rather than allowed to disappear into the recipient’s private possessions.

Fayd listened as carefully as any man hearing terms he had not helped draft. When the reading ended, he affirmed the undertaking and knelt.

The office had existed in people’s habits for weeks. Now it existed in the form by which another person could inherit its cupboards, its servants, and the right to ask the same questions.

Veralyn gave him the instrument.

A councillor asked whether the western evidence created any impediment to the appointment. His tone was mild enough that Lyra knew he had prepared the question before entering the room.

Pellam called for the relevant account.

She brought it.

“You were present?” the councillor asked.

“For the bargain and its performance. Not for every act described in these papers.”

“And Master Fayd’s connection to the Vigil?”

“Was acknowledged in the later meeting. The record of that meeting is here.”

“Do the papers show that he directed the acts under examination?”

“Some name people he dealt with. That does not establish that he directed every act attributed to them.”

The councillor appeared dissatisfied, though she could not tell whether he had wanted a stronger accusation or a cleaner assurance.

“You can say no more?”

“I can show you what the witnesses said separately from what the documents establish.”

Veralyn held out her hand.

Lyra gave her the two sheets. The queen read the passages Pellam indicated. Fayd remained standing beside the chair, offering no assistance.

“The appointment doesn’t dispose of the western proceedings,” Veralyn said. “Keep them where they belong. If there is an accusation to be made, make it properly. If there isn’t, I won’t invent one to avoid paying for a service I asked for.”

Pellam entered the decision. Lyra took the papers back.

Other instruments followed. Restitution for seized goods. Provision for the wounded. Authority to inspect the disputed drainage before the winter floods. A confirmation of command for Tylan Vayle, to serve as Lord Marshal.

Nobody objected to that one.

Lyra looked round when the silence continued beyond the usual moment for a reservation. Tylan stood near the door, his hands folded behind him, as if somebody else had been named and he were waiting for the next business.

Veralyn glanced towards him.

“Will you accept?”

“Yes, Majesty.”

“Good. We may need you to continue doing the work.”

A few people laughed. Tylan’s mouth moved slightly. Jaennis, seated beside the queen, watched both of them and then looked down at the papers in her lap.

Fayd’s further request came after the formal business, when the room had emptied enough for individual voices to carry without being raised.

He asked leave to open correspondence with houses beyond the present war: former imperial families, holders of old privileges, people whose allegiance might be cultivated before another army made a claim upon it.

Lyra wrote allegiance and waited for the name to follow.

“To Your Majesty herself,” he said.

Jaennis’s hands became still.

Lyra noticed because she had been turning a folded paper between her fingers during the preceding explanation. The paper stopped moving between her fingers.

“What will you offer?” Veralyn asked.

“A hearing. Recognition that their concerns need not pass through the men who currently profit by keeping them apart.”

“Which men?”

He named three. One had supported Valdais’s passage. Another had stayed neutral at considerable advantage. The third appeared in the western papers as a recipient of correspondence whose purpose remained disputed.

Jaennis asked about him first.

“He knows the roads,” Fayd said.

“So do carriers.”

“He also knows who can close them.”

“And who remembers that his family once collected money on them. You haven’t ceased to enjoy old claims.”

“I prefer to hear them before they arrive with soldiers.”

Veralyn took the list. She read it more slowly than Lyra expected, pausing at two names.

“You may prepare the letters. They come to me before they leave. No territories promised. No titles. No pardon for anything not already covered.”

Fayd bowed.

“And no offer of marriage,” Jaennis said.

Veralyn looked at her. “Especially not that.”

The laughter this time was easier. Lyra wrote the restrictions while they were still clear in her mind. Then she returned to the earlier word and underlined it so she could find it again.

Tarr offered her a place in the Correspondence that afternoon.

He did it while she was returning borrowed papers, with no effort to make the moment impressive. There was work examining accounts from different sources, checking the same crossing under different names, identifying which statements depended on a man already interested in the answer. She had shown she could do it. The office could pay her.

“Under you?” she asked.

“For the daily work. Under Master Fayd for the office.”

She looked towards the new cupboards.

“May I think about it?”

“Certainly. I need an answer by the end of the week, because the work will not wait longer than that.”

It was fair. He had not tried to make her caution seem insulting.

Pellam’s offer was less elegantly timed. He made it while struggling with a drawer which had swollen in the damp. Lyra helped him free it, and he told her that a continuing post had been authorised for the examination of petitions and witness accounts.

“Here?”

“Yes.”

“Doing what I’ve been doing?”

“More regularly. With wages and responsibility for the reports you sign.”

“Who receives them?”

“The chancery. Matters requiring the chancellor’s attention go through the proper office. You may bring a concern to me directly, and I will tell you if I cannot act on it.”

She thought of the two offers while walking back to her desk. Fayd’s office would be better equipped. Its messengers would travel farther. Tarr knew things she wanted to learn and had not withheld them merely because she worked elsewhere.

She also remembered the two accounts of Lisle’s men. Both had been available. Only one had gone forward until a letter from a household disturbed it.

She accepted Pellam’s post the following morning.

Tarr took her decision with a nod.

“We’ll read your reports anyway,” he said.

“Then I hope you’ll tell me when I’ve made a mistake.”

“If you promise to continue doing the same.”

She did.

Ysolt invited her to supper on the day the appointment was entered.

Her house stood in a narrow street behind the chapel, with a step polished by rain and a little sheltered place beside the door for wet shoes. A woman who had kept house with her for thirty years let Lyra in, took her cloak, and told her that Ysolt was pretending not to wait for news.

“I am waiting for the soup,” Ysolt said from the next room.

“Then you will be delighted that both have arrived.”

The room was warm. There were no fine hangings, but books stood on two shelves and a cat had claimed the cushion nearest the hearth. Lyra sat where she could see the door until the older woman told her she need not receive visitors on their behalf.

They ate fish with herbs and bread made that morning. The conversation went where it pleased. For a while nobody asked about the court at all.

When Ysolt finally asked whether she was content with the post, Lyra found the answer more difficult than she expected.

“I think it’s useful.”

“That wasn’t my question.”

“I know.”

The older woman waited.

“I wanted to know what to do next,” Lyra said. “Now I do, for a while. I hadn’t expected that to be enough.”

“Is it?”

“For a while.”

Ysolt nodded and passed her the bread.

On the walk home, Valora was lit for the victory. Lamps burned in upper windows, and boys were carrying a painted board towards the place where the public proclamation would be read. Lyra stepped aside to let them pass. One complained that the painter had made the queen’s horse too large. Another said horses ought to be large when they won battles.

She reached her room without needing a lantern of her own. The window looked into a court instead of onto the gallery where people fetched blankets. She shut the door, set her notebook on the table, and took Gyllan’s whetstone from the pocket in which she still carried it.

A letter from Amru waited beside the lamp. He had answered the request for written testimony and named the matters which could not be settled in her absence. She read them carefully. The hearing had not ceased to concern her because she had found work somewhere else.

She set out a clean sheet for the morning, then opened the notebook to the page where she had underlined allegiance.

Chapter Thirty-Four: The Celebration

The letter from Jaennis’s father arrived on the day Fayd received his appointment.

Pellam brought it himself. The outside had been folded and refolded until the paper was soft at the corners. Someone had written a new destination above the first, then crossed that out too.

Jaennis knew the hand before she saw the name.

For days she had known he was alive from people who had seen him, or spoken to people who had. Now he addressed her himself. He described the foot briefly, reported a child’s fever, and asked after Seniro and the queen. He had kept the drawing of Dunstan’s dog. He hoped the dog had escaped the quay.

She read that sentence twice.

“The carriage?”

“Its rider found him. They are bringing it down to the village.”

“Does the physician know about the child?”

“He will receive the copy today.”

Pellam laid Seniro’s copy of an earlier letter beside hers. Her father had warned against passage through Lisle’s land and named grain agents who might still be awaiting instructions. One had already entered Fayd’s correspondence. Another’s store had been emptied by Valdais’s men.

The warning had been reasonable when he wrote it. She had spent the previous evening hearing men praise how swiftly the crown’s new arrangements had overtaken old difficulties.

“Has Veralyn seen these?”

“No.”

“She will. Send my father an answer by two carriers. Tell him I have his letter. Tell him the carriage is coming and ask him to wait for it.”

She paused, looking at the little space he had left before his signature.

“Tell him I have sent shoes.”

Pellam’s mouth softened. He went out while she was still reading. When the door closed, she pressed the paper to her mouth, carefully, avoiding the ink.

The public hall smelled of warm wax and wet wool. People had come in from streets hung with cloth, bringing the day’s weather into the bright room on their hems. The lamps were reflected in polished cups along the sideboard. Beneath the dais, servants kept a narrow way clear so the people summoned for honours could reach the queen without climbing over one another.

Jaennis had chosen a gown that allowed her to stand beside Veralyn without resembling either a rival sovereign or a widow brought out for the occasion. The choice had taken longer than she cared to admit. Joan had rejected the first sleeve before Jaennis did and saved her a quarter of an hour.

Veralyn wore the dark gown laid out for her and no jewel except the one at her throat. Shadowsteel hung at her side. It spoiled the fall of the skirt. Jaennis adjusted the cloth before they went in, then stopped when she saw Veralyn watching her hands.

“You needn’t wear it tonight.”

“I know.”

That was all. Jaennis smoothed the cloth once more and took her arm.

The herald proclaimed the victory in Veralyn’s name. The room answered loudly enough that one of the musicians missed the next note. Jaennis felt the queen’s arm remain still beneath her own. She had expected a smile, perhaps the quick aside Veralyn used when ceremonial language became too much for her. None came.

When the herald finished, Veralyn inclined her head and accepted the acclaim.

The honours followed. Jaennis had read every name. She knew which had been removed after the report from Lisle’s household, which widows would receive provision, and which officers would be displeased that service had been acknowledged without promotion. Knowing did not prepare her for the man who came forward using two sticks and had to stop halfway to the dais.

Veralyn came down one step to meet him. She did not make him kneel. The room grew quiet enough to hear the sticks scrape when he turned away.

Tylan received his public confirmation as Lord Marshal without a word of opposition. He bowed, accepted the instrument, and seemed ready to return to the door until Veralyn caught his sleeve.

“Stay where I can find you for the next hour.”

He looked down at her hand. “I had intended to eat.”

“So had I.”

She smiled then, and Jaennis felt relief before she noticed how naturally Veralyn’s fingers rested on his arm. With other men the queen made room or withheld it. With Tylan she seemed not to measure the distance at all.

Jaennis told a servant to see that the new marshal received a plate before anyone came to argue with him. He heard, thanked her, and moved to the sideboard as though this were the more consequential appointment of the evening.

A councillor began on the question of future security. He spoke of the succession, taking care not to name marriage until he had made it appear that the subject concerned everyone except the woman standing before him.

Veralyn let him finish the sentence.

“Tonight I am receiving people who have returned from the war,” she said. “Bring the matter to council on its proper day.”

He bowed. Jaennis drew her towards the next group before he could discover a reason that tonight was the proper day.

The musicians began while the last honoured officers were still finding their places.

Jaennis had put them at both ends of the gallery so that the sound would carry without one set having to play against the noise of the entire room. The nearer players took the tune; the farther answered as people moved towards them. Along the walls, candlelight found the raised threads of the hangings and made the old hunting scenes appear to move. A white hound emerged from shadow when a servant passed with a lamp, then vanished again behind the shoulder of a woman in silver-grey.

The wax had cost more than Jaennis first authorised. She had approved the extra after seeing the gallery half lit, its far end like an empty house. Tonight everyone would be able to see the people they had come to admire or resent.

At the long table, late peaches lay in wine beside a dish of thin orange slices. The oranges had come upriver before the sack and been kept in the coolest cellar. The cook had asked whether she meant to serve the last of them. Jaennis had said yes, then changed the order so that half went to the smaller room where the wounded officers ate. She did not want them sent a second-best supper because they could not climb the gallery stairs.

A serving man paused at the wrong chair with the Ecktorian envoy behind him.

Jaennis saw the pause from halfway across the room. The envoy was looking at the places already occupied: the royal councillors nearest the queen, the captains returned from the campaign, a representative of the houses which had provided the relief. His own chair stood one place farther down than the man had expected.

He caught her eye and waited for her to recognise an oversight.

She went to him.

“The light is better here,” she said. “You can see the dancers without turning.”

“Your Majesty is attentive to every comfort.”

“I hope so.”

His glance moved to the place above his. It belonged to the woman representing the Fairport guild households, dressed in dark wool with an old chain at her throat. She had neither the envoy’s rank nor his confidence in a room where rank was examined closely.

“The arrangements are unusual,” he said.
