“So was the year.”

“May I convey that your court intends a permanent alteration in precedence?”

“You may convey that I have placed tonight’s guests. If your master wishes to discuss standing at future occasions, the chancery will receive him gladly.”

She let him consider the distinction. He could make the matter public and turn a place at supper into an objection to honouring people whose houses had burned. He could also sit, eat excellent food, and write whatever displeased account he wished afterward.

“I would not interrupt the queen’s pleasure,” he said.

“How kind.”

Jaennis introduced him to the guild woman before withdrawing. She knew the names of ships his countrymen owned and had opinions about their unloading charges. Within a minute he was listening closely enough to forget his chair.

The first dance took the room in two lines. Margery stepped into her place with a woman Jaennis knew by sight and had never spoken to at length. They met hands, separated, and returned laughing when a gentleman behind them misunderstood the turn. Catrin stood beside Joan near the musicians, watching the feet until she knew the pattern. When the next invitation came, she accepted it.

Jaennis watched her enter the line. The place beside Veralyn remained unclaimed for the moment. She could have sent Margery there at once. Instead she let the dance complete itself. Margery had come to enjoy the evening, and there was pleasure in watching her do so before it was put to any use.

Veralyn found Jaennis at the edge of the floor.

“Who taught them that turn?”

“The dancing master. Shall I have him examined?”

“It was the gentleman’s fault.”

“He will be relieved that you distinguish.”

The queen laughed. Her repaired shoes made almost no sound on the boards when she stepped beside Jaennis. She had chosen them without remark. Jaennis had wanted thanks when she sent for the repair; now their easy fit pleased her more than thanks would have.

“Dance?” Veralyn asked.

“Can you remember the order?”

“Walk me through the first part.”

She knew it. Jaennis could tell by the confidence with which she offered her hand at the right moment. She wanted to be led once, without the room asking whether leading her conferred authority over anything else.

They entered the figure together. Faces passed on either side, familiar enough that Jaennis could acknowledge them with a small turn of the head while attending to Veralyn’s hand. The queen moved well, lifting the heavy skirt just enough to clear her shoe. She had a line of new skin on her cheek and a rough place at the base of her thumb which caught against Jaennis’s glove.

At the return they were close for a breath, then separated again. Veralyn smiled at her across the space. Jaennis forgot the envoy, the wine, the women waiting for introductions. She wanted the next turn to arrive sooner.

When the dance ended, Veralyn kept her hand a moment longer than the figure required.

“You’ve done all this.”

“With considerable assistance.”

“I know. I meant you.”

Jaennis looked away first. Across the room a servant was trying to lower a smoking candle without dripping wax on a councillor. She found the ordinary hazard useful until she trusted her face again.

The masque followed. Jaennis had cut it by half at rehearsal and removed a speech explaining why the river had favoured the queen. The river, represented by three children old enough to remember their steps and young enough to enjoy the silver fabric, crossed the floor without stating an opinion about the war. Two adult performers quarrelled over a little painted bridge until the smallest child carried it away. The room laughed when they discovered its absence.

Veralyn watched without laughing. She looked towards Jaennis at the moment the bridge disappeared.

“Do they give it back?”

“At the end.”

“To whom?”

“To the man who brought it from the carpenter.”

The queen smiled then. “Good.”

Jaennis could not tell whether the answer had amused or reassured her. She called for more wine and asked whether the cut on her hand needed a fresh dressing. Veralyn said it did not.

Tylan was discovered near the sideboard halfway through the next dance. A woman who had known him since he was a boy took his plate away while Jaennis brought Veralyn to him. He looked from the plate to the floor and accepted what was plainly unavoidable.

“One,” he said.

“One,” Veralyn agreed.

He was too large for the intervals the dancing master had allowed. The couple ahead moved farther apart. Tylan watched the feet nearest him with the concentration he ordinarily gave to an uncertain ford, and completed the first figure without touching anyone he should not.

At the second, the queen turned early. He checked his step to avoid her and lost the beat. She laughed, apologised, and promptly did it again when the figure repeated. Jaennis saw him look down at her. His mouth moved; Veralyn laughed harder. Whatever he had said had been for her alone.

When the music ended, he returned to the place where his plate had been. The woman who had taken it had kept it warm beside the fire. He thanked her with such unmistakable gratitude that she promised to rescue him from the next invitation as well.

The queen came back flushed and thirsty. For a little while, as Margery approached and asked what Vayle had said, Jaennis could see the woman who had written of a northern song in the small hours of a campaign. She was here, wanting wine, wanting an answer worth teasing, finding faces in the room she wished to know better.

A man stepped forward to report that the search parties at Hollin had finished bringing in the dead. Jaennis saw Veralyn’s attention narrow before he reached the number.

“Tomorrow,” she told him softly.

He stopped. The queen had heard enough to know what she had done.

“I should hear it.”

“You will. Tomorrow, with the report before you.”

Veralyn looked towards the dancers. A little of the colour had gone from her face.

“How many still unnamed?”

The man answered. She repeated the number once and told him to have the lists ready in the morning. He withdrew, grateful to have completed his duty without being made to guess whose wish prevailed.

Jaennis took the queen’s empty cup.

“Come upstairs. There is music you needn’t stand up for.”

A councillor approached before Veralyn could answer. Jaennis left her to him and went ahead to see that the smaller room was ready. Behind her, the next dance began without waiting to see whether the sovereign meant to join it.

In the upper room, the linen had gone to its recipients and the rugs had returned to the floor. The fire had been allowed to settle before the guests arrived, so the heat came evenly instead of driving everyone away from the hearth. Pears stood in a shallow dish beside nuts and small cakes. Margery wore green, with the silver pear at her shoulder. Joan had chosen her own chair; Catrin sat beside her, close enough to speak without having to offer every remark to the room.

Veralyn arrived late. Jaennis took her hand before anyone else could claim her.

“Come here.”

She led her to the window, turned her face towards the lamp, and inspected the healing cut.

“Does it hurt?”

“Only when people ask me to look at things.”

Jaennis kissed the uninjured cheek.

“I was going to be very cross with you.”

“You can still be cross.”

“Later.”

Veralyn’s hand settled at her waist. Jaennis leaned into it, allowing herself the pleasure before she began arranging anything.

“My father’s written.”

“I heard. Is he coming?”

“As soon as the carriage reaches him.”

“Good.”

“He warns us about Lisle.”

Veralyn’s hand tightened briefly.

“He was late,” she said.

“My father?”

“Lisle.”

“I know.”

“No. I mean when we needed the road shut. The first report was wrong.”

Jaennis looked at her. “But it closed.”

“Yes.”

“And Tylan got through.”

“Yes.”

The queen’s gaze had moved beyond her to the little table where the cups stood. She looked as if she were calculating how many more people might arrive. Jaennis touched the back of her hand.

“You’re here now.”

Veralyn looked at her again and smiled. “So you keep telling me.”

Margery approached with wine. She offered the first cup to Jaennis and the second to Veralyn.

“I understand your cook has survived a campaign under terrible conditions.”

“My cook complains too much.”

“Then he and my husband would agree. Both believe I ask unreasonable questions about dinner.”

“What questions?”

“Why it costs so much. Why there isn’t more of it. Why a man who won’t eat onions must describe them for half an hour.”

Veralyn laughed, low and surprised. Jaennis had missed that laugh more than she had admitted in any letter.

“Sit with us,” she said.

Margery did. A little later Veralyn drew her into an argument about a song, claiming the version sung in the north was better. Margery challenged her to prove it. The musician, who had been instructed to wait for a suitable moment, looked at Jaennis and received no help. He chose one himself and began the tune.

Veralyn turned.

“You found it.”

“I sent it to you.”

“I thought you’d had someone improve it.”

“The northern singer charged too much to permit improvements.”

She smiled at that. When the second verse began, she looked down into her cup. Jaennis could not tell whether she was remembering the words or the woman who had first sung them. Then Margery supplied a line and Veralyn disputed the last word, and the room resumed around them.

Joan laughed at something Catrin said. Catrin had taken off the stiff ornament at her wrist and laid it beside her plate. Jaennis saw how much more easily she reached for things without it, and resisted the temptation to ask whether she was enjoying herself. A person who had refused one arrangement need not be made to approve every subsequent kindness.

When Veralyn rose to sing, the sword caught the arm of the chair. She freed it impatiently. The musician moved his instrument away from the scabbard before she stepped past him. His fingers missed a note, then found the next.

Jaennis thought she should have had the chairs set farther apart.

The queen sang badly at first. She had been shouting orders for days and would not begin lower when advised. At the second attempt she found the right register. Margery joined for the answer; Joan supplied a line neither knew. Catrin clapped when they finished, and Veralyn bowed so extravagantly that Jaennis reached out to keep her cup from spilling.

“You’re tired,” she said.

“I’m thirsty.”

“Both may be true.”

Veralyn leaned down and kissed her before taking the cup back. The room did not stop. Someone passed the pears; the musician turned a page. Jaennis felt the kiss warm in her face and knew she was smiling too openly to pretend it had been casual.

Margery moved to make room on the broad seat. Veralyn sat between them, still holding Jaennis’s hand.

“Your second verse was wrong,” Margery said.

“Was it?”

“Entirely.”

“Then you must teach me.”

“I had hoped you might ask.”

Jaennis watched the interest wake in Veralyn’s face. The old people said Baldvyn had looked at women that way, with the whole of his attention suddenly available. Her father had spoken of it once, when someone praised the king’s devotion to Elianor as though it answered what he had done to her. Conlin had put down his cup and said that nobody had ever doubted the devotion. The table had gone quiet.

Jaennis had asked for Margery to be here. She wanted her here still. There was a sting in it, sharper than when she had imagined the evening. Then Veralyn’s thumb moved over her knuckles, familiar and absent-minded, and Jaennis let herself want both the woman beside her and what might happen next.

Fayd waited in the outer gallery with a dispatch that could not safely wait until morning.

Jaennis went out to receive it. Veralyn remained inside, listening to Margery attempt the disputed verse. The sound followed Jaennis until she closed the door.

“A patrol near Kettling,” Fayd said. “No change in the main position. The marshal should see this before his morning orders.”

“Then send it to him.”

“A copy is already going.”

She read the short account. It gave a time, a place, the strength seen, and the distance from which it had been observed. His office had learned from the reports they had spent so many evenings correcting. She would not resent the improvement merely because he had made it.

“Nothing requiring her tonight,” she said.

“Unless she wishes to change the instructions for the patrols.”

“She will read it before she sleeps.”

He inclined his head. The light from the gallery window caught the silver threads at his cuff. He had dressed for the ceremony, then returned to work without changing. Jaennis wondered whether Aurel Dace had ever found him sitting at a table in clothes meant for an evening he had forgotten to attend.

Through the door came Veralyn’s laugh.

Fayd looked towards it. “She seems well.”

“She seems tired. I intend to let her forget that for an hour.”

“It has been an expensive campaign.”

“She has spent the whole evening telling me so.”

“Then she understands what must be made of the victory.”

Jaennis folded the dispatch.

“Tomorrow you may tell her what you think that is.”

“I am here to serve her.”

“So am I.”

He waited, courteous enough to let the answer remain complete. She disliked him for recognising that there was more.

“And to keep her,” she said.

His expression did not alter. He bowed and went towards the stairs. Jaennis watched until he passed beyond the last lamp, then returned to the room where Veralyn was holding her place.

Chapter Thirty-Five: The Household He Chooses

Berent had brought the borrowed girth back himself.

It would have been easier to send it with a servant. The groom at the riverward hall would have accepted it, and the boy would have saved two days on the road. Instead he had asked leave, found a carrier going that way, and returned with a signed note that stated the leather was sound.

Elyas read it in the courtyard at Tarlington.

“You didn’t need a signed note.”

“I thought I might.”

“Fair enough.”

The boy had grown thinner. His sleeves were too short, which seemed an unreasonable thing for a war to have accomplished. Elyas made a note to ask about a tailor and realised, with some discomfort, that he did not know which person in his future household would arrange such matters.

He could ask Mara. He should not assume she would arrange everything he noticed and nothing he failed to.

“Come inside,” he said. “Have you eaten?”

“Yes.”

“What?”

“Bread. Cheese. An apple.”

“Good.”

“I can remember to eat.”

“On some days.”

Berent grinned and went ahead.

The house had changed very little. That was becoming easier to bear. There were new scrapes on the passage wall where furniture had been moved for the wounded. A window that had stuck all Elyas’s childhood now opened, because someone had finally repaired it when a sick man needed air.

Braedyn’s study remained shut. Oria kept the key. Elyas did not ask for it.

Mara was in the room they had been lent, surrounded by papers. She had acquired a small table of her own and placed it where the light was better than at his.

“Your brother’s letter came.”

“Have you read it?”

“No. It was sealed.”

He broke the seal. Harald regretted the manner in which Elyas had chosen to announce so consequential an intention. He hoped there would be no haste. There were matters of property, expectation, and the dignity of the house which could not be settled by personal feeling.

At the end Harald asked when the company might return. He had received Elyas’s report and the captain’s list of wounded. The families would be provided for at home; he required the names of those unable to travel before making arrangements.

Elyas read the paragraph twice. His brother had sent the men. They had fought under Elyas’s orders, and some would never come back. He would answer every question Harald had asked.

“Well?” Mara asked.

“He would prefer we were less consequential.”

“Can we manage that?”

“Probably not.”

He gave her the letter. She read it without changing expression, then laid it beside a list of household expenses.

“I’ve been asking about places.”

“So have I.”

“What did you find?”

“A house with an excellent stable and a roof described as mostly sound.”

“How much of it?”

“The stable or the roof?”

“You’re going to be difficult about this.”

“Yes.”

She showed him her own list. One house had rooms enough but no ground. Another stood too far from the road for anyone without a horse to reach conveniently. The third needed repairs, but its present tenants were moving to live with their daughter after the harvest. Mara had asked whether they wanted to leave. She had not merely accepted the owner’s account.

“What would you do there?” he asked.

“What would we do there?”

He sat beside her.

She wanted a garden. She wanted a room where she could keep accounts and receive people without sending them through a bedchamber. She wanted wages paid on a day everybody could name, and space for the people who would actually live with them rather than for impressive visitors who might come once a year.

He wanted Berent to have somewhere to learn that was not always a camp. He wanted horses, books, a table long enough for friends, and a door he could enter without first deciding which part of himself belonged on the other side.

Gisla would keep the station open through the winter. Some of the men could not yet travel, and others had no household prepared to take them back. Mara meant to go twice a week, more often when needed. She had asked the nurse what she still had to learn and been given an answer long enough to keep her occupied until spring.

“Can you get there from this house?” Elyas asked.

“By the road. There’s a low stretch I need to see after rain.”

“We’ll ride it before we agree.”

“She says I must learn the dressings without having her check every one.”

“Will you?”

“Yes.”

She said it without looking up, occupied with the distances she had written beside the houses. He liked the certainty, and the fact that it required nothing of him except to understand that the days were hers.

They disagreed about the stable. She was right about the expense. He remained attached to it anyway.

A knock interrupted them. Berent brought a folded note from the steward and remained by the door.

“What is it?” Elyas asked.

“Captain Hew. I heard he’s walking with sticks.”

“He is.”

“Can I write to him?”

“You can.”

“I wanted to ask about the company. The men who were with us.”

“Then ask.”

The boy nodded, but did not leave.

“What else?”

“Will I still be with you? When you’re married?”

Mara looked at Elyas. This answer was his to give.

“If you want to remain my squire, yes.”

“I do.”

“Then we’ll need a room for you.”

“And somewhere for your things,” Mara said. “Preferably not the whole passage.”

Berent looked towards his bundle, which was occupying most of the doorway.

“I was going to move that.”

“We have every confidence,” Elyas said.

After the boy went out, Mara added a room to the list.

News arrived in pieces over the following days. Seniro remained with the army, arguing over the ground the turned lords now expected to recover. Valdais held farther south, with enough force that no one sensible mistook a retreat for a disappearance. The royal accounts were beginning to catch up with the promises that had made the battle possible.

The carriage had reached Constano. The physician advised two days’ rest before bringing him to Tarlington, and several more afterward. Jaennis’s messenger reported that he had asked for the grain agents’ replies before asking for fresh clothes. Elyas hoped someone had given him both.

A second message named the missing tutor among people sheltered upriver. He had left the warehouse with two children belonging to another household and followed their mother north by a different road. Mara sat down and read the name again. She found it among the names on her own list and marked him safe.

The court’s notices arrived with the same post. Fayd’s appointment had been confirmed. Lyra wrote separately, describing her new room and the hearings without saying that either made her comfortable. At the end she asked after Mara and told Berent that Pellam had found a place for the refugees’ lists among the records of missing people. Families were still being matched to names.

Elyas put that letter where he could answer it.

Constano arrived on a dry afternoon, when the courtyard could be crossed without laying boards over the mud.

Elyas went down at the sound of the carriage. He found his uncle arguing with the physician about whether he could manage the step. The physician had already put a broad stool beneath it and stood aside with the air of a man prepared to wait longer than his patient.

“I have been getting out of carriages all my life,” Constano said.

“Then this one should present no difficulty if we take our time.”

His uncle saw Elyas and raised his eyebrows. Elyas kept his face solemn. Together they helped him down, one hand offered at the elbow and no attempt to lift him before he asked.

Matteo followed, his arm still supported. Lucrezia got out after him with the youngest child asleep against her shoulder. The other children had travelled with Sella in the second carriage and were already asking where they would sleep. Aldith stood beside the baggage until the three rescued books had been carried indoors.

Oria came down the steps. She and Constano looked at one another for a moment before she embraced him. He bent his head over hers. Elyas turned away to take a bag from the driver, giving them as much privacy as a crowded courtyard allowed.

Inside, Constano asked after Seniro before asking for a chair. Oria answered the first question and supplied the second. His foot had been wrapped in clean linen beneath a broad shoe which fastened with ties. Elyas had never seen him wear anything so plain.

“Your daughter chose those?” he asked.

“She chose two pairs. One would fit a man considerably larger than I am.”

“Did you tell her?”

“I thanked her.”

Aldith brought the book containing Jaennis’s letters. Constano opened it, checked that a small folded drawing was still inside, and put it on the table. The physician asked whether he would rest now. He said he would, and immediately asked Elyas about the battle.

Elyas gave him the roads first. He had learned enough to know that his uncle would want to see where the supplies had gone before he could understand where the men had fought. Constano listened without interruption until Elyas mentioned Lisle.

“I warned Seniro against that passage.”

“He had your letter afterward.”

“How long afterward?”

“After the fighting.”

His uncle looked down at the book.

“The grain agent answered me. Through Fayd.”

“Yes.”

“Did the grain arrive?”

“Some of it. I know there was bread at the eastern camp.”

“That is a useful thing to know.”

He said it without bitterness. Elyas found the restraint harder to bear than a complaint would have been.

Mara came in with a basin and a clean cloth. She had been helping Sella settle the children, and one of their ribbons had caught on the cuff of her gown. She stopped when she saw the open book.

“Shall I come back?”

“No, please,” Constano said. “Lady Mara, is the water for me?”

She looked at him. Elyas waited, suddenly aware of every person within hearing.

“It is.”

“Then I would be grateful. I have been washing in water supplied by people who have had to carry it too far to heat it as well.”

She set the basin where he could reach it and helped undo the fastening at his wrist. He let her. When his hands were clean, he asked about the household she and Elyas meant to establish.

“We’re looking at a house,” she said.

“Good. Ask about the well before you admire the stable.”

“I have.”

He looked at Elyas then, mildly amused. “Of course you have.”

After supper, when the children were asleep and the physician had finally prevailed, Elyas found his uncle’s lamp still lit. He knocked and went in.

Constano was writing to Jaennis. The letter was short. Beside it lay the old household board, brought from the study and placed within reach. No pieces had been moved.

“Oria thought I might want it,” he said.

“Do you?”

“Not tonight.”

Elyas sat. In the passage a servant carried the last warm jug towards the Tirenos’ rooms. Somewhere upstairs one of the children began crying in sleep and was soothed before fully waking.

“Any word from Riarhin?” Constano asked.

“None here.”

“I wrote before I went to Fairport.”

“I know.”

“Perhaps she hasn’t received it.”

“Perhaps.”

His uncle put down the pen. There was no useful assurance to give. Elyas stayed while the ink dried and then carried the letter downstairs, where someone would see that it left in the morning.

On the next clear morning he and Mara went to see the third house.

It was smaller than the owner had implied and larger than they needed immediately. The current tenant showed them a patch of damp behind a cupboard before showing them anything he liked. His wife corrected his account of the well. Both seemed pleased when Mara asked about the garden rather than the view.

There was a pear tree against the south wall. Its branches had been trained badly and then left alone, with results the tenant described as vigorous.

Elyas stood beneath it while Mara inspected the kitchen.

He could hear her laughing at something the woman said. Berent was in the yard, gravely discussing the stable door with the tenant as if its hinge might determine the future of the house. There would be expenses they had not anticipated. Letters they would dislike. A wedding to arrange, and people who would find the arrangement an offence directed personally at them.

Mara came out carrying a small pear.

“Last of them,” she said. “She said to try it.”

He took a bite and immediately regretted the size of it.

“Good?”

He could not answer while swallowing.

She laughed and took the pear back. “Perhaps we should learn to preserve them.”

“Perhaps we should cut the tree down.”

“Certainly not.”

“So you’ve decided?”

“About the tree.”

He put an arm around her. She leaned against him and took a much smaller bite, making a face all the same.

“We’ll have to fix the roof,” she said.

“Yes.”

“And the well needs a new cover.”

“Yes.”

“And you can’t buy six horses because you’ve found six stalls.”

“Five?”

“Elyas.”

He kissed her. From the yard came the sound of Berent demonstrating the faulty hinge, followed by the tenant explaining that it had always done that.

Mara rested her forehead against his for a moment, then drew back.

“Come and look at the kitchen.”

He went with her.
